Annealed almost periodic entropy
Tim Austin

Abstract




Chapter 1

Introduction

Entropy for unitary representations

Fix a countable group
\Gamma
, and consider actions of two different kinds: measure-preserving actions on probability spaces (ergodic theory); and unitary actions on Hilbert spaces (representation theory). The analogy between these settings is widely appreciated. For example, it is illustrated clearly by Kechris’ adaptation of the relation of weak containment to measure-preserving systems
[
111
,
42
]
.

To develop this analogy, we identify certain common constructs on each side as follows. Observables (equivalently, finite measurable partitions) of a probability space are analogous to vectors in Hilbert space, or more generally to finite tuples of vectors. If
(X,\mu,T)
is a measure-preserving
\Gamma
-system and
\alpha:X\to A
is an observable, then it generates the shift-system
(A^{\Gamma},\alpha^{\Gamma}_{\ast}\mu,S)
, where
\alpha^{\Gamma}_{\ast}\mu
is the law of the
\Gamma
-indexed stochastic process
(\alpha\circ T^{g}:\ g\in\Gamma)
. If
\pi
is a unitary representation of
\Gamma
and
v\in H_{\pi}
, then it defines the positive definite function
\langle\pi(g)v,v\rangle
. This is the analog of a joint distribution. Its construction can be extended to finite tuples of vectors by considering matrix-valued positive definite functions.

Notions of entropy have come to play a central role in ergodic theory. These include the original Kolmogorov–Sinai entropy of a single probability-preserving transformations and its extension to actions of countable amenable groups, and also Lewis Bowen’s more recent notion of sofic entropy and its annealed version (originally called the ‘f-invariant’)
[
28
,
31
]
. The recent work
[
8
]
pursued these notions of entropy across the analogy from ergodic theory to representation theory. This led to new quantities that can be defined for positive definite functions associated to representations. It turns out that they enjoy a range of properties that continue the analogy with their predecessors in ergodic theory. But there are also new phenomena that appear for representations, and new arguments are available via the machinery of operator algebras. In particular, the most substantial theorems in
[
8
]
show that these entropy values for representations are often given by certain Fuglede–Kadison determinants. These results are interpreted as various non-commutative generalizations of Szegő’s limit theorem for positive definite Toeplitz determinants.

In
[
8
]
, the analog of sofic entropy is called almost periodic (‘AP’) entropy. It is defined in the generality of matrix-valued completely positive maps on a separable, unital C*-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
. To define it, one must first introduce an auxiliary ‘AP sequence’, meaning a sequence of finite-dimensional representations of
\mathfrak{A}
whose dimensions diverge. Along this AP sequence, one looks for finite-dimensional ‘models’ of a desired positive functional
\varphi
on
\mathfrak{A}
, and then AP entropy is defined in terms of the growth rates of the volumes of the sets of such models. This is the analog of how a sofic approximation is used to define sofic entropy, and is the reason for the term ‘almost periodic’.

After some preparations in Part
I
, the main new contributions of the present work are in Parts
II
and
III
.

Random AP sequences

Part
II
extends the theory of AP entropy from
[
8
]
to allow AP sequences
\boldsymbol{\pi}=(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
that are themselves random: see Section
5.1
. The main point is the resulting interaction between (i) the law of the chosen random AP sequence and (ii) the infinitary positive functional
\varphi
that one is trying to approximate. A range of new relationships emerge. The result is that we define two new entropy notions for a random AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}
and a positive functional
\varphi
.

The first is the ‘annealed AP entropy’
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
, which is introduced carefully in Section
5.3
. It is obtained by simply inserting an expectation over
\boldsymbol{\pi}
into the definition of deterministic AP entropy. Annealed AP entropy is the analog of Bowen’s ‘annealed sofic entropy’ from
[
31
]
(originally referred to as the ‘f-invariant’; see the discussion of terminology in
[
9
]
). Many of our results about it lie parallel to that theory, but sometimes with quite different proofs.

To be more explicit, let
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
be the space of completely positive maps from
\mathfrak{A}
to
\mathrm{M}_{k}
. For each
n
and any subset
O
of
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
, let

\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O):=\big\{[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]\in\mathbf{C}^{n}:\ [\langle\pi_{n}(\bullet)v_{j},v_{i}\rangle]_{i,j=1}^{k}\in O\big\}.

Now fix one element
\varphi
of
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
. We define its
annealed almost periodic entropy
along
\boldsymbol{\pi}
to be

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi):=\inf_{O}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\mathbf{E}\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(n)^{k}},

(1.1)

where
v(n)
is the volume of the unit ball in
2n
real dimensions, and where the infimum ranges over all neighbourhoods
O
of
\varphi
. The expectation refers to the random choice of
\pi_{n}
. We borrow the term ‘annealed’ for these averages from similar calculations in statistical physics: see the discussion following Definition
5.5
and in
[
131
, Section I.1]
.

Our second new entropy notion is the ‘zeroth-order AP entropy’
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
. It governs the probability as
n\to\infty
that
\varphi
approximately appears as the positive functional associated to
\pi_{n}
by some vector in that representation: see Section
5.4
for the precise definition. Zeroth-order AP entropy is more novel. Unlike annealed AP entropy, it depends only on the GNS representation of
\varphi
, and this leads to a general definition of a new invariant of separable representations under approximate equivalence.

After introducing these quantities, each of the three remaining chapters in Part
II
introduces an additional property that a random almost periodic sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}=(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
might possess, and derives consequences for its entropy functionals if it does.

Chapter
6
defines convergence of
\boldsymbol{\pi}
‘in the LD sense’. This is inspired by the definition of a large deviations principle (see Definition
D.1
). It is shown to yield a large deviations principle itself for the ‘upper tails’ of the representations
\pi_{n}
in the quotient topology (Theorem
6.5
). This, in turns, implies a large deviations principle for the associated operator norms of images of elements of
\mathfrak{A}
(Proposition
6.6
).

Chapter
7
begins with a different mode of convergence for
\boldsymbol{\pi}
: sufficiently fast convergence in probability of the associated tracial states
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}
to a limiting tracial state in
\mathfrak{A}_{+}^{\ast}
. The specific assumption we need is ‘superexponential convergence in probability’. If this holds, then we can deduce a tight connection between annealed and zeroth-order AP entropies: a formula relating them together with a Fuglede–Kadison determinant that we call the ‘three-entropy formula’ (Theorem
7.2
). When this holds, it has several consequences for the properties of both entropy functionals (Sections
7.3
and
7.4
).

Chapter
8
we consider the possibility that either the annealed or the zeroth-order AP entropy functional defined by
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is additive. If
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially in probability, then these two properties are actually equivalent (Proposition
8.1
), and if all of these properties hold then we say that
\boldsymbol{\pi}
‘has additive entropy functionals’. The major consequence of this assumption is that we can promote our quotient-topology large deviations principle to the strong-quotient topology, which is both stronger and better behaved (Theorem
8.10
).

Random AP sequences for free groups

With this general theory in hand, in Part
III
we restrict our attention to unitary representations of a group
\Gamma
that is freely generated by a finite set
S
. (Much of our work should have straightforward extensions to the free group of countable infinite rank, but the analysis may be complicated by technicalities such as additional convergence issues, and we do not address these here.)

The group
\Gamma
has many unitary representations in any desired dimension, since independent generators can be chosen arbitrarily. In particular, for each positive integer
n
, we can let
(\pi_{n}(s):\ s\in S)
be a tuple of
n
-by-
n
unitary matrices chosen independently at random from Haar measure, and then let
\pi_{n}
be the resulting unitary representation of the whole of
\Gamma
. These are the random AP sequences that we focus on in Part
III
. (They are also central examples for Voiculescu’s theory of ‘free probability’
[
179
,
178
]
, which already includes a notion of ‘free entropy’, but the variants of AP entropy that we study here are quite different.) To simplify notation, let us shorten
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
to
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
and
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
to
\mathrm{h}^{0}
for this sequence.

We find that this particular sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}
does satisfy all of the extra properties that were studied in Part
II
. As a result we obtain new probability laws for such independent random unitary matrices, including a large deviations principle that enhances the Collins–Male strong convergence theorem for such matrices (as well as giving a new, independent proof of that theorem itself).

These results follow from much more precise calculations that are possible with this random AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}
. These begin with a kind of first-moment calculation (explained further below) leads to concrete formulas for
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
. The next theorem states two of these; some others are derived in Section
12.3
. Let
B_{n}
denote the closed
n
-ball around the identity in the left Cayley graph of
\Gamma
. Let
\varphi[B_{n}]
denote the positive semi-definite
(k|B_{n}|)
-by-
(k|B_{n}|)
matrix determined by the elements
{\varphi(g^{-1}h)\in\mathrm{M}_{k}}
for
g,h\in B_{n}
, and similarly for other finite subsets of
\Gamma
.

Theorem A
.

The function
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
takes values in
[-\infty,\infty)
for each
k
. For any positive definite map
\varphi:\Gamma\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
, the value
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)
is equal to both

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Big(\log\mathrm{det}\,\varphi[B_{n}]-\sum_{s\in S}\log\mathrm{det}\,\varphi[B_{n}\cap sB_{n}]\Big)

(1.2)

and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Big(\sum_{s\in S}\log\mathrm{det}\,\varphi[B_{n}\cup sB_{n}]-(2r-1)\log\mathrm{det}\,\varphi[B_{n}]\Big)

(1.3)

(interpreting these as
-\infty
if any of the determinants appearing here equals
0
).

Before reaching this theorem, in Chapter
10
, we introduce an ‘entropy’ formalism for logarithms of determinants. This idea is classical, but the notation that accompanies it lets us write and manipulate quantities such as those above more easily. According to that formalism, formula (
1.3
) is the direct analog of Bowen’s original definition of annealed sofic entropy in
[
31
]
. More recently, essentially the same formulas as Bowen’s have appeared independently in the study of certain random matrix models constructed over sparse locally-tree-like random graphs: see, for example, the first displayed equation on
[
25
, p4]
, and the further references discussed in that paper. In fact, all of these formulas can be seen as descendants of the Bethe and Kikuchi entropy functionals from statistical physics: see the discussion in
[
9
, Subsection 3.3]
.

Large deviations principles

One of our first results about
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
shows that, in case
\varphi
is unital, we can replace
\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}/v(n)^{k}
in (
1.1
) with the unique unitary-invariant probability measure
m_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}
on the space
\mathrm{U}(k,n)
of all orthonormal
k
-tuples in
\mathbf{C}^{n}
(see Lemma
5.8
). This substitution of measures reveals a crucial advantage of working with annealed averages. The Fubini–Tonelli theorem lets us exchange probability and expectation like this:

\mathbf{E}m_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)=\mathbf{E}\int 1_{\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}\,dm_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}=\int\mathbf{P}(\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}\in O)\,dm_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}(V).

Since the distribution of
\pi_{n}
is invariant under the transitive action of
\mathrm{U}(n)
on
\mathrm{U}(k,n)
, the last integrand above is actually constant, and so it simply equals
\mathbf{P}(\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}\in O)
for some fixed choice of
V\in\mathrm{U}(k,n)
. Intuitively, the random function

\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}(g):=V^{\ast}\pi_{n}(g)V\qquad(g\in\Gamma)

describes how the random unitary matrices that generate
\pi_{n}
‘sit together’ through their actions on a fixed orthonormal
k
-tuple of vectors
V
. As a result, we see that
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
is really an exponent that governs certain probabilities relating to
\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}
as
n\to\infty
.

With this in mind, we might hope that
\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}
actually satisfies a large deviations principle with
-\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
as rate function. This turns out to be the case, and its proof comes with the calculations that lead back back to (
1.2
) and (
1.3
) above.

Our main result here is actually a large deviations principle for certain finite-dimensional restrictions of the random functions
\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}
. These restrictions are over subsets
F
of
\Gamma
that are ‘grounded’. This means that
F
contains
e
, is finite, and is connected in the left Cayley graph of
\Gamma
. These sets are introduced more carefully in Section
9.1
.

Fix
V\in\mathrm{U}(k,n)
. Given a grounded set
F
and also the random function
\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}
, consider the random finite matrices

Q^{\pi_{n}}[F]:=[\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}(g^{-1}h)]_{g,h\in F}.

(1.4)

These lie in the compact set
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
of positive semidefinite
F
-by-
F
block matrices that (i) have each block equal to
I_{k}
and (ii) are invariant under left-translation by
\Gamma
as far as possible (so they are ‘Toeplitz on
\Gamma
’). See Section
9.2
for a careful introduction.

Define the following function of an element
q
of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
:

h_{F}(q):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\log\mathrm{det}\,q-\sum_{s\in S}\log\mathrm{det}\,q[F\cap sF]&\quad\hbox{if $q$ is nonsingular}\\
-\infty&\quad\hbox{if $q$ is singular.}\end{array}\right.

Notice that
h_{F}(q)>-\infty
if and only if
q
is nonsingular, and that
h_{F}(q)
agrees with the expression inside the limit (
1.2
) when
F=B_{n}
and
\varphi
is unital.

Theorem B
.

Fix a grounded subset
F
of
\Gamma
and an element
q\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
.

a.

The random Gram matrix
Q^{\pi_{n}}[F]
is almost surely nonsingular for all sufficiently large
n
.

b.

For every neighbourhood
O
of
q
, we have

\log\mathbf{P}\big(Q^{\pi_{n}}[F]\in O\big)\geq h_{F}(q)\cdot n-o(n)\quad\hbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

c.

For every real number
a>h_{F}(q)
, there is a neighbourhood
O
of
q
such that

\log\mathbf{P}\big(Q^{\pi_{n}}[F]\in O\big)\leq an+o(n)\quad\hbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

Because
V
is fixed but
\pi_{n}
is random in this theorem, we sometimes refer to it as the ‘chosen-tuple’ large deviations principle. Theorem
B
implies a large deviations principle for the whole of
\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}
with rate function
-\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
by a standard argument about projective limits: see Corollary
12.2
.

We actually complete the proof of Theorem
B
first (in Chapter
11
and Section
12.1
), and then use it to prove Theorem
A
. Grounded subsets of
\Gamma
can be grown from
\{e\}
one element at a time (in the sense explained in Section
9.1
), and the proof of Theorem
B
is by induction along such a process. We prove formula (
1.2
) of Theorem
A
as a corollary of Theorem
B
. We then prove formula (
1.3
) as a corollary of formula (
1.2
) and some further manipulations in Section
12.3
. Related manipulations also lead to some other formulas for
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
, including two different series expansions for it in Corollaries
12.3
and
12.14
. One of these is the analog of Seward’s formula for annealed sofic entropy from
[
159
, Theorem 1.7]
, so we call it the ‘Seward expansion’. It plays an important role later in the work.

Our proofs of Theorem
B
and these other results all depend on a way of building up a positive definite function over a finitely generated free group through a sequence of enlargements of underyling grounded sets. At each stage, a space of possible extensions of a partially-defined positive definite function is parameterized by certain contraction matrices. These provide an analog for free groups of the classical Verblunsky coefficients for a sequence of orthogonal polynomials on the unit circle: see, for instance,
[
162
]
. We therefore refer to these contraction matrices as Verblunsky coefficients in our setting as well (Definition
9.5
). (Beware that they also depend on choosing a certain enumeration of the group
\Gamma
in advance.)

When we obtain
\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}
at random as above, it is described by a random sequence of Verblunsky coefficients. Our main technical step towards Theorem
B
is the result that any finite initial segment of this random sequence of Verblunsky coefficients becomes independent once
n
is large enough: see Theorem
11.6
and Corollary
11.7
. This is the analog for free groups of a theorem of Killip and Nenciu from
[
112
]
for single random unitary matrices, and our use of it to prove Theorem
B
resembles recent work on a large-deviations approach to Szegő’s limit theorems
[
82
,
33
]
.

Having developed the basic theory of
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
this far, we can develop various consequences by combining those theorems with the general theory of annealed and zeroth-order AP entropy developed in Part
II
. First we study the probability that a given positive definite function
\varphi:\Gamma\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
is asymptotically associated to our random AP sequence
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
at all. For the sake of brevity, we describe our main results here less formally than above, referring to the main text for details. Assume that
\varphi
is unital for simplicity. Then, roughly put, we show that small neighbourhoods
O
of
\varphi
satisfy

\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)\approx e^{\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)\cdot n+o(n)}\qquad\hbox{as}\ n\to\infty,

where the exponent can be obtained from Theorem
A
and the relation

\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi):=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(t\varphi+(1-t)(\tau\otimes I_{k}))

(see Lemma
7.3
), and where
\tau
is the regular character on
\Gamma
.

Several further consequences follow from this interpretation of
\mathrm{h}^{0}
. First, it provides the exponential decay of upper-tail probabilities for the random operator norms
\|\pi_{n}(a)\|
for any
a\in C^{\ast}\Gamma
. This large deviations principle is a natural companion to the Collins–Male theorem
[
51
]
giving strong asymptotic freeness of these random representations. Our study of
\mathrm{h}^{0}
also leads to a generalization of Veblunsky’s form of Szegő’s theorem to free groups, extending the discussion of this analogy from
[
8
]
.

Finally, Chapter
13
proves the last major theorem of our work. This identifies which positive definite functions
\varphi
have
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)=0
, or equivalently which ones are asymptotically associated to the random sequence
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
with high probability.

Theorem C
.

Let
\varphi:\Gamma\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
be positive definite. If
\varphi
is tempered, then for every neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi
we have

\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)\to 1.

On the other hand, if
\varphi
is not tempered, then there are a neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi
and a positive value
c
such that

\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)\leq e^{-cn+o(n)}.

Theorem
C
is not really a new result. The case
k=1
is equivalent to the Collins–Male theorem from
[
51
]
. That in turn is deduced from the analogous theorem for GUE random matrices due to Haagerup and Thorbjørnsen
[
96
]
, which was the first real breakthrough in this area when it appeared. Other proofs have been given more recently as well. In addition, the case of larger
k
is reduced to the case
k=1
fairly easily. However, our new proof is independent of those previous works, and seems to have little in common with them. Very roughly, the existing methods in this area depend on controlling the expected traces of powers or other transforms of a random matrix
\pi_{n}(a)
so well that one can infer bounds on expected operator norms as a consequence. By contrast, annealed AP entropy lets us work directly with positive definite functions on
\Gamma
, without ever picking an element
a
of
C^{\ast}\Gamma
and considering the resulting random operator
\pi_{n}(a)
or its norm.

The second part of Theorem
C
requires most of the work. It amounts to proving that
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)<0
whenever
\varphi
is unital and not tempered. We do this by combining the Seward expansion of
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
with a classic characterization of tempered positive definite functions on finitely generated free groups due to Haagerup
[
94
]
. With both of those in hand, the remaining work depends on some careful but elementary estimates. It occupies Sections
13.1
–
13.4
, and is following by a discussion in Section
13.5
.

Acknowledgements

This work depended on insightful conversations and correspondence with many people. In this regard I am particularly grateful to Nir Avni, Uri Bader, Lewis Bowen, Peter Burton, Amir Dembo, Michael Magee, Magdalena Musat, Narutaka Ozawa, Sorin Popa, Mikael Rørdam, Brandon Seward, Dima Shlyakhtenko, Dan Timotin, Hugo Woerderman, and Ofer Zeitouni.

Part I
PREPARATIONS

Chapter 2

Groups, C*-algebras and representations

Throughout this book we assume familiarity with functional analysis at about the level of
[
149
]
,
[
153
]
, or
[
52
]
. This includes the definitions and basic properties of C*-algebras and von Neumann algebras. We adhere rather strictly to the following convention:

•

C
∗
-algebras may exist
in the abstract
;

•

a von Neumann algebra is a weak-operator closed
\ast
-subalgebra of
\mathfrak{B}(H)
for some particular Hilbert space
H
.

Beyond these foundations, we use many standard but slightly more advanced results about C*-algebras and operator algebras. We recall several of these more carefully in this chapter. For definiteness, I cite the standard texts by Conway
[
53
]
, Dixmier
[
62
,
63
]
, Folland
[
77
]
or Mackey
[
124
]
wherever possible. Several of the sections below end with a review of more advanced references for the interested reader. Readers with experience in this area may prefer to skip this chapter and refer back to it.

2.1

C*-algebras and their representations

In this book,
\mathfrak{A}
always denotes a
separable
C
∗
-algebra. In addition, we always study separable representations, meaning that they act on separable complex Hilbert spaces. We also always assume that
\mathfrak{A}
is
unital
unless stated otherwise. We usually denote a representation by a single letter such as
\pi
, and then write its Hilbert space as
H_{\pi}
when it is needed. Parts
II
and
III
of the book are largely concerned with sequences of finite-dimensional representations together with their limits in various modes of convergence. All such limits are still separable, so assuming separability from the outset does not entail any real loss of generality.

Our guiding examples throughout are the group C
∗
-algebras
C^{\ast}\Gamma
of countable groups
\Gamma
. The algebra
C^{\ast}\Gamma
is the maximal C
∗
-completion of the group algebra
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
, so it is indeed separable and unital. Many of our results would also apply to the group C
∗
-algebras of locally compact, second countable groups. The construction and main properties of these can be found in
[
77
, Section 7.1]
or
[
62
, Section 13.9]
. Any unitary representation of
\Gamma
extends uniquely to a unital representation of
C^{\ast}\Gamma
, and any unital representation of
C^{\ast}\Gamma
arises uniquely in this way. With this in mind, we pass back and forth between groups and their C
∗
-algebras rather freely, for instance by using the same notation for a representation of either.

Once a particular representation
\pi
is being considered, operator theory provides many auxiliary constructions of operators in
\mathfrak{B}(H_{\pi})
, for instance via the Borel functional calculus or symmetry considerations. These often fall outside the operator-norm closure of
\pi(\mathfrak{A})
, which is a C
∗
-algebra, but within its bi-commutant
\pi(\mathfrak{A})^{\prime\prime}
, which agrees with the weak-operator closure of
\pi(\mathfrak{A})
and is a von Neumann algebra.

If
\pi
is a representation of
\mathfrak{A}
and
M
is a closed
\pi
-invariant subspace of
H_{\pi}
, then we write
\pi^{M}
for the associated
subrepresentation
. A representation
\pi
is
irreducible
if it nonzero but its only closed invariant subspaces are
\{0\}
and
H_{\pi}
.

We use
\oplus
for general direct sums in the categories of Hilbert spaces or representations. For a single representation
\pi
and any
k\in\{1,2,\dots\}\cup\{\infty\}
, we write
\pi^{\oplus k}
for the direct sum of
k
copies of
\pi
, using the indexing set
\mathbf{N}
when
k=\infty
. We call this the
k
-fold inflation
of
\pi
.

If
\pi
is a representation of
\mathfrak{A}
, then a subset
S
of
H
is
cyclic
for
\pi
if it is not contained in any proper closed invariant subspace of
H_{\pi}
, or equivalently if

\overline{\sum_{v\in S}\pi(\mathfrak{A})v}=H_{\pi}.

If
\pi
and
\rho
are two representations of
\mathfrak{A}
, then:

•

\pi
is
equivalent
to
\rho
, written
\pi\simeq\rho
, if there is a unitary operator from
H_{\pi}
to
H_{\rho}
that intertwines
\pi
with
\rho
;

•

\pi
is
contained
in
\rho
, written
\pi\lesssim\rho
, if
\pi
is equivalent to a subrepresentation of
\rho
;

•

\pi
and
\rho
are
disjoint
, written
\pi\spoon\rho
, if no nontrivial subrepresentations of them are equivalent.

Another possible relation is that
\pi
is contained in an inflation of
\rho
. We do not introduce a separate term for this relation. If
H_{\pi}
is separable, then we can take it to mean that
\pi\lesssim\rho^{\oplus\infty}
. However, we do use a term for the resulting equivalence relation:
\pi
and
\rho
are
quasi-equivalent
if each is contained in an inflation of the other. See
[
62
, Proposition 5.3.1]
for several alternative formulations of quasi-equivalence.

The next proposition is a fundamental way to decompose representations.

Proposition 2.1
.

If
\pi
and
\rho
are two representations of
\mathfrak{A}
, then
\pi
has a unique invariant subspace
M
such that
\pi^{M}\lesssim\rho^{\oplus\infty}
and
\pi^{M^{\perp}}\spoon\rho
. The orthogonal projection onto
M
lies in the centre of
\pi(\mathfrak{A})^{\prime\prime}
. ∎

For unitary representations of groups, this is
[
124
, Theorem 1.11]
; the proof given there handles general C
∗
-algebras without change. This result also appeared independently in
[
167
]
with a proof in terms of decompositions of the universal representation of
\mathfrak{A}
.

The notions above do not require that
\mathfrak{A}
be unital. If
\mathfrak{A}
is non-unital, or if
\mathfrak{A}
is unital but a representation
\pi
of it is non-unital, then
\pi
may be
degenerate
in the sense that

\overline{\mathrm{span}}\{\pi(a)v:\ a\in\mathfrak{A},\ v\in H_{\pi}\}

is a proper subspace of
H_{\pi}
. Equivalently, this holds if
H_{\pi}
itself is not cyclic for
\pi
. Otherwise
\pi
is called
non-degenerate
. If
\pi
is irreducible then it is either
0
or non-degenerate, since the closed subspace above is
\pi
-invariant.

The
type
of a representation
\pi
is the type of the von Neumann algebra
\pi(\mathfrak{A})^{\prime\prime}
(if it has one): see
[
62
, Chapter 5]
. In the sequel, our leading example is often the left regular representation
\lambda
of a finitely generated group
\Gamma
, and this is subject to a classic theorem of Thoma from
[
168
,
169
]
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated group. If
\Gamma
is virtually Abelian, then
\lambda
is of Type I, and otherwise it is of Type II. ∎

Notes and further references

Besides the references used above, the basic theory of unitary group representations and their relation to the group C
∗
-algebra are recounted succinctly in
[
16
, Appendices A–C]
or in more detail in
[
15
, Chapters 1 and 2]
. The reference
[
74
, Chapter VI]
is also largely focused on groups but admits the generality of arbitrary C
∗
-algebras.

Types of representations, and in particular a fuller account of Thoma’s theorems, are described in
[
15
, Sections 7.C,D]
.

2.2

Closed and primitive ideals

In this section, we do
not
assume that
\mathfrak{A}
is unital.

Throughout this book, an
ideal
in a C*-algebra is always two-sided and closed. The
primitive
ideals are precisely the kernels of irreducible representatives, that the space of these is called the
primitive ideal space
, and that by default it is endowed with the Jacobson topology
[
62
, Section 3.1]
.

If
\mathfrak{A}
is separable, then so is any irreducible representation, and hence any such representation is equivalent to one on a chosen separable ‘reference’ Hilbert space. We can therefore define the
set
of equivalence classes of irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{A}
. It is called the
spectrum
of
\mathfrak{A}
and denoted by
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
. We endow it with the usual Fell topology, which can be defined as the pull-back of the Jacobson or ‘hull-kernel’ topology under the map
[\pi]\mapsto\ker\pi
. See
[
62
, Chapter 3]
, which also presents various other constructions of the same topology. If
\mathfrak{A}=C^{\ast}\Gamma
, then
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
is called the
unitary dual
of
\Gamma
and denoted by
\widehat{\Gamma}
. See,
[
77
, Chapter 7]
or
[
62
, Chapter 18]
.

For any subset
S
of
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
, we define

\ker S:=\bigcap_{[\rho]\in S}\ker\rho.

On the other hand, for any ideal
\mathfrak{I}
of
\mathfrak{A}
, we define

\mathrm{hull}_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathfrak{I}:=\{[\rho]\in\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}:\ \rho|\mathfrak{I}=0\}=\{[\rho]\in\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}:\ \ker\rho\supset\mathfrak{I}\}.

The Fell topology on
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
is defined so that the closure of any
S\subset\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
is equal to

\mathrm{hull}_{\mathfrak{A}}\ker S.

In particular, if
\pi
is any representation, then its
support
is defined by

\mathrm{spt}\,\pi:=\mathrm{hull}_{\mathfrak{A}}\ker\pi.

If
\pi^{\prime}
is another representation, then it is
weakly contained
in
\pi
if
\ker\pi^{\prime}\supset\ker\pi
. This is equivalent to
\mathrm{spt}\,\pi^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{spt}\,\pi
because of a standard connection with positive functionals: see
[
62
, Theorem 3.4.4]
or Section
3.1
below.

If
\mathfrak{I}
is an ideal of
\mathfrak{A}
, then representations of
\mathfrak{I}
relate to representations of
\mathfrak{A}
like this
[
62
, Proposition 2.10.4]
:

Proposition 2.3
.

If
\pi
is a non-degenerate representation of
\mathfrak{I}
, then it has a unique extension to a representation of
\mathfrak{A}
on
H_{\pi}
. ∎

Any ideal
\mathfrak{I}
defines a partition of
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
into
\mathrm{hull}_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathfrak{I}
and its complement. By applying Proposition
2.3
and keeping track of the Fell topologies, this partition has the following properties (see
[
62
, Propositions 2.11.2 and 3.2.1]
).

Proposition 2.4
.

The subset
\mathrm{hull}_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathfrak{I}
is closed in
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
. For the Fell topologies, the map of lifting representations defines a homeomorphism

\widehat{\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{I}}\to\mathrm{hull}_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathfrak{I},

and the map of restricting representations defines a homeomorphism

\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus\mathrm{hull}_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathfrak{I}\to\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}.

∎

2.3

Elementary algebras and stiff parts

For any separable Hilbert space, we write
\mathfrak{K}(H)
for the C*-subalgebra of all compact operators in
\mathfrak{B}(H)
. A C*-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is called
elementary
if it is isomorphic
\mathfrak{K}(H)
for some
H
, and
semi-elementary
if it is isomorphic to a restricted direct sum of countably many elementary algebras: that is,

\mathfrak{A}\cong\Big\{(a_{n})_{n}\in\prod_{n}\mathfrak{A}_{n}:\ \{n:\ \|a_{n}\|>\varepsilon\}\ \hbox{is finite for every}\ \varepsilon>0\Big\};

(2.1)

see
[
62
, Subsection 1.9.14]
. (The term ‘elementary’ is standard
[
62
, Subsection 4.1.1]
, but ‘semi-elementary’ is not.)

A C*-algebra is semi-elementary if and only if it is isomorphic to a closed subalgebra of an elementary algebra
[
53
, Theorem 16.18]
. If
\mathfrak{A}
is elementary, then its only ideals are
\{0\}
and
\mathfrak{A}
, and its only irreducible representation is given by its isomorphism to some
\mathfrak{K}(H)
, which is unique up to unitary equivalence. More generally, if
\mathfrak{A}
is the sum in (
2.1
), then its primitive ideals are those that are nonzero in only a single summand, and this leads to the following (see also
[
53
, Theorem 16.11]
):

Proposition 2.5
.

If
\mathfrak{A}
is the restricted direct sum of a countable family
(\mathfrak{A}_{n})
of elementary algebras, then
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
consists of one isolated point for each index
n
; in particular, its Fell topology is discrete.

Any representation of
\mathfrak{A}
decomposes as a direct sum of irreducible representations, uniquely up to equivalence. Such a representation consists of compact operators if and only if every irreducible summand has finite multiplicity. ∎

Now consider an arbitrary separable representation
\pi
of
\mathfrak{A}
. Its
stiff ideal
is

\pi^{-1}[\mathfrak{K}(H_{\pi})]=\{a\in\mathfrak{A}:\ \pi(a)\ \hbox{is compact}\}.

The
stiff part
of
\pi
is the invariant subspace

M:=\overline{\mathrm{span}}\{\pi(a)v:\ a\in\pi^{-1}[\mathfrak{K}(H_{\pi})],\ v\in H_{\pi}\}.

Put another way,
\pi^{M}|\pi^{-1}[\mathfrak{K}(H_{\pi})]
is the non-degenerate part of
\pi|\pi^{-1}[\mathfrak{K}(H_{\pi})]
. We call
\pi
itself
stiff
if it is equal to its stiff part.

Beware that
\pi^{M^{\perp}}
may have a nontrivial stiff part of its own, but for a strictly larger stiff ideal. So the decomposition into
M
and
M^{\perp}
is not generally a decomposition into a stiff representation and a representation with no stiff part. On the other hand, any subrepresentation of a stiff representation is stiff.

Remark 2.6
.

In some references, including
[
6
]
and
[
53
]
, the stiff part of
\pi
is called the ‘essential part’. This seems unfortunate, because the case of a single unitary transformation reduces to the study of the essential spectrum
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(U)
, but with a mismatch in which objects are labeled ‘essential’. This is why I use the non-standard term ‘stiff’ instead.
\lhd

We can extend some conclusions from Proposition
2.5
to the stiff parts of more general representations. The next few results are partly covered by
[
175
, Corollary 1.6]
and the paragraph that precedes it. We return to this connection in Subsection
3.2
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
S
be a closed subset of
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
, let
[\pi]
be a stiff element of
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus S
, and let
\mathfrak{I}:=\pi^{-1}[\mathfrak{K}(H_{\pi})]\cap\ker S
. Then
\pi|\mathfrak{I}\neq 0
.

Proof.

Since
\pi
is stiff and irreducible,
\pi[\mathfrak{A}]\cap\mathfrak{K}(H_{\pi})
must equal the whole of
\mathfrak{K}(H_{\pi})
, by Proposition
2.5
. It therefore contains a non-decreasing sequence of finite-rank projections
P_{i}=\pi(e_{i})

(i=1,2,\dots)
that converge to
I_{H_{\pi}}
in the strong operator topology. On the other hand,
S
is closed and it does not contain
[\pi]
, so there exists
a\in\ker S\setminus\ker\pi
. Now we have

\pi(e_{i}a)x=P_{i}\pi(a)x\to\pi(a)x

for every
x\in H_{\pi}
. Since every product
e_{i}a
lies in
\mathfrak{I}
, this shows that
\pi|\mathfrak{I}\neq 0
.
∎

Corollary 2.8
.

Let
\pi
be a separable representation and let
[\rho]
be an isolated point of
\mathrm{spt}\,\pi
which is stiff. Let
m\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}\cup\{\infty\}
be the multiplicity of
\rho
in
\pi
, and let the resulting decomposition of
\pi
be
\pi^{\prime}\oplus\rho^{\oplus m}
, so
\pi^{\prime}\spoon\rho
.

a.

We have
m\geq 1
.

b.

The support of
\pi^{\prime}
does not contain
[\rho]
.

c.

The summand
\rho^{\oplus m}
is contained in the stiff part of
\pi
if and only if
m<\infty
.

Proof.

Part (a).
Let
S:=\mathrm{spt}\,\pi\setminus\{[\rho]\}
, and let
\mathfrak{I}:=\rho^{-1}[\mathfrak{K}(H_{\rho})]\cap\ker S
. The set
S
is closed by assumption, and
[\rho]
is a stiff element of
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus S
, so Lemma
2.7
tells us that
\rho|\mathfrak{I}\neq 0
. On the other hand,

\ker(\rho|\mathfrak{I})=\ker\rho\cap\ker S=\ker\mathrm{spt}\,\pi=\ker\pi.

Therefore
\rho|\mathfrak{I}
defines an isomorphism from
\mathfrak{I}/\ker\pi
to
\mathfrak{K}(K_{\rho})
, so
\mathfrak{I}/\ker\pi
is an elementary algebra. Since
\pi|\mathfrak{I}
also defines a representation of this algebra, Proposition
2.5
gives that
\rho|\mathfrak{I}
is contained in
\pi|\mathfrak{I}
. By Proposition
2.3
, this implies that
\rho
is contained in
\pi
, so
m\geq 1
.

Part (b).
Since
\pi^{\prime}\lesssim\pi
, we have
\mathrm{spt}\,\pi^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{spt}\,\pi
. In addition,
\mathrm{spt}\,\pi^{\prime}
cannot contain
[\rho]
, for otherwise
[\rho]
would be an isolated point of this set, and part (a) would give
\rho\lesssim\pi^{\prime}
, contradicting the definition of
\pi^{\prime}
.

Part (c).
If
m=\infty
, then
\rho^{\oplus m}(a)
is either
0
or non-compact for any
a\in\mathfrak{A}
, so this cannot be contained in the stiff part of
\pi
. On the other hand, if
m<\infty
, then the ideal
\mathfrak{I}
contains elements
a
such that
\pi^{\prime}(a)=0
and
\rho^{\oplus m}(a)
is compact, so these witness that
\rho^{\oplus m}
is in the stiff part of
\pi
.
∎

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
\pi
be a separable representation with stiff ideal
\mathfrak{I}
and stiff part
M
. Let
S
be the set of all
[\kappa]\in\mathrm{spt}\,\pi
with the following properties:

•

\kappa
is stiff;

•

[\kappa]
is an isolated point of
\mathrm{spt}\,\pi
;

•

if
m(\kappa)
is the multiplicity of
\kappa
in
\pi
, then
m(\kappa)<\infty
.

Then

\pi^{M}\simeq\bigoplus_{[\kappa]\in S}\kappa^{\oplus m(\kappa)},

Proof.

Since
\pi^{M}|\mathfrak{I}
is non-degenerate and
\pi^{M^{\perp}}|\mathfrak{I}=0
, these two restrictions are disjoint, and hence so are
\pi^{M}
and
\pi^{M^{\perp}}
. For every
[\kappa]\in S
, Corollary
2.8
implies that
\kappa^{\oplus m(\kappa)}
is contained in
\pi^{M}
. Therefore it remains to prove that these summands exhaust
\pi^{M}
.

By replacing
\mathfrak{A}
with
\mathfrak{A}/\ker\pi
if necessary and calling on the first homeomorphism from Proposition
2.4
, we may assume that
\pi
is injective and hence
\mathrm{spt}\,\pi=\mathfrak{A}
.

Now
\pi|\mathfrak{I}
is an injective representation of
\mathfrak{I}
, and
\pi^{M}|\mathfrak{I}
is its non-degenerate part. Therefore
\pi^{M}|\mathfrak{I}
is semi-elementary, and so Proposition
2.5
tells us that
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}
consists of isolated points.

Also,
\pi|\mathfrak{I}
is a non-degenerate representation of
\mathfrak{I}
by compact operators, so it is a direct sum of elements of
\widehat{\mathfrak{I}}
, each with finite multiplicity, by the second part of Proposition
2.5
. Applying the second homeomorphism from Proposition
2.4
, and then Proposition
2.3
, this becomes a decomposition of
\pi^{M}
itself as a direct sum of isolated stiff elements of the open set
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}\setminus\mathrm{hull}_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathfrak{I}
, each with finite multiplicity.
∎

2.4

Positive functionals and completely positive maps

If
\pi
is a representation of
\mathfrak{A}
and
v\in H_{\pi}
, then the formula

\Phi^{\pi}_{v}(a):=\langle\pi(a)v,v\rangle\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{A})

defines a positive linear functional on
\mathfrak{A}
. All positive linear functionals arise this way because of the GNS construction.

More generally, let
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}\in H_{\pi}
, and regard the tuple
V:=[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
as a linear map from
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
to
H_{\pi}
. Then the formula

\Phi^{\pi}_{V}(a):=V^{\ast}\pi(a)V=[\langle\pi(a)v_{j},v_{i}\rangle]_{i,j=1}^{k}

(2.2)

defines a linear map from
\mathfrak{A}
to
\mathrm{M}_{k}
. Notice that the order of the indices in each matrix entry matches the convention for the Gram matrix of a tuple of vectors in (
A.1
). We sometimes write
\Phi^{\pi}_{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}}
instead of
\Phi^{\pi}_{V}
.

The map
\Phi^{\pi}_{V}:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
lies in the class of
completely positive
maps. The basic theory of these can be found in
[
53
, Section 34]
; this particular instance of them is
[
53
, Example 34.3(b)]
. We say that the completely positive map in (
2.2
) is
associated to
\pi
by
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
. Alternatively, we adapt a term from information theory by calling
\Phi^{\pi}_{V}
the
type
of the tuple
V
in
\pi
(compare the usage in
[
56
, Section 11.1]
, for example). Much of our work later concerns classifying the tuples in a representation space according to their type.

In this work we do not need the definition of complete positivity directly, but only a few of its basic consequences. First, completely positive maps are continuous
[
53
, Proposition 33.4]
. Let
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})
be the space of all continuous linear maps from
\mathfrak{A}
to
\mathrm{M}_{k}
, endowed with the pointwise topology (equivalently, the weak
∗
topology applied to each matrix entry), and let
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
be the subset of all completely positive (not just positive) ones. The definition of complete positivity consists of a family of closed linear inequalities, showing that
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
is a closed cone in
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})
for the pointwise topology. Finally, a generalization of the GNS construction shows that any
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
has the form in (
2.2
) for some representation
\pi
and tuple
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
. If this tuple is cyclic for
\pi
, then
\pi
and the tuple are unique up to unitary equivalence; in this case we call them a
minimal dilation
of
\varphi
, and often denote the representation by
\pi_{\varphi}
. This fact is a special case of Stinespring’s theorem
[
53
, Theorem 34.7]
.

A completely positive map
\varphi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
is called
normalized
if
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{k}\varphi(1)=1
, and
unital
if
\varphi(1)=I_{k}
. We denote the sets of these by
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\Sigma_{k}^{\mathrm{u}}(\mathfrak{A})
, respectively. In particular,
\Sigma_{1}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\Sigma_{1}^{\mathrm{u}}(\mathfrak{A})
are both just the usual state space of
\mathfrak{A}
. For each
k
, the set
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
is a compact and convex subset of
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})
, and
\Sigma_{k}^{\mathrm{u}}(\mathfrak{A})
is a further compact convex subset of
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
.

If
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
and
Q\in\mathrm{M}_{k}
, then the map defined by

\psi(a):=(Q^{\mathrm{T}})^{\ast}\varphi(a)Q^{\mathrm{T}}\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{A})

(2.3)

is also completely positive. This may be checked directly from the definition of complete positivity, or by observing the corresponding relationship between associating vectors. If
\varphi
is associated to a representation
\pi
by the tuple
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
, then the map
\psi
in (
2.3
) is associated to
\pi
by the
k
-tuple defined by

y_{i}:=\sum_{j}q_{ij}v_{j}\qquad(1\leq i\leq k).

Identifying such
k
-tuples with elements of
H_{\pi}\otimes\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
, and writing them as column vectors of height
k
with entries in
H_{\pi}
, we can alternatively write this new tuple as

\left[\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{k}\end{array}\right]:=(I_{H_{\pi}}\otimes Q)\left[\begin{array}[]{c}v_{1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{k}\end{array}\right].

(2.4)

(We put transposes in (
2.3
) in order to avoid them in (
2.4
).) If
\varphi(1)
is non-singular, then one can often use (
2.3
) with
Q=\varphi(1)^{-1/2}
to reduce results about
\varphi
to the unital case.

If
\Gamma
is a countable group, then any element
\varphi
of
\mathfrak{B}(C^{\ast}\Gamma,\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
may be restricted to define a map from
\Gamma
itself to
\mathrm{M}_{k}
:

g\mapsto\varphi(\delta_{g})\qquad(g\in\Gamma).

In the sequel we often write simply
\varphi(g)
instead of
\varphi(\delta_{g})
in this situation. If
\varphi=\Phi^{\pi}_{V}
for some representation
\pi
and
k
-tuple
V
as in (
2.2
), then this becomes

\Phi^{\pi}_{V}(g)=[\langle\pi(g)v_{j},v_{i}\rangle]\qquad(g\in\Gamma).

(2.5)

In representation theory, the function on
\Gamma
given by
\langle\pi(g)v,u\rangle
is called the
(u,v)
-matrix element
of the representation.

If a function
\varphi:\Gamma\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
arises this way, then it is a
positive definite
map (sometimes called a ‘map of positive type’). This means that it is bounded and satisfies

\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq k}\varphi_{ij}(a_{i}^{\ast}a_{j})=\sum_{g,h\in\Gamma,\,1\leq i,j\leq k}\overline{a_{i,h}}a_{j,g}\varphi_{ij}(h^{-1}g)\geq 0

(2.6)

for any
a_{1}
, …,
a_{k}\in\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
. For instance, for the map in (
2.5
), this holds because the sum in (
2.6
) is just equal to the squared length of the vector
\sum_{i}\pi(a_{i})v_{i}
. On the other hand, if
\varphi
is any map on
\Gamma
satisfying (
2.6
), then another variant of the GNS construction produces a unitary representation of
\Gamma
to which
\varphi
is associated as in (
2.5
). Using this,
\varphi
then extends to a completely positive map on the whole of
C^{\ast}\Gamma
. The case
k=1
of this result can be found in
[
77
, Section 3.3]
or
[
62
, Section 13.4]
. The proof for larger
k
is analogous.

These results establish a bijection between positive definite maps from
\Gamma
to
\mathrm{M}_{k}
and the space
\mathfrak{B}(C^{\ast}\Gamma,\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
. Under this bijection, the weak
∗
topology on
\mathfrak{B}(C^{\ast}\Gamma,\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
corresponds to the usual weak
∗
topology of
\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma;\mathrm{M}_{k})
, and when restricted to bounded subsets it coincides with the topology of pointwise convergence for functions on
\Gamma
. We carry all of our previous notation and terminology for completely positive maps such as ‘assocation’, ‘dilations’, and ‘types’ over to this setting in the obvious way.

When working with a countable group
\Gamma
, we often write
\Sigma_{k}(\Gamma)
instead of
\Sigma_{k}(C^{\ast}\Gamma)
and regard it as a space of maps on
\Gamma
itself, and similarly with
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
. Both of these spaces are convex subsets of
\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma;\mathrm{M}_{k})
, and compact by the Banach–Alaoglu theorem.

Example 2.10
.

On any countable group
\Gamma
, the function
1_{\{e\}}
is positive definite. It is associated to the left regular representation on
\ell^{2}(\Gamma)
by the function
\delta_{e}
(that is, by itself). It is called the
regular character
of
\Gamma
. We sometimes denote it by
\chi_{\mathrm{reg}}
, and its extension to
C^{\ast}\Gamma
by
\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}
. More generally,
\chi_{\mathrm{reg}}\otimes I_{k}
is the
regular

\mathrm{M}_{k}
-valued positive definite function. ∎

Example 2.11
.

If
H
is a subgroup of
\Gamma
, then
1_{H}
is positive definite. It is associated to the quasi-regular representation of
\Gamma
on
\ell^{2}(\Gamma/H)
by the function
\delta_{eH}
.∎

For a free group, we discuss another simple family of positive definite functions with more diverse properties in Example
9.8
below.

The connections between positive functionals and representations are a foundational topic in operator algebras. Their generalizations to matrix-valued completely positive maps can often be proved either by a fairly mechanical generalization of the scalar-valued proofs, or via the following device.

If
\mathfrak{A}
is a separable C
∗
-algebra and
k
is a positive integer, then
\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
is also a separable C
∗
-algebra in a canonical way
[
53
, Section 34]
. If
\pi
is a representation, then we define a representation
\pi^{(k)}
of
\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
on
H^{\oplus k}
by combining
\pi
with the usual rules of matrix-vector multiplication:

\pi^{(k)}([a_{ij}])\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\vdots\\
v_{i}\\
\vdots\end{array}\right]:=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\vdots\\
\sum_{j=1}^{k}\pi(a_{ij})v_{j}\\
\vdots\end{array}\right].

The construction of
\pi^{(k)}
from
\pi
sets up a bijective correspondence between representations of
\mathfrak{A}
and of
\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
, and it respects the relations of containment and disjointness (see
[
8
, Lemma 4.6]
, for example).

Now suppose that
\varphi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
is completely positive, and define a linear functional on
\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
by the pairing

\langle\varphi,a\rangle:=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{ij}\varphi_{ij}(a_{ij}).

(2.7)

If
\varphi
is associated to a representation
\pi
by the tuple
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}\in H_{\pi}
, and
a=[a_{ij}]\in\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
, then we find that

\langle\varphi,a\rangle=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{ij}\langle\pi(a_{ij})v_{j},v_{i}\rangle=\frac{1}{k}\left\langle\pi^{(k)}(a)\left[\begin{array}[]{c}v_{1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{k}\end{array}\right],\left[\begin{array}[]{c}v_{1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{k}\end{array}\right]\right\rangle.

(2.8)

That is, the pairing functional
\langle\varphi,\cdot\rangle
from (
2.7
) is the type of the single vector
k^{-1/2}[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]^{\mathrm{T}}
in
\pi^{(k)}
. Because of the GNS construction and Stinespring’s theorem, it follows that the pairing in (
2.7
) defines a canonical isomorphism from
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})
to
\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})^{\ast}
, and that it identifies the closed cone
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
with
\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})^{\ast}_{+}
. Note the identity

\|\langle\varphi,\cdot\rangle\|_{\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})^{\ast}}=\langle\varphi,1\otimes I_{k}\rangle=\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{k}\varphi(1).

(2.9)

It is a simple but important consequence of positivity: see
[
53
, Corollary 7.3]
.

The weak
∗
topology on
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})
is generally not metrizable, or even first countable. However, since
\mathfrak{A}
is separable, the restriction of that topology is metrizable on any subset of
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
that is bounded in the dual norm obtained from
\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})_{+}^{\ast}

[
52
, Proposition V.5.1]
. Because of (
2.9
), the subsets
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\Sigma_{k}^{\mathrm{u}}(\mathfrak{A})
are examples. These subsets are also weak
∗
-closed and convex, and hence compact by the Banach–Alaoglu theorem.

The next lemma lies between these cases. It must surely be classical, but I have not found a suitable reference.

Lemma 2.12
.

The restriction of the weak
∗
topology to
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
is locally compact and second countable.

Proof.

First, by considering pairing functionals on
\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
instead of positive definite maps on
\mathfrak{A}
, we may reduce to the case
k=1
. Having done so, let

U_{r}:=\{\varphi\in\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}_{+}:\ \varphi(1)<r\}\qquad(r>0).

Each of these sets is relatively weak
∗
-open in
\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}_{+}
. On the other hand, by (
2.9
), the weak
∗
-closure
\overline{U_{r}}
is equal to the intersection of
\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}_{+}
with a closed ball of radius
r
in
\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}
. Therefore
\overline{U_{r}}
is metrizable and also compact, the latter by the Banach–Alaoglu theorem, and hence it is second countable. So
\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}_{+}
is covered by the sequence
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
, …of open subsets, each of which is precompact in
\mathfrak{A}_{+}^{\ast}
and second countable in the weak
∗
topology.
∎

Example 2.13
.

Given
\varphi\in\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}_{+}
and a positive integer
k
, the tensor product
\varphi\otimes I_{k}
defines a completely positive map
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
. If
\varphi
is associated to
\pi
by
\xi
, then
\varphi\otimes I_{k}
is associated to
\pi^{\oplus k}
by the vectors

\xi_{i}=[0,\dots,0,\xi,0,\dots,0]^{\mathrm{T}}\qquad(i=1,2,\dots,k),

(2.10)

where only the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
coordinate of
\xi_{i}
is nonzero.

Now pairing as in (
2.7
) yields

\langle\varphi\otimes I_{k},a\rangle=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i}\varphi(a_{ii})=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i}\langle\pi^{(k)}(a)\xi_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle=(\varphi\otimes\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{k})(a)

for
a=[a_{ij}]\in\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
. However, this does not associate
\varphi\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{k}
to
\pi^{(k)}
by a single cyclic vector. To do that, we must use instead the larger representation
\widetilde{\pi}:=\pi\otimes\mathrm{mult}_{k}
, where
\mathrm{mult}_{k}
is the representation of
\mathrm{M}_{k}
on itself by left multiplication, regarded as a unitary representation by giving
\mathrm{M}_{k}
the Hilbert–Schmidt inner product. This gives

(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{k})(a)=\langle\widetilde{\pi}(a)(\xi\otimes I_{k}),\xi\otimes I_{k}\rangle\qquad(a\in\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})).

Since
\xi\otimes I_{k}
is also cyclic for
\widetilde{\pi}
, this identifies the GNS representation of
\varphi\otimes\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{k}
.

Alternatively, the constant
k
-by-
k
matrix

\varphi_{ij}:=\varphi\quad(1\leq i,j\leq k)

is also positive definite. If
\varphi
is associated to
\pi
by
\xi
, then this matrix is associated to the same representation
\pi
by the
k
-tuple
\xi
, …,
\xi
. ∎

With the pairing isomorphism in hand, many standard results about positive functionals can be generalized to
\mathrm{M}_{k}
-valued completely positive maps simply by citing the scalar-valued results for
\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
rather than
\mathfrak{A}
: see
[
8
, Section 4.3]
. In later sections we recall a few of these that we need directly in this book.

Notes and further references

The textbook
[
142
]
is dedicated to completely positive and completely bounded maps between operator algebras. The basic results that we need are mostly covered in Chapters 1–6 of that book. In particular, Stinespring’s theorem is
[
142
, Theorem 4.1]
. It is originally from
[
165
]
; the chapter notes in
[
142
]
give a more complete guide to original references.

Some more recent uses of completely positive maps in the study of C
∗
-algebras, including group C
∗
-algebras, can be found in
[
38
, Sections 1.2 and 2.5 and Appendix D]
.

The general construction of a unitary representation from a positive definite function appears first in work of Gelfand and Raikov
[
85
]
. It is an analog for groups of the GNS construction for a state on a C
∗
-algebra. Similarly, the generalization to
\mathrm{M}_{k}
-valued positive definite functions on a group is a cousin of Stinespring’s theorem. It can be found (with a further generalization) in
[
116
]
, or in the standard text
[
142
, Theorem 4.8]
, where the key ideas are traced back to Naimark. The relationship between positive definite functions on a group and completely positive maps on the group C
∗
-algebra is discussed further at the end of
[
142
, Chapter 4]
.

The proofs of all these representation theorems come from the same mould, and for this reason many authors now attach the label ‘GNS’ to all of them. Examples are
[
16
, Appendix C]
and
[
15
, Section I.B]
, which recount all this basic theory in some detail in the case
k=1
. However, beware that the versions for groups are not quite special cases of the versions for C
∗
-algebras. This is because the notions of ‘positivity’ in their hypotheses are not known to match beforehand. In Stinespring’s theorem, the complete positivity of the map is understood with reference to positivity of elements in a C
∗
-algebra. On the other hand, a positive definite function
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
is positive when applied to elements of the form
a^{\ast}a
for
a\in\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
, but these may not be all of the elements of
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
that are positive when regarded as elements of
C^{\ast}\Gamma
. For this reason, it is not clear beforehand that positive definiteness of a function on a group implies complete positivity of the extended map on the group C
∗
-algebra. This turns out to be the case, but I do not know a way to see this that does not essentially construct the minimal dilation in the process.

Group C
∗
-algebras appear as a diverse source of examples of C
∗
-algebras in recent references such as
[
58
]
,
[
38
]
and
[
144
]
. The survey
[
140
]
explains specific connections between group C
∗
-algebras and Connes’ embedding conjecture. In this reference, Ozawa considers carefully how notions of positivity for various classes of
∗
-algebra relate to positivity in C
∗
-algebra completions of them. This generalizes the discussion above of the relationship between Naimark’s and Stinespring’s theorems. The overarching theme here is generalizations of Hilbert’s 17th problem, and it has also been widely studied: see
[
99
]
or the references in
[
13
, Chapter 3]
, for example.

2.5

Tracial functionals and vectors

A linear functional
\tau
on
\mathfrak{A}
is
tracial
if

\tau(ab)=\tau(ba)\qquad(a,b\in\mathfrak{A}).

(2.11)

The study of these is motivated by two fundamental families of examples. First, if
\pi
is a
d
-dimensional representation with
d
finite, then the normalized trace on
\mathfrak{B}(\mathbf{C}^{d})
pulls back to the tracial state
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d}\circ\pi
on
\mathfrak{A}
. Second, for group C
∗
-algebras, tracial states correspond to characters of the group.

If a tracial positive functional
\tau
is associated to the representation
\lambda
on
H
by a vector
\xi
, then (
2.11
) becomes an identity for
\xi
:

\langle\lambda(a)\lambda(b)\xi,\xi\rangle=\langle\lambda(b)\lambda(a)\xi,\xi\rangle\qquad(a,b\in\mathfrak{A}).

(2.12)

In any representation, a vector satisfying (
2.12
) is called
tracial
. This identity can also be re-arranged into the form

\langle\lambda(b)\xi,\lambda(a^{\ast})\xi\rangle=\langle\lambda(a)\xi,\lambda(b^{\ast})\xi\rangle\qquad(a,b\in\mathfrak{A}).

In this form, we can take weak-operator limits in
a
and then separately in
b
to conclude that the positive functional

\widetilde{\tau}(A):=\langle A\xi,\xi\rangle

(2.13)

is actually tracial on the whole von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{N}:=\lambda(\mathfrak{A})^{\prime\prime}
. Clearly
\xi
is still cyclic for
\mathfrak{N}
. It follows that
\xi
is also cyclic for
\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}

[
63
, Corollary I.6.1]
, hence also separating for both
\mathfrak{N}
and
\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}

[
63
, Proposition I.1.5]
, and that
\mathfrak{N}
and
\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}
are both finite von Neumann algebra
[
63
, Proposition I.6.9(ii)]
.

If
\Gamma
is a countable group, then a state on
C^{\ast}\Gamma
is tracial if and only if it arises from a
character
of
\Gamma
, meaning a unital positive definite function
\chi
that satisfies

\chi(g^{-1}hg)=\chi(h)\qquad(g,h\in\Gamma).

(2.14)

Example 2.14
.

If
\Gamma
is any countable discrete group, the regular character
\chi_{\mathrm{reg}}
satisfies (
2.14
) – this explains the term ‘regular character’. ∎

Example 2.15
.

If
H
is a subgroup of
\Gamma
, then the positive definite function
1_{H}
is a character if and only if
N
is normal. These examples are called
quasi-regular
characters. ∎

Returning to a general von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{N}
acting on a Hilbert space
H
, if
\xi
is tracial for
\mathfrak{N}
with associated trace
\widetilde{\tau}
as in (
2.13
), then the formula

J(A\xi):=A^{\ast}\xi\qquad(A\in\mathfrak{N})

(2.15)

gives a well-defined involution on the subspace
\mathfrak{N}\xi
. The map
J
is anti-linear, and it converts inner products to their conjugates as a consequence of the trace identity. In particular, it is an isometry of the restriction of the norm of
H
. It therefore extends by continuity to an involution of the whole of
H
that has the same properties, which we still denote by
J
. It is called the
canonical involution
associated to
\mathfrak{N}
and
\xi
. This construction is recounted in
[
63
, Section I.5.1]
in the alternative framework of Hilbert algebras, which are shown to be equivalent to algebras with tracial cyclic vectors in
[
63
, Section I.6.2]
. The following facts can all be found in those sections.

Lemma 2.16
.

The canonical involution has the following properties:

i.

J\xi=\xi
;

ii.

JA\xi=A^{\ast}\xi
for every
A\in\mathfrak{N}
;

iii.

the map
A\mapsto JAJ
is an involutive
\ast
-anti-automorphism of
\mathfrak{B}(H)
;

iv.

the map from (iii) preserves
\widetilde{\tau}
and exchanges the von Neumann subalgebras
\mathfrak{N}
and
\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{B}(H)
. ∎

Notes and further references

Tracial vectors and canonical involutions already appear in the work of Murray and von Neumann
[
134
,
135
,
136
]
, with additional early contributions by Ambrose
[
2
,
3
]
. A more complete set of original references is listed in
[
63
, Sections I.5.1–2]
.

In general, representations having tracial vectors (or more complicated but related structures) occupy a special place and often admit a finer analysis. Their basic theory is covered in
[
62
, Chapters 6 and 17]
, and
[
15
, Chapters 10–12]
covers finer aspects for unitary group representations.

2.6

Comparing completely positive maps

By the Riesz–Markov theorem, positive Borel measures on a compact metric space may be regarded as positive functionals on a space of continuous functions. Completely positive maps on general C*-algebras enjoy various properties that generalize this example.

Let
\varphi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
and
\psi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{\ell}
be completely positive. If
k=\ell
, then
\varphi\leq\psi
in the
positive definite
ordering if
\psi-\varphi
is still positive definite.

For arbitrary
k
and
\ell
, here are two other possible relations:

•

\varphi
is
absolutely continuous
with respect to
\psi
, written
\varphi\ll\psi
, if
\pi_{\varphi}\lesssim\pi_{\psi}^{\oplus\infty}
;

•

\varphi
and
\psi
are
mutually singular
, written
\varphi\perp\psi
, if
\pi_{\varphi}\spoon\pi_{\psi}
.

This terminology deliberately suggests the analogy with measures. In these two items we sometimes omit mentioning the ‘reference’ function
\psi
when it is clear from the context.

These definitions lead to analogs of the Lebesgue decomposition and Radon–Nikodym theorem from measure theory. The first of these is obtained by applying Proposition
2.1
to
\pi_{\varphi}
and
\pi_{\psi}
.

Corollary 2.17
.

Given
\varphi
and
\psi
as above, there is a unique decomposition

\varphi=\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}+\varphi_{\mathrm{sing}}

(2.16)

into positive summands such that
\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}\ll\psi
and
\varphi_{\mathrm{sing}}\perp\psi
. This decomposition is linear in
\varphi
. ∎

We refer to (
2.16
) as the
Lebesgue decomposition
of
\varphi
with respect to
\psi
. As far as I know, for positive functionals the earliest appearance of this result was in
[
167
]
. The case of
\mathrm{M}_{k}
-valued completely positive maps has a similar proof, or it is derived via the pairing isomorphism in
[
8
, Proposition 4.10]
.

Analogs of the Radon–Nikodym theorem for completely positive maps are more complicated. They separate into several strands which require different assumptions and allow for the non-commutativity of
\mathfrak{A}
in different ways. We need one of these results in particular. It is given in Proposition
2.18
below. It applies when a completely positive map
\varphi
is absolutely continuous with respect to a tracial state
\tau
. We need some additional preparations before formulating it.

The statement of Proposition
2.18
involves operators on a Hilbert space
H
that are closed and densely defined but unbounded. The basic theory of these can be found in
[
52
, Chapter X]
, including the unbounded version of the spectral theorem
[
52
, Section X.4]
which leads to an unbounded extension of the polar decomposition
[
52
, Exercise X.4.6]
(or see
[
149
, Section VIII.9]
).

A closed and densely defined operator
T
is
affiliated
to a von Neumann algebra
\mathfrak{N}
if it commutes with every unitary element of
\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}

[
63
, Exercise I.1.10]
. This includes the assertion that those unitary elements preserve
\mathrm{dom}\,T
. The collection of operators affiliated to
\mathfrak{N}
is closed under forming adjoints, and if such an operator
T
has polar decomposition
U|T|
, then the uniqueness of that decomposition implies that
U\in\mathfrak{N}
and that
|T|
is also affiliated to
\mathfrak{N}
. If
\mathfrak{N}
is a finite von Neumann algebra, then sums and products of affiliated operators are also still affiliated operators, provided each of these constructions is followed by taking graph closures
[
63
, Exercise III.1.13.c]
.

Now let
\tau
be a tracial state on
\mathfrak{A}
, and let it be associated to its GNS representation
\lambda
by the cyclic vector
\xi
. From these ingredients, form the
k
-tuple
\xi_{1}
, …,
\xi_{k}
in
H_{\lambda}^{\oplus k}
as in (
2.10
). Let
\mathfrak{N}:=\lambda(\mathfrak{A})^{\prime\prime}
, and define the normal tracial functional
\widetilde{\tau}
on
\mathfrak{N}
using the vector
\xi
as in (
2.13
), so
\tau=\widetilde{\tau}\circ\lambda
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
\varphi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
be completely positive and
\lambda
-normal. Then
\varphi
is associated to
\lambda^{\oplus k}
by the tuple
T\xi_{1}
, …,
T\xi_{k}
for some non-negative operator
T
affiliated to
\lambda^{\oplus k}(\mathfrak{A})^{\prime}
such that
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k}\in\mathrm{dom}\,T
. ∎

This theorem is proved in full
[
8
, Proposition 4.9]
. The case
k=1
is classic result of Dye: see
[
66
, Theorem 4]
and also
[
156
, Theorem 14]
. The general case again follows via the pairing isomorphism.

Since
\mathrm{dom}\,\,T
contains every
\xi_{i}
, the spectral measure
E(\cdot)
of
T
satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|T\xi_{i}\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{2}\ \langle E(dt)\xi_{i},\xi_{i}\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{2}\ (\tau\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{k})E(dt)<\infty.

(2.17)

This property is indicated by calling
T

square-integrable
with respect to
\tau\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{k}
. As far as I know, this terminology originates in
[
156
, Subsection 3.4]
.

The next definition offers more ways to compare completely positive maps. It is borrowed from Furstenberg’s classic paper
[
81
]
in ergodic theory, and was used this way for representations in
[
8
, Definition 4.4]
. Let
k
and
\ell
be positive integers, and let

K\>=\{1,\dots,k\}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad L:=\{k+1,\dots,k+\ell\}.

For any
(k+\ell)
-by-
(k+\ell)
matrix
M
, we write
M[K]
for its
K
-by-
K
submatrix, and similarly for
L
.

Definition 2.19
.

Let
\varphi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
and
\psi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{\ell}
be completely positive. A
joining
of them is a completely positive map
\theta:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k+\ell}
such that

\theta(a)[K]=\varphi(a)\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\theta(a)[L]=\psi(a)\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{A}).

In particular, the
diagonal joining
is defined by

\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi)(a):=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\varphi(a)&0\\
0&\psi(a)\end{array}\right]\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{A}).

The joinings of
\varphi
and
\psi
describe all the ways these two maps can ‘sit together’ inside a single representation. If
\theta
is a joining, then
\pi_{\theta}
contains copies of
\pi_{\varphi}
and
\pi_{\psi}
that together span the whole of
H_{\theta}
. The minimal dilation of
\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi)
is equivalent to
\pi_{\varphi}\oplus\pi_{\psi}
, and
\varphi
and
\psi
are disjoint if and only if
\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi)
is their only joining.

If
\varphi
and
\psi
are both normalized, then so is any joining
\theta
of them. This follows from the simple relation

\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{k+\ell}\theta(1)=\frac{k}{k+\ell}\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{k}\theta(1)[K]+\frac{\ell}{k+\ell}\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{\ell}\theta(1)[L].

(2.18)

However, unital maps may have non-unital joinings.

Notes and further references

The Lebesgue decomposition from Corollary
2.17
has a more sophisticated characterization in terms of extensions of these maps to the von Neumann completion
\pi_{\psi}(\mathfrak{A})^{\prime\prime}
: one can show that
\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}
is the largest completely positive map which (i) is bounded above by
\varphi
and (ii) extends to a normal completely positive map on
\pi_{\psi}(\mathfrak{A})^{\prime\prime}
. See
[
108
, Section 10.1]
, particularly their Theorem 10.1.15(iii) and Proposition 10.1.17, where these results are obtained using an auxiliary construction with the universal representation of
\mathfrak{A}
. (Our Corollary
2.17
is more general in allowing the cases
k\geq 2
and
\ell\geq 2
, but the differences required in the proofs are cosmetic.) A third alternative approach to this Lebesgue decomposition is possible using Simon’s ‘Lebesgue decomposition’ for unbounded quadratic forms
[
161
]
.

Affiliated operators also have their origins in
[
134
]
. A careful introduction to working with them can be found in
[
122
, Chapter 8]
, where the goal is applications to
L^{2}
-invariants in topology.

The proof of Proposition
2.18
can be assembled from other standard structural results on von Neumann algebras with cyclic tracial vectors. The paper
[
8
]
sketches this with reference to
[
63
, Section III.1]
. Another textbook account is in
[
108
, Sections 7.1–3]
, where the representation of normal states using single vectors is provided by
[
108
, Theorem 7.2.3]
.

Several other ‘Radon–Nikodym’ theorems hold between positive functionals under various assumptions, such as for normal functionals on von Neumann algebras. These reflect the non-commutativity of the algebra in different ways. Two early examples are due to Sakai, and are covered in
[
155
, Section 1.24]
; another textbook treatment that includes several variants is
[
108
, Sections 7.2–3]
.

In ergodic theory, joinings are a very rich class of objects indeed, and have proven valuable across a wide variety of topics. The textbook
[
86
]
develops the whole of ergodic theory from this point of view as far as possible. Joinings also appear briefly in
[
111
, Section 10.(C)]
to help establish some properties of weak containment of measure-preserving group actions, which is perhaps close in spirit to the present work. Their representation theoretic counterparts are simpler, because one can use orthogonal complements to break up representations. But they still prove to be a convenient way of capturing several constructions in the sequel.

2.7

Fuglede–Kadison determinants

Now let
\mathfrak{A}
be a C
∗
-algebra and
\tau
be a tracial positive functional on it. For any positive invertible
a\in\mathfrak{A}
we define its
Fuglede–Kadison determinant with respect to
\tau
by

\Delta_{\tau}a:=\exp(\tau(\log a)).

(This definition is sometimes extended to other invertible elements
a
by applying it to
|a|
, but we do not need this.) This definition extends the standard formula

(\det A)^{1/n}=\exp\mathrm{tr}_{n}\log A\qquad(A\in\mathrm{M}_{n+}).

If
\mathfrak{N}
is a von Neumann algebra,
\tau
is a normal tracial positive fuctional on it, and
A\in\mathfrak{N}
is positive and invertible, then
\Delta_{\tau}A
can be expressed in terms of the spectral resolution of
A
. In this setting its basic properties are covered in
[
63
, Section I.6.11]
. More recently, this formulation has also permitted an extension to certain affiliated operators: a careful exposition with further references is given in
[
95
, Section 2]
.

If
T
is a non-negative operator affiliated to
\mathfrak{N}
and
E(\cdot)
is its spectral measure on
[0,\infty)
, then
T
is called
log-integrable
with respect to
\tau
if

\int_{0}^{\infty}\log^{+}t\ \tau E(dt)<\infty

(note that this controls the singularity of
\log
at
\infty
but not at
0
). The property of square integrability from (
2.17
) implies log-integrability, because
\log^{+}t
is dominated by
t^{2}
for all
t
. If
T
is log-integrable, then we define its
Fuglede–Kadison determinant
with respect to
\tau
to be

\Delta_{\tau}T:=\exp\int_{0}^{\infty}\log t\ \tau E(dt).

(2.19)

The integral here is well-defined by log-integrability, but may take the value
-\infty
, in which case
\Delta_{\tau}T:=0
.

Now suppose that
\varphi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
is a
\lambda
-normal completely positive map. By Proposition
2.18
, there is a unique non-negative square-integrable operator
T
affiliated to
\lambda^{\oplus k}(\mathfrak{A})^{\prime}
such that
\varphi
is the type of the tuple
T\xi_{1}
, …,
T\xi_{k}
in
\lambda^{\oplus k}
. This representation of
\varphi
using
T
can be seen as an equivariant analog of the representation of a positive semidefinite matrix
Q
as the square of another such matrix
V
. In that situation we have
\det Q=(\det V)^{2}
, and this motivates us to define

\Delta_{\tau}\varphi:=(\Delta_{\tau\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{k}}T)^{2}.

(2.20)

Once again, this may take any value in
[-\infty,\infty)
. If
\varphi
is not necessarily
\lambda
-normal, then we can still consider the quantity
\Delta_{\tau}\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}
, referring to the Lebesgue decomposition from Corollary
2.17
.

From the formula (
2.19
), let us note the following scaling relations:

\Delta_{\tau}(s\varphi)=s\Delta_{\tau}\varphi\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\Delta_{s\tau}(\varphi)=(\Delta_{\tau}\varphi)^{s}\qquad(s>0).

(2.21)

The determinant
\Delta_{\tau}\varphi
can also be obtained from a variational principle. This principle essentially goes back to
[
7
, Definition 4.3.7]
, although it is phrased there in a more specialized situation. See
[
8
, Proposition 4.12]
for a version that implies the one we need here.

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
\lambda
and
\tau
be as above. Then any
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A};\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
satisfies

(\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}})^{1/k}=\inf\big\{\langle\varphi,a\rangle:\ a\in\mathrm{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})\ \hbox{positive and invertible}\\
\hbox{and}\ \Delta_{\tau\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{k}}(a)\geq 1\big\}.

(2.22)

∎

The
k^{\mathrm{th}}
root on the left-hand side of (
2.22
) reflects the use of the un-normalized functional
\tau\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{k}
rather than
\tau\otimes\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{k}
on the right-hand side of (
2.20
). If we use the normalized functional, then this power disappears, by the second relation in (
2.21
).

Fuglede–Kadison determinants for completely positive maps play a role in just one place later in our work, but it is crucial. This is the determinant formula for almost periodic entropy in Theorem
4.9
below. Some key arguments in the general theory in Part
II
depend on this formula.

Actually, they mostly depend on the
existence
of such a formula, along with just a few general properties of Fuglede–Kadison determinants; we do not need to compute with it explicitly. We next recall the general properties that we need. Each of them is a fairly direct consequence of either (
2.20
) or (
2.22
). The reader willing to take these on faith should be able to understand the remaining chapters without knowing anything else about Fuglede–Kadison determinants.

Proposition 2.21
.

Fix
\mathfrak{A}
,
\tau
, and
\lambda
as above, let
\Delta:=\Delta_{\tau}
, and consider the functionals

\varphi\mapsto\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}\qquad(\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+})

for each positive integer
k
. They have the following properties.

a.

They are upper semicontinuous for every
k
.

b.

For every
k
and elements of
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
, they satisfy the inequalities

\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}\leq(\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}(1))^{n}

and

\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}\leq\Delta\psi_{\mathrm{ac}}\qquad\hbox{whenever}\ \varphi\leq\psi.

c.

They are log-concave:

\log\Delta(t\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}+(1-t)\psi_{\mathrm{ac}})\geq t\log\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}+(1-t)\log\Delta\psi_{\mathrm{ac}}

whenever
\varphi,\psi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
and
t\in[0,1]
.

d.

They are multiplicative on diagonal joinings :

\Delta(\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi)_{\mathrm{ac}})=\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}\cdot\Delta\psi_{\mathrm{ac}}

whenever
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
and
\psi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{\ell})_{+}
. ∎

All of these results are contained in
[
8
, Corollaries 3.11 and 4.13]
and the remarks that follow them.

Notes and further references

Since its introduction in
[
79
,
80
]
, the Fuglede–Kadison determinant has played an increasingly important role in various connections of operator algebras to others parts of mathematics. A succinct survey of some of these is given in
[
59
]
. A much more thorough exposition with a view towards handling to
L^{2}
-invariants in topology is given in
[
122
, Section 3.2]
; see also
[
122
, Chapter 13]
on the approximation and determinant conjectures.

2.8

Approximate association and typical tuples

Much of our work later depends on classifying tuples of vectors in a representation according to their types.

Definition 2.22
.

For any positive integer
k
and subset
O
of
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})
, let

\mathcal{X}(\pi,O):=\big\{[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]^{\mathrm{T}}\in H_{\pi}^{\oplus k}:\ \Phi^{\pi}_{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}}\in O\big\}.

The elements of
\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)
are the
O
-typical
tuples of the representation
\pi
.

We often use Definition
2.22
when
O
is a small neighbourhood around a given ‘target’ completely positive map
\varphi
. In this case we may write informally about elements of
\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)
as ‘approximately
\varphi
-typical vectors’. This resembles the use of terms such as ‘microstate’ or ‘good model’ in free probability and the study of sofic entropy in ergodic theory (compare
[
30
, Section 2.3]
, for example).

For a group C
∗
-algebra
C^{\ast}\Gamma
, completely positive maps to
\mathrm{M}_{k}
can be identified with positive definite maps from
\Gamma
itself to
\mathrm{M}_{k}
, as explained in the last part of Section
2.4
. We modify our notation accordingly by writing
\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)
when
\pi
is any representation of
\Gamma
and
O
is any set of functions from
\Gamma
to
\mathrm{M}_{k}
. All the general properties established in the rest of this subsection carry over without change.

As a vector space topology, the weak
∗
topology on
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})
also defines a canonical uniform structure on this space (see, for instance,
[
68
, Section 8.1]
for the basics of uniform structures). Restricted to any bounded subset of
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})
, this uniform structure is generated by any translation-invariant metric compatible with the topology. We need this uniform structure at a few points through the following lemma:

Lemma 2.23
.

For any
\pi
and
k
, the type map

H_{\pi}^{\oplus k}\mapsto\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k}):[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]^{\mathrm{T}}\mapsto\Phi^{\pi}_{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}}

is continuous, and uniformly continuous on any bounded subset of
H_{\pi}^{\oplus k}
.

Proof.

These properties are elementary for the inner product map
H_{\pi}\times H_{\pi}\to\mathbf{C}
. They follow for types by arguing pointwise for each
i
,
j
, and
a\in\mathfrak{A}
.
∎

We also need a notation for the images of the maps in Lemma
2.23
when we restrict to orthonormal tuples.

Definition 2.24
.

For any representation
\pi
, we set

\Sigma_{k}(\pi):=\big\{\Phi^{\pi}_{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}}:\ v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in H_{\pi}\ \hbox{and}\ \|v_{1}\|^{2}+\cdots+\|v_{k}\|^{2}=k\big\}

and

\Sigma_{k}^{\mathrm{u}}(\pi):=\big\{\Phi^{\pi}_{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}}:\ v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in H_{\pi}\ \hbox{are orthonormal}\big\}.

These are the subsets of elements of
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
and
\Sigma_{k}^{\mathrm{u}}(\mathfrak{A})
that are associated to
\pi
.

For any representation
\pi
