and subset
O
of
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
, observe that

O
meets
\Sigma_{k}(\pi)
if and only if
\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)\neq\emptyset
.

Now let
\pi
be a separable representation and
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
. We say that
\varphi
is
approximately associated
to
\pi
if it lies in the closure in
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
of the set of maps associated to
\pi
. Because of the second countability from Lemma
2.12
, this holds if and only if there is a sequence of
k
-tuples
v_{n,1}
, …,
v_{n,k}
in
H_{\pi}
such that

\Phi^{\pi}_{v_{n,1},\dots,v_{n,k}}(a)\to\varphi(a)\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{A}).

If
\varphi
is normalized (respectively, unital), and the sequence of tuples
v_{n,1}
, …,
v_{n,k}
witnesses the convergence above, then we can always perturb those tuples slightly so that they are normalized (respectively, orthonormal). Compare, for instance, parts (iv) and (v) of
[
110
, Proposition 2.2]
. It follows that an element of
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
(respectively,
\Sigma_{k}^{\mathrm{u}}(\mathfrak{A})
) is approximately associated to
\pi
if and only if it lies in the closure
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}
(respectively,
\overline{\Sigma_{k}^{\mathrm{u}}(\pi)}
).

A relative of approximate association has a bigger place in the literature. A unital completely positive map
\varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
is
weakly associated
to a representation
\pi
if it can be approximated by convex combinations of elements of
\Sigma_{k}(\pi)
: that is, if
\varphi\in\overline{\mathrm{conv}}\,\Sigma_{k}(\pi)
. This definition is conventional only in the case
k=1
, but no new ideas are needed if we allow larger values of
k
.

The term ‘approximately associated’ is not standard. I have chosen it to make contact with ‘approximate equivalence’ in the next chapter. See the notes at the end of Section
3.3
for a further discussion of this choice.

We can describe the relationship between approximate and weak association in terms of dilations. This is because we always have
\overline{\mathrm{conv}}\,\Sigma_{k}(\pi)=\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi^{\oplus\infty})}
, so a functional is weakly associated to
\pi
if and only if it is approximately associated to
\pi^{\oplus\infty}
. See, for instance,
[
110
, Proposition 2.2]
, where this result is formulated for unitary group representations, but the proof given works with only cosmetic changes in the general case.
On the other hand, if
\varphi
happens to be an extreme point of
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
, so its GNS representation is irreducible, then allowing a convex hull makes no difference: such a
\varphi
is weakly associated to another representation
\pi
if and only if it is approximately associated (see, for instance, the proof of
[
62
, Theorem 3.4.10]
).

We can compare representations themselves by comparing their sets of approximately or weakly associated completely positive maps. This classical idea is the basis for several important notions in representation theory such as Fell’s quotient topology. We return to that in the next chapter.

Example 2.25
.

When
\mathfrak{A}=C^{\ast}\Gamma
, the left regular representation
\lambda
plays a distinguished role in the representation theory of
\mathfrak{A}
. A positive definite function
\varphi
on
\Gamma
is called
tempered
if it is approximately associated to
\lambda
. Since the left and right regular representations of
\Gamma
are equivalent, we could equivalently define temperedness using the right regular representation throughout.

Temperedness is more often defined with ‘weakly associated’ in place of ‘approximately associated’, but in the case of
\lambda
these definitions coincide. This is a classic result of Takenouchi: see
[
166
]
or
[
62
, Proposition 18.3.5(a)]
.

Tempered positive definite functions on free groups are the key objects in Theorem
C
, and they play an important role in much of Part
III
. ∎

If a map
\varphi
is associated to a representation
\pi
, then it is clearly also approximately associated. The next lemma is a robust version of this fact. It lies inside the standard result that approximate containment is a transitive relation between representations.

Lemma 2.26
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
and
\psi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{\ell})_{+}
, and assume that
\psi
is approximately associated to
\pi_{\varphi}
. Then for any neighbourhood
U
of
\psi
there is a neighbourhood
V
of
\varphi
such that

\mathcal{X}(\pi,V)\neq\emptyset\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathcal{X}(\pi,U)\neq\emptyset.

(2.23)

for any representation
\pi
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi
be associated to
\pi_{\varphi}
by the cyclic tuple
x_{1}
, …,
x_{k}
. By cyclicity and Lemma
2.23
, there is an
\ell
-by-
k
matrix
[a_{ij}]
of elements of
\mathfrak{A}
such that the tuple defined by

y_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\pi_{\varphi}(a_{ij})x_{j}\qquad(i=1,2,\dots,\ell)

satisfies
\gamma:=\Phi^{\pi}_{y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}}\in U
.

In terms of
\varphi
, this new map
\gamma
is given by the equations

\displaystyle\gamma_{ii^{\prime}}(b)

\displaystyle=\langle\pi_{\varphi}(b)y_{i^{\prime}},y_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{k}\langle\pi_{\varphi}(ba_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})x_{j^{\prime}},\pi_{\varphi}(a_{ij})x_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{k}\varphi_{jj^{\prime}}(a_{ij}^{\ast}ba_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})\qquad(b\in\mathfrak{A},\ 1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq\ell).

(2.24)

Having fixed the matrix
[a_{ij}]
and also
b
, the last displayed expression above is weak
∗
continuous in
\varphi
. Therefore there is a neighbourhood
V
of
\varphi
such that, if we perturb
\varphi
so that it stays inside
V
, the corresponding perturbation to
\gamma
given by the formula above stays inside
U
. This property of
V
translates into (
2.23
).
∎

If
\varphi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
is completely positive and
Q\in\mathrm{M}_{k}
, then our earlier equation (
2.3
) is the special case of (
2.8
) when
k=\ell
and each entry
a_{ij}
is a scalar. Let us record a slightly more explicit relationship between the sets of approximately typical vectors for
\varphi
and for
\psi
in this case.

Lemma 2.27
.

Suppose that
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
, and let

\psi(a):=(Q^{\mathrm{T}})^{\ast}\varphi(a)Q^{\mathrm{T}}\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{A})

for some
Q\in\mathrm{M}_{\ell,k}
. For any neighbourhood
O
of
\psi
there is a neighbourhood
U
of
\varphi
such that

(I_{H_{\pi}}\otimes Q)[\mathcal{X}(\pi,U)]\subset\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)

(2.25)

for any representation
\pi
, recalling the notation from (
2.4
).

Proof.

For the first assertion, consider a
k
-tuple
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
in
H_{\pi}
, and define the
\ell
-tuple
y_{1}
, …,
y_{\ell}
by

[y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}]^{\mathrm{T}}:=(I_{H_{\pi}}\otimes Q)[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]^{\mathrm{T}}.

If these two tuples have respective types
\varphi^{\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime}
, then this is the relation (
2.4
) again, so
\psi^{\prime}
is related to
\varphi^{\prime}
as in (
2.3
). Now the existence of
U
for a given
O
follows by the continuity of sums and scalar multiplication.
∎

Chapter 3

Approximate equivalence and the strong-quotient topology

In this chapter we recall the notion of approximate equivalence of representations, and study natural topologies on the space of approximate equivalence classes.

Within operator algebras, the study of approximate equivalence originates in works by Glimm
[
87
]
and Voiculescu
[
175
]
. Related topologies on spaces of representations were studied in the same era by Fell
[
71
,
73
]
, including the ‘quotient’ topology that we recall below.

More recently, the main precedent for this chapter is Abért and Elek’s study of related ideas for measure-preserving systems in
[
1
]
; see also
[
171
]
and
[
42
]
. Those papers introduce a natural topology on the space of approximate equivalence classes and show that it is compact. Abért and Elek already include variants for unitary representations as well. We include complete proofs of our versions of these results. Firstly, this is in order to present them in the generality of C
∗
-algebras, although this does not require any novel ideas. We also take a slightly different approach, avoiding the use of ultralimits from
[
1
,
171
]
, and leading to a description of the space of approximate equivalence classes as the ‘summary space’ in Definition
3.5
. Many of the terms and conventions we use here appeared for the first time in
[
8
, Subsection 8.3]
. But we have more need for them now, so the present treatment is more complete.

The material in this chapter is not new, but not as standard as the preceding chapter. Readers familiar with
[
1
]
may find that skimming this chapter quickly suffices.

3.1

Approximate equivalence and containment

Definition 3.1
.

If
\pi
and
\rho
are two separable representations of
\mathfrak{A}
, then
\pi
is
approximately contained
in
\rho
, written
\pi\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\rho
, if there is a sequence of unitary embeddings
U_{n}:H_{\pi}\to H_{\rho}
such that

U_{n}^{\ast}\rho(a)U_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{WOT}}}{{\to}}\pi(a)\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{A}).

If each of
\pi
and
\rho
is approximately contained in the other, then they are
approximately equivalent
, written
\pi\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\rho
.

Clearly containment and equivalence imply approximate containment and approximate equivalence, respectively.

An introduction to approximate equivalence is given in
[
53
, Section 41]
. While the results we need about this notion are classical, beware that notation and terminology are somewhat variable across the literature.

Approximate equivalence turns out to have several alternative definitions whose strengths initially appear very different. The parts of this story that we need are covered by the next two results.

Lemma 3.2
.

If
\pi
and
\rho
are separable representations, then
\pi\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\rho
if and only if
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}\subset\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\rho)}
for every
k
.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
).
Let
\pi\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\rho
. It suffices to prove that
\Sigma_{k}(\pi)\subset\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\rho)}
for every
k
, so let
\varphi=\Phi^{\pi}_{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}}
for some tuple
v_{1}
, …
v_{k}
in
H_{\pi}
. Let
(U_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of unitary operators as in Definition
3.1
. Then

(\Phi^{\rho}_{U_{n}v_{1},\dots,U_{n}v_{k}}(a))_{ij}=\langle U_{n}^{\ast}\rho(a)U_{n}v_{j},v_{i}\rangle\to\langle\pi(a)v_{j},v_{i}\rangle=\varphi(a)\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{A}),

so
\varphi
is approximately associated to
\rho
.

(
\Leftarrow
).
First observe that, for any positive integer
k
, we have
\dim H_{\pi}\geq k
if and only
H_{\pi}
contains an orthonormal
k
-tuple, and so

\dim H_{\pi}=\sup\{k:\ \Sigma_{k}^{\mathrm{u}}(\pi)\neq\emptyset\}.

Therefore our assumption implies that
\dim H_{\pi}\leq\dim H_{\rho}
. Now we use the containments
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}\subset\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\rho)}
to assemble partial isometries between finite-dimensional subspaces of
H_{\pi}
and
H_{\rho}
, and then extend these to unitary embeddings. See, for instance, the proof of
[
111
, Proposition H.2]
and the remark that follows it (this reference is only for group representations, but the method is the same).
∎

Theorem 3.3
.

For separable representations
\pi
and
\rho
, the following are equivalent:

i.

they are approximately equivalent;

ii.

they satisfy
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}=\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\rho)}
for every
k
;

iii.

there is a sequence of unitary operators
U_{n}:H_{\pi}\to H_{\rho}
such that

\|\pi(a)-U_{n}^{\ast}\rho(a)U_{n}\|\to 0\quad\hbox{for every}\ a\in\mathfrak{A}.

∎

Having proved Lemma
3.2
, the only non-trivial part of Theorem
3.3
is that (i) or (ii) implies (iii). This is covered by parts (b) and (c) of
[
53
, Theorem 41.12]
. (Beware that Conway temporarily uses the notation “
\cong_{\mathrm{w}}
” for another of the formulations of approximate equivalence in
[
53
, Theorem 41.12]
, but there it does not mean weak equivalence in the sense used in the present work.)

In general, approximate equivalence is strictly weaker than equivalence, but strictly stronger than weak equivalence (see Section
2.2
). We locate it more precisely between these other notions in Section
3.2
. Analogously with Lemma
3.2
, a separable representation
\pi
is weakly contained in another one
\rho
if and only if

\overline{\mathrm{conv}}\,\Sigma_{1}(\pi)\subset\overline{\mathrm{conv}}\,\Sigma_{1}(\rho).

This leads to the characterization of weak containment in terms of supports recalled in Section
2.2
: see
[
62
, Theorem 3.4.4]
. We can also write the support of a general representation
\pi
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{spt}\,\pi

\displaystyle=\{[\rho]\in\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}:\ \rho\ \hbox{weakly contained in }\ \pi\}

\displaystyle=\{[\rho]\in\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}:\ \rho\ \hbox{approximately contained in }\ \pi\}.

Both formulas follow from the equivalences proved in
[
62
, Section 3.4]
.

Now consider again a separable representation
\pi
. By Theorem
3.3
, the sequence of closed sets
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}
for
k\geq 1
determines the approximate equivalence class of a separable representation
\pi
. Henceforth we often abbreviate that sequence to
\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi)}
.

Definition 3.4
.

We call
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}
the
k
-summary
of
\pi
, and
\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi)}
the
summary
of
\pi
. We call the assignment
\pi\mapsto\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi)}
the
summary map
of
\mathfrak{A}
.

I do not know a standard name for
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}
, so the term ‘summary’ is new.

The summary map of
\mathfrak{A}
takes values in the space

\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{K}(\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})),

where
\mathcal{K}(X)
denotes the hyperspace of any topological space
X
(see Appendix
E
).
Our next goal is to describe its image more explicitly.

Definition 3.5
.

The
summary space
of
\mathfrak{A}
is the set
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
of all
Z_{\bullet}\in\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{K}(\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A}))
that have the following two closure properties:

i.

if
\varphi\in Z_{k}
then
\Sigma_{\ell}(\pi_{\varphi})\subset Z_{\ell}
for every
\ell
;

ii.

if
\varphi\in Z_{k}
and
\psi\in Z_{\ell}
then some joining of them lies in
Z_{k+\ell}
.

If
\Gamma
is a discrete group, then its
summary space

\mathcal{Z}\Gamma
is the copy of the space
\mathcal{Z}(C^{\ast}\Gamma)
obtained our identification of
\Sigma_{k}(\Gamma)
with
\Sigma_{k}(C^{\ast}\Gamma)
.

Proposition 3.6
.

An element
Z_{\bullet}
of
\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{K}(\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A}))
is equal to
\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi)}
for some separable representation
\pi
if and only if it lies in
\mathcal{Z}A
.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
).
Let
Z_{\bullet}:=\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi)}
. It satisfies property (i) by the transitivity of approximation association (see Lemma
2.26
). To check property (ii), suppose that
\varphi\in\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}
and
\psi\in\overline{\Sigma_{\ell}(\pi)}
. Then there are sequences of
k
-tuples
X_{n}
and
\ell
-tuples
Y_{n}
in
H_{\pi}
such that

\Phi^{\pi}_{X_{n}}\to\varphi\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\Phi^{\pi}_{Y_{n}}\to\psi\qquad\hbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

The sequence of combined types
\Phi^{\pi}_{[X_{n},Y_{n}]}
is normalized by (
2.18
), and hence norm-bounded by (
2.9
). It therefore has subsequential limits by the Banach–Alaoglu theorem and the metrizability of
\Sigma_{k+\ell}(\mathfrak{A})
. Any of these is a joining of
\varphi
and
\psi
that lies in
\overline{\Sigma_{k+\ell}(\pi)}
.

(
\Leftarrow
).
Given
Z_{\bullet}
, we must construct a suitable representation
\pi
.

Let
S_{k}
be a countable dense subset of
Z_{k}
for each
k
, and let
S
be their union. Enumerate
S
as
\{\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\dots\}
, and let
\rho_{i}=\pi_{\varphi_{i}}
for each
i
.

For notational purposes, let
\pi_{0}
be a trivial representation. We now define by recursion a sequence of representations
\pi_{i}
,
i\geq 1
, that has the following properties:

•

\pi_{i+1}
contains both
\pi_{i}
and
\rho_{i+1}
for each
i\geq 0
;

•

each
\pi_{i}
has a finite cyclic tuple;

•

Z_{\bullet}
contains
\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{i})}
for each
i
.

To begin the recursion, let
\pi_{1}:=\rho_{1}
. Now suppose that
\pi_{1}
, …,
\pi_{i}
have already been constructed. Since
\pi_{i}
and
\rho_{i+1}
both have finite cyclic tuples, and since

Z_{\bullet}\supset\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{i})}\cup\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\rho_{i+1})},

applying property (ii) and then property (i) from Definition
3.5
gives a new representation
\pi_{i+1}
with a finite cyclic tuple that contains both
\pi_{i}
and
\rho_{i+1}
and satisfies
Z_{\bullet}\supset\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{i+1})}
. This continues the recursion.

To finish, let
\pi^{M_{i}}_{i+1}
be a subrepresentation equivalent to
\pi_{i}
, and define

\pi:=\pi_{1}\oplus\pi_{2}^{M_{1}^{\perp}}\oplus\pi_{3}^{M_{2}^{\perp}}\oplus\cdots.

This contains an increasing sequence of subrepresentations equivalent to
\pi_{1}
,
\pi_{2}
, …, and their union is dense in
H_{\pi}
. It follows by Lemma
2.23
that

\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}=\overline{\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{i})}\subset Z_{k}\qquad\hbox{for each}\ k.

On the other hand, we also have

\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}\supset\overline{\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\Sigma_{k}(\rho_{i})}\supset Z_{k}\qquad\hbox{for each}\ k

by the density of our initial choice of each
S_{k}
.
∎

Notes and further references

The first main results about approximate equivalence of representations are due to Voiculescu
[
175
]
, building on some fundamental technical results of Glimm
[
87
]
. See also
[
6
]
for a survey and a different approach that introduced some versatile new techniques. Later accounts such as the one in
[
53
]
generally follow the lines of
[
6
]
.

3.2

Compact operators and stiff parts

Voiculescu’s results from
[
175
]
show how to place approximate equivalence as a relation lying between equivalence and weak equivalence: it is stronger than weak equivalence, but only through the stiff parts of the representations involved (see Section
2.3
), on which it remembers multiplicity of irreducible summands and so implies true equivalence.

The next theorem is the engine of the work in
[
175
]
, and the theorem after it is the main new characterization of approximate equivalence from that paper. That paper also contains a further characterization as equivalence modulo the algebra of compact operators, but we do not need this here.

Theorem 3.7

(
[
175
, Theorem 1.3]
)
.

If
\rho
and
\pi
are two separable representations with the property that
\pi(a)
is zero whenever
\rho(a)
is compact, then
\rho\oplus\pi\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\rho
. ∎

Theorem 3.8

(Part of
[
175
, Theorem 1.5]
)
.

Two separable representations
\pi
and
\rho
are approximately equivalent if and only if all of the following hold:

•

they have the same kernel,

•

they have the same stiff ideal, and

•

their stiff parts are equivalent. ∎

Here is a special case of Theorem
3.8
that is worth recording explicitly:

Corollary 3.9
.

A separable representation
\pi
has no stiff part if and only if
\pi^{\oplus\infty}\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\pi
. ∎

For example, if
\lambda
is the left regular representation of a countably infinite group
\Gamma
, then it satisfies the conditions in Corollary
3.9
by a theorem of Takenouchi
[
166
]
(see Example
2.25
above). For finitely generated groups, Thoma’s theorem (Theorem
2.2
) gives even more.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated group and let
\lambda
be its left regular representation.

a.

The support of
\lambda
has no isolated points that are stiff.

b.

If
\pi
is another representation weakly equivalent to
\lambda
, then
\pi\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\lambda
.

Proof.

Part (a).
This requires separate proofs depending on whether
\Gamma
is virtually Abelian or not. The case of virtually Abelian groups reduces quickly to commutative Pontrjagin duality, and we omit further details here. In the second case, if
\mathrm{spt}\,\lambda
contains an isolated stiff member
[\rho]
, then some multiple of
\rho
is a direct summand of
\lambda
, by Corollary
2.8
. But then the resulting subrepresentation of
\lambda
would create a Type I summand in
\lambda(\Gamma)^{\prime\prime}
, contradicting Theorem
2.2
.

Part (b).
If
\pi
is weakly equivalent to
\lambda
, then they have the same support. By part (a) and Proposition
2.9
, it follows that the stiff parts of
\pi
and of
\lambda
are both trivial. Now approximate equivalence follows from Theorem
3.8
.
∎

In the sequel, we need a relative of Theorem
3.8
that gives the consequences for stiff parts if
\pi\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\rho
. This is slightly tricky, because in this situation the non-stiff part of
\pi
may contain some of the stiff part of
\rho
. But we can still prove the following. It is not contained directly in
[
175
]
, but follows by similar arguments.

Lemma 3.11
.

Suppose that
\rho\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\pi
, and let
a\in\mathfrak{A}
.

a.

We have
\|\pi(a)\|\leq\|\rho(a)\|
.

d.

We have
\mathrm{rank}\,\pi(a)\leq\mathrm{rank}\,\rho(a)
.

c.

If
\rho(a)
is compact then
\pi(a)
is compact.

Proof.

Part (a).
We have

\displaystyle\|\pi(a)\|^{2}=\|\pi(a^{\ast}a)\|

\displaystyle=\sup\{\langle\pi(a^{\ast}a)v,v\rangle:\ v\in H_{\pi},\ \|v\|=1\}

\displaystyle=\sup\{\varphi(a^{\ast}a):\ \varphi\in\overline{\Sigma_{1}(\pi)}\},

and similarly for
\|\rho(a)\|^{2}
. This proves part (a) because
\overline{\Sigma_{1}(\pi)}\subset\overline{\Sigma_{1}(\rho)}
.

Part (b).
For any positive integer
\ell
, we have
\mathrm{rank}\,\rho(a)\leq k
if and only if

\mathrm{det}\,\varphi(a^{\ast}a)=0\qquad\hbox{for every}\ \varphi\in\bigcup_{\ell\geq k+1}\overline{\Sigma_{\ell}(\rho)},

and similarly for
\pi(a)
. This proves part (b) because
\overline{\Sigma_{\ell}(\rho)}\supset\overline{\Sigma_{\ell}(\pi)}
for each
\ell
.

Part (c).
Since
\pi(a)
is compact if and only if
\pi(a^{\ast}a)
is compact, we may reduce to the case when
a
is non-negative. In this case, by the spectral theorem for self-adjoint compact operators,
\pi(a)
is compact if and only if it is the norm limit of a sequence of elements of
\pi[\mathfrak{A}]
that have finite-rank. Now the conclusion follows from parts (a) and (b). ∎

Corollary 3.12
.

If
\rho\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\pi
, then
\rho
is approximately equivalent to a representation that contains
\pi
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{I}
be the stiff ideal of
\rho
, let
M
be the stiff part of
\rho
, and let

N:=\overline{\mathrm{span}}\{\pi(a)v:\ v\in H_{\pi},\ a\in\mathfrak{I}\}.

Since
\mathfrak{I}
is an ideal,
M
and
N
are invariant subspaces of
\rho
and
\pi
, respectively.

On the one hand, if
a\in\mathfrak{I}
, then
\pi^{N^{\perp}}(a)=0
by construction. So Theorem
3.7
gives
\rho\oplus\pi^{N^{\perp}}\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\rho
. On the other hand,
\rho^{M}|\mathfrak{I}
and
\pi^{N}|\mathfrak{I}
are two non-degenerate representations of
\mathfrak{I}
by compact operators, and Lemma
3.11
shows that

\mathrm{rank}\,\rho^{M}(a)=\mathrm{rank}\,\rho(a)\geq\mathrm{rank}\,\pi(a)=\mathrm{rank}\,\pi^{N}(a)\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{I}).

Therefore Proposition
2.5
gives
\rho^{M}|\mathfrak{I}\gtrsim\pi^{N}|\mathfrak{I}
. Since
\rho^{M}|\mathfrak{I}
is non-degenerate, this implies the same property for
\pi^{N}|\mathfrak{I}
, and now Proposition
2.3
gives
\rho^{M}\gtrsim\pi^{N}
. Putting these conclusions together, we find that

\pi\simeq\pi^{N}\oplus\pi^{N^{\perp}}\lesssim\rho^{M}\oplus\pi^{N^{\perp}}\lesssim\rho\oplus\pi^{N^{\perp}}\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\rho.

∎

The results above shed light on the failure of the sets
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\rho)}
to be convex for some representation
\rho
. This can happen, but only if
\rho
has a non-trivial stiff part, in which case the multiplicities of irreducible subrepresentations are then constrained under approximate equivalence.

Notes and further references

Considering compact operators in the image of a representation leads to the definition of liminary and postliminary C
∗
-algebras
[
62
, Sections 4.3–4]
. Their representation theory is the most rigid and complete among separable C
∗
-algebras, culminating in Glimm’s theorem from
[
88
]
that a C
∗
-algebra is postliminary if and only if all of its representations are of Type I. An accessible introduction to this class of C
∗
-algebras is given in
[
5
, Chapter 4]
, and textbook accounts that include Glimm’s theorem are
[
62
, Chapter 9]
and
[
155
, Section 4.6]
.

Folland discusses the significance of these results for representations of groups in
[
77
, Section 7.2]
. The group C
∗
-algebras of various classes of connected Lie groups turn out to be postliminary, but Thoma’s theorems show that this is very rare among discrete groups: see
[
15
, Section 7.D]
.

For representations of a general separable C
∗
-alegbra, the connection between approximate equivalence and compact operators goes back to the foundational papers
[
175
,
6
]
, and is also covered in
[
53
, Sections 41 and 42]
. Among the most consequential results is that, if
\pi\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\rho
, then, in addition to the vanishing of the operator norms in part (iii) of Theorem
3.3
, one can also require that the differences
\pi(a)-U_{n}^{\ast}\rho(a)U_{n}
be compact operators. This fact is another part of
[
53
, Theorem 41.12]
. This aspect has become central because of connections with the study of
\mathrm{Ext}
, K-theory and K-homology for operator algebras initiated by Brown, Douglas and Fillmore
[
35
,
36
]
. It is the main focus of some more recent accounts: see, for instance,
[
101
, Chapter 3]
.

3.3

The quotient and strong-quotient topologies

The summary space
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
is a subset of
\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{K}(\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A}))
. We can endow that product space with the product of either the Vietoris topologies or the lower Vietoris topologies (see Appendix
E
), and then restrict that product topology to
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
. Having done so, we refer slightly abusively to either the Vietoris or the lower Vietoris topology ‘on’
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
.

These two topologies on
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
have different properties and capture different phenomena. We start with the lower Vietoris topology, which leads to Fell’s classical quotient topology on representations themselves.

3.3.1
The lower Vietoris and quotient topologies

Let
R
be any set of separable representations, or of equivalence classes of such. Then we can use the summary map

R\to\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}:\ \big(\ \pi\ \hbox{or}\ [\pi]\ \big)\ \mapsto\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi)}

(3.1)

to pull the lower Vietoris topology on
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
back to a topology on
R
. Like the lower Vietoris topology itself, this pullback is second countable. We call it the
quotient topology
on
R
. We often indicate it by the single letter ‘q’ in a phrase a such as ‘q-open’.

When
R=\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
, the quotient topology agrees with the Fell topology. It has many other equivalent characterizations: see
[
62
, Chapter 3]
for a full account, or
[
77
, Section 7.2]
for a quicker overview in the case of a group C
∗
-algebra.

However, these characterization can diverge outside the class of irreducible representations. Fell studied two of these possibilities in
[
71
,
73
]
, calling them the ‘quotient’ and ‘inner hull-kernel’ topologies. Our present need is for the first of these, but later practice has generally assigned Fell’s name to the second. So we follow Fell himself in referring to the ‘quotient topology’ here. It can be defined as above, or as the quotient to equivalence classes of the pointwise weak operator topology on spaces of actual representations. The equivalence of these definitions follows by the arguments in
[
73
]
or
[
62
, Section 3.5]
.

Upon restricting to the summary space, we can simplify the family of basic open sets for the lower Vietoris topology.

Lemma 3.13
.

For any positive integer
k
and open subset
U
of
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
, let

\mathcal{W}(k,U):=\Big\{Z_{\bullet}\in\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{K}(\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})):\ Z_{k}\cap U\neq\emptyset\Big\}.

Then the collection of all subsets of
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
of the form
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}\cap\mathcal{W}(k,U)
is a base for the lower Vietoris topology on
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
.

Proof.

The sets
\mathcal{W}(k,U)
are a sub-base for the product of the lower Vietoris topologies on
\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{K}(\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A}))
by definition. The point is to show that they actually become a base once we intersect with
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
.

To this end, suppose that
Z_{\bullet}\in\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
and
Z_{\bullet}\in\mathcal{W}(k,U)\cap\mathcal{W}(\ell,V)
. Choose a representation
\pi
so that
Z_{\bullet}=\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi)}
(Proposition
3.6
), and now choose tuples
X
and
Y
in
H_{\pi}
so that
\Phi^{\pi}_{X}\in U
and
\Phi^{\pi}_{Y}\in V
. Now Lemma
2.26
gives a neighbourhood
W
of
\Phi^{\pi}_{[X,Y]}
such that, if
Z^{\prime}_{\bullet}\in\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
and
Z^{\prime}_{k+\ell}\cap W\neq\emptyset
, then also
Z^{\prime}_{k}\cap U\neq\emptyset
and
Z^{\prime}_{\ell}\cap V\neq\emptyset
. This shows that

\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}\cap\mathcal{W}(k,U)\cap\mathcal{W}(\ell,V)\supset\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}\cap\mathcal{W}(k+\ell,W)\ni Z_{\bullet}.

∎

Pulling Lemma
3.13
back to a collection
R
of representations, we find that a base for the quotient topology on
R
is given by the sets of the form

\{\pi\in R:\ \mathcal{X}(\pi,U)\neq\emptyset\}

as
k
ranges over positive integers and
U
ranges over open subsets of
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
, and similarly if
R
is a set of equivalence classes of representations.

Like lower Vietoris topologies in general, the quotient topology can fail to be T
1
. It can even fail to be T
0
in case the summary map is not injective. Unpacking the definitions, we find that, for two representations
\rho
and
\pi
, the singletons
\{[\rho]\}
and
\{[\pi]\}
have the same closures in the quotient topology if and only if
\rho\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\pi
. In case
\rho
and
\pi
are irreducible, one can also replace approximate equivalence with weak equivalence here
[
62
, Section 3.4]
.

3.3.2
The Vietoris and strong-quotient topologies

Now consider the space
\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{K}(\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A}))
with the product of the Vietoris topologies of its coordinate factors. Each of those is compact, and hence so is the whole product. Since we assume
\mathfrak{A}
is separable, each
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
is metrizable (see
[
52
, Proposition V.5.1]
, for example). Therefore this countable product topology is also metrizable, and hence so is its restriction to
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
. It may therefore be understood in terms of sequences, and specifically using the criterion in (
E.1
). Specializing to the present setting, this becomes the following.

Lemma 3.14
.

A sequence
(Z_{n,\bullet})_{n\geq 1}
in
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
converges to a limit in the Vietoris topology if and only if, for every positive integer
k
, every
\varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
satisfies one of the following:

i.

every neighbourhood of
\varphi
meets
Z_{n,k}
for all sufficiently large
n
;

ii.

some neighbourhood of
\varphi
meets
Z_{n,k}
for only finitely many
n
.

In this case,

\displaystyle\lim_{n}Z_{n,k}

\displaystyle=\{\varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A}):\ \varphi\ \hbox{satisfies (i)}\}

\displaystyle=\{\lim_{n}\varphi_{n}:\ \varphi_{n}\in Z_{n,k}\ \hbox{and}\ (\varphi_{n})_{n\geq 1}\ \hbox{converges}\}.

∎

Let us use this lemma to verify the following basic property.

Lemma 3.15
.

The space
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
is a Vietoris-closed subset of
\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{K}(\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A}))
.

Proof.

Let
(Z_{n,\bullet})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
that converges to
Z_{\bullet}
. We must show that
Z_{\bullet}
still satisfies properties (i) and (ii) from Definition
3.5
.

Property (i).
Suppose that
\varphi\in Z_{k}
,
\psi\in\Sigma_{\ell}(\pi_{\varphi})
, and
U
is a neighbourhood of
\psi
. Then Lemma
2.26
gives a neighbourhood
V
of
\varphi
such that

\mathcal{X}(\pi,V)\neq\emptyset\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\mathcal{X}(\pi,U)\neq\emptyset

(3.2)

for any representation
\pi
.

Since
\varphi\in Z_{k}
, Lemma
3.14
gives a sequence of elements
\varphi_{n}\in Z_{n,k}
that converges to
\varphi
. These elements witness that
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{\varphi_{n}},V)
is nonempty for all sufficiently large
n
, and so
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{\varphi_{n}},U)
is also nonempty for all sufficiently large
n
, by (
3.2
). Therefore
U\cap Z_{n,\ell}
is also nonempty for all sufficiently large
n
. By another appeal to Lemma
3.14
, this shows that
\psi\in Z_{\ell}
.

Property (ii).
Let
\varphi\in Z_{k}
and
\psi\in Z_{\ell}
. By Lemma
3.14
, there are sequences
\varphi_{n}\in Z_{n,k}
converging to
\varphi
and
\psi_{n}\in Z_{n,\ell}
converging to
\psi
. For each
n
, property (ii) for
Z_{n,\bullet}
gives a joining
\theta_{n}
of
\varphi_{n}
and
\psi_{n}
that lies in
Z_{n,k+\ell}
. By compactness, the sequence
(\theta_{n})_{n\geq 1}
has at least one subsequential limit in
\Sigma_{k+\ell}(\mathfrak{A})
. This limit lies in
Z_{k+\ell}
by another appeal to Lemma
3.14
, and it is a joining of
\varphi
and
\psi
.
∎

Thus, with its Vietoris topology, the space
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
is again compact and metrizable (compare
[
1
, Theorem 4]
and
[
171
, Theorem 5.1]
).

By comparing Lemma
3.13
with the definition of the Vietoris topology, we obtain a counterpart of that lemma for this topology.

Lemma 3.16
.

Consider the sets that have the form

\big\{Z_{\bullet}\in\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}:\ Z_{\ell}\subset O\ \hbox{and}\ Z_{k}\cap U\neq\emptyset\big\}

for some positive integers
k
and
\ell
and some open subsets
O
of
\Sigma_{\ell}(\mathfrak{A})
and
U
of
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
. These are a base for the Vietoris topology of
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
.

Proof.

For any
\ell
and
O
as above, let

\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(\ell,O):=\Big\{Z_{\bullet}\in\prod_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{K}(\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})):\ Z_{\ell}\subset O\Big\}.

Let
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
be positive integers and consider open subsets
O_{1}
of
\Sigma_{\ell_{1}}(\mathfrak{A})
and
O_{2}
of
\Sigma_{\ell_{2}}(\mathfrak{A})
. Now let

L_{1}=\{1,\dots,\ell_{1}\},\qquad L_{2}:=\{\ell_{1}+1,\dots,\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}\},

and

O:=\{\varphi\in\Sigma_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}(\mathfrak{A}):\ \varphi[L_{1}]\in O_{1}\ \hbox{and}\ \varphi[L_{2}]\in O_{2}\}.

The axiom from Definition
3.5
give

\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}\cap\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(\ell,O)\subset\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}\cap\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(\ell_{1},O_{1})\cap\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(\ell_{2},O_{2}).

Arguing as for Lemma
3.13
, and using the fact already proved in that lemma, the present lemma follows as well.
∎

If
R
is any set of separable representations or their equivalence classes, then we can pull the Vietoris topology from
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
back to
R
through the map (
3.1
). This is essentially the definition of the topologies (on measure-preserving actions or representations) given in
[
1
,
171
]
. Following
[
8
]
, we call this the
strong-quotient topology
. We often refer to it by the abbreviation ‘sq’ in phrases such as ‘sq-open’ or ‘sq-convergence’. The latter is one of the basic modes of convergence that we study for sequences of finite-dimensional representations later.

Definition 3.17
.

A completely positive map
\varphi
is
asymptotically associated
to
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
if every neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi
satisfies
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset
for infinitely many
n
.

Suppose that
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
. Because of the second countability from Lemma
2.12
,
\varphi
is asymptotically associated to
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
as above if and only if there are a subsequence
n_{1}<n_{2}<\dots
and tuples
v_{i,1}
, …,
v_{i,k}
in
H_{\pi_{n_{i}}}
such that

\Phi^{\pi_{n_{i}}}_{v_{i_{1}},\dots,v_{i_{k}}}\to\varphi.

If the sequence
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
sq converges, then the limit of
\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{n})}
is precisely the set of maps asymptotically associated to
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, by Lemma
3.14
.

3.3.3
Other topologies and their relations

Fell introduced the quotient topology in
[
71
,
73
]
alongside another option which he called the ‘inner hull kernel’ topology. The latter is now often called the ‘Fell topology’. In our terminology, the Fell topology can also be expressed as the pullback of a lower Vietoris topology. The differences from the quotient topology are that the Fell topology (i) allows only the case
k=1
, and (ii) depends on which positive functionals can be approximated by
convex combinations
of those associated to a given representation. As a result of (ii), the Fell topology is insensitive to multiplicities of subrepresentations, whereas the quotient topology is not in general. To capture (ii) by pulling back a lower Vietoris topology, we apply that topology to
\overline{\mathrm{conv}}\,\Sigma_{1}(\pi)
rather than
\overline{\Sigma_{1}(\pi)}
for each representation
\pi
.

On the other hand, neither the quotient topology nor the Fell topology controls the values of operator norms
\|\pi(a)\|
for a fixed element
a
of
\mathfrak{A}
and a variable choice of the representation
\pi
. In general these norms are only lower semicontinuous in those topologies, not continuous; see, for instance,
[
62
, Section 3.3]
. Recent years have seen a swell of interest in a mode of convergence for representations called ‘strong convergence’, which is defined precisely to control these operator norms; see, for intance, the recent survey
[
126
]
. That convergence, in turn, can be defined by pulling back a Vietoris rather than a lower Vietoris topology. However, like the Fell topology, it consider only the closed convex hulls of the sets
\Sigma_{1}(\pi)
for a representation
\pi
.

The next table summarizes this point of view on the Fell, quotient, strong, and strong-quotient topologies.

This mode…

… means convergence of …

… in this topology

Fell

\overline{\mathrm{conv}}\,\Sigma_{1}(\pi_{n})

lower Vietoris

quotient

\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})}
for each
k

lower Vietoris

strong

\overline{\mathrm{conv}}\,\Sigma_{1}(\pi_{n})

Vietoris

strong-quotient

\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})}
for each
k

Vietoris

In the study of AP entropy, it is important that we consider approximate types without allowing convex combinations. This is why the quotient and strong quotient topologies are of of greater interest to us here.

In general, given a sequence
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of separable representations and another representation
\pi
, the convergence of
\pi_{n}
to
\pi
is a different property in the different topologies. But sometimes properties of
\pi
imply that some of them coincide, such as in the next lemma.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated group and
\lambda
be its left regular representation. If
\pi_{n}\to\lambda
strongly, then
\pi_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{sq}}}{{\to}}\lambda
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{Z}\Gamma
is compact and metrizable, the sequence
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
has a subsequential limit in the strong-quotient topology, say
\rho
. It suffices to prove that
\rho\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\lambda
. Since sq-convergence implies strong convergence, this limit must satisfy

\|\rho(a)\|=\lim_{n}\|\pi_{n}(a)\|=\|\lambda(a)\|\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{A}).

This implies that
\rho
and
\lambda
are weakly equivalent, and now the result follows from Proposition
3.10
.
∎

Notes and further references

For a locally compact group
\Gamma
, Godemont defined a topology on the unitary dual
\widehat{\Gamma}
in
[
89
]
, and showed that it corresponds to the Jacobson topology on
\mathrm{Prim}(\Gamma)
. His definition is in terms of the weakly associated sets of positive definite functions, and he also defined the relation of weak containment on representations of
\Gamma
in the process. For a general C
∗
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, Fell then introduced his topology on
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
and related it to weak containment in
[
72
]
. He studied its generalizations to larger classes of representations in
[
73
]
, for the purpose of applying it to induced representations. Thorough textbook accounts of this theory are given in
[
16
, Appendix F]
for group representations and
[
74
, Chapter VII]
for C
∗
-algebras. Note that the latter reference calls the resulting topology on a space of representations the ‘regional topology’.

The terms ‘approximate association’ and ‘approximate containment’ are not standard. I have chosen them to make the connection with ‘approximate equivalence’ (which is a standard term). Nevertheless, the idea of approximate containment does have a well-established place in the literature: see, for instance, the way that Doran and Fell initially define the ‘regional topology’ in
[
74
, Section VII.1]
.

Another convention is to refer to approximate containment as ‘weak containment in the sense of Zimmer’: see, for instance,
[
16
,
111
,
15
,
1
]
. Zimmer does indeed use the term ‘weak containment’ in
[
184
, Definition 7.3.5]
for what we call ‘approximate containment’. However, in
[
184
]
Zimmer himself attributes this definition to Fell’s original papers. I have not found it there explicitly, but it is suggested rather naturally by Fell’s manipulations with the quotient topology: see, for example,
[
73
, Lemma 1.3]
. The preface to
[
74
]
suggests that the full history is complicated.

Chapter 4

Almost periodic sequences and almost periodic entropy

In this final chapter of Part
I
, we recall the basic theory of a new quantity recently introduced in
[
8
]
. This is ‘almost periodic’ (or ‘AP’) entropy. It depends on a completely positive map and a fixed sequence of finite-dimensional representations called an ‘almost periodic sequence’. It is a rough analog of sofic entropy for representations of C*-algebras rather than measure-preserving group actions. It is the deterministic predecessor of the two new notions of entropy that we introduce in Part
I
: ‘annealed’ and ‘zeroth-order’ AP entropy.

4.1

Almost periodic sequences

Definition 4.1

(Almost periodic sequence)
.

An
almost periodic
(‘
AP
’)
sequence
for
\mathfrak{A}
is a sequence of finite-dimensional representations of
\mathfrak{A}
whose dimensions tend to
\infty
.

The quotient and strong-quotient topologies from Section
3.3
offer two possible modes of convergence for an AP sequence. Neither of these depends on the representations being finite-dimensional.

We sometimes need another mode of convergence which is specific to finite-dimensional representations. For an AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}
, this is the convergence of the pulled-back traces on
\mathfrak{A}
: that is,
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
in the weak
∗
topology for some tracial state
\tau
on
\mathfrak{A}
. This is rather different from the other two modes, but the phenomenon of measure concentration does give rise to a connection: see Proposition
4.7
(b) below.

All of these modes of convergence appear naturally in explorations of how the finite-dimensional representations are distributed among all representations in either topology. We next recall a few aspects of this long-running story.

Maximal almost periodicity

A group
\Gamma
is called
maximally almost periodic
if it has an AP sequence
(\pi_{i})_{i\geq 1}
that separates the elements of the group. For finitely generated groups, this property turns out to be equivalent to residual finiteness. Maximally almost periodic groups were introduced by von Neumann
[
172
]
and treated by Weil in
[
183
, Chapter VII]
and Dixmier in
[
62
, Sections 16.4–5]
. A more modern introduction can be found in
[
15
, Subsection 4.C.b]
. See also
[
38
, Section 6.4.2]
for a connection from maximal almost periodicity to Kirchberg’s ‘factorization property’.

If
\mathfrak{A}=C^{\ast}\Gamma
where
\Gamma
is countable and discrete, then an AP sequence whose tracial states converge to
\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}
is called
asymptotically regular
. Such an AP sequence is necessarily separating, so a group must be maximally almost periodic to have such a sequence. On the other hand, starting from any AP sequence that separates the elements of
\Gamma
, a standard construction of running tensor products produces a new such sequence whose tracial states converge to
\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}
. So a finitely generated group has an asymptotically regular AP sequence if and only if it is residually finite, by the result recalled above.

Residual finite dimensionality

A general C
∗
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is called
residually finite dimensional
if it has a faithful family of finite-dimensional representations. This is equivalent to the subset
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathrm{fd}}
of finite-dimensional irreducible representation classes being dense in
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
for the Fell topology. For a group C
∗
-algebra, this is strictly stronger than maximal almost periodicity in general, and it is not known to hold for many groups. One important family of examples is free groups, for which this property follows from work of Lubotzky and Shalom
[
121
]
. In fact, they prove the even stronger result that representations induced by finite permutation actions are dense in the Fell topology. By an extension of this argument one can actually approximately ‘count’ the finite-permutation actions that weakly approximate a given measure-preserving free-group action. This discovery emerges independently from Bowen’s works
[
31
,
27
,
27
]
on annealed sofic entropy. The survey
[
42
]
goes over this story carefully.

4.1.1
Free groups and their quotients

The properties of maximal almost periodicity and residual finite dimensionality identify rather special classes of groups. However AP sequences and AP entropy can be used to study much larger classes of groups by requiring that the group law hold only ‘in the limit’ along an AP sequence. To be specific, let
\Gamma
be any countable group, and write it as
F/N
for some free group
F
and normal subgroup
N
. Now we can look for finite-dimensional representations of
F
whose pulled-back tracial states converge to
1_{N}
, rather than finite-dimensional representations of
\Gamma
whose tracial states converge to
1_{\{e\}}
. This allows considerable extra flexibility, because those finite-dimensional representations of
F
need not have trivial restriction to
N
until we take their limit. Allowing convergence in this sense, the availability of finite-dimensional approximants to the regular representation of
\Gamma
is equivalent to
\Gamma
being what Rădulescu called ‘hyperlinear’: see, for instance, the original paper
[
150
]
or the introductions in
[
143
]
or
[
44
]
.

In this way, free groups are essentially universal among all countable groups for the purposes of the present work, since the desired convergence for other groups can always be captured by choosing the right limit character over the free group. For this reason, results that are formulated for free groups, including the results of Part
III
below, could still lead to new understanding of other classes of groups or other C
∗
-algebras entirely.

Focusing on free groups in particular, we arrive at Voiculescu’s theory of ‘free probability’ and the use of probabilistic techniques. For example, if
\Gamma
is freely generated by
\{s_{1},\dots,s_{r}\}
, and
\boldsymbol{\pi}=(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is an AP sequence for it, then
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is asymptotically regular if and only if the tuples of generators

\pi_{n}(s_{1}),\dots\pi_{n}(s_{r})\in\mathrm{U}(d_{n})\qquad(n\geq 1)

are
asymptotically free
in Voiculescu’s sense: see the survey
[
177
]
or textbooks such as
[
179
,
100
]
or
[
4
, Chapter 5]
.

Free groups have many such sequences, but they may not be easy to construct explicitly. Instead, one of the basic results of free probability theory asserts that ‘randomly chosen’
n
-dimensional representations are asymptotically free with high probability as
n\to\infty
. Such random AP sequences are the beginning of our work in Part
II
below; see Theorems
5.1
and
5.2
for asymptotic freeness itself.

Beyond this, essentially following
[
43
,
51
]
, an AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}=(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
for a free group
\Gamma
is called
strongly asymptotically free
if it converges strongly to the regular representation. Here, too, the known proofs that such sequences exist for free groups are probabilistic. They include the result of Collins and Male
[
51
]
for uniform random representations (which is based on an analogous result for the Gaussian unitary ensemble by Haagerup and Thorbjørnsen
[
96
]
) and the result of Bordenave and Collins
[
22
]
for random permutations; we recall these formally as Theorems
5.3
and
5.4
below. See also the survey
[
126
]
. Note that, while those references discuss strong convergence, this immediately implies sq-convergence by Lemma
3.18
as well, since the limit is a regular representation.

For any countable group
\Gamma
, if an AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}
converges strongly to the left regular representation
\lambda
, then any subsequential limit of the sequence
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}
in the compact space
\Sigma_{1}(\Gamma)
extends to a tracial state on the reduced C
∗
-algebra
C_{\mathrm{red}}^{\ast}\Gamma:=C^{\ast}(\lambda)
. In case
C_{\mathrm{red}}^{\ast}\Gamma
has only the regular tracial state, it follows that
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}
must also to
\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}
. This property is quite widespread. For free groups it is a classical result of Powers
[
145
]
, so strong asymptotic freeness does indeed imply asymptotic freeness. For other groups, the definitive recent work
[
34
]
explains the story in full.

Remark 4.2
.

This discussion above also has much in common with the study of various modes of convergence for sparse sequences of large finite graphs. This link consists of instructive analogies more than formal results, but offers considerable guidance and motivation for our study. It is discussed more fully in
[
1
]
.
\lhd

4.2

Almost periodic entropy

Define the function

v(d):=\frac{\pi^{d}}{d!}\qquad(d=1,2,\dots).

(4.1)

Then
v(d)
is the volume of the unit ball in
\mathbf{C}^{d}

[
154
, Subsection 1.4.9]
.

Now let
\mathfrak{A}
be a separable unital C
∗
-algebra. Fix an AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}=(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, a positive integer
k
, and an element
\varphi
of
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
. Let
d_{n}
be the dimension of
\pi_{n}
for each
n
.

Definition 4.3
.

The
almost periodic
(‘
AP
’)
entropy of
\varphi
along
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is the quantity

\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi):=\inf_{O}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{d_{n}}\log\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}},

(4.2)

where the infimum runs over all neighbourhoods of
\varphi
in
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
.

Recall Definition
2.22
for the set
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)
of
O
-typical vectors. By the monotonicity of these sets in
O
, we can restrict
O
to any base of neighbourhoods at
\varphi
in formula
4.2
without changing the resulting value.

The next results are some of the basic properties of AP entropy established in
[
8
]
. The first is an immediate consequence of its definition and Lemma
D.3
.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\pi}
and
k
, the function
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
is upper semicontinuous on
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
. ∎

Proposition 4.5
.

Fix a positive integer
k
and a map
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
.

a.

We have

\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)\leq\log\det\varphi(1).

b.

If
Q
is an invertible
k
-by-
k
matrix, and we define

\psi(a):=Q^{\ast}\varphi(a)Q\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{A}),

(4.3)

then

\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\psi)=2\log|\det Q|+\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi).

∎

These facts are Lemma 9.7 and Proposition 9.10(a) in
[
8
]
, respectively. We extend them to annealed AP entropy in Chapter
5
.

Some proofs about AP entropy are easier to digest when
k=1
. Several properties are proved in
[
8
]
by starting with that case and then using pairings as in (
2.7
) and the general identity

\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{(k)}}(\langle\varphi,\cdot\rangle)=\frac{1}{k}\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)\qquad(\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}).

(4.4)

See
[
8
, Lemma 9.8]
.

We do not use identity (
4.4
) much in the present book, but we do cite some properties of AP entropy that are proved this way in
[
8
]
. Some of those proofs use (
4.4
) in combination with the following alternative formula for the AP entropy of a state, which is
[
8
, Lemma 9.12]
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\varphi\in\Sigma(\mathfrak{A})
, and let
\mathcal{O}
be a neighbourhood base at
\varphi
in
\Sigma(\mathfrak{A})
. Then

\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=\inf_{O\in\mathcal{O}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{d_{n}}\log\sigma_{2d_{n}-1}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O).

∎

4.3

Convergence of traces

Several further properties of AP entropy can be deduced if the normalized traces
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}
are known to converge to a limiting trace in
\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}_{+}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Assume that
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
, and let
\lambda
be the GNS representation of
\tau
. Let
k
be a positive integer and let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
.

a.

If
\varphi
is approximately associated to
\lambda^{\oplus\infty}
, then
\varphi
is asymptotically associated to
\boldsymbol{\pi}
.

b.

If
\varphi_{\mathrm{sing}}
is asymptotically associated to
\boldsymbol{\pi}
, then so is the whole of
\varphi
.

c.

If
\varphi(1)
is non-singular, then equality holds in Proposition
4.5
(a) if and only if
\varphi=\tau\otimes\varphi(1)
.

∎

Parts (a), (b) and (c) here are
[
8
, Corollary 9.15]
,
[
8
, Corollary 9.16]
and
[
8
, Proposition 9.17]
, respectively. They all stem from an application of measure concentration for the high-dimensional spherical measures
\sigma_{2d-1}
. Since the trace of a matrix is invariant under unitary conjugation, if
\pi
is a
d
-dimensional representation, then averaging over all of
\mathrm{U}(d)
gives

\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d}\circ\pi=\int_{\mathrm{S}^{2d-1}}\Phi^{\pi}_{x}\ d\sigma_{2d-1}(x),

(4.5)

where
\sigma_{2d-1}=m_{\mathrm{U}(1,n)}
is the uniform distribution on
\mathrm{S}^{2d-1}
. When
d
is large, an appeal to measure concentration improves on (
4.5
) considerably:
\Phi^{\pi}_{x}
is actually close to
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d}\circ\pi
for ‘most’
x\in\mathrm{S}^{2d-1}
individually. Now one can combine this conclusion with Lemma
2.26
.

In the sequel, we need approximate versions of Proposition
4.7
(a) and (b) that apply to a single high-dimensional representation, rather than to a whole AP sequence.

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
\tau
be a tracial state of
\mathfrak{A}
, let
\lambda
be its GNS representation, and let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
.

a.

Assume that
\varphi
is approximately associated to
\lambda^{\oplus k}
. Then, for every neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi
, there are a positive integer
d_{0}
and a neighbourhood
V
of
\tau
such that the following holds:

If
\pi
is a
d
-dimensional representation satisfying

d\geq d_{0}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d}\circ\pi\in V

then
\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)\neq\emptyset
.

b.

For every neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi
there are a positive integer
d_{0}
and neighbourhoods
U
of
\varphi_{\mathrm{sing}}
and
V
of
\tau
such that the following holds:

If
\pi
is a
d
-dimensional representation satisfying

d\geq d_{0},\qquad\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d}\circ\pi\in V,\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\mathcal{X}(\pi,U)\neq\emptyset

then
\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

We show how to deduce part (a) from Proposition
4.7
(a). The proof of (b) follows in the same way from Proposition
4.7
(b), so we leave this to the reader.

Suppose that the result is false, and let
O
be a neighbourhood of
\varphi
for which it fails. Because of Lemma

2.12
, we can choose a countable neighbourhood base
V_{1}\supset V_{2}\supset\dots
at
\tau
. Then a diagonal argument gives a sequence
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of finite-dimensional representations such that

d_{n}\geq n\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\in V_{n}

but
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)=\emptyset
, meaning that
O
does not meet
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})}
. Together, these properties of
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
show that it is an AP sequence which contradicts Proposition
4.7
(a).
∎

4.4

The determinantal formula

Given a tracial state
\tau
and a completely positive map
\varphi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
, Corollary
2.17
decomposes
\varphi
into a summand
\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}
that is absolutely continuous with respect to
\tau
and another summand
\varphi_{\mathrm{sing}}
that is singular with respect to
\tau
. The most substantial theorem about AP entropy from
[
8
]
is the following formula: see
[
8
, Theorem C]
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Assume that
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
, and let
\Delta
be the Fuglede–Kadison determinant associated to
\tau
. If
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
is asymptotically associated to
\boldsymbol{\pi}
, then

\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=\log\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}.

Remark 4.10
.

Theorem
4.9
can be regarded as a non-commutative generalization of Szegő’s classic limit theorem for positive definite Toeplitz determinants (see
[
162
,
163
]
). It is discussed more fully from this point of view in
[
8
]
, which includes other theorems that also have this flavour. The most basic connection here is that the limit in Szegő’s theorem can also be interpreted as the logarithm of a Fuglede–Kadison determinant applied to the absolutely continuous part of a positive functional.

Szegő’s original theorem connects to several finer aspects of the study of orthogonal polynomials on the unit circle, and I am not aware of generalizations for most of these to the setting of Theorem
4.9
. However, in the special case of uniformly random AP sequences for free groups, we do find a tighter connection with Szegő’s theorem in which many of these additional details reappear. This begins with the definition of ‘generalized Verblunsky coefficients’ in Section
9.2
below, and continues in Section
12.4
and in the discussion of open problems related to annealed AP entropy in Chapter
14
.
\lhd

Even if
\varphi
is asymptotically associated to
\boldsymbol{\pi}
, it may happen that
\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}=0
, in which case its logarithm is still equal to
-\infty
. However, very importantly, there is at most one possible finite value for
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
once we know that
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
.

In the sequel, we need to cite a reformulation of Theorem
4.9
that gives an approximation for a single high-dimensional representation, rather than referring to an AP sequence. This is the next theorem.

Theorem 4.11
.

Fix
\tau
and
\varphi
as above.

a.

For any
h>\log\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}
, there are a positive integer
d_{0}
, a neighbourhood
V
of
\tau
, and a neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi
such that the following holds:

If
\pi
is a
d
-dimensional representation of
\mathfrak{A}
satisfying

d\geq d_{0}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d}\circ\pi\in V,

then

\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd}\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)}{v(d)^{k}}\leq e^{hd}.

b.

If
\log\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}>h>\infty
, and if
O
is any neighbourhood of
\varphi
, then there are a positive integer
d_{0}
, a neighbourhood
V
of
\tau
, and another neighbourhood
O^{\prime}
of
\varphi
such that the following holds:

If
\pi
is a
d
-dimensional representation of
\mathfrak{A}
satisfying

d\geq d_{0},\quad\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d}\circ\pi\in V,\quad\hbox{and}\quad\mathcal{X}(\pi,O^{\prime})\neq\emptyset,

then

\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd}\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)}{v(d)^{k}}\geq e^{hd}.

Proof.

Both parts can be proved from Theorem
4.9
by a diagonal argument and a contradiction, similarly to the proof of Corollary
4.8
. We give the details for part (a) and leave part (b) to the reader.

Suppose that part (a) is false for some
\tau
and
\varphi
. Then it fails for some
h>\log\Delta\varphi
. Because of Lemma
2.12
, we can choose countable neighbourhoods bases
V_{1}\supset V_{2}\supset\cdots
at
\tau
and
O_{1}\supset O_{2}\supset\cdots
at
\varphi
. Now a diagonal argument gives a sequence
(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of finite-dimensional representations such that

d_{n}\geq n\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\in V_{n}

but

\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O_{n})}{v(d_{n})^{k}}\geq e^{hd_{n}}.

Letting
O
be any other neighbourhood of
\varphi
, we must have
O\supset O_{n}
for all sufficiently large
n
, and at that point
\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)
is bounded below by the last inequality above. Substituting this lower bound into Definition
5.5
and taking the infimum over
O
, we arrive at the inequality

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)\geq h,

which contradicts Theorem
4.9
.
∎

Several corollaries are derived from Theorem
4.9
in
[
8
, Subsection 9.6]
. We recall a few of these next. Some or our results later are probabilistic generalizations of these, with closely related proofs.

The first corollary relates the limits of the sequences
(\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n}))_{n\geq 1}
,
k=1,2,\dots
, to properties of the entropy functional
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
. To do this, it studies a general element
\varphi
of
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
by using it to form the perturbations

\varphi_{t}:=\tau\otimes I_{k}+t\varphi\qquad(t\geq 0).

Corollary 4.12
.

Assume that
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi\to\tau
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})

\displaystyle=\{\varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A}):\ \mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi_{t})\geq 0\ \forall t\geq 0\}

\displaystyle=\{\varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A}):\ \mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi_{t})\to 0\ \hbox{as}\ t\downarrow 0\}

for each positive integer
k
, and so
\boldsymbol{\pi}
strong-quotient converges if and only if we have
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}}=\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
whenever
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}
is a subsequence of
\boldsymbol{\pi}
. ∎

See
[
8
, Corollary 9.23]
.

The next corollary gives conditions for AP entropy to be additive for diagonal joinings or concave.

Corollary 4.13
.

Assume that
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi\to\tau
, and let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
and
\psi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{\ell})_{+}
.

a.

If
\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi)
is asymptotically associated to
\boldsymbol{\pi}
, then

\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi))=\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)+\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\psi).

b.

If
k=\ell
,
t\in[0,1]
, and
t\varphi+(1-t)\psi
is asymptotically associated to
\boldsymbol{\pi}
, then

\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(t\varphi+(1-t)\psi)\geq t\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)+(1-t)\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\psi).

∎

Parts (a) and (b) of this corollary are contained within
[
8
, Corollary 9.24]
and
[
8
, Corollary 9.26]
, respectively. (Those original corollaries are slightly more detailed.)

Part II
ENTROPY FOR RANDOM REPRESENTATIONS

Chapter 5

Random almost periodic sequences and notions of entropy

5.1

Random almost periodic sequences

Our first new topic is a generalization of AP entropy which allows the individual representations in an AP sequence to be random. Formally, assume that
\mathfrak{A}
is unital, and let
\mathrm{Rep}_{n}(\mathfrak{A})
denote the space of unital
\ast
-representations of
\mathfrak{A}
on
\mathbf{C}^{n}
for each
n
. This is a space of maps
\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}(\mathbf{C}^{n})
, and we endow it with the topology of pointwise convergence as in
[
62
, Subsection 3.5.2]
. This topology is Polish because
\mathfrak{A}
is separable
[
62
, Proposition 3.7.1]
.

Fix a background probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbf{P})
. On this space, a
random almost periodic
(‘
AP
’)
sequence
is a sequence of random elements
\pi_{n}\in\mathrm{Rep}_{d_{n}}(\mathfrak{A})
,
n=1,2,\dots
for some divergent sequence of positive integers
(d_{n})_{n\geq 1}
. We always write
\mathbf{E}
for expectation with respect to
\mathbf{P}
.

The assumption that every
\pi_{n}
is defined on the same background probability space lightens notation, but we only ever consider quantities that involve one value of
n
at a time. As a result, the properties we consider depend only on the sequence of individual distributions of each
\pi_{n}
; the coupling of those distributions is unimportant. The reader wanting a definite choice may always assume that they are independent.

5.2

Main examples for free groups

We now introduce some of our main examples. Let
\Gamma
be the group freely generated by a set
S
of size
r
, and let
\mathfrak{A}:=C^{\ast}\Gamma
. In addition, let
(d_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a divergent sequence of positive integers, and let
\nu_{n}
be a Borel probability measure on
\mathrm{U}(d_{n})
for each
n
. For each
n
, let
\pi_{n}
be a random element of
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\mathrm{U}(d_{n}))
such that the generators
\pi_{n}(s)
,
s\in S
, are independent and all have distribution
\nu_{n}
. By the universal property of
C^{\ast}\Gamma
for unitary representations of
\Gamma
, each
\pi_{n}
extends uniquely to a random unitary representation of
C^{\ast}\Gamma
.

Within this construction, two further special cases stand out:

•

For each
n
, let
d_{n}=n
, and let
\nu_{n}
be the Haar probability measure of
\mathrm{U}(d_{n})
. We call the resulting example a
uniformly random AP sequence
for
\Gamma
(or for
C^{\ast}\Gamma
).

•

Alternatively, let
d_{n}=n
, and let
\nu_{n}
be the uniform distribution on the finite set of
n
-by-
n
permutation matrices. Then
\pi_{n}
is the linear representation defined by a uniformly random permutation representation of
\Gamma
. We call the resulting example a
uniformly random permutation AP sequence
for
\Gamma
(or for
C^{\ast}\Gamma
).

Both uniformly random AP sequences and permutation AP sequences have a large literature, although the terminology of ‘AP sequences’ is not standard. Many important discoveries have versions for both. We next recall a few of these that we refer to again later.

The first two are for uniformly random AP sequences. They are versions of Voiculescu’s fundamental discovery relating random matrices to finite von Neumann algebras. In the terminology of free probability it is called ‘asymptotic freeness’. See
[
176
, Theorems 3.8 and 3.9]
(and note that the first of these covers rather more than we formulate below).

Theorem 5.1
.

If
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is a uniformly random AP sequence, then the expectation
\mathbf{E}(\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n})
converges to
\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}
in
\Sigma_{1}(\Gamma)
as
n\to\infty
. ∎

Combined with a standard application measure concentration, Theorem
5.1
actually implies the following. I include the proof of Theorem
5.2
because we need the particular shape of the resulting estimate later.

Theorem 5.2
.

If
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is a uniformly random AP sequence, then for every neighbourhood
V
of
\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}
in
\Sigma_{1}(\Gamma)
there is a positive constant
c
such that

\mathbf{P}(\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\not\in V)=O(e^{-cn^{2}}).

Proof.

The topology of
\Sigma_{1}(\Gamma)
agrees with the topology of pointwise convergence. Recalling the usual sub-base for this topology, it suffices to prove that, for each
g\in\Gamma
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
c>0
such that

\mathbf{P}(|\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\pi_{n}(g)-\chi_{\mathrm{reg}}(g)|\geq\varepsilon)=O(e^{-cn^{2}}).

By Theorem
5.1
, this follows if we find
c>0
such that

\mathbf{P}(|\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\pi_{n}(g)-\mathbf{E}(\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\pi_{n}(g))|\geq\varepsilon/2)=O(e^{-cn^{2}}).

Finally, this follows from Theorem
C.4
because, for each
g\in\Gamma
, the map

(\pi_{n}(s):\ s\in S)\mapsto\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\pi_{n}(g)

is
O(|g|/n)
-Lipschitz for the Hilbert–Schmidt metric on
\mathrm{U}(n)^{r}
.
∎

More recent results about random AP sequences have concerned strong convergence. The next is contained within the Collins–Male theorem from
[
51
, Theorem 1.4]
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Uniformly random AP sequences for a finitely-generated free group sq-converge to the regular representation. ∎

The paper
[
51
]
is about strong convergence, but since the limit is a regular representation this implies sq-convergence by Lemma
3.18
. In
[
51
]
, strong convergence in turn is deduced from a famous result of Haagerup and Thorbjørnsen about independent GUE random matrices
[
96
]
.

The analog of Theorem
5.3
also holds for random permutations. It is due to Bordenave and Collins: see
[
22
, Theorem 3]
.

Theorem 5.4

(Bordenave–Collins theorem)
.

Uniformly random permutation AP sequences for a finitely-generated free group converge strongly to the regular representation in probability. ∎

Once again
[
22
]
is about strong convergence, but this implies sq-convergence by Lemma
3.18
. Theorem
5.4
generalizes a famous older theorem of Friedman
[
78
]
about spectral gaps of random regular graphs.

The survey
[
126
]
discusses a number of other examples of strong convergence. In all of those examples, the limits are left regular representations of countable groups, so whenever those groups are finitely generated we can actually conclude strong-quotient convergence because of Lemma
3.18
.

Since the original proofs of Theorems
5.3
and
5.4
, other approaches have also appeared. The recent works
[
48
,
49
]
are particular noteworthy for offering a unified approach to several such results that uses very limited information about the underlying probability model. Our Theorem
C
offers yet another proof of Theorem
5.3
that seems to be quite different again.

Part
III
below is mostly dedicated to uniformly random AP sequences. On the other hand, it turns out that uniformly random permutation AP sequences are more degenerate from our point of view. This is because the most important fluctuations in behaviour that arise from permutations are only polynomially unlikely in
n
, not exponentially unlikely. We still refer to these permutation examples to illustrate a few points below, but the main results of our theory seem to be trivial for these. See Example
5.20
below.

Given any other sequence of compact subgroups
G_{n}
of
\mathrm{U}(n)
, we could obtain another example by taking
\nu_{n}
to be the Haar measure of
G_{n}
. This offers a wide range of very concrete further examples. We do not explore them in detail in this book, but discuss a few as possible directions for further research in Chapter
14
. That chapter also discusses some examples of possible interest for other groups or other C*-algebras.

Notes and further remarks

A single random unitary matrix with distribution
m_{\mathrm{U}(n)}
is one of the most well-studied models in random matrix theory, where this distribution is traditionally called the ‘circular unitary ensemble’: see, for instance,
[
130
, Section 10.3]
. Its analysis is driven by Weyl’s classical integration formula for central functions on unitary groups
[
4
, Proposition 4.1.6]
.

However, the interaction of several independent random unitary matrices is not amenable to such exact calculations. The alternative point of view provided by Voiculescu’s free probability theory has been key to uncovering many phenomena concerning such random tuples. Many aspects of this work can be found in textbooks such as
[
4
]
or
[
100
]
.

5.3

Annealed almost periodic entropy

We now fix a random AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}=(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
for the rest of this chapter. When needed, the dimension of
\pi_{n}
is denoted by
d_{n}
.

Recall the ball-volume function
v
from (
4.1
).

Definition 5.5

(Annealed AP entropy)
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
. Its
annealed almost periodic
(‘
AP
’)
entropy along
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is the quantity

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi):=\inf_{O}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{d_{n}}\log\mathbf{E}\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}},

(5.1)

where the infimum runs over all neighbourhoods of
\varphi
.

In case each
\pi_{n}
is deterministic, this simplifies back to Definition
4.3
. In Definition
5.5
, as previously, we may restrict
O
to lie in any chosen neighbourhood base at
\varphi
without changing the value of
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
, by monotonicity.

Since
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
is an infimum over neighbourhoods of
\varphi
, Lemma
D.3
gives the following extension of Lemma
4.4
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Each restriction
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}|\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
is upper semicontinuous. ∎

The term ‘annealed’ is borrowed from the statistical physics of disordered materials: see, for instance,
[
131
]
. Here it means that we apply the expectation directly to a random cardinality or a random volume in phase space. Other options are available: for example, we briefly discuss the associated ‘quenched’ average in Section
7.4
below. The annealed average is often easiest to study because Fubini’s theorem allows us to exchange
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}
in the formula (
5.1
): this fact becomes crucial during our work in Part
III
. On the other hand, when working with random AP sequences, one can sometimes move between annealed and other kinds of averages quite directly. This is explained following the three-entropy formula of Theorem
7.2
.

In ergodic theory, the analogous generalization from deterministic to annealed sofic entropy appears in the second displayed equation after
[
27
, Definition 4]
.

We can also extend Proposition
4.5
with only small changes to the proofs. Since these are short, we include them for completeness.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
be positive definite.

a.

We have
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)\leq\log\det\varphi(1)
. In particular, if
\varphi(1)
is singular, then
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=-\infty
.

b.

If
Q
is an invertible
k
-by-
k
matrix and

\psi(a):=(Q^{\mathrm{T}})^{\ast}\varphi(a)Q^{\mathrm{T}}\qquad(a\in\mathfrak{A}),

then

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\psi)=2\log|\det Q|+\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi).

Proof.

Part (a).
Let
h>\log\det\varphi(1)
. Then Theorem
C.2
(b) (the ‘method of types’ for log-determinants) gives a neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi(1)
in
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
such that the sets

T(d,O):=\{X\in\mathrm{M}_{d,k}:\ X^{\ast}X\in O\}

satisfy

\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2dk}T(d,O)}{v(d)^{k}}\leq e^{hd+o(d)}\qquad\hbox{as}\ d\to\infty.

(5.2)

Now let

U:=\{\psi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}:\ \psi(1)\in O\}.

Then

\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},U)\subset T(d_{n},O)

for every
n
and every value of
\pi_{n}
. Therefore, by (
5.2
), the neighbourhood
U
witnesses the upper bound
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)\leq h
. By the arbitrariness in
h
, this completes the proof.

Part (b).
Let
O
be any neighbourhood of
\psi
. Then Lemma
2.27
gives a neighbourhood
U
of
\varphi
such that

(I_{d_{n}}\otimes Q)[\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},U)]\subset\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)

for every
n
and every value of
\pi_{n}
,
and hence

\displaystyle\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)

\displaystyle\geq|\det(I_{d_{n}}\otimes Q)|^{2}\cdot\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},U)

\displaystyle=|\det Q|^{2d_{n}}\cdot\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},U)

for every
n
and every value of
\pi_{n}
. The determinant here is squared because we must regard
I_{d_{n}}\otimes Q
as a linear map in
2kd_{n}
real dimensions for the purpose of computing volumes (see, for instance,
[
154
, Subsection 1.3.5]
). Inserting this into Definition
5.5
, we obtain

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{d_{n}}\log\mathbf{E}\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}}\geq 2\log|\det Q|+\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi).

By the arbitrariness of
O
, this gives the desired inequality in one direction. It holds in the other direction by applying the same argument with
\varphi
and
\psi
switched and with
Q^{-1}
in place of
Q
.
∎

If
\varphi
is a state on
\mathfrak{A}
, then Lemma
4.6
asserts that the normalized volumes in the definition of AP entropy may be replaced with normalized surface-area measures on spheres. If
\varphi
is a normalized completely positive map for some
k>1
, then this lemma may be applied to
\langle\varphi,\cdot\rangle
. Our next result generalizes that lemma in two ways. First, it is a version for annealed AP entropy along a random AP sequence, which once again recovers the previous version if the sequence is deterministic. Secondly, it allows
k>1
by introducing the Haar measures on the sets
\mathrm{U}(k,d_{n})
of orthonormal tuples (see Appendix
A.1
for this notation). These are precisely the tuples whose types are unital.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
be unital, and let
\mathcal{O}
be any base of neighbourhoods around
\varphi
in
\Sigma_{k}^{\mathrm{u}}(\mathfrak{A})
. Then

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=\inf_{O\in\mathcal{O}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{d_{n}}\log\mathbf{E}m_{\mathrm{U}(k,d_{n})}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O).

(5.3)

The proof is similar to Lemma
4.6
. The main difference is that, in order to handle
\mathrm{U}(k,d_{n})
with
k>1
, we need the more complicated integration formula from Proposition
C.1
in place of integration in polar coordinates.

Proof.

Consider the collection
\mathcal{W}
of all sets that have the form

W=\{\psi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}:\ \psi(1)\in U,\ \psi(1)^{-1/2}\cdot\psi\cdot\psi(1)^{-1/2}\in O\}

for some open neighbourhood
U
of
I_{k}
in
\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}
and some
O\in\mathcal{O}
. Since
\mathcal{O}
is a base of neighbourhoods around
\varphi
in
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
, this
\mathcal{W}
is a base of neighbourhoods around
\varphi
in
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
. Therefore, as remarked following Definition
5.5
, we may express
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
as an infimum over
\mathcal{W}
.

However, if
W
is the set above, then its special form turns into the equation

1_{\mathcal{X}(\pi,W)}(VQ^{1/2})=1_{\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)}(V)\cdot 1_{U}(Q)\qquad(V\in\mathrm{U}(k,d),\ Q\in\mathrm{M}_{k+})

for any
d
-dimensional representation
\pi
. As a result, Proposition
C.1
gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{vol}_{2kd}\mathcal{X}(\pi,W)

\displaystyle=v(k,d)\int_{\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}}(\det Q)^{d-k}\int_{\mathrm{U}(k,d)}1_{\mathcal{X}(\pi,W)}(VQ^{1/2})\ dm_{\mathrm{U}(k,d)}(V)\ d\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}(Q)

\displaystyle=v(k,d)\cdot m_{\mathrm{U}(k,d)}\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)\cdot\int_{U}(\det Q)^{d-k}\ d\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}(Q).

Finally, for any
\varepsilon>0
, we may choose
U
so small that

e^{-\varepsilon}<\det Q<e^{\varepsilon}\qquad\hbox{for every}\ Q\in U.

Substituting these inequalities into the integral formula above, they lead to

e^{-d\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}U\cdot m_{\mathrm{U}(k,d)}\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)\leq\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd}\mathcal{X}(\pi,W)}{v(k,d)}\\
\leq e^{d\varepsilon}\cdot\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}U\cdot m_{\mathrm{U}(k,d)}\mathcal{X}(\pi,O).

By the asymptotic (
C.7
), and because
O
and
W
range over neighbourhood bases of
\varphi
in
\Sigma_{k}^{\mathrm{u}}(\Gamma)
and
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
respectively, substituting these inequalities into Definition
5.5
and letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
yields (
5.3
).
∎

If
\varphi
is not unital but
\varphi(1)
is invertible, then we can apply Lemma
5.8
to
\varphi(1)^{-1/2}\cdot\varphi\cdot\varphi(1)^{-1/2}
and combine the result with Proposition
4.5
(b).

5.4

Zeroth-order AP entropy

Consider
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
