and a random AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}
. Heuristically, our next notion of entropy estimates the probability of asymptotic association as
n\to\infty
.

Definition 5.9
.

The
zeroth-order almost periodic
(‘
AP
’)
entropy of
\varphi
along
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi):=\inf_{O}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{d_{n}}\log\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset),

(5.4)

where the infimum runs over all neighbourhoods of
\varphi
.

The term ‘zeroth-order’ invokes the Rényi entropy of order zero of a random variable. This is simply the logarithm of the probability that the random variable is not zero: see, for instance,
[
56
, eqn. (17.98)]
. As with Rényi entropy, we could also define ‘order-
p
entropy’ for any other
p>0
, recovering our original annealed entropy
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
when
p=1
. But this does not seem to provide much additional insight: see the discussion in Section
7.4
below.

Since
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
is an infimum over neighbourhoods of
\varphi
, Lemma
D.3
gives the following partner of Lemma
5.6
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Each restriction
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}|\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
is upper semicontinuous. ∎

It turns out that
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
is much less sensitive as a function of
\varphi
than
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
. We prove shortly that
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
depends only on the minimal dilation
\pi_{\varphi}
and is monotone under approximate containment of representations. These properties motivate an extension of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
to general representations in Definition
5.13
below.

Lemma 5.11
.

If
\psi
is approximately associated to
\pi_{\varphi}
, then
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\psi)\geq\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.26
, if
U
is any neighbourhood of
\psi
then there is a neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi
such that

\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)\neq\emptyset\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathcal{X}(\pi,U)\neq\emptyset

for any representation
\pi
. With this choice of
O
, it follows that

\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)\leq\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},U)\neq\emptyset)\qquad(n\geq 1).

Taking logarithms, normalizing, letting
n\to\infty
, and then taking the infimum over
U
, this turns into the desired inequality.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

If
\pi_{\varphi}\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\pi_{\psi}
, then
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\psi)
. ∎

Lemma
5.11
inspires our more general definition of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
.

Definition 5.13
.

For any separable representation
\pi
, its
zeroth-order AP entropy along
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=\inf\big\{\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\Phi^{\pi}_{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}):\ k\geq 1,\ x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in H_{\pi}\big\}.

Further properties of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
flow from Definition
5.13
rather similarly to the traditional development of Kolmogorov–Sinai entropy in ergodic theory, which can be defined as a supremum over partitions. See, for example,
[
181
, Sections 4.4–6]
. Let us stress, however, that
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
is defined as an infimum rather than a supremum, even though Definition
5.13
and Kolmogorov–Sinai entropy follow the same sign convention for an ‘entropy’.

Sometimes other formulas for zeroth-order entropy are convenient.

Lemma 5.14
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\inf\Big\{\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi):\ \varphi\in\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})}\Big\}

(5.5)

\displaystyle=\inf_{U}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{d_{n}}\log\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U),

(5.6)

where the infimum on the second line runs over all q-neighbourhoods of
\pi
.

Proof.

Definition
5.13
may be written in the equivalent form

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=\inf\Big\{\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi):\ \varphi\in\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})\Big\}.

The formula (
5.5
) follows from this together with the upper semicontinuity from Lemma
5.10
.

To prove formula (
5.6
), first observe that we may restrict attention to sets
U
that lie in any choice of neighbourhood base at
\varphi
, because the infimum is monotone in
U
. Having done so, Lemma
3.13
tells us that it suffices to consider q-neighbourhoods that have the form

U=\{\rho:\ \overline{\Sigma_{k}(\rho)}\ \hbox{meets}\ O\}

for some positive integer
k
and some open subset
O
of
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
that meets
\Sigma_{k}(\pi)
. This choice of
U
satisfies

\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)=\mathbf{P}(\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})\ \hbox{meets}\ O)=\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset).

Therefore the infimum over all neighbhourhoods
U
of this form turns into the infimum of the expression in Definition
5.9
over
\varphi\in\bigcup_{k}\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
, as required.
∎

The next two results allow us to restrict which vectors in a representation we consider when computing
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
. The first is analogous to the Kolmorogov–Sinai generator theorem
[
181
, Theorem 4.17]
.

Lemma 5.15
.

If the tuple
x_{1}
, …,
x_{k}
is cyclic for
\pi
, then

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\Phi^{\pi}_{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}).

Equivalently, if
\varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
, then
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi_{\varphi})=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
.

Proof.

The inequality “
\leq
” follows at once from the definition of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
. On the other hand, if
y_{1}
, …,
y_{\ell}
is any other tuple in
H_{\pi}
, then
\Phi^{\pi}_{y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}}
is associated to the GNS representation of
\Phi^{\pi}_{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}
, since the latter equals the whole of
\pi
by cyclicity. Therefore Lemma
5.11
shows that
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\Phi^{\pi}_{y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}})
is bounded below by
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\Phi^{\pi}_{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}})
.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

With Lemma
5.15
in hand, we use the notations
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
and
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi_{\varphi})
interchangeably in the sequel.
\lhd

The next lemma generalizes Lemma
5.15
for representation that do not have finite cyclic tuples. It is an analog of
[
181
, Theorems 4.21 and 4.22]
.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\pi
be a representation of
\mathfrak{A}
, and let
S
be a subset of
H_{\pi}
such that the linear span of
\{\pi(a)x:\ a\in\mathfrak{A},x\in S\}
is dense in
H_{\pi}
. Then

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=\inf\big\{\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\Phi^{\pi}_{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}):\ k\geq 1,\ x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in S\big\}.

Proof.

The inequality “
\leq
” follows at once from the definition of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
.

For the reverse direction, suppose that
Y=[y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}]
is a tuple in
H_{\pi}
.

For any tuple
X=[x_{1},\dots,x_{k}]
in
H_{\pi}
, let
M_{X}
be the closed
\pi
-invariant subspace of
H_{\pi}
generated by
X
. By our assumption on
S
, there are tuples
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
, …drawn from
S
and further
\ell
-tuples
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
, …such that
Y_{n}
lies in
M_{X_{n}}
and
Y_{n}\to Y
, and hence also
\Phi^{\pi}_{Y_{n}}\to\Phi^{\pi}_{Y}
by Lemma
2.23
. By Lemma
5.10
and Lemma
5.15
, it follows that

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\Phi^{\pi}_{Y})\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\Phi^{\pi}_{Y_{n}})\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi^{M_{X_{n}}})=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\Phi^{\pi}_{X_{n}}).

Since each
X_{n}
is a tuple drawn from
S
, this proves the inequality “
\geq
”.
∎

Using Lemma
5.14
, we can improve Lemma
5.11
to the following monotonicity for an arbitrary pair of representations
\pi
and
\rho
.

Corollary 5.18
.

If
\rho
is approximately contained in
\pi
, then
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\rho)\geq\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)
. In particular, the function
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
on representations is an invariant of approximate equivalence (and hence certainly of unitary equivalence).

Proof.

If
\varphi
is approximately associated to
\rho
, then it is also approximately associated to
\pi
, so this follows from formula (
5.5
).
∎

Remark 5.19
.

In general
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
is
not
an invariant of weak equivalence, because it can sometimes detect multiplicities of subrepresentations. This becomes clear from Proposition
8.4
below.
\lhd

Part
III
below is dedicated to annealed and zeroth-order AP entropy and related quantities for uniformly random AP sequences for free groups. By contrast, zeroth-order AP entropy turns out to be somewhat degenerate for uniformly random
permutation
representations.

Example 5.20
.

Let
\Gamma
be freely generated by
S=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{r}\}
, and for each
n
let
\pi_{n}
be a uniformly random permutation representation of
\Gamma
on
\mathbf{C}^{n}
.

For this group, Lubotzky and Shalom proved in
[
121
]
that any nonempty quotient-open set of separable representations
U
contains some finite permutation representation
\rho
, say on
\{1,\dots,m\}
.

For each
n\geq m
, let
V_{n}
be the subspace of
\mathbf{C}^{n}
spanned by the first
m
coordinates, so this has a natural identification with
\mathbf{C}^{m}
. Now consider the events

E_{n}:=\{\pi_{n}:\ \pi_{n}(s_{i})|V_{n}=\rho(s_{i})\ \hbox{for}\ i=1,2,\dots,m\}\qquad(n\geq m).

Then a standard calculation gives

\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)\geq\mathbf{P}(E_{n})=\frac{((n-m)!)^{r}}{(n!)^{r}}\geq\frac{1}{n^{mr}}.

Observe that this decays more slowly than any decaying exponential function of
n
as
n\to\infty
. Since
r
is fixed throughout, and
m
depends only on
U
, we may finish with an infimum over
U
to conclude that
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=0
for every separable representation
\pi
. ∎

On the other hand, I do not know an exact description of
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
when
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is a uniformly random permutation AP sequence for a free group.below.

Chapter 6

Convergence in the large deviations sense

6.1

Definition and relation to the quotient topology

We begin with a definition for fairly general functions of open subsets of a Polish space, and then return our attention to random AP sequences.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a Polish space and let
\mathcal{O}
be a base for its topology. Consider a sequence of functions
f_{n}:\mathcal{O}\to[-\infty,\infty)
(
n=1,2,\dots
), and assume that every
f_{n}
is monotone under set inclusion. Finally, consider a function
f:\mathcal{X}\to[-\infty,\infty)
.

Definition 6.1
.

The sequence
(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges to
f
in the
large deviations
(‘
LD
’)
sense
if the following hold:

i.

If
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
a>f(x)
, then
x
has a neighbourhood
U\in\mathcal{O}
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(U)<a.

ii.

If
x\in\mathcal{X}
, then any neighbourhood
U
of
x
in
\mathcal{O}
satisfies

\liminf_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(U)\geq f(x).

More compactly, this says that
(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
LD converges if

\inf_{U\in\mathcal{O},\ U\ni x}\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(U)=\inf_{U\in\mathcal{O},\ U\ni x}\liminf_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(U)

(6.1)

for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and then this common expression is the limit
f(x)
. In order for this to hold,
f
must be upper semicontinuous, by Lemma
D.3
.

By the monotonicity of each
f_{n}
in the definition above, if
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is a subfamily of
\mathcal{O}
that is still a base, then the sequence
(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges to
f
in the LD sense if and only if the sequence
(f_{n}|\mathcal{O}^{\prime})_{n\geq 1}
does.

The term ‘LD’ reflects our basic example: if
\mathcal{X}
is compact,
\mathcal{O}
is any base for its topology, and
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence of probability measures obeying the LDP on
\mathcal{X}
with speed
n
and rate function
I
(see Definition
D.1
), then

\frac{1}{n}\log(\mu_{n}|\mathcal{O})\to-I\qquad\hbox{in the LD sense}.

Convergence in the LD sense is equivalent to a kind of stability upon passing to subsequences:

Lemma 6.2
.

Consider a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
as above, and define

f(x):=\inf_{U\in\mathcal{O},\ U\ni x}\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(U)\qquad(x\in\mathcal{X}).

The sequence converges in the LD sense if and only if we also have

\inf_{U\in\mathcal{O},\ U\ni x}\limsup_{i\to\infty}f_{n_{i}}(U)=f(x)

for any divergent integer sequence
1\leq n_{1}\leq n_{2}\leq\cdots
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, any neighbourhood
U
of
x
in
\mathcal{O}
, and any divergent integer sequence
1\leq n_{1}\leq n_{2}\leq\dots
, we have

\liminf_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(U)\leq\limsup_{i\to\infty}f_{n_{i}}(U)\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(U).

So if (
6.1
) holds then it controls the subsequence.

(\Leftarrow)
.
We prove the contrapositive, so suppose that
f_{n}
does not converge to
f
in the LD sense. By (
6.1
), there must be a point
x\in\mathcal{X}
and a neighbourhood
V
of
x
such that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(V)<\inf_{U\in\mathcal{O},\ U\ni x}\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(U).

Choose a divergent integer sequence
1\leq n_{1}\leq n_{2}\leq\dots
such that

\lim_{i\to\infty}f_{n_{i}}(V)=\liminf_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(V).

By the monotonicity of the set functions
f_{n}
, it follows that

\inf_{U\in\mathcal{O},\ U\ni x}\limsup_{i\to\infty}f_{n_{i}}(U)\leq\lim_{i\to\infty}f_{n_{i}}(V)<f(x).

∎

Now consider again an AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}
for a separable unital C*-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
. Let
\mathcal{T}_{k}
be the topology of
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
for each
k
.

Definition 6.3
.

The sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}

converges in the large deviations
(‘
LD
’)
sense
if the functions

\frac{1}{d_{n}}\log\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)\qquad(O\in\mathcal{T}_{k},\ n=1,2,\dots)

(6.2)

converge to
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
in the LD sense.

This terminology is adapted fairly directly from an analogous usage for ‘large deviations convergence’ of sequences of graphs with uniformly bounded degrees: see
[
26
]
.

Example 6.4
.

If each
\pi_{n}
is deterministic, then each probability value appearing in (
6.2
) is either
0
or
1
. From this observation and Lemma
3.14
it follows that a deterministic AP sequence converges in the LD sense if and only if it strong-quotient converges. ∎

Convergence of
\boldsymbol{\pi}
in the LD sense has the following consequence.

Theorem 6.5
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\pi}
converges in the LD sense, and fix a separable representation
\pi
of
\mathfrak{A}
.

a.

If
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\pi)>h>-\infty
, then for any q-neighbourhood
U
of
\pi
we have

\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)\geq e^{hd_{n}-o(d_{n})}.

b.

If
h>\mathrm{h}^{0}(\pi)
, then
\pi
has a q-neighbourhood
U
such that

\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)\leq e^{hd_{n}+o(d_{n})}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.13
, we may restrict attention to the base of q-neighbourhoods that have the form

U=\{\rho:\ \mathcal{X}(\rho,O)\neq\emptyset\}

for some positive integer
k
and some open subset
O
of
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
such that
\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)
is nonempty. However, for this neighbourhood
U
we have

\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)=\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset),

so in this case the estimates in parts (a) and (b) boil down to the definition of convergence in the LD sense. Taking an infimum over associated positive definite functions completes the proof. ∎

Theorem
6.5
looks like a large deviations principle for the sequence
\pi_{n}
in the quotient topology. However, that topology is far from metrizable, so this theorem does not fall within the usual scope of large deviations theory. Comparing with results for real-valued random variables, Theorem
6.5
is more like an estimate on the probabilities of ‘upper tails’ than an estimate on the probabilities of landing close to specific values. In the next subsection we turn it into precisely such an esimate for operator norms.

Under additional assumptions, Theorem
8.10
below improves Theorem
6.5
to a large deviations principle in the strong-quotient topology, which is much better behaved.

Notes and further references

In the context of general large deviations theory, a close relative of convergence in the LD sense have been studied in
[
103
]
; see also
[
61
, Section 4.7]
.

6.2

Upper tails for operator norms

Proposition 6.6
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\pi}
converges in the LD sense, and let
a\in\mathfrak{A}
be non-negative. For each
c>0
, let

S_{c}:=\{\varphi\in\Sigma_{1}(\mathfrak{A}):\ \varphi(a)\geq c\},

and define

I(c):=\sup\{\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi):\ \varphi\in S_{c}\}.

Then

I(c)\cdot d_{n}+o(d_{n})\geq\log\mathbf{P}(\|\pi_{n}(a)\|\geq c)\geq\sup\{I(c^{\prime}):\ c^{\prime}>c\}\cdot d_{n}-o(d_{n})

as
n\to\infty
(with the obvious adjustments if
I(c)=-\infty
).

We prove this proposition from Theorem
6.5
. The proof is essentially a version of the contraction principle from (
D.2
), but we present it from scratch because the quotient topology is not Polish.

Proof.

Upper bound.
Let
h>I(c)
. By the definition of convergence in the LD sense, every
\varphi\in S_{c}
has a q-neighbourhood
O_{\varphi}
which satisfies

\log\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O_{\varphi})\neq\emptyset)\leq hd_{n}+o(d_{n}).

Since
S_{c}
is quasi-compact in the quotient topology, we can cover it with finitely many such neighbourhoods, say
O_{\varphi_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup O_{\varphi_{m}}
. Therefore, since the operator norm
\pi_{n}(a)
is achieved by some vectors in the finite-dimensional sphere
\mathrm{S}^{2n-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\log\mathbf{P}(\|\pi_{n}(a)\|\geq c)

\displaystyle\leq\log\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O_{\varphi_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup O_{\varphi_{m}})\neq\emptyset)

\displaystyle\leq hd_{n}+o(d_{n}).

By the arbitrariness of
h
, this proves the upper bound.

Lower bound.
Let
c^{\prime}>c
, and assume that
I(c^{\prime})>-\infty
, for otherwise the result is vacuous. Let
\varepsilon>0
, and pick
\varphi\in S_{c^{\prime}}
such that
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\varphi)>I(c^{\prime})-\varepsilon
. Then the set

O:=\{\psi\in\Sigma_{1}(\Gamma):\ \psi(a)>c\}

is a neighbourhood of
\varphi
, and so convergence in the LD sense gives

\log\mathbf{P}(\|\pi_{n}(a)\|\geq c)\geq\log\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)\\
\geq\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)\cdot d_{n}-o(d_{n})\geq(I(c^{\prime})-\varepsilon)\cdot d_{n}-o(d_{n}).

By the arbitrariness of
c^{\prime}
and
\varepsilon
, this completes the proof.
∎

The conclusion of Proposition
6.6
simplifies to an exact asymptotic if
I
is continuous at
c
.

Proposition
6.6
can be adapted to a general element
b
of
C^{\ast}\Gamma
by applying it to
a:=b^{\ast}b
.

Remark 6.7
.

Proposition
6.6
says nothing about the lower tails of
\|\pi_{n}(a)\|
. Indeed, one would expect these to behave quite differently in general. This is because the event that
\|\pi_{n}(a)\|\leq c
requires that
\pi_{n}(a)
be uniformly bounded over the whole sphere
\mathrm{S}^{2n-1}
, whereas the event that
\|\pi_{n}(a)\|\geq c
requires large behaviour at only one point.

For example, if
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is a uniformly random AP sequence for a free group, then those lower tails would be controlled by the conjectured variational principle for the ‘topological’ version of Voiculescu’s free entropy: see
[
178
]
. Here we run into the question of whether the ‘lim-sup’ definition of free entropy can be improved to the kind of limit that occurs in a large deviations principle, now with ‘speed’
n^{2}
rather than
n
. For two or more independent random matrices (self-adjoint or unitary) this remains a major open problem. I doubt whether a full understanding of the lower tails of those operator norms can be achieved without its solution. These matters lie far outside the present work.
\lhd

Chapter 7

The three-entropy formula

7.1

Convergence of traces in probability

Several results about AP entropy require the convergence of the traces on
\mathfrak{A}
pulled back from an AP sequence. Some of these arguments have valuable analogs for annealed AP entropy, provided the traces pulled back from a random AP sequence converge in probability
fast enough
.

Specifically, we write that
{\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau}

superexponentially in probability
if, for every neighbourhood
V
of
\tau
and every
c>0
, we have

\mathbf{P}(\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\not\in V)=o(e^{-cd_{n}})\qquad\hbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

This implies that the expectations
\mathbf{E}(\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n})
also converge to
\tau
.

By Voiculescu’s Theorem
5.2
, uniformly random AP sequences for free groups have this property. By contrast, uniformly random permutation AP sequences for free groups do not.

Now let
\lambda
be the GNS representation of
\tau
. Consider a general representation
\pi
, and let
\pi^{M}
be the subrepresentation given by applying Proposition
2.1
to
\pi
and
\lambda
, so
\pi^{M}\lesssim\lambda^{\oplus\infty}
and
\pi^{M^{\perp}}\spoon\lambda
. The next lemma is a probabilistic generalization of Proposition
4.7
(b).

Lemma 7.1
.

If
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially in probability, then

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi^{M^{\perp}}).

In particular, for any completely positive map
\varphi
we have
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi_{\mathrm{sing}})
.

Proof.

Since
\pi^{M^{\perp}}
is contained in
\pi
, the inequality “
\leq
” follows from Corollary
5.18
.

To prove the reverse inequality, we may assume that
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi^{M^{\perp}})>-\infty
. Let
\varphi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
be any completely positive map associated to
\pi
, and let
O
be a neighbourhood of
\varphi
in
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
.

By projecting the associating vectors of
\varphi
to
M
and
M^{\perp}
, we obtain the Lebesgue decomposition
\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}+\varphi_{\mathrm{sing}}
of
\varphi
with respect to
\tau
(see Corollary
2.17
). Now Corollary
4.8
(b) gives a positive integer
d_{0}
and neighbourhoods
V
of
\tau
and
U
of
\varphi_{\mathrm{sing}}
. Once
d_{n}\geq d_{0}
, the conclusion of that corollary yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{P}(\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\in V\ \hbox{and}\ \mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},U)\neq\emptyset)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},U)\neq\emptyset)-\mathbf{P}(\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\not\in V).

The first term here is at least
\exp(\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi^{M^{\perp}})\cdot d_{n}-o(d_{n}))
by definition, and the second term decays to zero superexponentially in
d_{n}
by a assumption. Therefore

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{d_{n}}\log\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)\geq\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi^{M^{\perp}})

for any such map
\varphi
and neighbourhood
O
. Taking the infimum over these completes the proof.
∎

Under some further assumptions, Proposition
8.8
below makes Lemma
7.1
much more precise.

7.2

The three-entropy formula

At this point, for a completely positive map
\varphi
, we have met three different notions of entropy:

•

AP entropy along a fixed AP sequence, which always equals either
-\infty
or the Fuglede–Kadison log-determinant from Theorem
4.11
.

•

Annealed AP entropy.

•

Zeroth-order AP entropy, which depends only on the GNS representation
\pi_{\varphi}
.

Consider again a random AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}=(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
. Let
\tau
be a tracial state on
\mathfrak{A}
, and let
\Delta
be the Fuglede–Kadison determinant defined from
\tau
. The three notions of entropy above are related by the following basic formula.

Theorem 7.2

(Three-entropy formula)
.

Assume that
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially in probability, and let
\varphi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
be completely positive. Then

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)+\log\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}.

(7.1)

Proof.

We prove (
7.1
) as a pair of inequalities.

Step 1: (
\leq
).
Let
h_{0}>\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi_{\varphi})
and
h_{1}>\log\Delta_{\tau}\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}
, and in addition let
{R>\max_{i}\varphi_{ii}(1)}
.

On the one hand, the definition of zeroth-order entropy gives a neighbourhood
O_{0}
of
\varphi
such that
\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O_{0})\neq\emptyset)\leq e^{h_{0}d_{n}}
for all sufficiently large
n
.

On the other hand, for this choice of
h_{1}
, part (a) of Theorem
4.11
(a) gives a positive integer
d_{0}
, a neighbourhood
V
of
\tau
, and a neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi
. By shrinking
O
further if necessary, we may also assume that
O\subset O_{0}
and that every
\psi\in O
satisfies
\max_{i}\psi_{ii}(1)<R
. The second of these properties implies that
\mathcal{X}(\pi,O)
is contained in
B_{R}(0)^{k}
for every
d
-dimensional representation
\pi
, where
B_{R}(0)
is the radius-
R
ball in
\mathbf{C}^{d}
.

Since
d_{n}\geq d_{0}
for all sufficiently large
n
, we can now estimate the relevant expected volume like this:

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\Big[\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}}\,;\,\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}\not\in V\Big]+\mathbf{E}\Big[\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}}\,;\,\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}\in V\Big]

\displaystyle\leq R^{2kd_{n}}\cdot\mathbf{P}(\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}\not\in V)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathbf{E}\Big[\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}}\,;\,\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}\in V,\ \mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O_{0})\neq\emptyset\Big]

We can insert the event
\{\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O_{0})\neq\emptyset\}
into the last expectation here, because if
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O_{0})=\emptyset
then also
\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)=\emptyset
and so
\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)=0
.

We now bound the two displayed terms above separately. Since
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially in probability, and
R
is a fixed real number, the first term decays to
0
superexponentially fast. On the other hand, on the event
\{\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}\in V\}
, our appeal to Theorem
4.11
gives

\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}}\leq e^{h_{1}d_{n}}

for all sufficiently large
n
, and so the second term is at most

e^{h_{1}d_{n}}\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O_{0})\neq\emptyset)\leq e^{(h_{0}+h_{1})d_{n}}

for all sufficiently large
n
.

Inserting these upper bounds into the definition of
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
, we see that it is at most
h_{0}+h_{1}
. By the arbitrariness of
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
, the proves the desired inequality.

Step 2: (
\geq
).
Assume that the right-hand side of (
7.1
) is finite, and let
h_{0}<\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi_{\varphi})
and
h_{1}<\log\Delta_{\tau}\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}
.

Let
O
be any neighbourhood of
\varphi
. For this choice of
h_{1}
and
O
, Theorem
4.11
(b) gives a positive integer
d_{0}
, a neighbourhood
V
of
\tau
, and another neighbourhood
O^{\prime}
of
\varphi
. As a result, for all sufficiently large
n
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{E}\Big[\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}}\,;\,\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}\in V,\ \mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O^{\prime})\neq\emptyset\Big]

\displaystyle\geq e^{h_{1}d_{n}}\cdot\mathbf{P}(\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}\in V,\ \mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O^{\prime})\neq\emptyset)

\displaystyle\geq e^{h_{1}d_{n}}\cdot\big(\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O^{\prime})\neq\emptyset)-\mathbf{P}(\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}\not\in V)\big).

By the definition of zeroth-order entropy and the assumption that
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially in probability, the last line above is at least

e^{(h_{1}+h_{0})d_{n}}-o(e^{(h_{1}+h_{0})d_{n}}).

Inserting this lower bound into the definition of
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
, we arrive at
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)\geq h_{0}+h_{1}
. By the arbitrariness of
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
, the completes the proof.
∎

7.3

Mollifying and convergence in the LD sense

Continue to assume that
\boldsymbol{\pi}=(\pi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a random AP sequence with
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially in probability. Theorem
7.2
expresses
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
in terms of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
and a Fuglede–Kadison determinant. The relationship can sometimes be re-arranged, but we must be careful: it can happen that
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
and
\log\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}
both equal
-\infty
, and then we cannot necessarily recover the value of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
.

We can work around this difficulty by applying Theorem
7.2
in a different way. If
\varphi:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
is completely positive, then we can use it to form the perturbations

\varphi_{t}:=\tau\otimes I_{k}+t\varphi\qquad(t\geq 0),

which are still completely positive. These appear in a probabilistic generalization of Corollary
4.12
. We separate this into the lemma and proposition below.

Lemma 7.3
.

Under the assumptions above, we have

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi_{t})\geq\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)

for every
t>0
, and the left-hand side converges to
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
as
t\downarrow 0
.

In particular,
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=0
if and only if
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi_{t})\geq 0
for every
t>0
.

Proof.

Since
\tau\otimes I_{k}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\tau
and the Lebesgue decomposition from Corollary
2.17
is linear, we have,

(\varphi_{t})_{\mathrm{ac}}=(\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}})_{t}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad(\varphi_{t})_{\mathrm{sing}}=t\varphi_{\mathrm{sing}}.

Two appeals to Lemma
7.1
give

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi_{t})=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(t\varphi_{\mathrm{sing}})=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi_{\mathrm{sing}})=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)

whenever
t>0
. Inserting this into (
7.1
) gives

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi_{t})=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)+\log\Delta((\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}})_{t}).

(7.2)

Now the result follows from the inequalities

0=\log\Delta(\tau\otimes I_{k})\leq\log\Delta((\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}})_{t})\leq k\cdot\log(1+t\cdot\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{k}\varphi(1)).

These are special cases of the second and first inequalities in Proposition
2.21
(b), respectively.
∎

Again let
\mathcal{T}_{k}
be the topology of
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
for each
k
, and recall the definitions from Section
6.1
.

Proposition 7.4
.

If
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially in probability, then the sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}
converges in the LD sense if and only if the functions

\frac{1}{d_{n}}\log\mathbf{E}\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}}\qquad(O\in\mathcal{T}_{k}\setminus\{\emptyset\},\ n=1,2,\dots)

(7.3)

converge to
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
in the LD sense for every
k
.

Proof.

This is easiest via the subsequence stability characterization in Lemma
6.2
. Fix a divergent integer sequence
1\leq n_{1}\leq n_{2}\leq\cdots
, and notice that the subsequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}:=(\pi_{n_{i}})_{i\geq 1}
still has traces that converge superexponentially in probability. By Lemma
6.2
, it suffices to show that

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}=\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}}

as functions on
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
.

If
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}}=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
on
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
and
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
, then two applications of Theorem
7.2
give

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)+\log\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}}(\varphi)+\log\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}=\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}}(\varphi).

On the other hand, if
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}}=\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
on
\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
, then we can make two appeals to Corollary
7.3
:

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=\inf_{t>0}\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\tau\otimes I_{k}+t\varphi)=\inf_{t>0}\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}}(\tau\otimes I_{k}+t\varphi)=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{\prime}}(\varphi).

∎

7.4

Some further consequences

If
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is a deterministic AP sequence, then Corollary
4.13
(b) asserts that
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}|\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
is concave for each
k
up to a possible failure of asymptotic association. For a random AP sequence such that
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially in probability, a probabilistic generalization asserts that
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{\mathrm{ann}}
is concave wherever
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
is concave. This follows directly from Theorem
7.2
and the concavity of
\log\Delta
from Proposition
2.21
(c).

However,
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
itself can certainly fail to be concave in general. Indeed, this is clear from Lemma
7.3
, where each
\varphi_{t}
is a convex combination but we meet a discontinuity as
t\downarrow 0
in case
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\varphi)\neq 0
.

Next we consider the possibility of entropy of ‘other orders’. Let
\varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
and let
p
be a positive real number. If
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially in probability, then essentially the same proof as for Theorem
7.2
gives the generalization

\inf_{O}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{d_{n}}\log\mathbf{E}\big[(m_{\mathrm{U}(k,d_{n})}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O))^{p}\big]=\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\pi_{\varphi})+p\cdot\log\Delta_{\tau}\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}.

Theorem
7.2
is the case
p=1
. Letting
p\downarrow 0
, we recover simply
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\pi_{\varphi})
, so this further justifies the name ‘zeroth-order’. This shows why defining AP entropies of other ‘orders’ often has limited value: they are often simply obtained from
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}
and
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
by linear interpolation.

Finally, let us consider the possibility of ‘quenched’ rather than ‘annealed’ AP entropy. If
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=0
, then this means that

\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)\geq e^{-o(d_{n})}

for every neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi
. In some cases, a concentration of measure phenomenon lets us promote this conclusion to the assertion that any
\varphi
satisfying
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=0
actually has

\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)\to 1

(7.4)

for every neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi
. For instance, this extra assumption does hold for uniformly random AP sequence for free groups, by a standard combination of Theorems
5.3
and
C.4
(see Lemma
12.6
below). In this case another kind of average may be of interest: a ‘quenched’ average, which should capture the high-probability behaviour of the random volumes
\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)
. This may not be reflected accurately by the annealed average because the expectation in (
5.1
) may be dominated by larges volume on a low-probability event. Instead, a quenched average would be accomplished by setting

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{quen}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi):=\inf_{O}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{d_{n}}\mathbf{E}\log\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}{v(d_{n})^{k}}.

That is, we move the expectation outside the logarithm compared to (
5.1
). This suppresses the tail of the distribution of the random volume
\mathrm{vol}_{2kd_{n}}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)
, and so prevents the expectation from being dominated by rare events.

However, this quenched average can again be expressed easily in terms of other quantities already studied. Specifically, under our present assumptions, another variation on the proof of Theorem
7.2
gives

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{quen}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\log\Delta_{\tau}\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}&\quad\hbox{if}\ \mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=0\\
-\infty&\quad\hbox{if}\ \mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)<0.\end{array}\right.

If we do not assume the convergence in (
7.4
), then the analysis of
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{quen}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)
is more complicated. But without some concentration phenomenon there may not be a well-defined ‘high probability’ behaviour of those random volumes at all, and then the formula that defines
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{quen}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
has less interest anyway.

Remark 7.5

(h)
.

Similarly to AP entropy, one can define both annealed and quenched versions of sofic entropy. Annealed sofic entropy is the invariant that Bowen introduced in
[
31
,
27
]
under the name ‘f-invariant’. However, it is not known in ergodic theory whether a simple relationship holds between annealed and quenched sofic entropy for uniform random permutation actions. This is a significant open problem. Without analogs of the arguments sketched above, quenched sofic entropy remains highly mysterious.

Nevertheless, using uniform random permutation actions on finite sets, one can copy all of the basic steps above to define a natural notion of zeroth-order sofic entropy in ergodic theory. As far as I know no significant results have been obtained about it. The story presumably diverges from the case of AP entropy quite early: for instance, I would not expect any simple substitute for Theorem
7.2
for sofic entropy. Nonetheless, it would be of great interest to prove a result like convergence in the LD sense for this notion, or to investigate properties like additivity for Cartesian products (compare with Chapter
8
below).
\lhd

Chapter 8

Additive entropy functionals

In this chapter we explore the consequences of an additional property sometimes enjoyed by annealed and zeroth-order AP entropy.

8.1

Additive entropy functionals

Fix a random AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}
. We now consider a probabilistic generalization of the additivity of AP entropy from Corollary
4.13
(a). As with the property of concavity discussed in Section
7.4
, this turns into a relationship between annealed and zeroth-order AP entropy.

Proposition 8.1
.

If
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially in probability, then the following are equivalent:

i.

whenever
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
and
\psi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{\ell})_{+}
for some
k
and
\ell
, we have

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi))=\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)+\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\psi);

(8.1)

ii

whenever
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
and
\psi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{\ell})_{+}
for some
k
and
\ell
, we have

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi))=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)+\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\psi).

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{k})_{+}
and
\psi\in\mathfrak{B}(\mathfrak{A},\mathrm{M}_{\ell})_{+}
.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii).
Observe that

\tau\otimes I_{k+\ell}+t\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi)=\mathrm{diag}(\tau\otimes I_{k}+t\varphi,\tau\otimes I_{\ell}+t\psi)

for any
t>0
. Therefore property (i) and Corollary
7.3
give

\displaystyle\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi))

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\big(\tau\otimes I_{k+\ell}+t\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi)\big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\tau\otimes I_{k}+t\varphi)+\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\tau\otimes I_{\ell}+t\psi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)+\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\psi).

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i).
This follows from the three-entropy formula (Theorem
7.2
), because the expression
\log\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}}
is also additive in
\varphi
(see Proposition
2.21
(c)). In these instances of the three-entropy formula, some of the terms may equal
-\infty
, but none can equal
+\infty
, so no ambiguous cancellations can appear.
∎

Problem 8.2
.

I suspect that, for a general random AP sequence, the additivity in (
8.1
) can hold for all
\varphi
and
\psi

only
if
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}
converges to a limiting tracial state superexponentially in probability. Indeed, it may be enough to know that (
8.1
) holds whenever
\varphi
and
\psi
are both tracial states.

Recalling the definition of zeroth-order entropy, property (ii) in Proposition
8.1
can be written informally as

\mathbf{P}\big(\pi_{n}\ \hbox{approx. contains}\ \mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi)\big)\\
\approx\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\ \hbox{approx. contains}\ \varphi)\cdot\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\ \hbox{approx. contains}\ \psi).

That is, to leading exponential order, the appearance of a tuple in
\pi_{n}
that is approximately typical for
\varphi
neither helps nor hinders the chance of finding another
orthogonal
tuple that is approximately typical for
\psi
.

Definition 8.3
.

We say that
\boldsymbol{\pi}

has additive entropy functionals
if (i)
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially fast in probability and (ii) the equivalent conditions in Proposition
8.1
hold.

Note that we include superexponential convergence of traces as part of this definition. This convention seems to be most convenient later.

Our examples of uniformly random AP sequences for free groups do have additive entropy functionals: see Proposition
8.4
below. This is a key step in the work of Part
III
.

If
\boldsymbol{\pi}
has additive entropy functionals, then zeroth-order entropy is actually additive in the following stronger sense. Its proof is somewhat analogous to the proof that Kolmogorov–Sinai entropy is additive for Cartesian products of measure-preserving systems
[
181
, Theorem 4.23]
.

Proposition 8.4
.

Assume that
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
superexponentially fast in probability and that
\boldsymbol{\pi}
has additive entropy functionals.

Let
(\rho_{i}:\ i\in I)
be any other family of representations of
\mathfrak{A}
, and let
\rho
be their direct sum. Then

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\rho)=\inf\Big\{\sum_{i\in J}\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\rho_{i}):\ J\subset I,\ J\ \hbox{finite}\Big\}.

In the sequel, we usually write simply

\sum_{i\in I}\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\rho_{i})

for the infimum on the right-hand side in Proposition
8.4
.

Proof.

Let
H_{i}
be the space of
\rho_{i}
for each
i
, so the space of
\rho
is

H:=\bigoplus_{i\in I}H_{i}.

Let
S
be the set of vectors in
H
that are non-zero in at most one coordinate. This satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
5.17
, so

\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\rho)=\inf\big\{\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\Phi^{\rho}_{X}):\ X\ \hbox{is a tuple drawn from}\ S\big\}.

If
X
is a tuple drawn from
S
, then it uses only finitely many of the direct summands in
H
. Therefore, permuting the entries of
X
if necessary, we may write it as the concatenation of some sub-tuples, say
X_{i_{1}}
, …,
X_{i_{\ell}}
, so that
X_{i_{j}}
is a tuple of vectors nonzero in only the
i_{j}^{\mathrm{th}}
coordinate. Since these sub-tuples lie in orthogonal sub-representations of
\pi
, the assumed additivity of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
gives

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\Phi^{\rho}_{X})=\sum_{r=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\Phi^{\rho}_{X_{i_{r}}}).

By taking the infimum over all possible choices of
i_{1}
, …,
i_{r}
and then
X_{i_{1}}
, …,
X_{i_{r}}
, this completes the proof.
∎

Additive entropy functionals have several consequences for
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
.

Proposition 8.5
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{\pi}
has additive entropy functionals, let
\tau
be the limit of
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}
, and let
\lambda
be the GNS representation of
\tau
.

a.

There exists a separable representation
\kappa
such that

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=0\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\pi\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa

for any other separable representation
\pi
. This representation
\kappa
is unique up to approximate equivalance. It approximately contains
\lambda
and has no stiff part.

b.

Let
\kappa
be as in part (a) and let
\pi
be any other separable representation. Let
M
be the subspace of
H_{\pi}
given by Proposition
2.1
so that
\pi^{M}
is contained in
\kappa^{\oplus\infty}
and
\pi^{M^{\perp}}
is disjoint from
\kappa
. Then
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\pi)=\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\pi^{M^{\perp}})
.

c.

Let
\kappa
be as in part (a). If
\pi\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\rho\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
and
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\rho)
, then
\pi\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\rho
.

Remark 8.6
.

In the free-group examples of Part
III
below, we actually find that
\kappa\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\lambda
. This follows from Theorem
C
, and it implies Theorem
5.3
. This conclusion is consistent with part (a) above because of Takenouchi’s theorem that
\lambda
has no stiff part (see Example
2.25
).
\lhd

Proof.

Part (a).
For each
k
, let

Z_{k}:=\{\varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A}):\ \mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi)=0\}.

We show that
(Z_{k})_{k\geq 1}
lies in
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
and satisfies the closure properties in Definition
3.5
.

•

Each
Z_{k}
is closed because
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
is upper semicontinuous.

•

Next we prove that each
Z_{k}
is nonempty. Let

h:=\sup\{\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\varphi):\ \varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})\}.

By the upper semicontinuity of
\varphi
it suffices to show that
h=0
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Then, by the definition of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
, every
\varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
has a neighbourhood
O
such that

\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)\leq\exp((h+\varepsilon)d_{n}+o(d_{n})).

Since
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
is compact, it can be covered by a finite subfamily of such neighbourhoods, say
O_{1}
, …,
O_{m}
. Since the total probability must equal
1
, it follows that

1=\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O_{1}\cup\cdots\cup O_{m})\neq\emptyset)\\
\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O_{i})\neq\emptyset)\leq m\cdot e^{(h+\varepsilon)d_{n}+o(d_{n})}.

Since
m
is fixed and
d_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, this is possible only if
h+\varepsilon\geq 0
. Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, we must actually have
h=0
.

•

If
\varphi\in Z_{k}
and
\psi\in\Sigma_{\ell}(\pi_{\varphi})
, then for any neighbourhood
O
of
\psi
Lemma
2.26
gives a neighbourhood
U
of
\varphi
such that

\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset)\geq\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},U)\neq\emptyset).

Since
\varphi\in Z_{k}
, the right-hand side here is bounded below by any decaying exponential function of
d_{n}
. Therefore the same is true of the left-hand side. Since
O
is arbitrary, this shows that
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\psi)=0
.

•

If
\varphi\in Z_{k}
and
\psi\in Z_{\ell}
, then additivity gives

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\mathrm{diag}(\varphi,\psi))=0,

and so the diagonal joining lies in
Z_{k+\ell}

Now
\kappa
is the representation promised by Proposition
3.6
for this
(Z_{k})_{k\geq 1}
.

By a degenerate case of Lemma
7.1
, we have
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\lambda)=0
, so the defining property of
\kappa
gives
\lambda\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
.

Finally, since
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\kappa)=0
, Proposition
8.4
gives also
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\kappa^{\oplus\infty})=0
. But this implies that
\kappa^{\oplus\infty}\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
, by the defining property of
\kappa
, so
\kappa
has no stiff part by Corollary
3.9
.

Part (b).
The assumption of additivity gives
\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\pi)=\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\pi^{M^{\perp}})+\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\pi^{M})
. Since
\pi^{M}\lesssim\kappa^{\oplus\infty}\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
, the second of these terms vanishes by the defining property of
\kappa
.

Part (c).
By Corollary
3.12
, we may adjust
\pi
within its approximate equivalence class in order to assume that
\pi\gtrsim\rho
, and hence
\pi\simeq\rho\oplus\pi_{1}
for some representation
\pi_{1}
. Now additivity and our assumptions give

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\rho)+\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi_{1})=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\rho)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi_{1})=0.

By part (a), it follows that
\pi_{1}\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
, and hence
\pi\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\rho\oplus\kappa
.

Now suppose that
a\in\mathfrak{A}
and
\rho(a)
is compact. Since we assume that
\rho\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
, Lemma
3.11
(c) gives that
\kappa(a)
is also compact. But since
\kappa^{\oplus\infty}\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
by part (a),
\kappa[\mathfrak{A}]
contains no nonzero compact operators, so in fact
\kappa(a)=0
. Therefore the hypotheses of Theorem
3.7
are satisfied. It tells us that
\rho\oplus\kappa\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\rho
. ∎

Intuitively, Proposition
8.5
(b) asserts that
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
is ‘coercive’ for approximate containment of finite-entropy representations.

Notice that Lemma
7.1
is subsumed by Proposition
8.5
(a) and (b) whenever the latter are available.

Consider the special case when
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is deterministic. By passing to a subsequence if necessary, we can also assume that
\boldsymbol{\pi}
sq-converges. Then we obtain the following characterization in terms of the structures studied in Section
3.2
.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\pi}
be a deterministic AP sequence with
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{d_{n}}\circ\pi_{n}\to\tau
and
\pi_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{sq}}}{{\to}}\rho
. Then
\boldsymbol{\pi}
has additive entropy functionals if and only if
\rho
has no stiff part, and in this case
\rho
agrees with the representation
\kappa
that appears in Proposition
8.5
(a) up to approximate equivalence.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be another separable representation. Since all probabilities are
0
or
1
,
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)
equals either
0
or
-\infty
, and by the definition of sq-convergence it does so according to

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=0\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\pi\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\rho.

If
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
has additive entropy functionals, then this is precisely the rule that defines
\kappa
from Proposition
8.5
(a). Now that proposition gives
\rho\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
and that
\rho
has no stiff part.

On the other hand, if
\rho
has no stiff part, and two representations
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are both approximately contained in
\rho
, then
\pi\oplus\pi^{\prime}
is approximately contained in
\rho^{\oplus 2}
, which is still approximately equivalent to
\rho
by Corollary
3.9
. This shows that
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
is additive.
∎

For a general random AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}
, we can connect Propositions
8.4
and
8.5
with the results of Section
3.2
as follows.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
\pi
be a separable representation and let
M
be its stiff part. Assume that
\boldsymbol{\pi}
has additive entropy functionals, and let
\kappa
be the representation from Proposition
8.5
(a).

Decompose
\pi^{M}
as a direct sum of finite inflations of irreducible representations as in Proposition
2.9
, say

\pi^{M}=\bigoplus_{i}\kappa_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}}.

If
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)>-\infty
then (i)
\pi^{M^{\perp}}\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
, and (ii) we have

\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\pi)=\sum_{i}m_{i}\cdot\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\kappa_{i}).

(8.2)

In particular, if
\boldsymbol{\pi}
has additive entropy functionals, then
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
is uniquely determined by (i) the approximate equivalence class of
\kappa
and (ii) the restriction of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
to the subset of stiff elements of
\widehat{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Proof.

If
a\in\mathfrak{A}
and
\pi(a)
is compact, then
\pi^{M^{\perp}}(a)=0
by the definition of the stiff part of
\pi
, and hence also
(\pi^{M^{\perp}})^{\oplus\infty}(a)=0
. As a result, Theorem
3.7
gives

\pi\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\pi\oplus(\pi^{M^{\perp}})^{\oplus\infty}\simeq\pi^{M}\oplus(\pi^{M^{\perp}})^{\oplus\infty}.

Now Corollary
5.18
and Proposition
8.4
give

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi^{M})+\infty\cdot\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi^{M^{\perp}}).

This can be finite only if
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi^{M^{\perp}})=0
, and so
\pi^{M^{\perp}}\lesssim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
by Proposition
8.5
(a). At that point we are left with

\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi^{M})=\sum_{i}m_{i}\cdot\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\kappa_{i}),

by a second use of additivity.
∎

In general, if
\pi
is a separable representation of
\mathfrak{A}
with a nontrivial stiff part, and if
\mathfrak{I}
is its stiff ideal, then
\pi
defines an exact sequence of C*-algebras:

0\to\mathfrak{I}/\ker\pi\to\mathfrak{A}/\ker\pi\to\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{I}\to 0.

Since
\mathfrak{I}/\ker\pi
is semi-elementary, we can define from this an element of the extension semigroup
\mathrm{Ext}(\mathfrak{A}/\mathfrak{I})
: see, for example,
[
101
, Section 2.7]
.

Problem 8.9
.

Can this invariant shed any further light on the behaviour of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
, for instance in concrete examples such as those of Par
III
below?

8.2

Large deviations in the strong-quotient topology

The most significant consequence of additivity is the following improvement on Theorem
6.5
.

Theorem 8.10
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\pi}
converges in the LD sense and that its entropy functionals are additive. Let
\kappa
be a representation as in Proposition
8.5
(a), and let
\pi
be another separable representation.

a.

If
\pi\not\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\lambda
, then
\pi
has an sq-neighbourhood
U
such that
\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)
decays faster than any exponential function of
n
.

b.

If
\pi\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
, and
a>\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)
, then
\pi
has an sq-neighbourhood
U
such that

\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)\leq e^{ad_{n}+o(d_{n})}\qquad\hbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

c.

If
\pi\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
has
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)>-\infty
,
U
is any sq-neighbourhood of
\pi
, and
a<\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)
, then

\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)\geq e^{ad_{n}-o(d_{n})}\qquad\hbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

If we also know that
\kappa\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\lambda
, then Theorem
8.10
is really a large deviations principle for the summaries
Z_{\bullet}(\pi_{n})
in the compact and metrizable space
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
with rate function

I(\overline{Z_{\bullet}(\pi)})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)&\qquad\hbox{if}\ \pi\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\lambda\\
\infty&\qquad\hbox{if}\ \pi\not\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\lambda\end{array}\right.

(see Definition
D.1
). The right-hand side here does indeed depends only on
\overline{Z_{\bullet}(\pi)}
by Theorem
3.3
, the invariance of
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
under approximate equivalence, and the fact that approximately containing
\kappa
is an invariant of approximate equivalence. Because of Theorem
C
, the uniformly random AP sequences for free groups studied later fall into this case.

In general, Theorem
8.10
leaves a ‘gap’ for those
\pi
that approximately contain
\lambda
but not
\kappa
, and we may not obtain a complete large deviations principle as a result.

Our proof of Theorem
8.10
goes through two technical lemmas. We take all of the hypotheses of Theorem
8.10
to hold from here until the end of the proof of that theorem.

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
\pi
be a separable representation. For any compact subset
L
of
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
and any

h_{1}>\sup\{\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi):\ \overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi)}\in L\}

we have

\mathbf{P}(\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{n})\in L)\leq e^{h_{1}n+o(n)}.

Proof.

Whenever
\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi)}\in L
, Theorem
6.5
gives a q-neighbourhood
U_{\pi}
of
\pi
such that

\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U_{\pi})\leq e^{h_{1}n+o(n)}.

Since every
U_{\pi}
is also an sq-neighbourhood and
L
is compact,
L
can be covered by finitely many of the sets
U_{\pi}
, so we also have

\mathbf{P}(\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{n})\in L)\leq e^{h_{1}n+o(n)}.

(8.3)

∎

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
\pi
be a separable representation such that
\pi\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
and
\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)>-\infty
. For some positive integer
k
, let
K
be a compact subset of
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
which is disjoint from
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}
. Then there are
c>0
and a q-neighbourhood
U
of
\pi
such that

\mathbf{P}\big(\pi_{n}\in U\ \hbox{and}\ \Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})\cap K\neq\emptyset\big)\leq e^{(\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)-c)n+o(n)}.

(8.4)

Proof.

Step 1.
We first consider the special case when
K
is the singleton
\{\psi\}
, and prove that there are
c>0
, a neighbourhood
O
of
\psi
, and a q-neighbourhood
U
of
\pi
such that the following holds:

If
\rho\in U
and
\mathcal{X}(\rho,O)\neq\emptyset
then
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\rho)<\mathrm{h}^{0}(\pi)-c
.

We prove this by contradiction. Since both
\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
and the quotient topology are second countable, if the conclusion is false then there is a sequence of separable representation
\rho_{n}
such that

\rho_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{q}}}{{\to}}\pi,\quad\rho_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{q}}}{{\to}}\pi_{\psi},\quad\hbox{and}\quad\liminf_{n}\mathrm{h}^{0}(\rho_{n})\geq\mathrm{h}^{0}(\pi).

By passing to a subsequence, we may assume that
\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\rho_{n})}\to\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\rho)}
in
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
for some limit representation
\rho
. Then
\rho
approximately contains both
\pi
and
\pi_{\psi}
, and it satisfies
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\rho)\geq\mathrm{h}^{0}(\pi)
by the Vietoris upper-semicontinuity of
\mathrm{h}^{0}
. But the first two conditions here imply that
\rho
is not approximately equivalent to
\pi
, and so this lower bound on
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\rho)
contradicts Proposition
8.5
(b).

Step 2.
Now consider a general compact set
K
. For each
\psi\in K
, Step 1 gives a neighbourhood
O_{\psi}
of
\psi
, a q-neighbourhood
U_{\psi}
of
\pi
, and a constant
c_{\psi}>0
.

By compactness, we can choose
\psi_{1}
, …,
\psi_{n}\in K
such that
K
is contained in
O:=O_{\psi_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup O_{\psi_{n}}
. Let

U_{0}:=U_{\psi_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap U_{\psi_{n}}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad c:=\min\{c_{\psi_{1}},\dots,c_{\psi_{n}}\},

so now we have
\rho^{0}(\rho)\leq\mathrm{h}^{0}(\pi)-c
whenever
\rho\in U_{0}
and
\mathcal{X}(\rho,O)\neq\emptyset
.

By shrinking
U_{0}
further if necessary, we may also assume that

U_{0}=\{\rho:\ \Sigma_{\ell}(\rho)\cap V_{0}\neq\emptyset\}

for some positive integer
\ell
and open subset
V_{0}
of
\Sigma_{\ell}(\Gamma)
. Since this must be a neighbourhood of
\pi
, we can also choose some
\varphi\in\Sigma_{\ell}(\pi)\cap V_{0}
, and now we can choose a neighbourhood
V
of
\varphi
with
\overline{V}\subset V_{0}
. Let

U:=\{\rho:\ \Sigma_{\ell}(\rho)\cap V\neq\emptyset\}.

Finally, let

L:=\{Z_{\bullet}\in\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}:\ Z_{\ell}\cap\overline{V}\neq\emptyset\ \hbox{and}\ Z_{k}\cap K\neq\emptyset\}.

This is a compact subset of
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
for the Vietoris topology, and any
\rho
satisfying
\Sigma_{\bullet}(\rho)\in L
also satisfies
\rho\in U_{0}
and
\mathcal{X}(\rho,O)\neq\emptyset
, and hence
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\rho)\leq\mathrm{h}^{0}(\pi)-c
. Now applying part (b) of Lemma
8.11
to
L
, we finally obtain

\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U\ \hbox{and}\ \Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})\cap K\neq\emptyset)\leq\mathbf{P}(\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{n})\in L)\leq e^{(\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)-c)n+o(n)},

as required.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.10
.

Part (a).
If
\pi
does not approximately contain
\lambda
, then there are a positive integer
k
, an element
\varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\lambda)
, and a neighbourhood
U
of
\varphi
such that
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}
is disjoint from
\overline{U}
. By Lemma
2.26
,
\chi_{\mathrm{reg}}
has a neighbourhood
V
such that

\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},V)\neq\emptyset\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},U)\neq\emptyset.

We therefore obtain

\mathbf{P}(\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})\subset\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})\setminus\overline{U})=\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},\overline{U})=\emptyset)\leq\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},V)=\emptyset),

and this is eventually smaller than any exponential function of
n
by our assumption on the traces. Since the left-hand event displayed above is defined by a Vietoris neighbourhood of
\pi
, this implies the desired upper bound.

Part (b).
Let
\pi
be a separable representation and let
a>\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)
. By Theorem
6.5
,
\pi
has a q-neighbourhood
U
such that

\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)\leq e^{ad_{n}+o(d_{n})}\qquad\hbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

Since
U
is also an sq-neighbourhood, this proves part (b).

Part (c).
We may restrict attention to any choice of base of neighbourhoods for the sq-topology at
\pi
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.13
, it suffices to consider an sq-neighbourhood of the form

W:=\{Z_{\bullet}\in\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}:\ Z_{\ell}\cap O_{1}\neq\emptyset,\ Z_{k}\subset O_{2}\}

for some positive integers
k
and
\ell
and open subsets
O_{1}\subset\Sigma_{\ell}(\mathfrak{A})
and
O_{2}\subset\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})
(see Lemma
3.16
). Let

U_{1}:=\{\rho:\ \mathcal{X}(\rho,O_{1})\neq\emptyset\},

so this is a q-neighbourhood of
\pi
.

Let
K:=\Sigma_{k}(\mathfrak{A})\setminus O_{2}
, so this is compact and disjoint from
\overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi)}
. Now Lemma
8.12
gives some
c>0
and a q-neighbourhood
U_{2}
of
\pi
such that the upper bound (
8.4
) holds with
U_{2}
in place of
U
. Finally, let
U:=U_{1}\cap U_{2}
, so this is still a q-neighbourhood of
\pi
, and observe that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{n})}\in W)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U\ \hbox{and}\ \overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})}\subset O_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)-\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U\ \hbox{and}\ \overline{\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})}\cap K\neq\emptyset)

\displaystyle\geq e^{\mathrm{h}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}^{0}(\pi)n-o(n)}-e^{(\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)-c)n+o(n)},

where we bound the first term from below by Theorem
6.5
(a) and bound the absolute value of the second term by Lemma
8.12
. Comparing the asymptotic behaviours as
n\to\infty
, this gives the desired probability lower bound.
∎

Remark 8.13
.

When
\kappa\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\lambda
, Theorem
8.10
is a large deviations principle for sequences of hyperspace-valued random variables. Previous examples of such principles have been proved for Minkowski sums of independent and identically distributed random compact sets: as far as I know these begin with
[
46
]
. However, the underlying probability law in Theorem
8.10
is very different.
\lhd

Remark 8.14
.

Theorem
8.10
is also worth comparing to some other recent results on random matrices, although they are not all phrased in terms of random closed sets. In particular, the recent preprint
[
54
]
handles the larger eigenvalues of certain Wigner matrices in a regime where those authors must also contend with large deviations that can be due to a mixture of mechanics, leading to possibly non-convex rate functions as a result. (I am grateful to Ofer Zeitouni for bringing this reference to my attention.)
\lhd

One potential application of Theorem
8.10
is new examples of representations that are approximable by finite-dimensional representations in the strong-quotient topology.

Proposition 8.15
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\pi}
converges in the LD sense and that its entropy functionals are additive. Let
\kappa
be as in Proposition
8.5
(a).

If
\pi
is a separable representation with
\pi\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\kappa
and
{\mathrm{h}^{0}(\pi)>-\infty}
, then
\pi
is a strong-quotient limit of an AP sequence.

Proof.

The Vietoris topology on
\mathcal{Z}\mathfrak{A}
is second countable, and hence so is the strong quotient topology on separable representations. Therefore, for the first conclusion, it suffices to show that every strong quotient neighbourhood
U
of
\pi
contains finite-dimensional representations of arbitrarily high dimension. However, by Theorem
8.10
, our assumptions on
\pi
give

\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)\geq e^{\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)n-o(n)},

and this is strictly positive for all sufficiently large
n
.
∎

Lewis Bowen has pointed out to me a connection between results like Proposition
8.15
and the ‘matricial field’ property of C
∗
-algebras introduced by Blackadar and Kirchberg in
[
20
]
. We record this quickly, but refer to that paper for the necessary definitions and basic theory. See also the surveys in
[
37
]
, or
[
38
, Sections 11.1–2]
or the papers
[
148
]
or
[
67
]
. The key point is that, if
\boldsymbol{\pi}
strong-quotient converges to
\pi
, then the composition

\mathfrak{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\dots)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\prod_{i\geq 1}\mathrm{M}_{d_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{quotient}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\prod_{i\geq 1}\mathrm{M}_{d_{i}}\Big/\penalty\big\{(A_{i})_{i\geq 1}:\ \|A_{i}\|\to 0\big\}

has kernel equal to
\ker\pi
, so it witnesses the following.

Corollary 8.16
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
8.15
, the C*-algebra
\pi[\mathfrak{A}]
has the matricial field property. ∎

Part III
RANDOM AP SEQUENCES FOR FINITELY GENERATED FREE GROUPS

Chapter 9

Free groups and their positive definite functions

Much of Part
III
studies random positive definite functions on a finitely generated free group that are obtained by fixing a vector in
\mathbf{C}^{n}
and then choosing a representation uniformly at random. Before introducing randomness, we need some preliminaries about the geometry of these groups, and also a way of parametrizing those positive definite functions. That parametrization generalizes the classical Verblunsky coefficients of a positive definite function on
\mathbf{Z}
.

Most of the results in Section
9.1
are standard, but the point of view in Section
9.2
is somewhat new.

9.1

Geometry of finitely generated free groups

Cayley graphs

Let
\Gamma
be a group generated by a finite subset
S
not containing
e
. Then the set
S\cup S^{-1}
defines a Cayley graph on the vertex set
\Gamma
. The group
\Gamma
is free on
S
if and only if this graph is a tree
[
157
, Proposition I.15]
. In this work we use Cayley graphs mostly in this special case.

To be more precise, we have a choice between the left and right Cayley graphs. We mostly use the
left
Cayley graph, and later references to ‘the Cayley graph’ all imply this choice. The edges of this graph are the unordered pairs
\{g,sg\}
for
g\in\Gamma
and
s\in S\cup S^{-1}
.

The graph metric of the Cayley graph is a right-invariant metric on
\Gamma
. For a group element
g
, its
length

|g|
is its distance from
e
in this metric. Equivalently, it the minimal length of a word in
S\cup S^{-1}
that evaluates to
g
. We write
B_{n}
and
S_{n}
for the closed ball and sphere of radius
n
around
e
, respectively. In particular,
S_{1}=S\cup S^{-1}
.

Our main uses for this graph boil down to the following. We wish to interpret paths in the graph as sequences of moves around an orbit of a point under an action of
\Gamma
, where each individual step is implemented by a generator or its inverse. Since our actions are all from the left, this requires that we use the left Cayley graph. (By contrast, the right Cayley graph is important as a graph on which
\Gamma
acts naturally by graph automorphisms.)

If
\Gamma
is the free group on
S
, then every element of
\Gamma
has a unique representation as a reduced word in
S\cup S^{-1}
. If elements
g
and
h
of
\Gamma
are represented by reduced words
v
and
w
respectively, then
g
is adjacent to
h
in the left Cayley graph if and only if one of those words is an extension of the other by a single letter on the left. More generally, if
v
is an extension of
w
by any number of letters on the left (resp. right), then we call
h
a
suffix
(resp.
prefix
) of
g
. For any
g
, its suffixes (resp. prefixes) are the vertices along the shortest path from
e
to
g
in the left (resp. right) Cayley graph.

If
E
is a subset of
\Gamma
, then its
exterior boundary
is the set of elements of
\Gamma
that are not in
E
but are adjacent to
E
in the left Cayley graph. Similarly, the
interior boundary
of
E
is the exterior boundary of
\Gamma\setminus E
.

Grounded subsets

Assume now that
\Gamma
is freely generated by
S
. The following class of finite subsets of
\Gamma
plays a central role throughout the remaining chapters.

Definition 9.1
.

A finite subset
F
of
\Gamma
is
grounded
if it contains
e
and is connected in the left Cayley graph.

Grounded sets may be visualized as the finite subtrees of the left Cayley graph that are rooted at the identity. They (or their analogs for the right Cayley graph) appear naturally in many works on free groups. For instance, they offer revealing choices of transversals to subgroups in Schreier’s theorem: see, for example,
[
157
, Proposition I.16]
. They also arise implicitly in the procedure of ‘splitting’ observables that Bowen uses for proving some of the properties of annealed sofic entropy (the ‘f-invariant’) in
[
31
]
. Our term for them follows
[
140
, Section 14]
.

If
F
is a grounded subset of
\Gamma
, and
g
is any element of the exterior boundary of
F
, then
F\cup\{g\}
is also grounded. We often abbreviate such a union to
F\cup g
in the sequel. Because the Cayley graph of
\Gamma
is a tree and
F
is a connected subset of it, in this case there is a unique element
s
of
S\cup S^{-1}
such that
g\in sF
, and then we call
F\cup g
an
enlargement of
F
in direction
s
. By removing interior boundary points one at a time and then reversing the process, any grounded set may be reached from
\{e\}
by a sequence of enlargements.

Several proofs about grounded sets in the sequel are by
induction on the grounded set
. For a desired assertion, this means we prove (i) that it holds for
\{e\}
, and then (ii) that if it holds for a grounded set
F
then it also holds for any enlargement of
F
. This implies the assertion for any grounded set by the assembly procedure described above. This is essentially the “dévissage” procedure used to analyse general finite trees in
[
157
]
.

In some of our inductive proofs of this kind, we must keep careful track of the directed edges of the Cayley graph that are contained in each grounded set. These directed edges are labeled by the generators from
S
that give rise to them: the directed edges within
F
that are labeled by generator
t
emanate from the vertices in
t^{-1}F\cap F
. The next lemma tracks how these families of edges grow when
F
itself is enlarged.

Lemma 9.2
.

If
F
is a grounded set and
F^{\prime}=F\cup g
is an enlargement in direction
s\in S\cup S^{-1}
, then

F^{\prime}\cap sF^{\prime}=(F\cap sF)\cup\{g\},

F^{\prime}\cap s^{-1}F^{\prime}=(F\cap s^{-1}F)\cup\{s^{-1}g\},

and

F^{\prime}\cap tF^{\prime}=F\cap tF\quad\hbox{for all}\ t\in(S\cup S^{-1})\setminus\{s,s^{-1}\}.

Proof.

Let
t\in S\cup S^{-1}
, and suppose that

h\in(F^{\prime}\cap tF^{\prime})\setminus(F\cap tF).

In particular,
h
lies in both
F^{\prime}
and
tF^{\prime}
, but must actually lie in at least one of
F^{\prime}\setminus F=\{g\}
and
tF^{\prime}\setminus tF=\{tg\}
. This gives us two cases:

•

If
h=g
, then we must also have
g\in tF^{\prime}
, and hence
t^{-1}g\in F
. Since
g
is an external boundary point of
F
, this is possible only if
t=s
.

•

If
h=tg
, then we must also have
tg\in F^{\prime}
, and hence
tg\in F
. Since
g
is an external boundary point of
F
, this is possible only if
t=s^{-1}
.

∎

There are many ways one can begin with
\{e\}
and grow a sequence of grounded sets that exhaust
\Gamma
by enlargements. At a few points below we need such sequences explicitly. A
grounded enumeration
is an enumeration

e=g_{0},g_{1},g_{2},\dots

of
\Gamma
such that
g_{i+1}
is an external boundary point of
\{g_{0},\dots,g_{i}\}
for every
i
. In any grounded enumeration,
\{g_{0},\dots,g_{i}\}
is a grounded set for every
i
, and each of these sets is an enlargement of its predecessor for
i\geq 1
. For example, any enumeration that satisfies

0=|g_{0}|\leq|g_{1}|\leq|g_{2}|\leq\cdots

(9.1)

is grounded – we refer to these examples as
length-first
enumerations.

An even more specialized way to select an enumeration that satisfies (
9.1
) is to place a total order on
S\cup S^{-1}
, and then apply the
length-lexicographic
ordering to elements of
\Gamma
by regarding them as reduced words. In this ordering,
g
precedes
h
if and only if either
|g|<|h|
, or
|g|=|h|
and the first letter of
g
that differs from the corresponding letter of
h
precedes that letter of
h
.

This particular kind of grounded enumeration plays a special role in a series expansion of annealed AP entropy that we derive in Section
12.3
, and which is then an essential ingredient in the proof of Theorem
C
in Chapter
13
.

9.2

Verblunsky coefficients for positive definite functions

Let
\varphi\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
(recall this notation from the discussion of group C
∗
-algebras in Section
2.4
). For any finite
F\subset\Gamma
, we can consider the
F
-by-
F
block matrix

\varphi[F]:=[\varphi(g^{-1}h):\ g,h\in F],

(9.2)

where each block lies in
\mathrm{M}_{k}
. These belong to the following sets.

Definition 9.3
.

For any finite subset
F
of
\Gamma
, we write
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
for the set of all positive semidefinite elements
Q
of
\mathrm{M}_{F}(\mathrm{M}_{k})
that (i) have all diagonal blocks
Q(g,g)
equal to
I_{k}
, and (ii) satisfy the symmetry

Q(g_{1},h_{1})=Q(g_{2},h_{2})\quad\hbox{whenever}\ g_{1},g_{2},h_{1},h_{2}\in F\ \hbox{and}\ g_{1}^{-1}h_{1}=g_{2}^{-1}h_{2}.

(9.3)

We refer to the elements of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
as
partial positive definite functions over
F
.

We write
\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F)
for the subset of nonsingular members of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
.

If
\varphi\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
, then it is
nonsingular
if
\varphi[F]\in\Sigma^{\circ}_{k}(F)
for every finite
F\subset\Gamma
.

Starting from
\varphi
, the matrices
\varphi[F]
in (
9.2
) are consistent in that
\varphi[F]
and
\varphi[F^{\prime}]
have the same submatrix indexed by
F\cap F^{\prime}
. On the other hand, given a consistent family of matrices
Q_{F}\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
indexed by some upwards-directed family of finite subsets
F
that cover
\Gamma
, we can set
\varphi(g):=Q_{F}(e,g)
for any
F
that contains
\{e,g\}
, and so produce an element of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
that gives rise to this family of matrices via (
9.2
). In this way,
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
is identified with the space of consistent families of such
F
-by-
F
block matrices.

For general locally compact groups, a classic question of abstract harmonic analysis asks when a partial positive definite function over a subset has a positive definite extension to the whole group. When
\Gamma=\mathbf{R}
and
F
is an interval, a classic result of Krein
[
113
]
shows that this is always possible. The corresponding result for discrete intervals in
\mathbf{Z}
is similar but simpler. On the other hand, even when
\Gamma=\mathbf{Z}^{2}
and
F
is a box, examples of Rudin
[
151
,
152
]
show that extension may be impossible, and similar examples seem to be available over most other groups. See
[
11
]
for recent advances and a selection of further references.

However, generalizing that result about
\mathbf{Z}
, extension is always possible when
\Gamma
is a free group and
F
is a grounded subset. That is, for any such
F
and any positive integer
k
, the map

\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)\to\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)

(9.4)

defined by (
9.2
) is surjective. Indeed, this is true even if we replace
\mathrm{M}_{k}
with
\mathfrak{B}(H)
for any complex Hilbert space
H
. This is essentially a result of Bakonyi and Timotin
[
10
, Theorem 4.3]
, although they do not consider general grounded sets explicitly. The variant of their proof in
[
140
, Lemma 25]
does allow this generality. That section of
[
140
]
offered strong inspiration for several of the arguments in this chapter and also Chapter
12
.

The key step in
[
140
, Section 14]
is showing that the submatrix map

\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F\cup g)\to\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)

(9.5)

is surjective when
F
is grounded and
F\cup g
is an enlargement of it. This implies the surjectivity of (
9.4
) by extending an element of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
through a sequence of enlargements that exhausts
\Gamma
.

This surjectivity can be recast as an example of the completion problem for
3
-by-
3
block positive semidefinite matrices: see Section
B.2
. In
[
140
, Lemma 24]
, Ozawa simply chooses the central completion of the relevant partial Gram matrix at each stage (see the remarks at the end of Section
B.2
).

Theorem
B
is a large deviations principle for certain random elements of the spaces
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
. It is proved by induction on
F
. Along a sequence of grounded sets
F
, a sequence of these random positive semidefinite matrices is revealed one enlargement at a time. To describe the distributions of these random matrices, we need not just the surjectivity of the map (
9.5
) for each
F
and
F\cup g
, but also a parametrization of all possible pre-images of an element of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
. We obtain this using Proposition
B.3
.

In the rest of this section we explain this parametrization in more detail. Suppose that
F
is a grounded set and that
F\cup g
is an enlargement of
F
in direction
s
. Let
Q\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
, and consider all possible extensions
Q^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F\cup g)
. Then
Q
is the submatrix of
Q^{\prime}
obtained by removing the block-row and block-column indexed by
g
. The symmetry (
9.3
) dictates some elements of this row and column: for example, we must have
Q^{\prime}(g,h)=Q(s^{-1}g,s^{-1}h)
whenever
h\in F\cap sF
. It turns out that these are the only entries of
Q^{\prime}
that are determined by
Q
through equation (
9.3
). This fact is contained within the proof of
[
140
, Lemma 25]
.

Lemma 9.4
.

If
h,a,b\in F
satisfy
a^{-1}b=h^{-1}g
, then
h\in F\cap sF
.

Proof.

Since
F
is grounded, it contains
e
and is connected in the left Cayley graph. Therefore, in addition to
a
and
b
,
F
contains all their suffixes. We may therefore cancel a common prefix and so assume that
a
and
b
begin with different letters.

Now there are two cases. First, if
s
is the first letter of the reduced word of
h
, then
s^{-1}h
is a suffix of
h
, so
s^{-1}h\in F
by connectedness.

On the other hand, if
s
is not the first letter of
h
, then
h
and
g
have no prefix in common. In this case there is no cancellation between the reduced words of
h^{-1}
and
g
when they are multiplied, as we have already arranged for
a
and
b
. Since
b\in F
and
a^{-1}b=h^{-1}g
, this is possible only if
