b
is a proper suffix of
g
, and therefore
h
is a proper suffix of
a
. Because of the equality
h^{-1}g=a^{-1}b
, this properness implies that
s^{-1}h
is still a suffix of
a
(now possibly equal to
a
), and so again it lies in
F
by connectedness.
∎

Now imagine ordering the set
F\cup g
so that
F\setminus sF
comes first,
F\cap sF
comes next, and
g
comes last. Use this ordering to write
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
as block matrices:

Q=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Q_{11}&Q_{12}\\
Q_{12}^{\ast}&Q_{22}\end{array}\right]\quad\hbox{and}\quad Q^{\prime}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}Q_{11}&Q_{12}&R\\
Q_{12}^{\ast}&Q_{22}&Q_{23}\\
R^{\ast}&Q_{23}^{\ast}&I_{k}\end{array}\right].

(9.6)

Let
Q^{?}
be the partial matrix obtained from
Q^{\prime}
by replaced
R
with
?
.

The possible extensions
Q^{\prime}
of
Q
are the positive semidefinite completions of
Q^{?}
. If
Q^{?}
is partially nonsingular, then these completions are parametrized by contractions from
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
to
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k|F\setminus sF|}
according to Proposition
B.3
. This partial nonsingularity holds if and only if
Q
is nonsingular, since the two relevant two-by-two-block submatrices of
Q^{?}
are both submatrices of
Q
by translation-invariance.

Definition 9.5
.

If
Q\in\Sigma^{\circ}_{k}(F)
and
Q^{\prime}
is an extension of it in
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F\cup g)
, then the
Verblunsky coefficient
of
Q^{\prime}
over
Q
is the element of
\Xi(k,k|F\setminus sF|)
that parametrizes it according to Proposition
B.3
.

If
\varphi\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
,
F
is a grounded set,
\varphi[F]
is nonsingular, and
F\cup g
is an enlargement of
F
, then the
Verblunksy coefficient
of
\varphi
from
F
to
F\cup g
is the Verblunsky coefficient of
\varphi[F\cup g]
over
\varphi[F]
.

The use of this term is extended from the classical case when
\Gamma=\mathbf{Z}
and
k=1
. By Bochner’s theorem, a unital positive definite function
\varphi
on
\mathbf{Z}
is the Fourier–Stieltjes transform of a probability measure
\mu
on the unit circle
\mathbf{K}
in
\mathbf{C}
. As a result, if
F=\{1,2,\dots,m\}
, then
\varphi[F]
is the Toeplitz matrix
[\langle z^{j},z^{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}]_{i,j=1}^{m}
. The associated sequence
(p_{n})_{n\geq 0}
of orthogonal polynomials is obtained by applying the Gram–Schmidt procedure to the sequence
1
,
z
,
z^{2}
, …in
L^{2}(\mu)
. These polynomials are related by a recursive formula due to Szegő
[
162
, Theorem 1.5.2]
. Provided
\mu
is not finitely supported, so
1
,
z
, …are linearly independent in
L^{2}(\mu)
, that recursion introduces an infinite sequence of coefficients
\alpha_{n}
called the Verblunsky coefficients. They lie in the open disk in the complex plane, and their geometric interpretation (explained in
[
162
, Section 1.5]
) shows that they are precisely the special case of our Definition
9.5
. The generalization with
\Gamma=\mathbf{Z}
but
k>1
has also been studied fairly completely: see the introduction and further references in
[
162
, Section 2.13]
, or the overview in
[
170
]
.

Our work in this section is a generalization for free groups of this classical parametrization of positive definite functions on
\mathbf{Z}
by their Verblunsky coefficients. This point of view is also taken by Bakonyi and Timotin in
[
10
]
(they refer to ‘Szegő parameters’ rather than Verblunsky). Our parametrization is a technical variation on theirs. Some of our other results later are also free-group generalization of known theorems about orthogonal polynomials.

Our next result is the free-group generalization of Verblunsky’s original theorem in this area: compare
[
162
, Theorem 1.7.11]
.

Corollary 9.6
.

Fix a grounded enumeration
g_{0},g_{1},\dots
of
\Gamma
, let
F_{n}:=\{g_{0},g_{1},\dots,g_{n}\}
for each
n
, and assume that
F_{n+1}
is an enlargement of
F_{n}
in direction
s_{n}
. Given
\varphi\in\Sigma^{\circ}_{k}(\Gamma)
, let
C_{n}
be the Verblunsky coefficient of
\varphi
from
F_{n}
to
F_{n+1}
. Then the map

\Sigma^{\circ}_{k}(\Gamma)\to\prod_{n\geq 0}\Xi^{\circ}(k,k|F_{n}\setminus s_{n}F_{n}|):\varphi\mapsto(C_{0},C_{1},\dots)

(9.7)

is a bijection.

Proof.

First suppose we know the sequence
C_{0},C_{1}
, …, for some
\varphi\in\Sigma^{\circ}_{k}(\Gamma)
. Then
\varphi(e,e)=I_{k}
, and once
\varphi[F_{n}]
is known, it and the contraction
C_{n}
determine
\varphi[F_{n+1}]
. So the given map is injective.

On the other hand, given any such sequence
C_{0},C_{1},\dots
, we can construct a compatible sequence of elements
Q_{n}\in\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F_{n})
by setting
Q_{0}:=I_{k}
and then recursively appealing to Proposition
B.3
. As explained at the start of the section, these all come from a single element of
\Sigma^{\circ}_{k}(\Gamma)
, and that element is nonsingular by construction. So the given map is surjective.
∎

By allowing generalized Schur complements, the inverse of the map (
9.7
) can be extended to parametrize the whole of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
, not just
\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(\Gamma)
. But this is more complicated, particularly when the topologies on those spaces of contractions must be taken into account, so instead we find ways to work around this case in the sequel. The necessary modifications are already a little fiddly when
\Gamma=\mathbf{Z}
. See, for instance,
[
33
, Introduction]
, where the authors have to allow finitely supported measures because they are working towards a large deviations principle on the space of
all
probability measures on
\mathbf{T}
.

We also sometimes need the continuity given by the following consequence of Corollary
B.6
.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
F
be a grounded set and let
F\cup g
be an enlargement of
F
in direction
s
. Given
Q\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F\cup g)
such that
Q[F]
is nonsingular, let
C
be its Verblunsky coefficient over
Q[F]
. Then the map

\displaystyle\big\{Q\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F\cup g):\ Q[F]\ \hbox{nonsingular}\big\}

\displaystyle\to\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F)\times\Xi(k,k|F\setminus sF|):

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle\mapsto(Q[F],C)

is a homeomorphism, and it maps
\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F\cup g)
onto
\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F)\times\Xi^{\circ}(k,k|F\setminus sF|)
.

Proof.

We identify
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F\cup g)
as the subset of
\mathrm{M}_{k(|F|+1)+}
defined by the symmetries (
9.3
); restrict the map from Corollary
B.6
to this subset; and then identify its image as described above.
∎

The use of Verblunsky coefficients to parametrize completely positive maps on free groups is a great advantage in the sequel. It has no clear analog in the ergodic theory of stationary processes over free groups. Tis enables new proofs about annealed AP entropy that are quite different from their counterparts for annealed sofic entropy, including in cases where those ergodic theoretic proofs do not extend.

For
\Gamma=\mathbf{Z}
, Verblunsky’s thoerem is the beginning of a long-running search for implications between properties of a measure
\mu
on
\mathbf{K}
and properties of its Verblunsky coefficients. Many such results are centrepieces of
[
162
,
163
]
. First in the line is a version of Szegő’s theorem in terms of Verblunsky coefficients: see
[
162
, Theorem 2.3.1]
, and also the end of Section
12.4
below. Several further examples are listed in
[
162
, Section 1.1]
: consider especially Baxter’s theorem about the absolute continuity of
\mu
and properties of its density
[
162
, Chapter 5]
; Rakhmanov’s theorem about everywhere-positivity of
\mu_{\mathrm{ac}}

[
163
, Chapter 9]
; and also implications from Verblunsky coefficients to essential spectra of measures (see the end of
[
162
, Section 3.4]
). It could be interesting to seek analogs of such implications over general free groups. Beware, however, that positive definite functions on a non-Abelian group are more mysterious than measures, and the conversion of the story into complex analysis via the spectral theorem ceases to be available. We do not pursue this topic further here.

Example 9.8
.

In
[
94
]
, Haagerup proved that the functions on a finitely generated free group
\Gamma
given by
\varphi(g)=e^{-t|g|}
are positive definite for all
t>0
. These were generalized to a non-radial family of examples in
[
60
]
, referred to as
Haagerup functions
. To define one of these, pick values
\varphi(s)=\varphi(s^{-1})
in the closed unit disk for each
s\in S
, and then extend to any reduced word by multiplication along the letters. These simple and instructive examples depend on the choice of the free generating set
S
, and they are never characters apart from the trivial examples
\chi_{\mathrm{reg}}
and
1_{G}
. They are also introduced carefully in
[
75
, Section 8.III]
. They generalize
[
162
, Examples 1.6.4 and 2.1.6]
from the classical case
\Gamma=\mathbf{Z}
.

If the Haagerup function
\varphi
is associated to
\pi=\pi_{\varphi}
by the cyclic vector
z
, and if
A
and
B
are two finite connected subsets of the
right
Cayley graph of
(\Gamma,S)
such that
A\cap B
is a singleton
\{g\}
, then the subspaces
\mathrm{span}\,\pi(A)z
and
\mathrm{span}\,\pi(B)z
are relatively orthogonal over
\mathbf{C}\cdot\pi(g)z
. This is an aspect of a more complete ‘independence’ phenomenon which is explained in
[
10
, Section 5]
. In view of our interpretation of ‘conditioning’ as projecting to an orthogonal subspace (see Section
10.2
), this shows that Haagerup functions are a representation theoretic analog of free-group Markov processes, as already noted by Burton and Juschenko in
[
41
]
. The reference
[
10
, Section 5]
also explains the generalization of this construction using matrices to obtain
\mathrm{M}_{k}
-valued positive definite functions.

Now pick a length-first enumeration
g_{0}
,
g_{1}
, …of
\Gamma
, let
F_{n}:=\{g_{0},g_{1},\dots,g_{n}\}
, and suppose that
F_{n+1}
is an enlargement of
F_{n}
in direction
s_{n}
. Let us show that the Verblunsky coefficient of
\varphi
from
F_{n}
to
F_{n+1}
vanishes for all
n
such that
|g_{n+1}|\geq 2
. For such a value of
n
, let
h
be the prefix of
g_{n+1}
of length
|g_{n+1}|-1
. Then
h
is one step from
g_{n+1}
towards
e
in the
right
Cayley graph. Since
|h|<|g_{n+1}|
,
h
also lies in
F_{n}
, because we picked a length-first enumeration. Finally,
s_{n}
is the first letter of the reduced word of
g_{n+1}
, and also of
h
because
h
still has length at least
1
. Overall, this shows that
h\in F_{n}\cap s_{n}F_{n}
, and
h
separates
g_{n+1}
from the rest of
F_{n}
in the right Cayley graph. Therefore Proposition
B.3
and the relative orthogonality from the previous paragraph give the desired vanishing of the Veblunsky coefficient.

On the other hand, as long as
|\varphi(s)|<1
for every
s\in S
, the first few Verblunsky coefficients all lie in the interior of the unit disk. Because of the formulas in Theorem
A
, this provides a family of examples with finite but non-zero annealed AP entropy.
\lhd

Example 9.9
.

In
[
10
]
, Bakonyi and Timotin also consider positive definite functions whose Verblunsky coefficients vanish beyond some positive radius
r
. Along a length-first enumeration
g_{0}
,
g_{1}
, …, this means that we choose the central extension once
|g_{n}|>r
, so these are simply the Haagerup functions when
r=1
. They generalize the Bernstein–Szegő polynomials
[
162
, Example 1.6.2]
from the case
\Gamma=\mathbf{Z}
, and can be used to approximate an arbitrary positive definite function by a generalization of
[
162
, Theorem 1.7.8]
.
\lhd

Many other distinguished families of free-group representations have been studied, and it could be interesting to study our notions of entropy in those as well. For example,
[
75
, Chapter 5]
introduces two other one-parameter families of irreducible free-group representations. Such examples are often constructed as ‘boundary representations’, and more of them are studied in
[
114
]
, which also includes several further references concerning this family.

Notes and further references

Even for locally compact Abelian groups, problems of characterizing and extending locally-defined positive definite functions quickly become rich and subtle, and have been studied widely. The monograph
[
107
]
is mostly dedicated to such questions, with a number of sections that allow non-Abelian Lie groups as well.

Alongside
[
162
,
163
]
, Nikolski’s book
[
139
]
offers a somewhat terser introduction to Toeplitz matrices and orthogonal polynomials. See
[
139
, Section 5.6]
for Szegő’s limit theorems for determinants and spectra. Toeplitz operators are central examples in more abstract operator theory for many reasons. Other aspects of their study are introduced in
[
64
, Chapter 7]
or
[
101
, Section 2.3]
.

Chapter 10

Log-determinants as entropies

This chapter explores an analogy between linear maps between finite-dimensional inner product spaces (and a suitable notion of log-determinant) and discrete random variables (and discrete Shannon entropy).

This is an interlude from our study of positive definite functions or representations of free groups, which resumes in the next chapter. The formalism that we develop in this chapter offers simplicity and extra intuition for several calculations that we need to perform later with random Verblunsky coefficients.

The analogy between log-determinants and entropy is almost as old as information theory itself. The tightest link is actually with differential entropy rather than discrete entropy. If
(X_{1},\dots,X_{k})
is a multivariate Gaussian random vector with covariance matrix
Q
, then its differential Shannon entropy equals
\log\det Q
up to normalization
[
56
, Theorem 8.4.1]
. From this point of view, the ‘analogy’ sketched above is really between discrete and differential entropy. Just a few key differences must be kept in mind, particularly these: (i) differential entropy depends on the ambient dimension in which a random vector takes it values; (ii) differential entropy can have either sign, or even equal
-\infty
. These warnings apply directly to log-determinants as well.

All the standard identities and inequalities enjoyed by differential entropy have formulations for log-determinants. They are, in turn, largely analogous to the properties of discrete Shannon entropy, with a few key differences. This can be understood as a consequence of their meaning for Gaussian distributions, but the results are elementary enough that one can also regard the two sets of properties as equal cousins with analogous proofs. We prefer the second point of view in this work, where log-determinants play an essential role throughout, but in a purely ‘linear setting’ where Gaussian random variables would be an extraneous construct.

In this chapter we introduce a notation for log-determinants as a kind of entropy, along with its extension to conditional entropy and mutual information, and we establish the basic properties that we need. We also invoke a standard ‘method-of-types’ interpretation for log-determinants (see Theorem
C.2
), and show in Section
10.3
how it implies some large deviations principles for more complicated random Gram matrices. Those offer a method-of-types interpretation for conditional log-determinant entropy as well.

None of the ideas in this chapter are new. On the contrary, the analogy between log-determinants and other entropy-like quantities has appeared in many different forms across pure and applied mathematics for decades. However, for this very reason, our later work is made easier by having all the finite-dimensional results that we need collected in one place and with a consistent notation.

10.1

Abstract log-determinants

Let
K
and
H
be complex inner product spaces of equal finite dimension, and let
T
be a linear map from
K
to
H
.

We may express
T
as a matrix using any choice of orthonormal bases
\mathcal{B}
for
K
and
\mathcal{C}
for
H
. Let
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be two such choices for
K
, let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be two such choices for
H
, and let

M:=\!\phantom{i}_{\mathcal{C}}[T]_{\mathcal{B}}\quad\hbox{and}\quad M^{\prime}:=\!\phantom{i}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}[T]_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}.

Then these matrices are related by

M^{\prime}=U^{\prime}MU,

where
U
is a base-change matrix from
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
U^{\prime}
is a base-change matrix from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. Since we assume all these bases are orthonormal, so are
U
and
U^{\prime}
, and as a result we have

\det M^{\prime}=\det U^{\prime}\cdot\det M\cdot\det U.

(10.1)

Taking absolute values, this shows that the number

|\det M|

does not depend on the choice of the bases
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
provided they are orthonormal. Henceforth we write this number simply as
|\det T|
, and regard it as an abstract property of linear transformations between finite-dimensional inner product spaces of equal dimension. (So, importantly, this use of ‘
\det
’ can appear only inside an absolute value unless specific bases for our inner product spaces have been chosen.)

More generally, if
K
and
H
are any inner product spaces with
\dim K<\infty
, then we extend our definition above as follows.

Definition 10.1
.

If
T
is a linear injection from
K
to
H
, so
\mathrm{rank}\,T=\dim K
, then we regard it as a map from
K
to
\mathrm{ran}\,T
and then define
|\det T|
as above (that is, we ‘ignore the rest of
H
’).

If
T
is not injective, so
\mathrm{rank}\,T<\dim K
, then we let
|\det T|:=0
.

This agrees with our previous definition if
\dim K=\dim H
because in that case
|\det T|=0
if and only if
\mathrm{rank}\,T<\dim H
. We must keep the assumption that
\dim K<\infty
so that the determinants in (
10.1
) are defined.

This generalized notion of determinant may also be evaluated as follows.

Lemma 10.2
.

If
T:K\to H
as above, then

|\det T|^{2}=\det T^{\ast}T.

(10.2)

Proof.

If necessary, we may replace
H
by
\mathrm{ran}\,T
without affecting either side of (
10.2
), and so we may assume that
H=\mathrm{ran}\,T
.

If
T
is non-injective, then
T^{\ast}T
is singular, and both sides of (
10.2
) are zero.

If
T
is injective, then
\dim K=\dim H
. By choosing orthornomal bases, we may therefore assume that
K=H=\mathbf{C}^{\oplus\dim H}
, at which point (
10.2
) is an identity for classical matrix determinants.
∎

In equation (
10.2
), note that
T^{\ast}T
is a self-adjoint linear transformation from
H
to itself, and so it may be evaluated as a traditional determinant using any choice of orthonormal basis for
H
. However, beware that (
10.2
)
T^{\ast}T
and
T^{\ast}T
may not have the same determinant if
H
and
K
are different spaces.

If
T:K\to H
as above, and if
H^{\prime}
is any other inner product space that contains
\mathrm{ran}\,T
, then the value
|\det T|
is unchanged if we regard
T
as a map taking values in
H^{\prime}
rather than
H
. However, the choice of this target spaces does become important when we consider a composition of two linear transformations.

Lemma 10.3

(Multiplicativity)
.

Let

J\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T}}{{\longrightarrow}}K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\longrightarrow}}H

be linear maps between finite-dimensional inner product spaces, and assume that
T
is surjective. Then

|\det ST|=|\det S||\det T|.

Proof.

If
T
is not injective, then neither is
ST
, so in this case both sides of the desired equality equal
0
.

If
S
is not injective, then neither is
ST
, because
T
is surjective. So in this case also both sides of the desired equality equal
0
.

Finally, if
T
and
S
are both injective, then we may (i) restrict our attention from
H
to
\mathrm{ran}\,S
if necessary, and so assume that
S
is also surjective, and then (ii) pick bases of
H
,
K
and
J
so that
T
and
S
are both represented by square matrices. Now the desired equality follows by the multiplicativity of
\det
as a function of matrices.
∎

Example 10.4
.

The assumption that
T
is surjective is necessary in the lemma above. This is a consequence of our conventions for handling spaces of differing dimensions. To see this, consider the example

\mathbf{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{C}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{C}

where
Tx=(x,0)
and
S(x,y)=x
. Then
S
is not injective, so
|\det S|=0
, but
ST
is the identity of
\mathbf{C}
, so
|\det ST|=1
.

Remark 10.5
.

The convention above for extending determinants to maps from one vector space to another of possible higher dimension has widespread uses. For example, it gives a convenient expression of the area formula for integration against Hausdorff measures
[
70
, Sections 3.2–3]
. It is common to write these determinants in an alternative way offered by exterior algebra: see, for instance
[
182
, Exercise 2.13]
. If
V=[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
is a map from
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
to
H
, then that yields the formula

|\det V|=\|v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{k}\|.

We do not use this point of view later.
\lhd

10.2

Log-determinant entropy and related quantities

Let
T
be a linear map from a finite-dimensional inner product space
K
to another inner product space
H
, as in the previous section.

Definition 10.6
.

The
log-determinant entropy of
T
is the value

\mathrm{H}(T):=\log|\det T|,

with the usual convention that
\log 0:=-\infty
.

We sometimes call this just ‘entropy’ when no confusion can arise.

If
K=\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
, then we may identify
T
with
[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
for some
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}\in H
. In this case we sometimes write
\mathrm{H}(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})
in place of
T
.

Now consider also a subspace
L
of
H
. Let
\iota_{L}
be the inclusion map from
L
into
H
, let
P_{L}=\iota_{L}^{\ast}
be the orthogonal projection from
H
onto
L
, and let
P_{L}^{\perp}:=I_{H}-P_{L}
.

Definition 10.7
.

The
conditional log-determinant entropy of
T
given
L
is the value

\mathrm{H}(T\mid L):=\mathrm{H}(P_{L}^{\perp}T).

If
S
is another linear map into
H
, then we define

\mathrm{H}(T\mid S):=\mathrm{H}(T\mid\mathrm{ran}\,S).

Example 10.8
.

A single vector
v\in H
may be regarded as a linear map from
\mathbf{C}
to
H
. Then the definitions above give
\mathrm{H}(v)=\log\|v\|
, and more generally

\mathrm{H}(v\mid L)=\log\|v-P_{L}v\|

for any closed subspace
L
of
H
. ∎

As a heuristic principle, in Definition
10.7
and others in the sequel, ‘conditioning’ on a subspace
L
means
projecting everything to
L^{\perp}
. This intuition fits with our first version of the chain rule. Let
T:K\to H
and
S:L\to H
be two linear maps into the same space. Let us use the matrix notation
[T,S]
for the combined map

K\oplus L\to H:[x,y]^{\mathrm{T}}\mapsto Tx+Sy.

Proposition 10.9

(Chain rule, I)
.

We have

\mathrm{H}([T,S])=\mathrm{H}(S)+\mathrm{H}(T\mid S)

(including the possibility that both sides equal
-\infty
).

Proof.

If
\mathrm{ran}\,S
and
\mathrm{ran}\,T
are orthogonal, then
\mathrm{ran}([T,S])
is unitarily equivalent to
\mathrm{ran}\,S\oplus\mathrm{ran}\,T
, and this unitary equivalence converts the map
[T,S]
into
\mathrm{diag}(T,S)
, which satisfies

\mathrm{H}(\mathrm{diag}(T,S))=\mathrm{H}(T)+\mathrm{H}(S).

In the general case, let
J:=\mathrm{ran}\,S
, let
M:=P_{J}^{\perp}[\mathrm{ran}\,T]
, and let
\iota_{J}
and
\iota_{M}
be the inclusions of these spaces in
H
. Then
J
and
M
are orthogonal, their sum is equal to
\mathrm{ran}([T,S])
, and we have the factorization

[T,S]=[\iota_{M}P_{J}^{\perp}T+\iota_{J}P_{J}T,\iota_{J}P_{J}S]=[\iota_{M},\iota_{J}]\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}P_{J}^{\perp}T&0\\
P_{J}T&P_{J}S\end{array}\right],

where the matrix is regarded as a map from
K\oplus L
to
M\oplus J
. As such, this map is surjective, and so Lemma
10.3
and the special case of orthogonal spaces gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}([T,S])

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}([\iota_{M},\iota_{J}])+\mathrm{H}\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}P_{J}^{\perp}T&0\\
P_{J}T&P_{J}S\end{array}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=0+\mathrm{H}(S)+\mathrm{H}(P_{J}^{\perp}T)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}(S)+\mathrm{H}(T\mid S).

∎

Now consider two finite-dimensional subspaces
L
and
J
of
H
.

Definition 10.10
.

The
mutual information between
L
and
J
is the value

\mathrm{I}(L\,;\,J):=-\mathrm{H}(P_{J}^{\perp}\iota_{L}).

If
T
is a linear map from another inner product space
K
to
H
, then we define

\mathrm{I}(T\,;\,J):=\mathrm{I}(\mathrm{ran}\,T\,;\,J).

We may replace the second argument of
\mathrm{I}
by a linear map using the same convention.

Remark 10.11
.

For linear maps
S
and
T
taking values in
H
, observe that
\mathrm{I}(S\,;\,T)
depends only on the subspaces
\mathrm{ran}\,S
and
\mathrm{ran}\,T
. In particular, pre-composing either
S
or
T
with an invertible map of its domain does not change the value of
\mathrm{I}(S\,;\,T)
.

In information theory,
\mathrm{I}(S\,;\,T)
is equal (up to normalization) to the mutual information between the random images
S^{\ast}X
and
T^{\ast}X
, where
X
is a centred standard Gaussian random vector in
H
. The invariance property of
\mathrm{I}
described above then has a standard interpretation: this mutual information — unlike the differential entropy of either
S^{\ast}X
or
T^{\ast}X
alone — is independent of the scale or coordinate system in which those vectors are written. Compare, for instance, the discussion of ‘infinite precision’ on
[
123
, p180]
.
\lhd

Unlike log-determinant entropy, log-determinant mutual information cannot take arbitrary real values.

Lemma 10.12
.

Any subspaces
L
and
J
satisfy
\mathrm{I}(L\,;\,J)\geq 0
, with equality if and only if
L
and
J
are orthogonal.

Proof.

The map
P_{J}^{\perp}\iota_{L}
is a composition of contractions, so it as again a contraction, and therefore its determinant is at most
1
. Equality holds if and only if the map
P_{J}^{\perp}\iota_{L}
is actually an isometry, which is equivalent to
L\subset J^{\perp}
. ∎

The next result is a second chain rule, now involving log-determinant mutual information. Let
T:K\to H
be a linear map between finite-dimensional inner product spaces and let
L
be a subspace of
H
.

Proposition 10.13

(Chain rule, II)
.

We have

\mathrm{H}(T)-\mathrm{I}(T\,;\,L)=\mathrm{H}(T\mid L)

(including the possibility that both sides equal
-\infty
).

Proof.

Let
J:=\mathrm{ran}\,T
. Then

P_{L}^{\perp}T=(P_{L}^{\perp}\iota_{J})T.

Since
T
is surjective when regarded as a map from
H
to
J
, we may apply Lemma
10.3
to the composition on the right-hand side here (not necessarily to the one on the left). Since
\log
converts multiplication to addition, the result follows.
∎

Corollary 10.14
.

If
S
and
T
are two linear maps taking values in
H
, then

\mathrm{H}([S,T])=\mathrm{H}(S)+\mathrm{H}(T)-\mathrm{I}(S\,;\,T)

(including the possibility that both sides equal
-\infty
). In particular,
\mathrm{I}(\cdot\,;\,\cdot)
is a symmetric function of its two arguments.

Proof.

The identity follows by combining Propositions
10.9
and
10.13
, and then the symmetry holds because the rest of that identity is symmetric in
S
and
T
.
∎

Corollary 10.15
.

If
T
is a linear map into
H
and
L
is a subspace of
H
, then
\mathrm{H}(T\mid L)\leq\mathrm{H}(T)
, with equality if and only if either (i)
\mathrm{H}(T)=-\infty
or (ii)
\mathrm{ran}\,T
and
L
are orthogonal.

Similarly, if
S
and
T
are two linear maps into
H
, then

\mathrm{H}([S,T])\leq\mathrm{H}(S)+\mathrm{H}(T),

with equality if and only if ether (i) the right-hand side equals
-\infty
or (ii)
\mathrm{ran}\,S
and
\mathrm{ran}\,T
are orthogonal. More generally, if
R
is a third linear map into
H
, then

\mathrm{H}([R,S,T])+\mathrm{H}(R)\leq\mathrm{H}([R,S])+\mathrm{H}([R,T]).

(10.3)

Inequality (
10.3
) is often called ‘strong subadditivity’ in information theory.

Proof.

The first few conclusions all follow by combining Lemma
10.12
, Proposition
10.13
, and Corollary
10.14
. Finally, the inequality (
10.3
) follows by applying the unconditional version to
P_{L}^{\perp}S
and
P_{L}^{\perp}T
, where
L:=\mathrm{ran}\,R
, and then adding
2\mathrm{H}(R)
to both sides and using Proposition
10.9
.
∎

Now let
L
,
J
and
M
be three subspaces of
H
.

Definition 10.16
.

The
conditional mutual information between
L
and
J
given
M
is the quantity

\mathrm{I}(L\,;\,J\mid M):=\mathrm{I}(P_{M}^{\perp}[L]\,;\,P_{M}^{\perp}[J]).

We apply this definition to linear maps rather than subspaces by inserting the ranges of those linear maps, as previously.

If
M
is a subspace of
H
and
T
is a linear map taking values in
H
, then
\mathrm{ran}\,P_{L}^{\perp}T
is equal to
P_{L}^{\perp}[\mathrm{ran}\,T]
, so conditional mutual information applied to a pair of maps
T
and
S
satisfies

\mathrm{I}(T\,;\,S\mid M)=\mathrm{I}(P_{M}^{\perp}T\,;\,P_{M}^{\perp}S).

Lemma 10.17
.

If
L
and
J
are orthogonal subspaces of
H
then

P_{L+J}^{\perp}=P_{J}^{\perp}P_{L}^{\perp}.

Proof.

We have
(L+J)^{\perp}=L^{\perp}\cap J^{\perp}
, and now the result is a standard lemma for the orthogonal projection onto an intersection of complements of orthogonal subspaces.∎

Proposition 10.18

(Conditional chain rule)
.

If
T
is a linear map taking values in
H
, and
L
and
M
are subspaces of
H
, then

\mathrm{H}(T\mid M)-\mathrm{I}(T\,;\,L\mid M)=\mathrm{H}(T\mid L+M)

(including the possibility that both sides equal
-\infty
).

Proof.

Observe that

L+M=\underbrace{P_{M}^{\perp}[L]+M}_{\mathrm{orthogonal}},

and hence Lemma
10.17
gives

P_{L+M}^{\perp}T=P_{P_{M}^{\perp}[L]}^{\perp}P_{M}^{\perp}T.

Combining this with the definitions above, the desired equality may be written

\mathrm{H}(P_{M}^{\perp}T)-\mathrm{I}(P_{M}^{\perp}T\,;\,P_{M}^{\perp}[L])=\mathrm{H}(P_{P_{M}^{\perp}[L]}^{\perp}P_{M}^{\perp}T)=\mathrm{H}(P_{M}^{\perp}T\mid P_{M}^{\perp}[L]).

This is now the unconditional chain rule from Proposition
10.13
applied to the map
P_{M}^{\perp}T
and subspace
P_{M}^{\perp}[L]
.
∎

Later, we often express log-determinant entropies in terms of Gram matrices. If
T
is a
k
-tuple in the inner product space
H
, and
Q=T^{\ast}T
is its Gram matrix, then Lemma
10.2
can be written

\mathrm{H}(T)=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{H}(Q).

(10.4)

We use this relation in a number of calculations later.

More generally, suppose that
T
and
S
are a
k
-tuple and
\ell
-tuple in
H
, respectively, and let
R:=T^{\ast}S
. We can express
\mathrm{H}(S\mid T)
using the combined Gram matrix
[T,S]^{\ast}[T,S]
through a generalization of (
10.4
). Assume for simplicity that
T
is nonsingular, so the Schur complement
S^{\ast}S-(T^{\ast}S)^{\ast}(T^{\ast}T)^{-1}(T^{\ast}S)
from (
B.3
) is defined. As explained in Section
B.1
, this Schur complement is the Gram matrix of the map
P_{\mathrm{ran}\,T}^{\perp}S
, and so it appears in the generalization of (
10.4
) for conditional entropy

\mathrm{H}(S\mid T)=\mathrm{H}(S\mid\mathrm{ran}\,T)=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{H}(S^{\ast}S-(T^{\ast}S)^{\ast}(T^{\ast}T)^{-1}(T^{\ast}S)).

(10.5)

Now let
C
be the contraction provided by Proposition
B.1
for the combined Gram matrix of
[T,S]
. Either by recalling the geometric interpretation of this contraction described in Section
B.1
, or by combining formula (
B.4
) with the chain rule (Proposition
10.13
) and re-arranging, we find a simple partner of the formula above for mutual information:

\mathrm{I}(S\,;\,T)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{H}(I_{\ell}-C^{\ast}C)=-\mathrm{H}((I_{\ell}-C^{\ast}C)^{1/2}).

(10.6)

To generalize further, let
R
be an
m
-tuple in
H
as well. Now the combined Gram matrix
Q=[T,S,R]^{\ast}[T,S,R]
naturally has a
3
-by-
3
block structure. This Gram matrix determines all the conditional log-determinant entropies and mutual informations among them. Explicit formulas for these that generalize (
10.4
) can be given in terms of
Q
. In particular, we obtain that

\mathrm{I}(T\,;\,R\mid S)=-\mathrm{H}((I_{m}-C^{\ast}C)^{1/2}),

(10.7)

where
C
is the contraction associated to this matrix by Proposition
B.3
. With our analogy between tuples of vectors and random variables in mind, the central completion (see Section
B.2
) is the analog of a relatively independent joint distribution for a triple of random variables (compare, for instance, the main technical construction in
[
32
]
).

When we write the results of this section in terms of Gram matrices and Schur complements, we also recognize them as classical properties and identities for logarithms of determinants:

•

the Schur determinant formula (
B.4
) corresponds to the chain rule (Proposition
10.9
);

•

Fischer’s inequality
[
102
, Theorem 7.8.5]
corresponds to the subadditivity of entropy (a special case of Corollary
10.15
);

•

the Koteljanskiĭ (also called Hadamard–Fischer) inequality
[
102
, Theorem 7.8.9]
corresponds to the strong subadditivity of entropy in (
10.3
).

Similarly, Szász’s inequality
[
102
, Thorem 7.8.11]
corresponds to one of Han’s inequalities
[
56
, Theorem 17.6.1]
, although we do not write these out here.

We sometimes need a notation that indicates which Gram matrix lies behind an entropy or related value, without manipulating that Gram matrix explicitly. Let
Q
be an
n
-by-
n
positive semi-definite matrix, and let
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
be three disjoint subsets of
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. Let
T
be an
n
-tuple in a Hilbert space that has Gram matrix
Q
, and let
T_{\alpha}
be the sub-tuple indexed by
\alpha
, and similarly for the other subsets. Then we set

\mathrm{H}_{Q}(\alpha\mid\beta):=\mathrm{H}(T_{\alpha}\mid T_{\beta}),\qquad\mathrm{I}_{Q}(\alpha\,;\,\beta\mid\gamma):=\mathrm{I}(T_{\alpha}\,;\,T_{\beta}\mid T_{\gamma}),

(10.8)

and similarly for other expressions. This usage is well-defined because the tuple
T
is determined by
Q
up to unitary equivalence, which does not change the absolute values of any log-determinants.

Notes and further references

It would be impossible to review the full history of log-determinants as entropies here; we can attempt only an indicative sketch.

An early reference from information theory which depends on this connection for applied purposes is
[
40
]
. The paper
[
50
]
broadened Burg’s work and demonstrated the convenience of information theoretic inequalities as basic techniques.

Stimulated by ideas such as those in
[
40
]
, many results in matrix analysis identify solutions to natural problems about positive semidefinite matrices through a variational principle. These include certain matrix completion problems, which may be phrased in terms of maximizing determinants (as in
[
93
]
) or maximizing ‘entropies’ (as in
[
12
]
), even if no operational meaning for entropy is intended. An analogous use of ‘entropy’ for certain integrals of logarithms appears in the study of orthogonal polynomials, interpolation problems and the ‘commutant lifting theorem’: see, for instance,
[
76
]
or
[
162
, Section 2.3]
. A modern account of such ‘maximum entropy’ arguments is given in
[
13
, Theorem 2.1.4 and Section 2.6]
. Some simple completion problems of this kind appear in Part
III
below.

From another point of view, the fact that log-determinants are precisely the differential entropies of appropriate multi-variate Gaussian distributions can be used to give new proofs of various classical inequalities for log-determinants by treating them as special cases of inequalities for differential entropy and mutual information. The classic paper on these proofs is
[
55
]
. The results in
[
55
, Section 3]
include the classical inequalities of Fischer, Koteljanskiĭ (also called Hadamard–Fischer) and Szász: it is worth comparing that treatment with the traditional linear algebra proofs (as in
[
102
, Section 7.8]
, for example). A textbook reference for the information-theoretic arguments is
[
56
, Section 17.9]
. Our own work in Section
10.2
offers a kind of ‘middle ground’. The techniques used here remain within linear algebra, but the ‘entropy’ formalism shows how close these results are to their information theoretic analogs.

From another direction, our log-determinant entropy
\mathrm{H}
is a special case of the relative entropy of density matrices as studied in quantum information theory, which is often denoted by
S
. In particular, in the notation of
[
18
, equation (4.19)]
, we have
\mathrm{H}(A)=-S(I_{k}\mid A)
whenever
A
is a
k
-by-
k
positive semidefinite matrix of trace equal to
1
.

10.3

Large deviations for random Gram matrices

This section interprets log-determinant entropy and related quantities as the negative rate functions in some large deviations principles for random Gram matrices. We apply these results in Chapter
12
below.

This story begins with the method-of-types estimates in Theorems
C.2
and
C.3
, although Appendix
C.2
does not adopt the ‘entropy’ terminology. We prove the results in this section by a reduction to Theorem
C.3
.

We proceed in two steps. The first step considers a special case and shows how log-determinant mutual information appears. The second generalizes the first by adding more ingredients, and arrives at log-determinant conditional mutual information as the negative rate function. These large deviations principles illustrate the intuitive meanings of these quantities.

Let
k
,
\ell
and
n
be positive integers with
n\geq k+\ell
. In
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
, let
e_{1}
, …,
e_{n}
be the standard basis, and let
E:=[e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell}]
. In addition, let
V
be a random element of
\mathrm{U}(k,n)
with distribution
m_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}
. The Gram matrices of
E
and
V
are
I_{\ell}
and
I_{k}
, respectively.

The matrix of inner products
E^{\ast}V
is a random element of
\Xi(k,\ell)
: it is simply given by the first
\ell
rows of
V
. We denote its distribution by

\sigma_{n,\ell,k}A:=m_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}\{V:\ E^{\ast}V\in A\}\qquad(A\subset\Xi(k,\ell),\ A\ \hbox{Borel}).

(10.9)

Example 10.19
.

Suppose that
k=1
, so that
V
is given by a single random vector
v
distributed uniformly over the sphere
\mathrm{S}^{2n-1}
, and
E^{\ast}v
is the vector of its first
\ell
coordinates. When
\ell=n
,
\sigma_{n,\ell,1}
is simply the usual normalized surface measure of
\mathrm{S}^{2n-1}
. When
\ell<n
, it is given by

\frac{(n-1)!}{\pi^{\ell}(n-\ell-1)!}\cdot(1-|y|^{2})^{n-\ell-1}\cdot 1_{\{|y|\leq 1\}}\cdot d\mathrm{vol}_{2\ell}(y)\qquad(y\in\mathbf{C}^{\oplus\ell}).

This standard calculation is given in
[
154
, Section 1.4]
, for example.

One can also derive an explicit formula for
\sigma_{n,\ell,k}
when
\ell
and
k
are both at least
2
, but it is more complicated, and we avoid using it in the sequel. ∎

Theorem 10.20
.

For fixed
k
and
\ell
, the sequence of distributions
(\sigma_{n,\ell,k})_{n\geq 1}
obeys the large deviations principle on
\Xi(k,\ell)
with speed
n
and rate function

-\mathrm{H}(I_{\ell}-C^{\ast}C)\qquad(C\in\Xi(k,\ell)).

Proof.

Since
\Xi(k,\ell)
is compact, exponential tightness holds automatically. It remains to prove conditions (i) and (ii) from Definition
D.1
.

Let
O_{11}
be a neighbourhood of
I_{\ell}
in
\mathrm{M}_{\ell+}
. Let
C\in\Xi(k,\ell)
and let
O_{12}
be a neighbourhood of it in that space. Finally, let
\varepsilon>0
, and let
O_{22}
be a neighbourhood of
I_{k}
in
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
which is so small that

e^{-\varepsilon}\leq\mathrm{det}\,Q^{\prime}_{22}\leq e^{\varepsilon}\qquad\hbox{for every}\ Q^{\prime}_{22}\in O_{22}.

(10.10)

(so, in particular,
O_{22}\subset\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}
).

Write a typical element
Q^{\prime}
of
\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell)+}
in
2
-by-
2
block form with
R^{\prime}
off the diagonal (see (
C.11
)), and let

O:=\{Q^{\prime}\in\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell)+}:\ Q^{\prime}_{11}\in O_{11},\ (Q_{11}^{\prime})^{-1/2}R^{\prime}(Q_{22}^{\prime})^{-1/2}\in O_{12},\ Q_{22}^{\prime}\in O_{22}\}.

As we vary
O_{11}
,
O_{12}
and
O_{22}
, such sets form a neighbourhood base in
\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell)+}
around

Q:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}I_{\ell}&C\\
C^{\ast}&I_{k}\end{array}\right].

Let
S:=I_{\ell}-C^{\ast}C
, the Schur complement of the
(1,1)
block within
Q
.

Define
T^{\prime}(n,O)
from
O
as in (
C.10
). Applying Proposition
C.1
and simplifying gives

\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}T^{\prime}(n,O)}{v(k,n)}=\sigma_{n,\ell,k}O_{12}\cdot\int_{O_{22}}(\mathrm{det}\,Q^{\prime}_{22})^{n-k}\ d\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}(Q^{\prime}_{22}).

Combined with (
10.10
), this re-arranges to give

e^{-\varepsilon(n-k)}\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}T^{\prime}(n,O)}{v(k,n)\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}O_{22}}\leq\sigma_{n,\ell,k}O_{12}\leq e^{\varepsilon(n-k)}\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}T^{\prime}(n,O)}{v(k,n)\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}O_{22}}.

(10.11)

To prove the probability lower bound, note that for any neighbourhood
O_{12}
and any
\varepsilon>0
we may choose
O_{22}
so that (
10.10
) holds, and then the left-hand side of (
10.11
) is estimated by Theorem
C.3
(a) to give

\sigma_{n,\ell,k}O_{12}\geq e^{(\mathrm{H}(S)-\varepsilon)n-o(n)}.

On the other hand, if
a>\mathrm{H}(S)
, then we may choose
O_{11}
,
O_{12}
and
Q_{22}
small enough that (
10.10
) holds, and now the right-hand side of (
10.11
) and Theorem
C.3
(b) give

\sigma_{n,\ell,k}O_{12}\leq e^{(a+\varepsilon)n-o(n)}.

By the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon
and
a
, this completes the proof.
∎

Example 10.21
.

When
k=1
as in Example
10.19
, so each
V
is given by a single random vector
v
, then Theorem
10.20
asserts that the random
k
-dimensional projection
E^{\ast}v
obeys a large deviations principle in the closed unit ball
\overline{B}
of
\mathbf{C}^{k}
with rate function

-\log(1-|y|^{2})\qquad(y\in\overline{B}).

(10.12)

This classical fact can also be read off directly from the formula in Example (
10.19
).

In this example, notice that
v
is a uniform random element of the sphere whose
real
dimension is
2n-1
. This might make it more natural to consider the large deviations rate function for speed
2n
rather than
n
. This would be compensated by changing the formula (
10.12
) into

-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-|y|^{2})=-\log\sqrt{1-|y|^{2}}.

However, by convention we always take the complex dimension
n
as the speed in this paper. This is also why the general rate function in Theorem
10.20
involves no square root, unlike the expression in (
10.6
). Analogous remarks apply to Theorem
B
in full, and to its 1-dimensional predecessor in
[
82
]
(see also
[
33
, Theorem 4.1]
), which is phrased with speed
n
and no square roots. ∎

The next property of our measures on
\Xi(k,\ell)
is also needed later.

Lemma 10.22
.

If
n\geq k+\ell
then

\sigma_{n,\ell,k}(\Xi^{\circ}(k,\ell))=1.

Proof.

If we fix
X\in\mathrm{U}(k,n)
and
Y\in\mathrm{U}(\ell,n)
, then it is equivalent to show that

m_{\mathrm{U}(n)}\{U:\ \|X^{\ast}UY\|<1\}=1.

The complement of this event is the same as the event that the concatenation
[X,UY]
is not an injection, or equivalently the event that

\det\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}I_{k}&X^{\ast}UY\\
Y^{\ast}U^{\ast}X&I_{\ell}\end{array}\right]=0.

Regarded as a function of
U
, this determinant is a polynomial in its entries. It does not vanish identically, because when
n\geq k+\ell
we can certainly make some choice of
U
so that the ranges of
V
and
UY
are disjoint subspaces. Therefore the zero set of this polynomial has zero measure.
∎

Because of Theorem
10.20
and Lemma
10.22
, Lemma
D.7
lets us transfer our large deviations principle between
\Xi(k,\ell)
and
\Xi^{\circ}(k,\ell)
. Indeed, Lemma
10.22
shows that
\sigma_{n,\ell,k}
is supported on the open subset
\Xi^{\circ}(k,\ell)
of
\Xi(k,\ell)
when
n\geq k+\ell
. Let
\sigma^{\circ}_{n,\ell,k}
be a Borel probability measure on
\Xi^{\circ}(k,\ell)
which agrees with the restriction of
\sigma_{n,\ell,k}
whenever
n\geq k+\ell

Corollary 10.23
.

For fixed
k
and
\ell
, the sequence of distributions
(\sigma^{\circ}_{n,\ell,k})_{n\geq 1}
obeys the LDP on
\Xi^{\circ}(k,\ell)
with rate function

-\mathrm{H}(I_{\ell}-C^{\ast}C)\qquad(C\in\Xi^{\circ}(k,\ell)).

Proof.

Theorem
10.20
gives the analogous conclusion for the set
\Xi(k,\ell)
of all contractions rather than its open subset
\Xi^{\circ}(k,\ell)
. To apply Lemma
D.7
, it remains to observe that
-\mathrm{H}(I_{\ell}-C^{\ast}C)=\infty
whenever
C\in\Xi(k,\ell)\setminus\Xi^{\circ}(k,\ell)
. Indeed, if
C\in\Xi(k,\ell)
is not a strict contraction, then the matrix
I_{\ell}-C^{\ast}C
has a nontrivial kernel, in which case the quantity above is equal to the log-determinant of a singular matrix, which equals
\infty
.
∎

We now generalize Theorem
10.20
to the setting of Section
B.2
, starting with three tuples of vectors as in (
B.6
). We first identify a certain distribution exactly, and then turn this into a more general large deviations principle. Thus, suppose that
n\geq k+\ell+m
, and fix three tuples

V=[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}],\quad U=[u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell}],\quad\hbox{and}\quad X_{0}=[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}].

Let
M
be
\mathrm{ran}\,U
, and let
G
be the compact subgroup of
\mathrm{U}(n)
that fixes
M
, so
G
is canonically isomorphic to
\mathrm{U}(M^{\perp})
. Let
W
be a random element of
G
with distribution
m_{G}
, and let
X=WX_{0}
.

Because
W
is unitary and it fixes
M
, the combined tuple
[U,X]=[U,WX_{0}]
always has the same joint Gram matrix as
[WU,WX_{0}]
, and hence the same as
[U,X_{0}]
. Intuitively,
X
is a tuple drawn ‘uniformly at random’ conditionally on having this joint Gram matrix with
U
. As a result, the overall joint Gram matrix

Q:=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}Q_{11}&Q_{12}&R\\
Q_{12}^{\ast}&Q_{22}&Q_{23}\\
R^{\ast}&Q_{23}^{\ast}&I_{m}\end{array}\right]:=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}V^{\ast}V&V^{\ast}U&V^{\ast}X\\
U^{\ast}V&U^{\ast}U&U^{\ast}X\\
X^{\ast}V&X^{\ast}U&X^{\ast}X\end{array}\right]

is random only in the blocks
R
and
R^{\ast}
. Thus, the partial matrix
Q^{?}:=\pi^{?}(Q)
is deterministic, fixed by our initial choice of
U
,
V
and
X_{0}
, and
Q
is a random element of
\Delta(Q^{?})
(see Section
B.2
for notation).

We now add the assumption that
Q^{?}
is partially nonsingular. In this case, the relation (
B.12
) provides a random contraction
C\in\Xi(k,m)
corresponding to the random completion
Q\in\Delta(Q^{?})
. Having fixed
Q^{?}
, each of
Q
and
C
determines the other uniquely and continuously, by Corollary
B.6
.

Proposition 10.24
.

Under the assumptions above,
C
has distribution
\sigma_{n-\ell,m,k}
.

Proof.

Let
P
be the orthogonal projection onto
M
. Since every
W\in G
satisfies
PW=WP=P
, the relevant block of
Q
is given by

\displaystyle V^{\ast}X

\displaystyle=V^{\ast}WX_{0}

\displaystyle=V^{\ast}PWX_{0}+V^{\ast}P^{\perp}WX_{0}

\displaystyle=V^{\ast}PX_{0}+V^{\ast}P^{\perp}WX_{0}

\displaystyle=V^{\ast}PX_{0}+(P^{\perp}V)^{\ast}W(P^{\perp}X_{0}).

The partial Gram matrix
Q^{?}
determines the two Schur complements
S_{11}
and
S_{33}
in (
B.11
). Using these and the terms that appear above, the random contraction
C
is given by

C=S_{11}^{-1/2}(P^{\perp}V)^{\ast}W(P^{\perp}X_{0})S_{33}^{-1/2}=(P^{\perp}VS_{11}^{-1/2})^{\ast}W(P^{\perp}X_{0}S_{33}^{-1/2}).

By (
B.2
),
S_{11}
and
S_{33}
are the Gram matrices of
P^{\perp}V
and
P^{\perp}X_{0}
, and therefore by (
A.3
) the tuples
P^{\perp}VS_{11}^{-1/2}
and
P^{\perp}X_{0}S_{33}^{-1/2}
are unitary maps from
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
into
M^{\perp}
and from
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus m}
into
M^{\perp}
, respectively. Since
W
corresponds to a uniformly random element of
\mathrm{U}(M^{\perp})
, making a final unitary identification of
M^{\perp}
with
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus(n-\ell)}
completes the proof.
∎

Proposition
10.24
leads to the following generalization of Theorem
10.20
. Fix a partially nonsingular partial Gram matrix
Q^{?}
as above, and for all sufficiently large
n
let
V_{n}
,
U_{n}
and
X_{n,0}
be three tuples in
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
whose Gram matrix lies in
\Delta(Q^{?})
. Let
M_{n}:=\mathrm{ran}\,U_{n}
, let
G_{n}
be the subgroup of
\mathrm{U}(n)
that fixes
M_{n}
, and let
W_{n}
be a random element of
G_{n}
with distribution
m_{G_{n}}
. Finally, let
X_{n}:=W_{n}X_{n,0}
, and let
Q_{n}
be the Gram matrix of the combined tuple
[V_{n},U_{n},X_{n}]
. This is a random element of
\Delta(Q^{?})
of the kind studied above.

Since
Q^{?}
is partially nonsingular, Corollary
B.6
establishes a homeomorphism between
\Delta(Q^{?})
and
\Xi(k,m)
. For any
Q\in\Delta(Q^{?})
, let
C
be the image of
Q
in
\Xi(k,m)
under this homeomorphism, and in particular let
C_{n}
the random contraction obtained from
Q_{n}
this way.

Corollary 10.25
.

The random Gram matrix
Q_{n}
obeys a large deviations principle in the space
\Delta(Q^{?})
with rate function

-\mathrm{H}(I_{\ell}-C^{\ast}C)\qquad(Q\in\Delta(Q^{?})).

(10.13)

Proof.

By the contraction principle, it is equivalent to prove that the random contraction
C_{n}
obeys the large deviations principle in
\Xi(k,m)
with rate function given by (
10.13
). This follows from Proposition
10.24
and Theorem
10.20
(since
n\to\infty
and
\ell
is fixed, the difference between
n
and
n-\ell
in the exponents vanishes).
∎

The assumption that
Q^{?}
be nonsingular is essential for making sense of Proposition
10.24
, because without it the contraction
C
is not even uniquely determined by
Q
. However, if this assumption does not hold, then we can still manoeuvre to apply Corollary
10.25
by first trimming some of the vectors in
V_{n}
,
U_{n}
or
X_{n,0}
(equivalently, removing some rows and columns of
Q_{n}
) until we reach nonsingularity of the remaining partial Gram matrix. This does not change the log-determinant mutual information from the original full Gram matrix. The steps are routine but fiddly, so we omit the details.

Chapter 11

Orbits in random representations

11.1

Conditioning on orbit patches

To reach Theorem
B
, we first develop some auxiliary calculations about conditioning on grounded subsets of orbits in a random representation. These conditional probability calculations have natural expressions in terms of the Haar measures of various closed subgroups of unitary matrices. The underlying principles are very general, so we formulate them for random homomorphisms from
\Gamma
to an arbitrary compact group
G
with a homogeneous space
X
. Quite possibly they have appeared in other works before, but I have not found a reference.

Let
G
be a compact metric group and let
m
be its Haar probability measure. More generally, if
gK
is any coset of a closed subgroup
K
of
G
, let
m_{gK}=\delta_{g}\ast m_{K}
be the translate of
m_{K}
to that coset.

The results of this section are phrased as identifying disintegrations of certain measures over certain maps. Disintegrations are also called ‘regular conditional probabilities’, and are classical constructs in measure theoretic probability. They can be used to express conditional expectations in terms of measurably-varying families of measures. The basic theory that we assume is covered in standard texts such as
[
141
, Sections V.6–8]
and
[
173
, Sections 4.2–3]
.

Suppose now that
L\subset K
is a nested pair of closed subgroups. Fix a coset
gK
, and write
gK/L
for the set of cosets of
L
that it contains: that is,

gK/L:=\{gkL\in G/L:\ k\in K\}.

Lemma 11.1
.

The family of measures

m_{gkL}\qquad(gkL\in gK/L)

is a disintegration of
m_{gK}
over the map

f:gK\to gK/L:gh\mapsto ghL.

Proof.

Following the definition in
[
141
, Section V.8]
, we must check two criteria. Firstly, for any
gkL\in gK/L
, we have

f^{-1}\{gkL\}=\{gk^{\prime}:\ gk^{\prime}L=gkL\}=gkL,

and
m_{gkL}
is supported on this fibre by definition. Secondly, we must check that

\int m_{gkL}\,dm_{K}(k)=m_{gK}.

Translating from the left by
g^{-1}
, we may reduce this to the case
g=e
, so it remains to check that

\int m_{kL}\,dm_{K}(k)=m_{K}.

This is now a standard relation between a compact group and a closed subgroup: see, for instance,
[
77
, Theorem 2.51]
(which is formulated for all unimodular locally compact groups).
∎

Now suppose that
G
acts on a homogeneous space
X
from the left. Given a point
x
of
X
, let
G(x)
denote its stabilizer. More generally, given a tuple
\mathbf{x}
in
X^{I}
for some index set
I
, let
G(\mathbf{x})
be the mutual stabilizer
\bigcap_{i}G(x_{i})
. In addition, given another tuple
\mathbf{y}
in
X^{I}
, let
H(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})
be the set of elements
g
of
G
such that

gx_{i}=y_{i}\qquad\forall i\in I.

If
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{x}
then this set is simply
G(\mathbf{x})
. In other cases it may be empty. If it is not empty, then it is equal to the left coset
hG(\mathbf{x})
and to the right coset
G(\mathbf{y})h
for any single element
h
in
H(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})
. When it is nonempty, we also write
m(\,\cdot\,|\,\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})
for
m_{H(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})}
. Observe that

m(\,\cdot\,|\,\mathbf{x};h\mathbf{y})=\delta_{h}\ast m(\,\cdot\,|\,\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}).

(11.1)

We may apply Lemma
11.1
to the cosets
H(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})
as follows. Assume that
H(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})
is nonempty, let
z\in X
, and let
w=gz
for some
g\in H(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})
. Then
H(\mathbf{x},z;\mathbf{y},w)
is still nonempty, because it contains
g
. For each such
g
, the set
H(\mathbf{x},z;\mathbf{y},w)
is equal to the set
gG(\mathbf{x},z)
, and it is contained in
H(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})=gG(\mathbf{x})
. Therefore Lemma
11.1
applies to tell us that the family

m(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{x},z;\mathbf{y},w)\qquad(w\in H(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})z)

is a disintegration of
m(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})
over the orbit-map
g\mapsto gz
.

We need a slightly more general version of this result that allows larger Cartesian products, but it is proved in the same way.

Corollary 11.2
.

Let
I_{1}
, …,
I_{r}
be finite index sets, and for each
i
let
\mathbf{x}_{i}
and
\mathbf{y}_{i}
be two
I_{i}
-tuples such that
H(\mathbf{x}_{i};\mathbf{y}_{i})
is nonempty. Let
j\in\{1,2,\dots,r\}
and let
z\in X
. Then the kernel

\prod_{i<j}m(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{x}_{i};\mathbf{y}_{i})\times m(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{x}_{j},z;\mathbf{y}_{j},w)\times\prod_{i>j}m(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{x}_{i};\mathbf{y}_{i})\qquad(w\in H(\mathbf{x}_{j};\mathbf{y}_{j})z)

is a disintegration of the measure

\prod_{i=1}^{r}m(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{x}_{i};\mathbf{y}_{i})

over the map

(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})\mapsto g_{j}z.

Proof.

The case
r=1
is outlined above. For the general case, in the group
G^{r}
, apply Lemma
11.1
to the large coset

\prod_{i=1}^{r}H(\mathbf{x}_{i};\mathbf{y}_{i})

and the smaller cosets

\prod_{i<j}H(\mathbf{x}_{i};\mathbf{y}_{i})\times H(\mathbf{x}_{j},z;\mathbf{y}_{j},w)\times\prod_{i>j}H(\mathbf{x}_{i};\mathbf{y}_{i})\qquad(w\in H(\mathbf{x}_{j};\mathbf{y}_{j})z).

∎

With the above preparations complete, we can return to random actions of a free group. Suppose now that
\Gamma
is freely generated by a finite set
S
of size
r
, and that a random homomorphism
\pi:\Gamma\to G
is obtained by choosing
g_{s}
for
s\in S
independently at random from
m
and then setting
\pi(s):=g_{s}
.

Where we apply Corollary
11.2
below, the tuples
\mathbf{x}_{i}
and
\mathbf{y}_{i}
are indexed by certain subsets of
\Gamma
. The following terminology helps keep track of them.

Definition 11.3
.

If
\pi\in\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,G)
and
x\in X
, then the
orbit map at
x
is

\pi^{(x)}(g):=\pi(g)x\qquad(g\in\Gamma).

If
F\subset\Gamma
, then the resulting
orbit patch
is the restriction
\pi^{(x)}|F
, which we regard as a tuple in
X^{F}
.

If we fix
F
and
x
, then the set of all orbit patches for different possible choices of
\pi
is a subset
Y_{F,x}
of
X^{F}
. It is closed as a continuous image of the compact space
\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,G)
. We refer to the tuples in
Y_{F,x}
as
F
-possible starting from
x
.

Now consider a grounded set
F
and a tuple
\mathbf{y}\in Y_{F,x}
. For each
t\in S
, the sets

t^{-1}F\cap F\quad\hbox{and}\quad F\cap tF

are naturally identified by left-translation by
t
. With this identification, we can consider the cosets
H(\mathbf{y}_{t^{-1}F\cap F};\mathbf{y}_{F\cap tF})
for each
t
. The fact that
\mathbf{y}
is
F
-possible implies that these cosets are all nonempty: if
\mathbf{y}=\pi^{(x)}|F
then
\pi(t)
must lie in
H(\mathbf{y}_{t^{-1}F\cap F};\mathbf{y}_{F\cap tF})
for every
t
.

Here is the main result of this section. It describes the conditional distribution of the generators of a random action given an orbit patch.

Proposition 11.4
.

Fix
x\in X
and let
F
be a grounded subset of
\Gamma
. Let
\pi
be a random action in which the generators
\pi(s)
,
s\in S
, are independent and distributed according to
m
. Then a regular conditional distribution for
(\pi(s):\ s\in S)
given the orbit patch
\pi^{(x)}|F
is given by

m_{F}(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{y}):=\prod_{s\in S}m(\,\cdot\,|\,\mathbf{y}_{F\cap s^{-1}F};\mathbf{y}_{sF\cap F})\qquad(\mathbf{y}\in Y_{F,x}).

(11.2)

Proof.

We prove this by induction on the grounded set
F
.

When
F=\{e\}
, the set
Y_{F,x}
is the singleton
\{x\}
. The left-hand side of (
11.2
) is simply equal to
m^{\times S}
, and so is the right-hand side because
F\cap sF
and
s^{-1}F\cap F
are both empty for every
s
.

So now suppose that the result is known for some grounded set
F
, and let
F^{\prime}=F\cup g
be an enlargement of it in direction
s
. Then, for any action
\pi
, we have
\pi(g)x=\pi(s)\pi(s^{-1}g)x
, and so
\pi^{(x)}|F^{\prime}
can be identified with
(\pi^{(x)}|F,\,\pi(s)\pi(s^{-1}g)x)
. Accordingly,
Y_{F^{\prime},x}
can be identified with a subset of
Y_{F,x}\times X
. For
\mathbf{y}\in Y_{F,x}
, let us write

Y^{\mathbf{y}}_{F^{\prime},x}:=\{w\in X:\ (\mathbf{y},w)\in Y_{F^{\prime},x}\}.

To extend the result from
F
to
F\cup g
, by the tower property of conditional expectation
[
173
, Theorem 4.5(vi)]
, it suffices to prove the following:

If
\pi
is a random action in which the generators are distributed according to
m_{F}(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{y})
, then a regular conditional distribution for
{(\pi(s):\ s\in S)}
given the image
\pi(s)y_{s^{-1}g}
is given by

m_{F^{\prime}}(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{y},w)\qquad(w\in Y^{\mathbf{y}}_{F^{\prime},x}).

(11.3)

To do this, we first identify the measures appearing in (
11.3
) more carefully. Writing an element of
Y_{F^{\prime},x}
as
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}:=(\mathbf{y},w)
, consider the factor measures

m(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{F^{\prime}\cap t^{-1}F^{\prime}};\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{tF^{\prime}\cap F^{\prime}})\qquad(t\in S).

If
t=s^{\pm 1}
(according as
s
lies in
S
or
S^{-1}
), then either the first or the second equality of Lemma
9.2
gives

m(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{F^{\prime}\cap t^{-1}F^{\prime}};\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{tF^{\prime}\cap F^{\prime}})=m(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{y}_{F\cap s^{-1}F},y_{s^{-1}g};\mathbf{y}_{sF\cap F},w).

On the other hand, if
t\neq s^{\pm 1}
, then the third equality of Lemma
9.2
gives

m(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{F^{\prime}\cap t^{-1}F^{\prime}};\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{tF^{\prime}\cap F^{\prime}})=m(\,\cdot\mid\mathbf{y}_{F\cap t^{-1}F};\mathbf{y}_{tF\cap F}).

(This is the key point where we need
F
to be grounded.)

Combining the identifications above, we see that the asserted conditional distribution is precisely the one given by Corollary
11.2
when we let
r=|S|
, index all the data by
S
rather than by
\{1,2,\dots,r\}
, and identify

I_{t},\ \mathbf{x}_{t},\hbox{and}\ \mathbf{y}_{t}\quad\hbox{with}\quad t^{-1}F\cap F,\ \mathbf{y}_{t^{-1}F\cap F},\ \hbox{and}\ \mathbf{y}_{F\cap tF}

for each
t\in S
. This continues the induction.
∎

Remark 11.5
.

In the proof above, the assumption that
F
is grounded enables us to apply Lemma
9.2
. Proposition
11.4
is not true if we omit that assumption. To see this more clearly, consider a simplified version of Proposition
11.4
that partitions the space of actions according to orbit patches but ignores conditional distributions. That version tells us that, if
\mathbf{y}
is
F
-possible starting from
x
, then

\big\{(\pi(s):\ s\in S)\in G^{S}:\pi(g)x=y_{g}\ \forall g\in F\big\}=\prod_{s\in S}H(\mathbf{y}_{s^{-1}F\cap F};\mathbf{y}_{F\cap sF}).

The left-hand side here is contained in the right-hand side for any subset
F
of
\Gamma
, but the reverse inclusion can fail if
F
is not grounded. For example, if
F
is actually well-separated in the sense that no two of its element are adjacent in the left Cayley graph of
\Gamma
, then the left-hand set above may still impose many restriction on the generators
(\pi(s):\ s\in S)
, but
F\cap sF=\emptyset
for every
s
, and so the right-hand set above is the whole of
G^{S}
.
\lhd

11.2

Random Verblunsky coefficients

Now consider again a random
n
-dimensional unitary representation
\pi
for some large
n
, and fix an orthonormal
k
-tuple
V
in
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
. We can turn the results of the previous section into a description of the distribution of
(\Phi^{\pi}_{V})[F]
for each grounded set
F
. This in turn is the key to our proof of Theorem
B
.

Let
Q^{\pi}[F]:=(\Phi^{\pi}_{V})[F]
to lighten notation. The description is recursive from one grounded set
F
to an enlargement
F\cup g
in direction
t
. When
Q^{\pi}[F]
is nonsingular, let

C^{\pi}_{F,g}\in\Xi(k,k|F\setminus tF|)

be the Verblunsky coefficient of
Q^{\pi}[F\cup g]
over
Q^{\pi}[F]
(Definition
9.5
). This is a random element of that space of contractions once we condition on the event that it is well-defined. For notation, recall the distributions
\sigma_{n,\ell,k}
on the space of contractions
\Xi(k,\ell)
defined in (
10.9
).

Theorem 11.6
.

If
n\geq k|F|
, then the following hold:

a.

Q^{\pi}[F]
is nonsingular almost surely, so
C^{\pi}_{F,g}
is defined almost surely;

b.

C^{\pi}_{F,g}
is independent from
Q^{\pi}[F]
;

c.

C^{\pi}_{F,g}
has distribution
\sigma_{n-k|F\cap tF|,k|F\setminus tF|,k}
.

Notice that we need part (a) of this theorem for parts (b) and (c) to make sense. Part (a) of this theorem also gives part (a) of Theorem
B
.

Proof.

We prove this by specializing the results of the previous section to the case when
G=\mathrm{U}(n)
and
X=\mathrm{U}(k,n)
.

For a grounded set
F
and an enlargement
F\cup g
in direction
t
, we prove the following implications among the parts of Theorem
11.6
:

\hbox{part (a) for $F$}\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \hbox{parts (b) and (c) for $(F,g)$}\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \hbox{part (a) for $F\cup g$}.

When
F=\{e\}
, we have
Q^{\pi}[F]=I_{k}
, so part (a) is immediate in this case. Starting from there, the above implications imply the result in full by induction on
F
.

So now assume that
n\geq k|F|
, and suppose we already know that
Q^{\pi}[F]
is nonsingular almost surely. Therefore the Verblunsky coefficient
C:=C^{\pi}_{F,g}
is defined almost surely. It takes values in
\Xi:=\Xi(k,k|F\setminus tF|)
. Let us also assume that
t\in S
; the case
t\in S^{-1}
is analogous.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbf{P})
be the background probability space, and let
\mathcal{G}
be the sigma-subalgebra of
\mathcal{F}
generated by the random orbit patch
\pi^{(V)}|F
. Since
Q^{\pi}[F]
is a function of this orbit patch, the law of total probability gives

\mathbf{P}\{Q^{\pi}[F]\in A,\ C\in B\}=\mathbf{E}\big[\mathbf{P}(C\in B\mid\mathcal{G});\ Q^{\pi}[F]\in A\big]

for any Borel subsets
A\subset\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
and
B\subset\Xi
.
Therefore parts (b) and (c) for
F
,
g
follow if we show that the constant
\sigma_{n-k|F\cap tF|,\,k|F\setminus tF|,\,k}(B)
is a version of
\mathbf{P}(C\in B\mid\mathcal{G})
.

Now,
C
is a function of
Q^{\pi}[F\cup g]
, and this in turn is a function of
(\pi^{(V)}|F,\,\pi(t))
. Therefore Proposition
11.4
asserts that a version of
\mathbf{P}(C\in B\mid\mathcal{G})
is given by

m(\{\pi(t):\ C\in B\}\mid\mathbf{y}_{F\cap t^{-1}F};\mathbf{y}_{tF\cap F})\qquad\hbox{when}\ \mathbf{y}:=\pi^{(V)}|F\in Y_{F,V}.

(11.4)

To use this expression, fix
\mathbf{y}\in Y_{F,V}
and also some
U_{0}\in H(\mathbf{y}_{F\cap t^{-1}F};\mathbf{y}_{tF\cap F})
, and let
U
be drawn at random from the Haar probability measure on
\mathrm{U}((\mathbf{y}_{F\cap tF})^{\perp})
. Under the conditional measure in (
11.4
),
\pi(t)
has the same distribution as
UU_{0}
, by the definitions in Section
11.1
, and then
Q^{\pi}[F\cup g]
has the same distribution as the Gram matrix of the combined tuple
[\mathbf{y}_{F\setminus tF},\ \mathbf{y}_{F\cap tF},\ UU_{0}y_{t^{-1}g}]
, where only the last entry is random.

If it happens that
Q^{\pi}[F]\in\Sigma^{\circ}_{k}(F)
, then the combined tuple
[\mathbf{y}_{F\setminus tF},\ \mathbf{y}_{F\cap tF}]
is linearly independent, and so is the combined tuple
[\mathbf{y}_{F\cap tF},\ UU_{0}y_{t^{-1}g}]
because it is contained in the translate
U\mathbf{y}
. We can therefore apply Proposition
10.24
, which tells us that the left-hand side of (
11.4
) is equal to
\sigma_{n-k|F\cap tF|,k|F\setminus tF|,k}(B)
, as required. Since
Q^{\pi}[F]\in\Sigma^{\circ}_{k}(F)
lies in
Y^{\circ}_{F,V}
almost surely by part (a) for
F
, this completes the proof of parts (b) and (c) for
(F,g)
.

Finally, by Lemma
10.22
,
\sigma_{n-k|F\cap tF|,\,k|F\setminus tF|,\,k}
is supported on the subset
{\Xi^{\circ}(k,k|F\setminus tF|)}
of proper contractions provided we have

n-k|F\cap tF|\geq k+k|F\setminus tF|,\quad\hbox{or equivalently}\quad n\geq k(|F|+1).

In this case, the dilation
Q^{\pi}[F\cup g]
of
Q^{\pi}[F]
is still almost surely nonsingular, by Proposition
B.3
. So if
n\geq k(|F|+1)
then we can now also conclude part (a) for
F\cup g
, so the induction continues.
∎

The independence given by part Theorem
11.6
(b) is a great advantage of working with Verblunsky coefficients during the subsequent proof of the rest of Theorem
B
. It has the following corollary. Fix any grounded enumeration
e=g_{0}
,
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
, …of
\Gamma
, and let
F_{i}:=\{g_{0},\dots,g_{i}\}
for each
i\geq 0
. Suppose that
F_{i+1}
is an enlargement of
F_{i}
in direction
s_{i+1}
for each
i
.

Corollary 11.7
.

Let
\pi
be a uniform random
n
-dimensional representation of
\Gamma
, and let
m:=\lfloor n/k\rfloor
. The resulting Gram matrix
Q^{\pi}[F_{i}]
is nonsingular almost surely for all
i=0,1,2\dots,m
, and the corresponding random Verblunsky coefficients

C^{\pi}_{F_{0},g_{1}},\ C^{\pi}_{F_{1},g_{2}},\ \dots,\ C^{\pi}_{F_{m},g_{m+1}}

(11.5)

are defined almost surely, are independent, and have respective distributions

\sigma_{n-k|F_{i}\cap s_{i+1}F_{i}|,k|F_{i}\setminus s_{i+1}F_{i}|,k}\qquad\hbox{for}\ i=0,1,2,\dots,m.

∎

If
k=r=1
, then (
11.5
) is the sequence of the first
m
Verblunsky coefficients of the random positive definite function on
\mathbf{Z}
given by the orbit of
e_{1}
under a uniform random
n
-by-
n
unitary matrix. In this case the independence of the first
n
of these random Verblunksy coefficients, together with their individual distributions, is a noted result of Killip and Nenciu
[
112
, Proposition 3.3]
. For
r=1
but
k\geq 2
, their result is generalized in
[
83
, Theorem 3.2]
. Corollary
11.7
is a further generalization of those results to random representations of higher-rank free groups.

One could also re-write Theorem
B
directly in terms of the sequences of Verblunsky coefficients that appear in Corollary
11.7
. The rate function has a simple sum form in terms of those coefficients: see Corollary
12.3
below.

Notes and further references

The result of Killip and Nenciu asserts that the Verblunsky coefficients of a single uniformly random unitary matrix are independent. This is an important step towards finding random-matrix models of various natural ensembles of
n
-point subset of the unit circle. These development and further references are discussed on
[
4
, p321]
.

Orthogonal polynomials on both the real line and the unit circle play a large role in calculations with the joint eigenvalue distributions of various random matrix models. For many of the most classical models of random self-adjoint or symmetric matrices, those joint distributions are determinantal with kernels expressed in terms of a related family of orthogonal polynomials on the real line. See, for instance, the example of the Gaussian unitary ensemble in
[
4
, Subsection 3.2.1]
and then the bibliographical notes that mention orthogonal polynomials on
[
4
, p184 and p321]
. For orthogonal polynomials on the unit circle, the connection begins with the appearance of certain Toeplitz determinants inside the Weyl intregration formula when computing expectations with respect to
m_{\mathrm{U}(n)}
: see
[
162
, pp67–9]
or
[
4
, Proposition 4.1.6 or Remark 4.1.7]
. However, these calculations for single random matrices are very precise, and I do not see analogs of them in the present setting. I do not know whether there is some more substantial connection between those appearances of orthogonal polynomials and the way we use a generalization of Verblunsky coefficients in the present work.

Rather than random positive definite functions, one can also study the orbits of single points in random actions of
\Gamma
by permutations of a finite set. Research on this topic contains uses of grounded subsets and other constructs that resemble our work in Chapter
9
and this chapter. See, for example,
[
137
,
120
,
117
,
146
]
.

Chapter 12

Large deviations and AP entropy

12.1

The chosen-tuple large deviations principle

Our next goal is Theorem
B
, so we now adopt the notation of that theorem. Using also the notation from Chapter
10
, we can write the negative of the desired rate function for our large deviations principle like this:

h_{F}(q):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{H}(q)-\sum_{s\in S}\mathrm{H}(q[F\cap sF])&\quad\hbox{if $q$ is nonsingular}\\
-\infty&\quad\hbox{if $q$ is singular}\end{array}\right.

for
q\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
. This is worth bearing in mind because later we manipulate this quantity using results such as the chain rules from Section
10.2
.

The proof of Theorem
B
is another induction on the grounded set
F
. The main ingredient is Theorem
11.6
. For an enlargement
F\cup g
of
F
, the basic idea for the inductive step is straightforward:

•

the inductive hypothesis gives us the LDP for
Q^{\pi_{n}}[F]
;

•

Theorem
11.6
and Corollary
10.25
tell us that the next Verblunsky coefficient
C
is independent from
Q^{\pi_{n}}[F]
and satisfies its own LDP;

•

Lemma
D.5
tells us how to combine these for the pair
(Q^{\pi_{n}}[F],C)
;

•

finally, since
Q^{\pi_{n}}[F\cup g]
is parametrized uniquely by
Q^{\pi_{n}}[F]
and
C
, applying the contraction principle should continue the induction.

There is a slight complication in the last of these steps. This is because
{Q^{\pi_{n}}[F\cup g]}
is not a continuous image of the pair
(Q^{\pi_{n}}[F],C)
until we exclude the event that
Q^{\pi_{n}}[F]
is singular. For this reason, the proof also involves passing between the desired target spaces
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
and
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F\cup g)
and their open subsets
\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F)
and
\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F\cup g)
. This is where we need Lemma
D.7
.

Proof of Theorem
B
.

Part (a) of Theorem
B
is already contained in Theorem
11.6
, so it remains to prove parts (b) and (c) (the two bounds of the LDP).

Step 1.
In view of part (a), we may choose Borel probability measures
\mu_{F,n}
restricted to the open subset
\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F)
of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
such that
\mu_{F,n}
agrees with the distribution of
Q^{\pi_{n}}[F]
for all sufficiently large
n
. In addition, we have

\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F)=\{-h_{F}<\infty\}

for any grounded set
F
. Therefore, by Lemma
D.7
, parts (b) and (c) of Theorem
B
are equivalent to the fact that
(\mu_{F,n})_{n\geq 1}
obeys the LDP on
\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F)
with rate function
-h_{F}|\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F)
. This is the version we prove in the remaining steps.

Step 2.
The rest of the proof is an induction on
F
. First, since
\varphi
is unital, we have

h_{\{e\}}(\varphi[\{e\}])=\mathrm{H}(I_{k})-0=0,

and the required large deviations principle is vacuous because
\Sigma_{k}^{\mathrm{u}}(\{e\})=\{I_{k}\}
. So now suppose the result is known for a grounded set
F
and consider an enlargement
F\cup g
in direction
s
.

Combining the inductive hypothesis with Corollary
10.23
and applying Lemma
D.5
, the product measures

\mu_{F,n}\times\sigma_{n-k|F\cap sF|,k|F\setminus sF|,k}\qquad(n\geq 1)

obey the LDP on the space

\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F)\times\Xi^{\circ}(k,k|F\setminus sF|)

(12.1)

with the rate function

-h_{F}(q)-\mathrm{H}(I_{k}-C^{\ast}C)\qquad((q,C)\in\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F)\times\Xi^{\circ}(k,k|F\setminus sF|)).

Step 3.
Under the inverse of the homeomorphism in Lemma
9.7
, the product set (
12.1
) is identified with
\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F\cup g)
, and Theorem
11.6
shows that
\mu_{F\cup g,n}
is the image of
\mu_{F,n}\times\sigma_{n-k|F\cap sF|,k|F\setminus sF|,k}
under this inverse homeomorphism. Therefore, by the identity (
10.7
), the measures
\mu_{F\cup g,n}
obey the LDP on
{\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F\cup g)}
with rate function

-h_{F}(q[F])+2\mathrm{I}_{q}(g\,;\,F\setminus sF\mid F\cap sF)\qquad(q\in\Sigma_{k}^{\circ}(F\cup g)),

using the notation from (
10.8
) for the second term.

To finish the proof we show that the expression above equals
-h_{F\cup g}(q)
. Since
q
and hence
q[F]
are nonsingular, we have

\frac{1}{2}\big(h_{F\cup g}(q)-h_{F}(q[F])\big)\\
=\mathrm{H}_{q}(F\cup g)-\mathrm{H}_{q}(F)-\sum_{t\in S}\big(\mathrm{H}_{q}((F\cup g)\cap t(F\cup g))-\mathrm{H}_{q}(F\cap tF)\big),

where all terms are finite. Lemma
9.2
tells us the difference between

(F\cup g)\cap t(F\cup g)\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad F\cap tF

for each
t\in S
. Using this and entropy identities, we can express the right-hand side above more succinctly in terms of conditional entropies. First, by the third equality from Lemma
9.2
, all terms in the sum with
t\neq s^{\pm 1}
cancel. Next, if
s\in S
and
t=s
, then by the first equality from Lemma
9.2
and the conditional chain rule (Proposition
10.18
) the remaining differences become

\mathrm{H}_{q}(g\mid F)-\mathrm{H}_{q}(g\mid F\cap tF)=-\mathrm{I}_{q}(g\,;\,F\setminus sF\mid F\cap sF),

as required in this case. Finally, if
s\in S^{-1}
and
t=s^{-1}
, then by the second equality from Lemma
9.2
the remaining differences become

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{q}(g\mid F)-\mathrm{H}_{q}(tg\mid F\cap tF)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}_{q}(g\mid F)-\mathrm{H}_{q}(g\mid sF\cap F)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{I}_{q}(g\,;\,F\setminus sF\mid F\cap sF),

where the first equality holds by the invariance of
q
under translation by
s=t^{-1}
, and the second follows by Proposition
10.18
as before. Once again this verifies the desired equality. This continues the induction and so completes the proof.
∎

Remark 12.1
.

It is worth comparing our appeal to Lemma
D.7
with the exposition of the case of positive definite functions on
\mathbf{Z}
in
[
33
]
, which does not involve a version of that lemma. Instead, those authors introduce a slightly larger space of finite or infinite sequences of Verblunsky coefficients that can be used to parametrize positive definite functions without assuming nonsingularity, then apply the Killip–Nenciu result to prove a large deviations principle there, and finish with a simple appeal to the contraction principle.

We could take a similar approach in our setting. However, over a non-cyclic free group, the resulting space of Verblunsky coefficients allowing singularity would be much more complicated than over
\mathbf{Z}
, and it seems easier to proceed via Lemma
D.7
as above.
\lhd

In the setting of Theorem
B
, the whole of
\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}
is a random element of the infinite-dimensional compact convex set
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
, which we can regard as a limit of its projections over grounded subsets of
\Gamma
. We now obtain an LDP for the whole of
\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}
.

Corollary 12.2
.

The random positive definite function
\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}
obeys the large deviations principle in the space
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
with rate function equal to

-\inf\big\{h_{F}(\varphi[F]):\ F\ \hbox{grounded}\}=-\lim_{n\to\infty}h_{B_{n}}(\varphi[B_{n}])\qquad(\varphi\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)).

(12.2)

Proof.

Since grounded sets form an upwards-directed cover of the whole of
\Gamma
, the first formula in (
12.2
) follows from Theorem
B
by an application of Lemma
D.6
(b). Since the balls
B_{n}
are all grounded and also form an upwards-directed cover of
\Gamma
, the same reasoning gives the analogous formula when
F
is restricted to the set of balls
\{B_{n}:\ n\geq 1\}
. By Lemma
D.6
(a), the quantity appearing inside that infimum is non-decreasing in
n
, so the infimum is equal to the limit as
n\to\infty
.
∎

Next, we observe that the negative of the rate function in Corollary
12.2
can also be written as an infinite series of contributions that appear as one enlarges a grounded set one element at a time to cover
\Gamma
. Let
e=g_{0}
,
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
, …be a grounded enumeration of
\Gamma
as discussed in Section
9.1
, let
F_{n}=\{g_{0},\dots,g_{n}\}
for each
n\geq 0
, and let
s_{n}\in S\cup S^{-1}
be such that
F_{n+1}
is an enlargement of
F_{n}
in direction
s_{n}
for every
n
.

Corollary 12.3
.

For any
\varphi\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}h_{B_{n}}(\varphi[B_{n}])=-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2\mathrm{I}_{\varphi}(g_{n+1}\,;\,F_{n}\mid F_{n}\cap s_{n}F_{n})

If
\varphi
is nonsingular and its Verblunsky coefficient from
F_{n}
to
F_{n}\cup g_{n+1}
is
C_{n}
, then this is also equal to

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{H}(I_{k}-C_{n}^{\ast}C_{n}).

Proof.

Repeating the calculation in Step 3 of the proof of Theorem
B
gives

h_{F_{n}}(\varphi[F_{n}])=-\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}2\mathrm{I}_{\varphi}(g_{i+1}\,;\,F_{i}\mid F_{i}\cap s_{i}F_{i})

by induction on
n
. If
\varphi
is nonsingular then the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
term of this sum is equal to
\mathrm{H}(I_{k}-C_{i}^{\ast}C_{i})
, by equation (
10.7
). These partial sums are non-increasing in
n
and converges to the desired infinite sum as
n\to\infty
. Since every grounded set is contained in
F_{n}
for all sufficiently large, the final conclusion is now another reformulation of Corollary
12.2
.
∎

The partial sums of the infinite series above depend on our particular choice of enumeration of
\Gamma
, but their limit does not. Presumably this fact can be proved directly using only rules for manipulating log-determinant entropies such as the chain rule, but I have not found such a proof.

It seems a little surprising that these partial sums can be telescoped into the relatively simple closed form of the series that appears in (
1.2
). In the analogous story in ergodic theory, a similar limit of closed-form expressions is found for annealed sofic entropy. But in that case a different proof can be given that handles a whole ball in
\Gamma
(or other grounded set) at once, so the appearance of a closed form is less surprising. This is the original derivation in
[
27
]
. I have not found an analog of that ergodic theoretic proof for annealed AP entropy, because it relies on certain bijections between sets to estimate their cardinalities. For us these would become maps between subsets of vector spaces that have no obvious reason to preserve volumes.

On the other hand, our use of Verblunsky coefficients has no predecessor in ergodic theory. They make the proof of Theorem
B
much easier, especially because of the independence given by Theorem
11.6
. One could try to turn our proof of Theorem
B
back into a derivation of annealed sofic entropy in ergodic theory. But without the independence given by Theorem
11.6
, this would presumably require some kind of conditional LDP for the conditional distribution of
Q^{\pi}[F\cup g]
given
Q^{\pi}[F]
(or their ergodic theory analogs). Conditional versions of LDPs appear in various places in the literature. The closest to what we would need may be
[
47
]
; see also the LDPs for random walks in
[
65
]
that must depend on starting points and other parameters, and the notion of ‘maxingales’ discussed in
[
147
, Chapter 2]
. But there does not seem to be a canonical form for such conditional LDPs, and uses of them tend to involve longer and more delicate estimates than we have needed above.

Before leaving this section, let us note another essential feature of Theorem
B
and Corollary
12.2
. These give large deviations principles on the compact convex spaces
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(F)
and
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
, respectively. However, in both cases the rate functions are usually not convex (that is,
h_{F}
and the limit in Corollary
12.2
are not concave). More precisely, the failure of concavity appears as soon as one leaves the subset of tempered elements of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
: see Chapter
13
below. This is a proper subset of that space provided
r\geq 2
, so we can attribute this failure to the non-amenability of non-Abelian free groups.

Because of the non-convexity, the machinery of Legendre–Fenchel transforms and the Gärnter–Ellis theorem (see
[
61
, Section 4.5]
, and also the discussion that accompanies their Lemma 4.1.21) are not available to us. As a result, the proofs we have given for Theorem
B
