and Corollary
12.2
are much closer to ‘first principles’ than those techniques.

12.2

Annealed and zeroth-order AP entropy

We can now turn the work of the preceding section into results about annealed and zeroth-order AP entropy for uniformly random AP sequences, including the first part of Theorem
A
.

Remark 12.4
.

Notation.
Starting in this section, we now write

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}:=\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\mathrm{h}^{0}:=\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}

when
\boldsymbol{\pi}
is a uniformly random AP sequence for a finitely generated free group.
\lhd

Proof of formula (
1.2
) of Theorem
A
.

Step 1.
If
\varphi(e)
is singular, then
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)=-\infty
, and the explicit convention in Theorem
A
says that (
1.2
) is also equal to
-\infty
in this case.

Step 2.
Now suppose that
\varphi
is unital. Fix a dimension
n
and an orthonormal tuple
V=[e_{1},\dots,e_{k}]
. Starting from the definition of
m_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}
as a pushforward of
m_{\mathrm{U}(n)}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}m_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\int_{\mathrm{U}(n)}1_{\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)}(UV)\ dm_{\mathrm{U}(n)}(U)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{U}(n)}\mathbf{P}(UV\in\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O))\ dm_{\mathrm{U}(n)}(U)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{U}(n)}\mathbf{P}(V\in\mathcal{X}(U^{\ast}\pi_{n}U,O))\ dm_{\mathrm{U}(n)}(U)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(V\in\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}\in O),

(12.3)

where the second equality holds by Tonelli’s theorem, and the fourth holds because the law of
\pi_{n}
is invariant under conjugation by any fixed element of
\mathrm{U}(n)
.

Inserting from (
12.2
), Lemma
5.8
and the second expression in (
12.2
) give

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)=\inf_{O}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\mathbf{P}(\Phi^{\pi_{n}}_{V}\in O)=\lim_{n\to\infty}h_{B_{n}}(\varphi[B_{n}]),

and this agrees with (
1.2
) in this case.

Step 3.
Finally, if
\varphi(e)
is nonsingular but not unital, then Proposition
5.7
(b) gives

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)=\log\det\varphi(e)+\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi_{1}),

(12.4)

where
\varphi_{1}:=\varphi(e)^{-1/2}\cdot\varphi\cdot\varphi(e)^{-1/2}
. On the other hand, for any finite subset
F
of
\Gamma
, we have

\varphi[F]=\mathrm{diag}(\underbrace{\varphi(e)^{1/2},\dots,\varphi(e)^{1/2}}_{F})\cdot\varphi_{1}[F]\cdot\mathrm{diag}(\underbrace{\varphi(e)^{1/2},\dots,\varphi(e)^{1/2}}_{F}),

and hence

\mathrm{H}(\varphi[F])=|F|\log\det\varphi(e)+\mathrm{H}(\varphi_{1}[F])

(where both sides may equal
-\infty
). Applying this equality to the balls
B_{n}
and their intersections
B_{n}\cap sB_{n}
, we find that

\displaystyle h_{B_{n}}(\varphi[B_{n}])

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}(\varphi[B_{n}])-\sum_{s\in S}\mathrm{H}(\varphi[B_{n}\cap sB_{n}])

\displaystyle=\log\det\varphi(e)+h_{B_{n}}(\varphi_{1}[B_{n}]),

where we have used the calculation

|B_{n}|-\sum_{s\in S}|B_{n}\cap sB_{n}|=\Big(1+2r\cdot\frac{(2r-1)^{n}-1}{2r-2}\Big)-r\cdot\Big(2\cdot\frac{(2r-1)^{n}-1}{2r-2}\Big)=1,

and where as usual either both sides are finite and equal or both sides are
-\infty
. Comparing this with (
12.4
), we see that the unital case of identity (
1.2
) implies the general case.
∎

Remark 12.5
.

All the real work in the proof above is being done by the first-moment calculation in (
12.2
). This is typical of the ways in which annealed averages can be simpler than other statistics in models such as ours.
\lhd

Formula (
1.3
) from Theorem
A
is proved following Lemma
12.11
in Section
12.3
.

We now turn our attention to zeroth-order AP entropy along
\boldsymbol{\pi}
. We need the following special consequence of concentration for our uniformly random AP sequence. The analog fails for uniformly random permutation AP sequences: see Example
5.20
.

Lemma 12.6
.

If
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)=0
, then in fact

\mathbf{P}\big(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset\big)\to 1

for every neighbourhood
O
of
\varphi
.

Proof.

Let
O
be a neighbourhood of
\varphi
. Choose a smaller neighbourhood
O^{\prime}
of
\varphi
and
\varepsilon>0
such that

\Phi_{X}^{\pi}\in O^{\prime}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\max_{s\in S}\|\pi(s)-\rho(s)\|<\varepsilon\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Phi^{\rho}_{X}\in O

for any
X\in\mathrm{U}(k,n)
and
n
-dimensional representations
\pi
and
\rho
. This implies that

B_{\varepsilon}(\{(\pi(s):\ s\in S):\ \mathcal{X}(\pi,O^{\prime})\neq\emptyset\})\subset\{(\pi(s):\ s\in S):\ \mathcal{X}(\pi,O)\neq\emptyset\},

where both sides are subsets of
\mathrm{U}(n)^{S}
, and
B_{\varepsilon}
is the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood in the maximum of the operator norm applied to each coordinate in
\mathrm{U}(n)^{S}
. By assumption, we have

\mathbf{P}\big(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O^{\prime})\neq\emptyset\big)\geq e^{-o(n)}.

That maximum of operator norms is
O(1)
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hilbert–Schmidt norm in Theorem
C.4
, so the set inclusion above and a standard consequence of exponential concentration give

\mathbf{P}\big(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},O)\neq\emptyset\big)=1-o(1).

∎

The next main result of this section identifies the limit in probability of the random sequence
\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})
in
\mathcal{Z}\Gamma
.

Proposition 12.7
.

For each positive integer
k
, the set

\{\varphi\in\Sigma_{k}(\Gamma):\ \mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)=0\}

is compact and nonempty, and the random sets
\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})
converge to it in probability in the Veitoris topology of
\mathcal{K}(\Sigma_{k}(\Gamma))
.

Proof.

Fix
k
, and accordingly regard
\Sigma_{k}(\Gamma)
as the domain of
\mathrm{h}^{0}
for the rest of the proof. We verify the conditions from Lemma
E.1
.

If
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)=0
and
U
is a neighbourhood of
\varphi
, then Lemma
12.6
gives

\mathbf{P}(\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})\cap U\neq\emptyset)=\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{X}(\pi_{n},U)\neq\emptyset)\to 1.

This verifies condition (i) from Lemma
E.1
.

On the other hand, if
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)<0
, then for some neighbourhood
U
of
\varphi
the probability
\mathbf{P}(\Sigma_{k}(\pi_{n})\cap U\neq\emptyset)
decays exponentially. This verifies condition (ii) from Lemma
E.1
.
∎

Later, we combine Proposition
12.7
with Theorem
8.10
to obtain a full large deviations principle for the uniformly random AP sequence in the strong quotient topology. However, that result also requires Theorem
C
below: in the notation of Theorem
8.10
, it tells that
\kappa=\lambda
in this example. See Theorem
13.21
.

We can now read off all our desired properties of
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
and
\mathrm{h}^{0}
.

Proposition 12.8
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\pi}
be a uniformly random AP sequence for a finitely generated free group.

a.

The traces
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}
converge to
\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}
superexponentially in probability.

b.

This random AP sequence has additive entropy functionals.

c.

This random AP sequence converges in the LD sense.

Proof.

Part (a).
This follows from Theorem
5.2
.

Part (b).
Consider the first of the formulas for
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)
given by Theorem
A
: the limit as
n\to\infty
of the expression

\mathrm{H}(\varphi[B_{n}])-\sum_{s\in S}\mathrm{H}(\varphi[B_{n}\cap sB_{n}]).

(12.5)

If
\varphi
is a diagonal joining, then every term here is additive, and hence so is
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
as a whole. So this property follows by part (a) and Proposition
8.1
.

Part (c).
In combination with part (a), the large deviations principle from Corollary
12.2
and identity (
12.2
) verify the LD convergence in Proposition
7.4
, so that proposition completes the proof. ∎

Any of the other formulas that we derive for
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
below could be used in the proof of Proposition
12.8
(b) with a similar amount of effort. But it seems to be much harder to prove this lemma directly from the interpretation of
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
as the large deviations rate function in Corollary
12.2
.

In future work, it would be interesting to explore further how
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
and
\mathrm{h}^{0}
behave for concrete examples of positive definite functions on
\Gamma
.

Problem 12.9
.

Consider a Haagerup function (Example
9.8
)
\varphi
which is not tempered. Provided
|\varphi(s)|<1
for every
s
, the formula for
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)
from Corollary
12.3
has only finitely many terms, and they are all finite. This implies that
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)>-\infty
by the three-entropy formula. But, on the other hand, Theorem
C
shows that
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)<0
if
\varphi
is not tempered, and for a non-Abelian free group this holds when the values
|\varphi(s)|
are close enough to
1
.

Another example could be the function
1_{H}
for
H
a nontrivial subgroup of
\Gamma
. I guess that
\mathrm{h}^{0}(1_{H})=-\infty
, but I have not proved this. Generalizing that example, a third possibility could be positive definite functions given by invariant random subgroups as in
[
15
, Section 15.F]
.

Finally, I also guess that any finite-dimensional representation
\pi
has
{\mathrm{h}^{0}(\pi)=-\infty}
, but I have not proved this either.

We return to some similar questions of this flavour in later chapters.

The next problem asks about another widely-studied operation on positive definite functions.

Problem 12.10
.

Given an inclusion of discrete groups, one can use induction to extend representations of the smaller to the larger, and this construction can be carried out at the level of positive definite functions
[
77
, Chapter 6]
. If they are both free groups, how do
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
and
\mathrm{h}^{0}
behave under this construction? The answer could be related to Seward’s work
[
158
]
in the ergodic theory setting.

12.3

Alternative formulas and expansions for annealed entropy

In this section we derive some alternative expressions for
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
. These include formula (
1.3
) from Theorem
A
, which is the direct analog of Bowen’s original formula from his introduction of annealed sofic entropy (then called the ‘f-invariant’). They also include the analog of a series expansion due to Seward
[
159
]
, which we derive from the running average of the limits in (
1.2
) and (
1.3
). This Seward expansion admits some more straightforward estimates than either (
1.2
) or (
1.3
) separately, and this greatly simplifies our proof of Theorem
C
later.

Throughout this section, we fix a positive definite function
\varphi:\Gamma\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
(it need not be unital now), and abbreviate
\mathrm{H}_{\varphi}
to
H
and
\mathrm{I}_{\varphi}
to
I
. Let us also name these two sequences:

\displaystyle E_{n}

\displaystyle:=H(B_{n+1})-\sum_{s\in S}H(B_{n+1}\cap sB_{n+1})

(12.6)

\displaystyle\hbox{and}\quad E_{n}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{s\in S}H(B_{n}\cup sB_{n})-(2r-1)\cdot H(B_{n}),

recalling that
r=|S|
. So
2E_{n}
is the
(n+1)^{\mathrm{th}}
term of the sequence in (
1.2
), and
2E_{n}^{\prime}
is the
n^{\mathrm{th}}
term of the sequence in (
1.3
). This normalization and indexing prove convenient shortly.

Next, we make a simple but crucial observation about balls in
\Gamma
:

B_{n+1}\cap sB_{n+1}=B_{n}\cup sB_{n}\qquad(s\in S,\ n\geq 0).

(12.7)

To see this, first note that
|sg|=|g|\pm 1
for every group element
g
. Consequently, the two sides of (
12.7
) both contain
B_{n}
and are both contained in
B_{n+1}
. Finally, if
|g|=n+1
, then
g
lies in
sB_{n+1}
if and only if its reduced work begins with an
s
, hence if and only if it lies in
sB_{n}
.

As a result of (
12.7
), we have the alternative expression

E_{n}=H(B_{n+1})-\sum_{s\in S}H(B_{n}\cup sB_{n}).

(12.8)

We use both (
12.6
) and (
12.8
) below.

Lemma 12.11
.

We have
E_{n+1}^{\prime}\leq E_{n}\leq E_{n}^{\prime}
for all
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

Step 1.
Writing
E_{n}
as in (
12.6
), we have

\displaystyle E_{n}-E_{n+1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2rH(B_{n+1})-\sum_{S\in S}\big(H(B_{n+1}\cap sB_{n+1})+H(B_{n+1}\cup sB_{n+1})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in S}\big(2H(B_{n+1})-H(B_{n+1}\cap sB_{n+1})-H(B_{n+1}\cup sB_{n+1})\big).

By the strong subadditivity inequality (
10.3
) and translation invariance, we have

H(B_{n+1}\cap sB_{n+1})+H(B_{n+1}\cup sB_{n+1})\leq H(B_{n+1})+H(sB_{n+1})=2H(B_{n+1}).

so
E_{n}-E_{n+1}^{\prime}
is a sum of non-negative terms.

Step 2.
Writing
E_{n}
as in (
12.8
), we have

E_{n}^{\prime}-E_{n}=2\sum_{s\in S}H(B_{n}\cup sB_{n})-H(B_{n+1})-(2r-1)\cdot H(B_{n}).

The sphere
S_{n+1}
is the disjoint union of
sB_{n}\setminus B_{n}
as
s
ranges over
S\cup S^{-1}
. Therefore conditioning and applying the subadditivity from Corollary
10.15
gives

H(S_{n+1}\mid B_{n})\leq\sum_{s\in S\cup S^{-1}}H(B_{n}\setminus sB_{n}\mid B_{n}).

(12.9)

Adding
2rH(B_{n})
and using the chain rule (Proposition
10.9
), this becomes

H(B_{n+1})+(2r-1)\cdot H(B_{n})\leq\sum_{s\in S\cup S^{-1}}H(B_{n}\cup sB_{n})=2\sum_{s\in S}H(B_{n}\cup sB_{n}),

where the second equality holds by translation-invariance. Re-arranging, this asserts that
E_{n}^{\prime}-E_{n}
is non-negative.
∎

Remark 12.12
.

The inequalities above have some overlap with those in
[
159
, Section 3]
(in the special case in which the sigma-subalgebra ‘
\Sigma
’ of that paper is trivial). That work largely inspired the proofs in this section. To be specific, (
12.9
) is essentially the same as the first inequality in
[
159
, Lemma 3.1]
, and its use to prove that
E_{n}\leq E_{n}^{\prime}
is roughly the same as in the first paragraph of the proof of
[
159
, Lemma 3.2]
. However, beyond that our routes diverge. The analog of the second inequality in
[
159
, Lemma 3.2]
does not appear in the proof above. In the notation above, the second paragraph of the proof of
[
159
, Lemma 3.2]
essentially uses the second inequality from
[
159
, Lemma 3.1]
to show that
E_{n+1}^{\prime}\leq(E_{n}+E_{n}^{\prime})/2
, rather than showing
E_{n+1}^{\prime}\leq E_{n}
as we have.
\lhd

Proof of formula (
1.3
) of Theorem
A
.

Formula (
1.2
) shows that
2E_{n}
converges to
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)
, and
2E_{n}^{\prime}
must converge to the same limit by Lemma
12.11
.
∎

Problem 12.13
.

When
\varphi
is unital, Theorem
B
says that the value
2E_{n}
gives the rate function in the large deviations principle obeyed by the random element

(\Phi^{\pi}_{V})[B_{n}]\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(B_{n}).

The values
2E_{n}^{\prime}
should have an analogous interpretation, but this time for the random
S
-tuples

\big((\Phi^{\pi}_{V})[B_{n}\cup sB_{n}]:\ s\in S\big)

(12.10)

taking values in the space

\Big\{(Q_{s}:\ s\in S)\in\prod_{s\in S}\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(B_{n}\cup sB_{n}):\ (Q_{s})[B_{n}]\ \hbox{is the same for all $s$}\Big\}.

If this is true, then both inequalities in Lemma
12.11
become instances of the contraction principle.

I believe one can prove this along the same lines as Theorem
B
without any really new ideas, but working with the the tuples in (
12.10
) is sure to be more complicated than working with
(\Phi^{\pi}_{V})[B_{n}
alone.

Lemma
12.11
implies that the sums
E_{n}+E_{n}^{\prime}
also converge to
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)
. This is significant, because these actually take a form that is arguably simpler than either sequence individually. From (
12.8
), we obtain

E_{n}+E_{n}^{\prime}=H(B_{n+1})-(2r-1)\cdot H(B_{n}).

(12.11)

All the terms that involve shifting balls by individual generators have canceled out. This sequence of averages is still non-increasing, since both
E_{n}
and
E_{n}^{\prime}
have this property. Starting from (
12.11
), we can now derive a non-negative series expansion for
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)
. It is crucial during our proof of Theorem
C
below.

Let
g_{n}=e
,
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
, …be any length-lexicographic ordering of
\Gamma
as described in Section
9.1
, and for each positive integer
N
let

P(g_{N}):=\{g_{0},g_{1},\dots,g_{N-1}\}:

that is, the set of predecessors of
g_{N}
in this ordering. Observe that

P(g_{N})=B_{n}\quad\hbox{when}\ N=|B_{n}|.

Corollary 12.14
.

If
\varphi:\Gamma\to\mathrm{M}_{k}
is positive definite, then
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)
is equal to

2H(B_{0})-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{n+1}}\big(H(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}\,|\,P(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}))\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-H(s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}\mid P(s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}))\big).

(12.12)

This is the analog of
[
159
, Theorem 1.7]
, one of the key technical innovations of that paper. For this reason we call it the
Seward expansion
of
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)
corresponding to the given ordering of
\Gamma
. (Our expression appears different from Seward’s by a factor of
2
, but this is just a consequence of our normalization choices.)

Proof.

For any
n\geq 0
, iterating the chain rule from Proposition
10.9
gives

H(S_{n+1}\mid B_{n})=\sum_{s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{n+1}}H(s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}\mid P(s_{n+1}\dots s_{1})).

When
n=0
, we combine this with (
12.11
) to obtain

\displaystyle E_{0}+E_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=H(B_{1})-(2r-1)\cdot H(B_{0})

\displaystyle=2H(B_{0})+H(S_{1}\mid B_{0})-2r\cdot H(B_{0})

\displaystyle=2H(B_{0})-\sum_{s\in S_{1}}\big(H(B_{0})-H(s\mid P(s))\big).

Similarly, for
n\geq 1
the increments of the sequence in (
12.11
) satisfy

\displaystyle(E_{n}+E^{\prime}_{n})-(E_{n-1}+E_{n-1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=H(S_{n+1}\mid B_{n})-(2r-1)\cdot H(S_{n}\mid B_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{n+1}}H(s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}\mid P(s_{n+1}\dots s_{1}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-(2r-1)\cdot\sum_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{n}}H(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}\mid P(s_{n}\dots s_{1})).

Since every reduced word in
S_{n}
has
(2r-1)
neighbours in
S_{n+1}
, the difference above is equal to

\sum_{s_{n+1}\dots s_{1}\in S_{n+1}}\big(H(s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}\,|\,P(s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}))-H(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}\mid P(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}))\big).

So (
12.12
) is the infinite series of increments of the sequence (
12.11
), and so this series converges to the same limit as that sequence.
∎

Remark 12.15
.

The argument above actually proves (
12.12
) for any length-first enumeration of
\Gamma
, not just length-lexicographic orderings. But our use of this formula in Chapter
13
does need some additional properties that are special to length-lexicographic orderings, so we restrict our attention to this case.

Nevertheless, we still obtain a different length-lexicographic ordering of
\Gamma
from any total ordering of
S\cup S^{-1}
. Different orderings give different formulas for
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
. Translating directly between one of the resulting Seward expansions and the other seems complicated, and becomes more so as
|g|\to\infty
. The flexibility to choose our ordering of
S\cup S^{-1}
is also important in our proof of Theorem
C
below: see Corollary
13.16

\lhd

Problem 12.16
.

With some re-arranging, the terms of a Seward expansion can also be written as log-determinant conditional mutual informations (see (
13.11
) below). Is there a simple description of the contraction matrices associated to those terms? Do they offer another parametrization of positive definite functions on free groups?

Problem 12.17
.

In
[
10
]
, Bakonyi and Timotin provide a different way of extending a partial positive definite function on
\Gamma
. They also proceed along a sequence of larger and larger finite subsets of
\Gamma
, but of a different kind. Their work also introduces its own analog of ‘Verblunsky coefficients’ over free groups. What is the joint distribution of their coefficients for a uniform random representation? Can those coefficients be used to give another expression for
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
?

Problem 12.18
.

In
[
159
]
, Seward remarks on how the vanishing of all but finitely many terms in his expansion characterizes the special classes of tree-indexed Markov process or their generalization to larger steps (see
[
29
]
or
[
158
]
). Presumably the analogous story is available here as well. Markov processes should correspond to Haagerup functions or their matrix generalizations (Example
9.8
). More generally, the higher-step Markov property should correspond to central extensions of partial positive definite functions on balls (see
[
10
, Section 5]
). This correspondence is already remarked by Burton and Juschenko in
[
41
]
. It complies with our general rule of thumb that conditional independence in probability corresponds to relative orthogonality in linear algebra.

12.4

Another look at the three-entropy formula

Now let
\varphi\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{u}}_{k}(\Gamma)
, let
g_{0}
,
g_{1}
, …be a grounded enumeration of
\Gamma
, and let
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
, …be the resulting Verblunsky coefficients for
\varphi
. Then Corollary
12.3
and Theorem
7.2
combine to give

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{H}(I_{k}-C_{n}^{\ast}C_{n})=\mathrm{h}^{0}(\pi_{\varphi})+\log\Delta\varphi_{\mathrm{ac}},

(12.13)

where
\Delta
is the Fuglede–Kadison determinant defined by
\chi_{\mathrm{reg}}
.

If
\Gamma=\mathbf{Z}
, then all positive definite functions are tempered, so
\mathrm{h}^{0}
is identically zero. Then the remaining terms in (
12.13
) are precisely Verblunsky’s form of Szegő’s limit theorem for determinants when
k=1
(see
[
162
, Theorem 2.3.1]
), or its matrix extension when
k>1
(see
[
162
, Theorem 2.13.5]
). These classical theorems have many different proofs. Among them, the proof via large deviations is a relatively recent discovery of Gamboa, Nagel and Rouault
[
82
]
; see also the survey
[
33
]
, which gives references to subsequent refinements and variants on this work by those same three authors. Our work here generalizes these results, and that large-deviations proof, to finitely generated free groups.

When
\Gamma
has at least two generators, it is not amenable, and
\mathrm{h}^{0}
may be nonzero. We thus discover that the free-groups generalization of Verblunsky’s form of Szegő’s theorem has an additional term. This term vanishes if
\varphi
is tempered. However, it turns out to be strictly negative if
\varphi
is not tempered because of Theorem
C
. In a sense, it ‘feels’ how far
\varphi
is from being tempered.

We can look at (
12.13
) another way. Let
a\in C^{\ast}\Gamma
, let
\lambda
be the regular representation with its usual tracial vector
\xi
, and let
\varphi:=\Phi^{\pi}_{\lambda(a)\xi}
. This is tempered by construction. In this case
k=1
, so the resulting Verblunsky coefficients are single vectors
c_{n}
, and (
12.13
) turns into

\log\Delta|a|=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\log(1-|c_{n}|^{2}).

(12.14)

As far as I know, this is a new formula for the regular Fuglede–Kadison determinant of any element of
C^{\ast}\Gamma
. Its utility is unclear, since estimating the quantities
|c_{n}|
may be difficult for most choices of
a
.

Problem 12.19
.

Give a direct derivation of (
12.14
) without going through
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
and
\mathrm{h}^{0}
.

Problem 12.20
.

Referring to the notation of (
12.13
), do any other simple properties of the sequence
(C_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of Verblunsky coefficients correspond to natural properties of the positive definite function
\varphi
itself?

In the classical case
\Gamma=\mathbf{Z}
, several chapters in
[
162
,
163
]
are devoted to connections of this kind requested in Problem
12.20
.

Chapter 13

The temperedness theorem

The main point of Theorem
C
is that the zeroth-order entropy of a representation vanishes if and only if that representation is approximately contained in the regular representation.

The proof of this occupies the first four sections below. In Section
13.1
we reduce our work to the study of cyclic representations. We also digress briefly to show how our proof of Theorem
C
gives a new proof of the Collins–Male theorem. In Section
13.2
we recall Haagerup’s classic characterization of tempered positive definite functions on free groups, and derive some other consequences of the specific estimates that appear in Haagerup’s work. Section
13.3
looks more closely at Seward expansions of the functional
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
: the main results are combinatorial properties of the various subsets of
\Gamma
that appear in such an expansion. Finally, Section
13.4
combines these preliminaries into the full proof of Theorem
C
. Once the proof is complete, we
offer some reflections on it in Section
13.5
.

The remainder of the chapter derives some consequences of Theorem
C
. The main ones are a large deviations principle for the random set-sequences
\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{n})
in the Vietoris topology of
\mathcal{Z}\Gamma
(Theorem
13.21
), and some results on the structure of representations with finite zeroth-order entropy.

In the rest of this chapter,
\Gamma
is a free group of rank
r
,
\varphi
is a
\mathbf{C}
-valued positive definite function on
\Gamma
, and we abbreviate
\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}
to
\tau
.

13.1

Reduction to the cyclic case

The heart of Theorem
C
is the following.

Theorem 13.1
.

If
\varphi
is not tempered, then
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)<0
.

Proof of Theorem
C
from Theorem
13.1
.

One implication already appears in Corollary
4.8
, so it remains to prove the reverse.

Suppose that
\pi
is any separable representation satisfying
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\pi)=0
. Writing it as a direct sum of cyclic representations, these must also all have
\mathrm{h}^{0}
equal to
0
by Proposition
8.4
. Then each cyclic representation is equivalent to the GNS representation of some positive definite function, so Theorem
13.1
shows that all those direct summands are approximately contained in the regular representation. Now the same follows for their sum
\pi
because
\lambda\simeq_{\mathrm{a}}\lambda^{\oplus\infty}
: see Example
2.25
and Corollary
3.9
.
∎

It remains to prove Theorem
13.1
. By Lemma
7.3
, it is equivalent to prove that

\inf_{t>0}\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\tau+t\varphi)<0.

(13.1)

This inequality is the main goal of the next three sections.

Before leaving this section, let us return to the Collins–Male theorem (Theorem
5.3
). Although we have mentioned this theorem during several earlier passages, none of the main results proved so far for annealed or zeroth-order AP entropy depend on this theorem, so we may use them to give a new proof of it.

New proof of the Collins–Male theorem from Theorem
C
.

Let
a\in C^{\ast}\Gamma
, and for
c>0
let
S_{c}
and
I(c)
be the set and exponent introduced in Proposition
6.6
, respectively. In view of that proposition, it suffices to prove that
I(c)<0
for every
c>\|\lambda(a)\|
. However, Theorem
C
shows that
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)<0
for every individual
\varphi\in S_{c}
, and now the negativity of
I(c)
follows because
S_{c}
is compact and
\mathrm{h}^{0}
is upper semicontinuous (Lemma
5.10
).
∎

Having re-proved the Collins–Male theorem, I expect one can quickly give a new proof of the Haagerup–Thorbjørnsen theorem by reversing the spectral-calculus steps in Collins and Male’s original proof in
[
51
]
. But I have not checked this carefully.

13.2

Some preliminaries on temperedness

For a free group
\Gamma
, Haagerup introduced in
[
94
]
some fundamental
\ell^{2}
-norm estimates for convolutions in
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
, and used them to deduce structural results about the reduced C
∗
-algebra and Fourier algebras of
\Gamma
and also a characterization of its tempered positive definite functions. That characterization is the basic ingredient for the work of this chapter, along with some other consequences of Haagerup’s original inequalities. This background is also recounted in
[
75
, Section 2.1]
.

As in Section
9.1
, we write
B_{n}
and
S_{n}
for the closed ball and sphere of radius
n
around
e
in the left Cayley graph of
\Gamma
, respectively. Haagerup’s characterization is the following.

Proposition 13.2

(
[
94
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

A positive definite function
\varphi
is tempered if and only if

\sqrt{\sum_{g\in S_{n}}|\varphi(g)|^{2}}=e^{o(n)}\quad\hbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

∎

Recall from Example
2.25
that, for the left regular representation of a countable group, approximate association and weak association agree, and so one can define ‘tempered’ using either.

Remark 13.3
.

Proposition
13.2
has been generalized to groups with length functions that satisfy ‘property RD’, which has also found other applications
[
105
,
106
]
.

For a positive definite function on any locally compact group, a superficially similar sufficient condition for temperedness is ‘almost square integrability’
[
57
]
. It generalizes the older study of ‘square integrable representations’ initiated by Godement
[
89
]
: see also
[
62
, Chapter 14]
. However, I have not found a way to use almost square integrability in the work of this chapter.
\lhd

Problem 13.4
.

Proposition
13.2
characterizes the temperedness of
\varphi
in terms of a growth condition on the values it takes. Is there a tractable characterization of temperedness in terms of generalized Verblunsky coefficients instead? Such a characterization could possibly make our work in this chapter much easier.

In the rest of this chapter, we write
\|\cdot\|_{2}
for the norm of
\ell^{2}(\Gamma)
. Thus, the left-hand side of the condition in Proposition
13.2
is equal to
\|\varphi 1_{S_{n}}\|_{2}
.

Haagerup’s original proof gives more: if
\varphi
is tempered then in fact

\|\varphi 1_{S_{n}}\|_{2}=O(n)\quad\hbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

Thus, here is a ‘gap’ in the possible growth-types of these sums over spheres for positive definite functions: they are at most linear if tempered, and roughly exponential if not. In the present work we need only the cruder characterization via subexponential growth as in Proposition
13.2
.

Remark 13.5
.

For a finitely generated free group
\Gamma
, another elegant characterization of those representations
\pi
that are approximately equivalent to the regular representation is the following: this holds if and only if
\pi
can be enlarged to a representation that extends to the whole algebra
{\Gamma\ltimes C(\partial\Gamma)}
. Sufficiency of this condition is shown in
[
115
]
, and then for necessity see
[
98
, Proposition 2.2]
. I do not know a way to connect that property to the work of this chapter. It could be very interesting to find one.
\lhd

Haagerup’s proof of Proposition
13.2
uses the following inequality.

Lemma 13.6

(
[
94
, Lemma 1.3]
)
.

If
a
and
b
are elements of
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
that are supported on
S_{n}
and
S_{m}
, respectively, then

\|(a\ast b)1_{S_{k}}\|_{2}\leq\|a\|_{2}\|b\|_{2}

when

|n-m|\leq k\leq n+m\quad\hbox{and}\quad k=n+m\mod 2.

The left-hand side is zero for all other
k
. ∎

We need this inequality itself via the following consequence.

Corollary 13.7
.

If
a
and
b
are elements of
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
that are supported on
B_{n}
and
B_{m}
, respectively, then

\|(a\ast b)1_{S_{k}}\|_{2}\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\|a\|_{2}\|b\|_{2}&\quad k=0\\
(n+m+1-k)^{2}\|a\|_{2}\|b\|_{2}&\quad 1\leq k\leq n+m\\
0&\quad k>n+m.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

First,
S_{0}=\{e\}
, and

(a\ast b)_{e}=\sum_{h}a_{h}b_{h^{-1}}.

This has absolute value at most
\|a\|_{2}\|b\|_{2}
by the CBS inequality.

Now suppose that
k\geq 1
. Decomposing over spheres of different radii, the triangle inequality gives

\|(a\ast b)1_{S_{k}}\|_{2}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\|((a1_{S_{j}})\ast(b1_{S_{i}}))1_{S_{k}}\|_{2}.

By Lemma
13.6
, for each
i
and
j
the summand here is zero unless

i+j\geq k\quad\hbox{and}\quad|i-j|\leq k

(ignoring the additional parity constraint). The number of terms that satisfy these inequalities is bounded above by
(n+m+1-k)^{2}
, and for these terms Lemma
13.6
gives the upper bound
\|a1_{S_{i}}\|_{2}\|b1_{S_{j}}\|_{2}
, which is at most
\|a\|_{2}\|b\|_{2}
.
∎

Remark 13.8
.

The use of the triangle inequality in the proof above is very crude, and the factor
(n+m+1-k)^{2}
can be improved with more care. Haagerup’s proof of
[
94
, Lemma 1.4]
achieves something like this. Such an improvement here could tighten some estimates later, but it would not have any conceptual consequences.
\lhd

Lemma
13.6
implies estimates for inner products in the GNS space of a given positive definite function. We present two versions of these in Lemma
13.9
and Corollary
13.10
below, but we prepare some more notation first.

Consider again our positive definite function
\varphi
, and now assume that it is normalized. During the proof of Theorem
C
, the norms around spheres
\|\varphi 1_{S_{n}}\|_{2}
appear both because of Proposition
13.2
and also as a result of another calculation inside the proof of Proposition
13.17
below. For technical reasons, spheres of even and odd radii occur slightly differently in the proof, and this makes it convenient to collect spheres into consecutive pairs. Thus, to lighten notation, let us write

w(n):=\|\varphi 1_{S_{2n-1}}\|_{2}+\|\varphi 1_{S_{2n}}\|_{2}\qquad(n\geq 1).

(13.2)

Now suppose that
\varphi
is associated to its GNS representation
\pi
by the cyclic vector
z
, and let
H:=H_{\pi}
. In addition, define

L:\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]\to H:a\mapsto\pi(a)z.

(13.3)

In the usual form of the GNS construction for a group
[
77
, equation (3.17)]
, the relationship between
\varphi
,
\pi
,
z
and
L
is given by

\langle La,Lb\rangle=\sum_{g,h}a_{g}\overline{b_{h}}\varphi(h^{-1}g)=\varphi(b^{\ast}\ast a)\qquad(a,b\in\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]).

(13.4)

Lemma 13.9
.

Assume that
\varphi
is normalized. Let
a
and
b
be elements of
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
, and suppose they are supported on
gB_{2n-i}
and
gB_{i}
respectively for some
g\in\Gamma
and some integers
n\geq 1
and
0\leq i\leq 2n
. Then

|\langle La,Lb\rangle|\leq W(n)\|a\|_{2}\|b\|_{2},

where

W(n):=1+4\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(n+1-\ell)^{2}w(\ell)\qquad(n\geq 1)

(13.5)

with
w(\ell)
as in (
13.2
).

Proof.

First, since

\langle La,Lb\rangle=\langle\pi(g)^{-1}La,\pi(g)^{-1}Lb\rangle=\langle L(\delta_{g^{-1}}\ast a),L(\delta_{g^{-1}}\ast b)\rangle,

we can assume without loss of generality that
g=e
.

Applying the triangle inequality and then the CBS inequality to (
13.4
) gives

|\langle La,Lb\rangle|=|\varphi(b^{\ast}\ast a)|\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}|\varphi((b^{\ast}\ast a)1_{S_{k}})|\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|(b^{\ast}\ast a)1_{S_{k}}\|_{2}\|\varphi 1_{S_{k}}\|_{2}.

Substituting the bound from Corollary
13.7
, and noting that
\|\varphi 1_{S_{0}}\|_{2}=|\varphi(e)|=1
, we deduce that

|\langle La,Lb\rangle|\leq\Big(1+\sum_{k=1}^{2n}(2n+1-k)^{2}\|\varphi 1_{S_{k}}\|_{2}\Big)\|a\|_{2}\|b\|_{2}.

(13.6)

To finish, we bound the coefficient in parentheses in (
13.6
) like this:

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{k=1}^{2n}(2n+1-k)^{2}\|\varphi 1_{S_{k}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\big((2n+1-(2\ell-1))^{2}\|\varphi 1_{S_{2\ell-1}}\|_{2}+(2n+1-2\ell)^{2}\|\varphi 1_{S_{2\ell}}\|_{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 1+4\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(n+1-\ell)^{2}(\|\varphi 1_{S_{2\ell-1}}\|_{2}+\|\varphi 1_{S_{2\ell}}\|_{2}).

This equals
W(n)
from (
13.5
).
∎

We apply Lemma
13.9
later via the following asymmetrical corollary of it.

Corollary 13.10
.

Assume that
\varphi
is normalized and satisfies
\varphi\geq c\tau
in the ordering of positive functionals for some
c>0
. For some
g\in\Gamma
and some integers
n\geq 1
and
0\leq i\leq 2n
, let
x
be an element of
L[\mathbf{C}^{gB_{2n-i}}]
, and let
b
be an element of
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
supported on
gB_{i}
. Then

|\langle x,Lb\rangle|\leq\frac{W(n)}{\sqrt{c}}\|x\|\|b\|_{2},

where
W(n)
is as in (
13.5
).

Proof.

Suppose that
x=La
with
a\in\mathbf{C}^{gB_{2n-i}}
. Our assumption on
\varphi
implies that

\|x\|^{2}=\|La\|^{2}=\varphi(a^{\ast}\ast a)\geq c\tau(a^{\ast}\ast a)=c\|a\|_{2}^{2}.

Use this to bound the factor of
\|a\|_{2}
in the conclusion of Lemma
13.9
.
∎

In our proof of Theorem
C
, a key role is played by the fact that, if
\varphi
is not tempered, then the quantities
w(n)
from (
13.2
) and
W(n)
from (
13.5
) are comparable in size for infinitely many values of
n
. This is Corollary
13.12
below. We deduce it from Haagerup’s characterization (Proposition
13.2
) and a variant of Gronwall’s lemma. (Gronwall’s original lemma is for functions of a continuous variable, and is a basic tool for studying partial differential equations: see, for instance,
[
69
, Appendix B.2.j]
. It has many discrete variants in the literature, but I have not found one that covers precisely what we need.)

Lemma 13.11

(Discrete Gronwall-type lemma)
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that the following holds. Let
v(1)
,
v(2)
, …be non-negative real numbers, and define

V(n):=1+4\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}(n+1-\ell)^{2}v(\ell)\qquad(n\geq 1)

(beware: this sum stops at
n-1
, whereas the sum in (
13.5
) stops at
n
). Let
A\geq 1
, and assume further that

[\quad\hbox{either}\quad v(n)\leq Ae^{\varepsilon n}\quad\hbox{or}\quad v(n)\leq\delta V(n)\quad]

(13.7)

for every
n\geq 1
. Then in fact
v(n)\leq Ae^{\varepsilon n}
for every
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

In anticipation of the rest of the proof, let

\delta:=\Big(1+\frac{4e^{2\varepsilon}(1+e^{-\varepsilon})}{(1-e^{-\varepsilon})^{3}}\Big)^{-1}.

(13.8)

The proof is by induction on
n
. To begin, observe from the definitions that
\delta V(1)=\delta<1
, whereas
Ae^{\varepsilon}>1
, so the second inequality in (
13.7
) is stronger than the first when
n=1
.

Now suppose that the desired bound is known up to some value of
n
, and consider assumption (
13.7
) at the argument
n+1
. If the first inequality in (
13.7
) holds at
n+1
, then there is nothing to prove. If the second inequality in (
13.7
) holds at
n+1
, then we combine it with the inductive hypothesis like this:

\displaystyle v(n+1)

\displaystyle\leq\delta+4\delta A\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(n+2-\ell)^{2}e^{\varepsilon\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\delta+4\delta Ae^{2\varepsilon+\varepsilon n}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}e^{-\varepsilon k}

\displaystyle\leq\delta A\Big(1+4e^{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}e^{-\varepsilon k}\Big)e^{\varepsilon(n+1)}.

Using standard formulas for the term-by-term derivatives of a geometric series, this last expression is equal to

\delta A\Big(1+\frac{4e^{2\varepsilon}(1+e^{-\varepsilon})}{(1-e^{-\varepsilon})^{3}}\Big)e^{\varepsilon(n+1)}.

By our choice in (
13.8
), this is at most
Ae^{\varepsilon(n+1)}
, so the induction continues.
∎

Corollary 13.12
.

Assume that
\varphi
is normalized, and define
w(n)
and
W(n)
as in (
13.2
) and (
13.5
), respectively. If
\varphi
is not tempered then there are positive integers
n_{1}<n_{2}<\cdots
such that

\log w(n_{i})\gtrsim n_{i}\quad\hbox{and}\quad w(n_{i})\simeq W(n_{i})

as
i\to\infty
(where the implied constants depend on
\varphi
).

Proof.

We assume the conclusion fails for some
\varphi
and prove that
\varphi
is tempered. Let
\varepsilon>0
, and let
\delta>0
be given by Lemma
13.11
for this choice of
\varepsilon
. By our assumption on
\varphi
, we must have

\hbox{either}\quad w(n)\leq e^{\varepsilon n}\quad\hbox{or}\quad w(n)\leq\frac{\delta}{1+4\delta}W(n)

for all but finitely many
n
. Hence, for some
A\geq 1
, we have

\hbox{either}\quad w(n)\leq Ae^{\varepsilon n}\quad\hbox{or}\quad w(n)\leq\frac{\delta}{1+4\delta}W(n)

for all
n\geq 1
. Also, the second of these possibilities re-arranges into

w(n)\leq\delta\Big(1+4\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}(n+1-\ell)^{2}w(\ell)\Big)

(that is, we re-arrange to remove the term with
\ell=n
from (
13.5
)).

We now have the conditions to apply Lemma
13.11
with
v(n):=w(n)
. It shows that
w(n)\leq Ae^{\varepsilon n}
for all
n
. Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, this implies that
\varphi
is tempered, by Proposition
13.2
.
∎

13.3

Manipulations with Seward expansions

Let
\varphi\in\Sigma_{1}(\Gamma)
, and let it be associated to a representation
\pi
by a cyclic vector
z
. Pick a total order on
S\cup S^{-1}
, denote it by ‘
<
’, and extend it to the resulting length-lexicographic ordering of
\Gamma
, as described in Section
9.1
(so
e
is the first element in this ordering). Having fixed this ordering, write
P(g)
for the set of predecessors of any group element
g
. This includes setting
P(e):=\emptyset
.

This ordering determines a Seward expansion of
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)
as in Corollary
12.14
. Since
\varphi(e)=1
, the first term of that expansion vanishes. By translation invariance, the remaining terms may be written using the quantities

\mathrm{H}_{\varphi}(e\mid(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})^{-1}P(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}))

for all reduced words
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}
. To express these quantities more succinctly, let

Q(g):=g^{-1}P(g)\qquad(g\in\Gamma).

(13.9)

The estimates that appear during the proof of Theorem
13.1
depend on some basic combinatorial properties of these sets.

First, since
P(g)
does not contain
g
itself,
Q(g)
does not contain
e
. Also, since

B_{|g|-1}\subset P(g)\subset B_{|g|},

we have

g^{-1}B_{|g|-1}\subset Q(g)\subset g^{-1}B_{|g|}.

Beware: the left translate of a ball by
g^{-1}
is still a ball in the
right
Cayley graph, but not in the
left
Cayley graph, which is the one that concerns us here. Inversion switches the two Cayley graphs, so if desired one could visualize
Q(g)^{-1}
as roughly a closed ball of radius
|g|
around
g
, except that it does not include the whole interior boundary of that ball.

The next three results describe relations among the sets
Q(g)
.

Lemma 13.13
.

Every reduced word
s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}
with
n\geq 0
satisfies

Q(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})\subset Q(s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}).

(13.10)

This inclusion is also central to the manipulations in
[
159
]
: see, for instance, the paragraph preceding
[
159
, Theorem 3.5]
.

Proof.

Translating on the left by
s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}
, it is equivalent to prove that

s_{n+1}P(s_{n}\dots s_{1})\subset P(s_{n+1}\cdots s_{1}).

This holds by the definition of the length-lexicographic ordering.
∎

With Lemma
13.13
in mind, we also define

C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}):=Q(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})\setminus Q(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})

for any nonempty reduced word
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}
. We refer to
C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})
as the
crescent of
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}
, motivated by the description of it in the next lemma. To motivate our interest in these, notice that the conditional chain rule (Proposition
10.18
) lets us re-arrange the Seward expansion from Corollary
12.14
into the following

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)=-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{n}}\mathrm{I}_{\varphi}(e\,;\,C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})\mid Q(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})).

(13.11)

In the end we do not use this formula directly in our work below; instead, the original expression in Corollary
12.14
is manipulated into a lower bound with a somewhat simpler form, and then crescents enter into our analysis of that quantity.

Lemma 13.14
.

Let
n\geq 1
, let
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}
be a reduced word, and let
C:=C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})
. Then

C\subset S_{2n-2}\cup S_{2n-1}\cup S_{2n}.

Moreover:

•

C\cap S_{2n-2}
is empty if either
n=1
or
s_{n-1}>s_{n}^{-1}
, and if
s_{n-1}<s_{n}^{-1}
then

C\cap S_{2n-2}=\big\{t_{2n-2}\cdots t_{1}:\ \hbox{$s_{1}^{-1}\cdots s_{n}^{-1}$ is a prefix of $t_{2n-2}\cdots t_{1}$}\big\};

•

we have

C\cap S_{2n-1}=\big\{t_{2n-1}\cdots t_{1}:\ \hbox{$s_{1}^{-1}\cdots s_{n}^{-1}$ is a prefix of $t_{2n-1}\cdots t_{1}$}\big\};

•

we have

C\cap S_{2n}=\big\{t_{2n}\cdots t_{1}:\ \hbox{$s_{1}^{-1}\cdots s_{n}^{-1}$ is a prefix of $t_{2n}\cdots t_{1}$ and $t_{n}<s_{n}$}\big\}.

Proof.

We first describe the set

P(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})\setminus s_{n}P(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})=\big\{h\in P(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}):\ s_{n}^{-1}h\not\in P(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})\big\}.

Suppose that
h
lies in this set and has reduced word
t_{m}\cdots t_{1}
. In the first place this implies
m\leq n
. It also implies
m\geq n-2
, for otherwise
s_{n}^{-1}h
would have length at most
n-2
and hence lie in
P(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})
.

Now consider the three possible values for
m
in turn:

•

If
m=n-2
, then since
s_{n}^{-1}h\not\in P(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})
we must have that
s_{n}\neq t_{n-2}
and
s_{n}^{-1}>s_{n-1}
, and these conditions are also sufficient.

•

If
m=n-1
, then since
s_{n}^{-1}h\not\in P(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})
we must have that
s_{n}\neq t_{n-1}
, and this is also sufficient for then
|s_{n}^{-1}h|=n
.

•

If
m=n
, then since
s_{n}^{-1}h\not\in P(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})
we must have either
s_{n}\neq t_{n}
, or
s_{n}=t_{n}
but
t_{n-1}>s_{n-1}
, and either of these possibilities is sufficient.

Translating by
s_{1}^{-1}\cdots s_{n}^{-1}
, these cases turn into the three intersections listed.
∎

Corollary 13.15
.

As
g
ranges over
\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
, the crescents
C(g)
are pairwise disjoint and their union is
\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
.

Proof.

None of the sets
Q(g)
contains
e
, so nor do any of the crescents.

On the other hand, if
t_{m}\cdots t_{1}
is a nonempty reduced word in
S\cup S^{-1}
, then it lies in the crescent
C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})
if and only if it lies in one of the three sets listed in Lemma
13.14
. There is exactly one choice of
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}
for which this is the case, determined as follows:

•

if
m=2p-1
, then
n=p
and
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}=(t_{2p-1}\cdots t_{p})^{-1}
;

•

if
m=2p
and
t_{p}<t_{p+1}^{-1}
, then
n=p
and
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}=(t_{2n}\cdots t_{n+1})^{-1}
;

•

if
m=2p
and
t_{p}>t_{p+1}^{-1}
, then
n=p+1
and
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}=(t_{2p}\cdots t_{p})^{-1}
.

∎

Finally, for
n\geq 1
, let us write

R_{n}:=\bigcup_{g\in S_{n}}C(g).

(13.12)

By Lemma
13.14
, this is the union of
S_{2n-1}
, some of
S_{2n-2}
, and some of
S_{2n}
. Its intersections with
S_{2n-2}
and
S_{2n}
depend on the original choice of ordering of
S_{1}
. In order to make contact with the estimates of the previous subsection, we need to be able to choose that ordering so that
R_{n}
sees ‘enough’ of those spheres, in the sense of the next lemma.

Corollary 13.16
.

Let
\varphi\in\Sigma_{1}(\Gamma)
. For every
n\geq 1
, there is a choice of ordering of
S_{1}
(which may depend on
\varphi
) such that, if
R_{n}
is the resulting union of crescents in (
13.12
), then

\|\varphi 1_{R_{n}}\|_{2}\geq\frac{w(n)}{2},

(13.13)

where
w(n)
is as in (
13.2
).

Proof.

Let
<
be a choice of ordering of
S_{1}
, and let
<^{\prime}
be its reverse: if
s,t\in S_{1}
are distinct, then

s<t\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad t<^{\prime}s.

Let
P^{\prime}
,
Q^{\prime}
,
C^{\prime}
, and
R^{\prime}
denote the analogs of
P
,
Q
,
C
and
R
constructed from
<^{\prime}
rather that
<
. Now observe that
R_{n}
always contains
S_{2n-1}
, and the third set in Lemma
13.14
gives that

R_{n}\cap S_{2n}=\{t_{2n}\cdots t_{1}\in S_{2n}:\ t_{n}<t_{n+1}^{-1}\}=S_{2n}\setminus R_{n}^{\prime}.

So
R_{n}\cup R_{n}^{\prime}
contains
S_{2n-1}\cup S_{2n}
, and therefore

\|\varphi 1_{R_{n}}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\varphi 1_{R^{\prime}_{n}}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\|\varphi 1_{S_{2n-1}\cup S_{2n}}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\varphi 1_{S_{2n-1}}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\varphi 1_{S_{2n}}\|_{2}^{2}.

Therefore, by the inequality of arithmetic and geometric means, at least one of the sets
R_{n}
and
R_{n}^{\prime}
must satisfy (
13.13
).
∎

Beware: the ordering found by this corollary depends on
n
. When we apply it during the proof of Proposition
13.17
later, we need it only for one value of
n
at a time.

13.4

Completed proof of Theorem
C

We have shown in Section
13.1
that Theorem
C
is implied by Theorem
13.1
, and that this in turn is implied by (
13.1
).

To prove (
13.1
), we need a lower bound on
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
in terms of quantities that are easier to estimate in terms of the individual values taken by
\varphi
. The next proposition accomplishes this (under a mild extra assumption) in terms of the sequences
w(n)
from (
13.2
) and
W(n)
from (
13.5
).

Proposition 13.17
.

If
\varphi
is normalized and
\varphi\geq c\tau
in the ordering of positive functionals for some
c>0
, then

-\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)=|\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)|\geq\sup_{n\geq 1}\frac{cw(n)^{2}}{8nW(n)^{2}}.

(13.14)

Before starting the proof of Proposition
13.17
, we show how it completes the main task of this chapter.

Proof of Theorem
13.1
given Proposition
13.17
.

Consider the mollified functions

\varphi_{s}:=\tau+s\varphi\qquad(s\geq 0).

These are not normalized, so define also

\psi_{t}:=(1-t)\tau+t\varphi\qquad(0\leq t\leq 1)

These are normalized, and we have
\varphi_{s}=(1+s)\psi_{t}
when
s=t/(1-t)
. Therefore Proposition
4.5
(b) gives

\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi_{s})=\log(1/(1-t))+\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\psi_{t})=O(t)+\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\psi_{t}).

(13.15)

Let
w_{t}(n)
and
W_{t}(n)
be the analogs of
w(n)
and
W(n)
defined with
\psi_{t}
in place of
\varphi
. The definition of
\psi_{t}
gives

w_{t}(n)=\sqrt{\sum_{g\in S_{2n-1}}|\psi_{t}(g)|^{2}}+\sqrt{\sum_{g\in S_{2n}}|\psi_{t}(g)|^{2}}=tw(n)\qquad(n\geq 1)

and

W_{t}(n)=1+4\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(n+1-\ell)^{2}w_{t}(\ell)=1-t+tW(n)\qquad(n\geq 1).

If
t\leq 1/2
, then
\psi_{t}\geq\tau/2
, and then Proposition
13.17
and the calculations above give

-\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\psi_{t})\geq\sup_{n\geq 1}\frac{w_{t}(n)^{2}}{16nW_{t}(n)^{2}}\geq\sup_{n\geq 1}\frac{t^{2}w(n)^{2}}{16n(1+tW(n))^{2}}.

(13.16)

If
\varphi
is not tempered, then by Corollary
13.12
we can choose positive integers
n_{1}<n_{2}<\dots
such that

\log w(n_{i})\gtrsim n_{i}\quad\hbox{and}\quad w(n_{i})\simeq W(n_{i}).

Let
t_{i}:=\min\{1/w(n_{i}),1/2\}
for each
i
. This is a sequence in
(0,1/2)
that converges to
0
, and our choices imply that

t_{i}w(n_{i})\simeq t_{i}W(n_{i})\simeq 1\quad\hbox{and}\quad n_{i}\simeq|\log t_{i}|.

Therefore (
13.16
) gives

-\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\psi_{t_{i}})\geq\frac{t_{i}^{2}w(n_{i})^{2}}{16n_{i}(1+t_{i}W(n_{i}))^{2}}\simeq\frac{1}{|\log t_{i}|}.

(13.17)

Letting
s_{i}:=t_{i}/(1-t_{i})
, and inserting this estimate into (
13.15
), we conclude that
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi_{s_{i}})
is strictly negative once
s_{i}
is sufficiently small.
∎

Remark 13.18
.

As the proof above shows, Proposition
13.17
is only just strong enough to imply (
13.1
). I believe this weakness is an artifact of the proof of Lemma
13.19
below. We discuss this issue further in Section
13.5
.
\lhd

We spend the rest of this section proving Proposition
13.17
. We deduce it from another, more technical inequality, which we present separately as Lemma
13.19
below.

To formulate this, consider again a total ordering ‘
<
’ of
S_{1}=S\cup S^{-1}
, and extend it to a lenth-lexicographic ordering of
\Gamma
. Assume that
\varphi\in\Sigma_{1}(\Gamma)
is associated to the representation
\pi
by the cyclic vector
z
, let
H:=H_{\pi}
, and let
L
be the linear map from
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
to
H
defined in (
13.3
). For each group element
g
, let

M(g):=L[\mathbf{C}^{Q(g)}]=\mathrm{span}(\pi(Q(g))z),

where
Q(g)
is as in (
13.9
). In particular,
M(e)=\{0\}
. Let
P_{M(g)}
be the orthogonal projection from
H
onto
M(g)
.

For each nonempty reduced word
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}
, these subspaces satisfy

M(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})\supset M(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1}),

and we can consider the difference of the orthogonal projections of
z
, say

y_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}:=(P_{M(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}-P_{M(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})})z.

(13.18)

Our more technical inequality concerns the squared lengths of these projections. It relates them to the
\ell^{2}
-norms of
\varphi
over the unions of crescents
R_{n}
defined in (
13.12
).

Lemma 13.19
.

If
\varphi\geq c\tau
, then the vectors above satisfy

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}}\|y_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{c}{nW(n)^{2}}\|\varphi 1_{R_{n}}\|_{2}^{2}

(13.19)

for every
n\geq 1
.

Proof of Proposition
13.17
from Lemma
13.19
.

Using the same length-lexicographic ordering as above, consider the resulting Seward expansion for
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)
from Corollary
12.14
.

By translation-invariance, it is a combination of the quantities

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{\varphi}(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1}\mid P(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1}))-\mathrm{H}_{\varphi}(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}\mid P(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}_{\varphi}(e\mid Q(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1}))-\mathrm{H}_{\varphi}(e\mid Q(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}))

\displaystyle=\log\|z-P_{M(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})}z\|-\log\|z-P_{M(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}z\|

(recall Example
10.8
for the second equality here). Since
z
is a unit vector, the Pythagorean identity turns this quantity into

\frac{1}{2}\big(\log(1-\|P_{M(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})}z\|^{2})-\log(1-\|P_{M(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}z\|^{2})\big).

Since
\log
has slope greater than
1
on
(0,1)
, this difference is at least

\frac{1}{2}\big(\|P_{M(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}z\|^{2}-\|P_{M(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})}z\|^{2}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\|y_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\|^{2},

where
y_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
is as in (
13.18
), using the Pythagorean identity again for the last equality.

Inserting this lower bound term-by-term into the Seward expansion, we have shown that

|\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{n}}\|y_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\|^{2}.

(13.20)

Since the terms on the right-hand side of (
13.20
) are non-negative, that inequality combines with Lemma
13.19
to give

|\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(\varphi)|\geq\sup_{n\geq 1}\frac{c}{2nW(n)^{2}}\|\varphi 1_{R_{n}}\|_{2}^{2}.

Finally, the left-hand side of this inequality does not depend on our chosen ordering of
S_{1}
, so we are free to optimize the right-hand side over this ordering. By Corollary
13.16
, doing so gives the inequality (
13.14
).
∎

It remains to prove Lemma
13.19
. This is the most demanding work of this section.

In order to relate the summands in (
13.19
) more directly to
\varphi
, we bound each norm
\|y_{s_{n}\dots s_{1}}\|
by picking another element of
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
as a ‘test vector’, applying
L
to it, and then applying Corollary
13.10
to the resulting inner products in
H
.

The choice of the test vectors is the really delicate aspect of the proof. To motivate it, let us first notice that, if we had

M(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})\ \perp\ \mathrm{span}(\pi(C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}))z)=L[\mathbf{C}^{C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}],

then
y_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
would equal the projection of
z
onto
L[\mathbf{C}^{C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}]
. While this orthogonality usually fails if
\varphi\neq\tau
, heuristically we can still imagine that
y_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
is ‘trying to stay close’ to
L[\mathbf{C}^{C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}]
, so it makes sense to test it against vectors from that subspace.

For example, consider the vector

a_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}:=\overline{\varphi}1_{C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}.

This is supported in
C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})
, and hence also in
Q(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})
. Intuitively,
La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
is a ‘first order’ approximation to the projection of
z
onto
L[\mathbf{C}^{C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}]
: for each individual
g\in C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})
, the vector
L(\overline{\varphi}(g)1_{\{g\}})
equals the projection of
z
onto the one-dimensional subspace
\mathbf{C}\cdot\pi(g)z
, and
La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
is the sum of these vectors. In addition, the image of
a_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
under
L
reflects its original length in
\ell^{2}(\Gamma)
through a simple special case of (
13.4
):

\langle La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}},z\rangle=\sum_{g\in C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}\overline{\varphi}(g)\varphi(g)=\|a_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}.

(13.21)

One could test each
y_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
against
La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
, but I have not been able to complete the proof using this idea alone. To see why, consider that

\displaystyle\langle y_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}},La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle P_{M(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\rangle-\langle P_{M(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})}z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\rangle-\langle P_{M(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})}z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\|a_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}-\langle P_{M(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1})}z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\rangle.

(13.22)

(The second of these equalities holds because
a_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
is supported in
Q(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})
and so
La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
lies in
M(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})
. The last equality is an application of (
13.21
).) In (
13.4
), the first term is a fairly simple function of
\varphi
. But I do not have a separate estimate for the second term which is precise enough to control the
difference
of these terms. Moreover, I expect that in some cases this difference is much smaller than either term separately. As a result, I have not been able to exert enough control over
\|y_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\|
by this approach.

To overcome this difficulty, we instead test each individual vector in (
13.20
) against a certain
sum
of vectors of the form
a_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
, carefully chosen so that the resulting inner products mostly cancel overall. We make this choice in (
13.23
) below. The remaining sum contains only a much smaller number of inner products of the form
\langle P_{M(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\rangle
, where both indexing words have length
n
, and we can then simplify all of these because of (
13.21
).

Here are the precise details.

Proof of Lemma
13.19
.

Step 1: choice of test vectors.
For any
i=0,1,\dots,n
and any
s_{i}\cdots s_{1}
, let

b_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{i+1}\cdots s_{n}\in S_{n-i}\\
s_{i}s_{i+1}\neq e\end{subarray}}a_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}=\overline{\varphi}\cdot 1_{\bigcup\{C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1}):\,s_{i+1}\cdots s_{n}\in S_{n-i},\,s_{i}s_{i+1}\neq e\}}.

(13.23)

(Notice that the terms of this sum are indexed by words of length
n
, even if
i
is smaller than
n
. This is necessary for the cancellation in Step 3 below.)

By Corollary
13.15
, for any such
i
we can decompose

\|\varphi 1_{R_{n}}\|^{2}_{2}=\sum_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}}\|b_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}.

Applying this identity and then the CBS inequality, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\cdot\|\varphi 1_{R_{n}}\|_{2}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}}\|y_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}\|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}}\|b_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}}\|y_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}}\|b_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}\|_{2}\cdot\|y_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}\|.

(13.24)

Step 2: comparing with inner products in
H
.
Fix a word
s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}
. The vector
y_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}
lies in
M(s_{i}\cdots s_{1})
, so it is the image under
L
of an element of
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
that is supported on
Q(s_{i}\cdots s_{1})\subset(s_{i}\cdots s_{1})^{-1}B_{i}
. On the other hand, the vector
b_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}
is itself an element of
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
, and is supported on the set

\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{i+1}\cdots s_{n}\in S_{n-i}\\
s_{i}s_{i+1}\neq e\end{subarray}}Q(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})\\
\subset\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{i+1}\cdots s_{n}\in S_{n-i}\\
s_{i}s_{i+1}\neq e\end{subarray}}(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})^{-1}B_{n}\subset(s_{i}\cdots s_{1})^{-1}B_{2n-i}.

Therefore, by Corollary
13.10
, the lower bound reached in (
13.4
) is at least

\frac{\sqrt{c}}{W(n)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}}|\langle y_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}},Lb_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}\rangle|\\
=\frac{\sqrt{c}}{W(n)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}}\Big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{i+1}\cdots s_{n}\in S_{n-i}\\
s_{i}s_{i+1}\neq e\end{subarray}}\big\langle(P_{M(s_{i}\dots s_{1})}-P_{M(s_{i-1}\dots s_{1})})z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\big\rangle\Big|,

where the second line simply unpacks the definitions of
y_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}
and
b_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}
.

Step 3: cancellation.
By the triangle inequality, the expression at the end of Step 2 is greater than or equal to

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{c}}{W(n)}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{i+1}\cdots s_{n}\in S_{n-i}\\
s_{i}s_{i+1}\neq e\end{subarray}}\big\langle(P_{M(s_{i}\dots s_{1})}-P_{M(s_{i-1}\dots s_{1})})z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\big\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{c}}{W(n)}\Big|\sum_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{n}}\Big\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}(P_{M(s_{i}\dots s_{1})}-P_{M(s_{i-1}\dots s_{1})})z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\Big\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{c}}{W(n)}\Big|\sum_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{n}}\langle P_{M(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\rangle\Big|,

where the last equality holds because
M(e)=\{0\}
.

Step 4: simplifying and re-arranging.
Since
La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}
lies in
M(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})
, we can omit the orthogonal projections
P_{M(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}
from the last line above. At that point, (
13.21
) gives

\sum_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{n}}\langle z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\rangle=\sum_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{n}}\|\varphi 1_{C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\varphi 1_{R_{n}}\|_{2}^{2}.

Concatenating the estimates obtained so far, we have shown that

\sqrt{n}\cdot\|\varphi 1_{R_{n}}\|_{2}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}}\|y_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}}\geq\frac{\sqrt{c}}{W(n)}\|\varphi 1_{R_{n}}\|_{2}^{2}.

Re-arranging and squaring, this becomes (
13.19
).
∎

Remark 13.20
.

In Lemma
13.19
, the left-hand side of (
13.19
) is non-decreasing with
n
, but the same may not be true of the right-hand side. This seems peculiar at first sight, but is natural in view of the proof. These estimate the norms appearing on the left-hand side in terms of inner products against a family of test vectors. Fox each fixed
n
, the test vector that we use for
y_{s_{i}\dots s_{i}}
depends explicitly on
n
, even when
i<n
, because we choose the test vectors in order to arrive at the telescoping sums at the end of Step 3 of the proof.

This telescoping has the crucial consequence that we never need a lower bound on the summands
\langle(P_{M(s_{i}\dots s_{1})}-P_{M(s_{i-1}\dots s_{1})})z,La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\rangle
in Step 3. This is how we avoid the difficulty discussed following (
13.4
).
\lhd

13.5

Some reflections on the proof

To prove Theorem
13.1
, the overarching need is to turn Haagerup’s characterization of temperedness (Proposition
13.2
) into a lower bound on the annealed entropy of
(1-t)\tau+t\varphi
for small values of
t
.

Having expressed
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
using the Seward expansion and then bounded it from below as in (
13.20
), the main insight is Step 3 in the proof of Lemma
13.19
. In this step, the expression we start with is re-arranged into a collection of telescoping sums, and most of the apparent complexity cancels as a result. Noticing how to arrive at this cancellation was the key to discovering the whole proof. It dictates the rather complicated choice of the sum

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{i+1}\cdots s_{n}\in S_{n-i}\\
s_{i}s_{i+1}\neq e\end{subarray}}a_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}

as the test vector that we use to estimate the norm of
y_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}
.

When
i\neq n
, this choice of vector does not seem very natural in itself, but I did not find any other way to arrive at the desired cancellation. I also suspect that this choice of test vectors makes for some rather inefficient estimates. The families of vectors that we must handle become

y_{s_{i}\cdots s_{1}}\qquad(1\leq i\leq n,\ s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i})

(13.25)

and

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{i+1}\cdots s_{n}\in S_{n-i}\\
s_{i}s_{i+1}\neq e\end{subarray}}La_{s_{n}\cdots s_{1}}\qquad(1\leq i\leq n,\ s_{i}\cdots s_{1}\in S_{i}).

(13.26)

These two families are fundamentally different. Along a growing sequence of reduced words
s_{1}
,
s_{2}s_{1}
, …,
s_{n}\cdots s_{1}
, the vectors in (
13.25
) are mutually orthogonal, whereas the vectors in (
13.26
) are all constructed by summing over possible continuations of the word seen so far, and thus should have ‘large pieces’ in common. In Step 2 of the proof of Lemma
13.19
, we apply the inequality of Corollary
13.10
to each of the inner products appearing between these pairs of vectors. But if the internal geometries of each family are so different, then this use of Corollary
13.10
could be very lossy.

This weakness is responsible for the factor of
n
in the denominator of (
13.14
). This factor should be removable in light of the reasoning about
\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}
that concludes the proof of Theorem
C
. Indeed, this factor of
n
is why the estimates we obtain on
{\mathrm{h}^{\mathrm{ann}}(t\varphi+(1-t)\tau)}
for various values of
t
give a proof of (
13.1
) only via the barely adequate inequality (
13.17
).

Methods for exploiting almost orthogonality when comparing large families of vectors are a classical topic in harmonic analysis on Euclidean spaces: see, for instance,
[
164
, Chapter VII]
. Moreover, many techniques from harmonic analysis on Euclidean spaces or homogeneous spaces have been adapted successfully functions on trees and free groups: see
[
45
]
or
[
75
]
for an introduction. However, I have not been able to turn any results from those fields into useful estimates here. The first problem seems to be that, even considering the individual crescents
C(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})
, their size grows exponentially with
n
, and this easily introduces new pre-factors into the relevant estimates that swamp any improvements obtained elsewhere. It could be interesting to look into this further.

13.6

Some consequences of the temperedness theorem

Theorem 13.21
.

For a finitely generated free group
\Gamma
, the uniformly random AP sequence
\boldsymbol{\pi}
obeys a large deviations principle in the strong-quotient topology of
\mathcal{Z}\Gamma
with speed
n
and rate function

I(\pi):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\mathrm{h}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\pi)&\qquad\hbox{if}\ \pi\gtrsim_{\mathrm{a}}\lambda\\
\infty&\qquad\hbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

This is the specialization of Theorem
8.10
to this random AP sequence. In the notation of that theorem, the crucial extra ingredient is the fact that
\kappa=\lambda
, which is Theorem
C
. This shows that there is no ‘gap’ in the formulation of Theorem
8.10
, and we obtain the full asserted large deviations principle.
∎

Remark 13.22
.

Theorem
13.21
is worth comparing with Voiculescu’s conjectured variational principle between ‘topological free entropy’ and ‘free capacity’ from
[
178
]
. When specialized to representations of a free group
\Gamma
, the unknown part of that principle should be a consequence of the following heuristic phenomenon: given a character
\chi
on
\Gamma
and a sufficiently large integer
n
, ‘most’
n
-dimensional representations
\pi
of
\Gamma
that satisfy
\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{n}\circ\pi\approx\chi
also satisfy
\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi)\approx\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{\chi})}
in the Vietoris topology.

When
\chi
is the character of the regular representation
\lambda
, this is implied by the Collins–Male theorem. Theorem
13.21
does not imply any other new cases, because in the regime covered by Theorem
13.21
the characters of the random representations
\pi_{n}
are still typically close to regular, and the ‘excess’ of
\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{n})
over
\overline{\Sigma_{\bullet}(\lambda)}
is caused by only a small subset of vectors in the unit sphere of
\mathbf{C}^{n}
. This is also why the exponents that appear in Theorem
13.21
are only of order
n
, whereas the more ‘global’ constraints on representations required for Voiculescu’s conjecture would lead to exponents of order
n^{2}
. But Theorem
13.21
does have the same spirit of providing probabilistic control over the sequence
\Sigma_{\bullet}(\pi_{n})
in a new regime.
\lhd

We now consider positive definite functions that are not tempered but have finite annealed and zeroth-order AP entropy. The specialization of Proposition
8.15
applies to these, but we do not write the conclusion out again. Simple examples include the Haagerup function
\varphi
from Example
9.8
provided
|\varphi(s)|<1
for every
s
. Provided
r>1
, this condition is strictly weaker than temperedness by an application of Proposition
13.2
. Therefore the GNS representation of
\varphi
has finite zeroth-order AP entropy as well, and therefore any Haagerup function
\varphi
satisfying that inequality gives a C
∗
-algebra
C^{\ast}(\pi_{\varphi})
that is MF.

In addition, if
\mathrm{h}^{0}(\varphi)>-\infty
, then Proposition
8.8
tells us that

\pi_{\varphi}\simeq(\hbox{stiff})\oplus(\hbox{tempered}).

For conrete examples such as Haagerup functions, this suggests that
\pi_{\varphi}
could actually have a rather tractable structure, at least outside the tempered part. I do not know a route to this insight that does not involve
\mathrm{h}^{0}
.

Problem 13.23
.

If
\varphi
is a Haagerup function with
\max_{s}|\varphi(s)|<1
, then what is the stiff non-tempered part of
\pi_{\varphi}
?

Chapter 14

Some further directions and open problems

Previous chapters have already contained a selection of potential research problems that emerge naturally during the course of our work. This final chapter describes a few more directions that could be explored next. Some of these are rather open-ended.

Other models of random unitary representations

Let
\Gamma
be freely generated by
S=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{r}\}
, and for each
n
let
\pi_{n}
be a uniformly random permutation representation of
\Gamma
on
\mathbf{C}^{n}
, as in Example
5.20
. As shown in that example, if
U
is a non-empty quotient-open set of separable representations, then the probabilities
\mathbf{P}(\pi_{n}\in U)
cannot decay no faster than polynomially in
n
.

Problem 14.1
.

Is this decay actually governed by a sensible power-law in
1/n
, and is there some kind of analog of AP entropy that computes or estimates those powers?

Note that the first-moment calculation in (
12.2
) is less obviously helpful for estimating probabilities that are no exponentially small.

Another option is to move away from uniform randomness by ‘planting’ a small number of vectors with prescribed behaviour in each of the representations
\pi_{n}
. This would be the analog of planted models of random graphs, which have been widely studied in combinatorics and theoretical computer science (see
[
21
]
for a first example). Technically, ‘planting’ in our context should probably mean choosing the generators
(\pi_{n}(s):\ s\in S)
initially from a distribution that has the form we have already met on the right-hand side of (
11.2
). So it could be that this model of randomness requires few new ideas compared to our work above. Among its benefits one could hope to find a definition and an interpretation of ‘relative’ annealed AP entropy. These should be analogous to their predecessors in ergodic theory studied in
[
160
]
.

Tensor products

One can also combine a random choice of unitary-matrix generators with operations such as tensor products: see
[
24
]
, for example. A different construction with tensor products is essential to Hayes’ method in his recent proof of the Peterson–Thom conjecture in
[
97
]
. That proof was initially conditional on strong asymptotic freeness of certain random tensor product representations, but this ingredient has now been provided: see
[
17
]
and also
[
23
]
.

Problem 14.2
.

Is any version of annealed AP entropy available in settings such as these? And can it be estimated?

Fixed matrices

Many of the known results about asymptotic freeness or strong asymptotic freeness gain extra strength because one can also allow a sequence of fixed matrices alongside the random ones, and show that the whole partially-random collections of matrices still satisfy ‘as much (strong) asymptotic freeness as possible’. See the full results in
[
51
]
, for example.

Problem 14.3
.

Is there a variant of annealed AP entropy that can help to analyse these models?

Orthogonal or symplectic representations

Most of our work should be straightforward to adapt to independent random orthogonal matrices, and I doubt there are any surprises waiting. This is because the main results in Part
III
do not depend on the spectral theorem for unitary matrices anywhere. The case of symplectic matrices is not quite so clear to me. Note that the main result of Chapter
11
already applies to any of these settings.

Analogies with free probability

Formally, our only appeal to free probability in Part
III
is through Theorems
5.1
and
5.2
. But other points of resemblance are widespread. Do other known ideas or constructions from the study of free entropy in free probability have adaptations to the study of annealed AP entropy? See
[
100
, Chapter 6]
for an introduction to that theory.

Other groups

To construct our random finite-dimensional unitary representations of a free group, we simply choose a unitary matrix for each free generator independently from the Haar measure
m_{\mathrm{U}(n)}
. As pointed out to me by Uri Bader, the distribution of the resulting random representation does not depend on the chosen free generating set for the group. This is because any two sets of free generators are related by an automorphism of the free group, and any such automorphism is a composition of Nielsen transformations
[
138
]
. An easy check shows that each Nielsen transformation converts one tuple of independent Haar-distributed unitary matrices into another, and so the same is true of any automorphism of the group by induction. (See, for example,
[
84
]
and the references listed there for much more on this measure-preserving action.)

It is also natural to study uniform random finite-dimensional representations of free products of other cyclic groups, and it should be straightforward to develop a resulting theory of annealed AP entropy. For permutations and annealed sofic entropy, this generalization is presented in
[
9
]
. The formula for annealed sofic entropy for such groups is connected to a classical entropy-like functional introduced by Kikuchi (see
[
9
, Section 3]
), and I expect the analog of this story holds for annealed AP entropy.

However, beyond this class of free-product groups, it is more difficult to define ‘natural’ measures on representation varieties. Some progress of this kind is due to Goldman for surface groups
[
90
]
. See also
[
133
,
39
,
125
,
127
]
. When such a natural finite measure exists, one can sensibly use it to define ‘annealed’ AP entropies for those groups. I do not know how tractable or useful the resulting theory might be. More fundamentally still, I do not know whether these measures satisfy analogs of the main properties we have used for the Haar measures on
\mathrm{U}(n)
, such as the measure concentration from
C.4
.

For a Cartesian product
\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}
of two other groups, this discussions asks for a natural way to generate a pair of commuting finite-dimensional representations of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
. While there may not be a single canonical measure on such pairs, one possibility is offered by Kronecker products of separate representations of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
(see
[
77
, Section 7.3]
). Studying these would bring us back to our questions about tensor products above. In general, questions about whether such tensor-product representations can effectively ‘see’ all representations of
\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}
have long-known connections to Connes’ embedding problem and related phenomena. Much more on these topics can be found in
[
144
]
,
[
38
]
, or
[
140
]
, for example.

Part IV
APPENDICES

Appendix A
Basic notation and conventions

A.1

Linear algebra

We assume several standard ideas from linear algebra and matrix analysis in the sequel. For definiteness I use
[
102
]
as a reference wherever possible

Throughout this work, our focus is restricted to linear algebra and functional analysis over
\mathbf{C}
rather than
\mathbf{R}
. This is the appropriate choice for studying unitary representations and C
∗
-algebras later. Much of our work could be re-fashioned over
\mathbf{R}
without requiring major new ideas.

For any positive integer
k
, we write
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
for the space of height-
k
column vectors with complex entries. More generally, if
H
is a vector space, then we write
H^{\oplus k}
for the
k
-fold
inflation
of
H
, which is the vector space of height-
k
column vectors with entries in
H
. If
S
is a set, possible infinite, and
H
is a Hilbert space, then we extend this notation further by writing
H^{\oplus S}
for the Hilbert-space direct sum of an
S
-indexed family of copies of
H
, still regarded as a space of column vectors. This insistence on column vectors is slightly unusual in functional analysis, but for finite
k
it enables us to use matrix-vector notation from linear algebra in places where it greatly simplifies some reasoning

We write
\mathrm{M}_{n,k}
for the vector space of
n
-by-
k
matrices over the complex numbers, and identify these with linear maps from
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
to
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
using matrix-vector multiplication. By writing such a matrix as
[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
, where
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
are its columns, we identify it with a
k
-tuple of vectors in
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
. We generalize this notation further by allowing columns from a general vector space
H
, meaning that a linear map from
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
to
H
may still be written in the form
[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
. This identification of tuples with linear maps is so convenient that we often abuse notation by calling the linear map
V
itself a ‘
k
-tuple of vectors in
H
’. If
H
is an inner product space, the adjoint
V^{\ast}
is the map from
H
to
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
whose output coordinates are given by the inner products with the vectors
v_{i}
.

We abbreviate
\mathrm{M}_{k,k}
to
\mathrm{M}_{k}
and regard it as a
\ast
-algebra over
\mathbf{C}
in the usual way. We write
I_{k}
for the
k
-by-
k
identity matrix. We write
\mathrm{tr}_{k}
and
\mathrm{det}
for the usual trace and determinant on any such algebra, and we set

\overline{\mathrm{tr}}_{k}M:=k^{-1}\mathrm{tr}_{k}\,M\qquad(M\in\mathrm{M}_{k}).

We write
\mathrm{M}_{k,\mathrm{sa}}
for the subspace of self-adjoint members of
\mathrm{M}_{k}
,
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
for the subset of positive semidefinite members of
\mathrm{M}_{k}
, and
\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}
for the further subset of positive definite members. The set
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
is a closed cone in
\mathrm{M}_{k}
, and
\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}
is the relative interior of
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
in
\mathrm{M}_{k,\mathrm{sa}}
. This means that, if
A
is positive definite, then any sufficiently small self-adjoint perturbation of
A
is still positive definite; and this stability property characterizes the positive definite matrices among the positive semidefinite ones.

For a linear operator on an inner product space, or a matrix that can be regarded as such, the notation
\|\cdot\|
means the operator norm by default. We occasionally write it as
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
where disambiguation may be helpful.

We also use the following less standard notations. When
k\leq n
, we write
\mathrm{GL}(k,n)
for the set of all linear injections from
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
to
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
, or equivalently
n
-by-
k
matrices with linearly independent columns. When
k=n
this is the usual general linear group
\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbf{C})
. We write
\mathrm{U}(k,n)
for the subset of unitary embeddings, or equivalently matrices whose columns are orthonormal. When
k=n
this gives the unitary group
\mathrm{U}(n)
. Finally, we write
\Xi(k,n)
for the subset of elements of
\mathrm{M}_{n,k}
that are contractions for the Euclidean distances on
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
and
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
, and
\Xi^{\circ}(k,n)
for the further subset of strict contractions. These are the closed and open unit balls of
\mathrm{M}_{n,k}
in the operator norm, respectively.

A.2

Gram matrices, embeddings, and projections

Let
H
be a complex inner product space and let
V=[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
be
k
-tuple in
H
, interpreted as a linear map from
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
to
H
. The
Gram matrix

Q
of this tuple is the
k
-by-
k
matrix of their inner products:

Q=[\langle v_{j},v_{i}\rangle]_{i,j=1}^{k}=V^{\ast}V.

(A.1)

Gram matrices are positive semidefinite, and every positive semidefinite matrix can be written as a Gram matrix. The choice of this representation is not unique, but given
Q
one can make the canonical choice
H:=\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
and
V:=Q^{1/2}
: this is the only choice for which
V
is again positive semidefinite.

The Gram matrix in (
A.1
) nonsingular if and only if the vectors are linearly independent, hence if and only if
V
is injective. More generally, a positive semidefinite matrix
Q
is the Gram matrix of a tuple of vectors in some inner product space
H
if and only if
