\dim H\geq\mathrm{rank}\,Q
, and in this case the choice of those vectors is unique up to a unitary transformation of
H
. These facts follow at once from
[
102
, Theorem 7.2.10]
, for example. The Gram matrix
Q
has all diagonal entries equal to
1
if and only if every
v_{i}
is a unit vector.

Let
V=[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
and
Q=V^{\ast}V
be as above. Any orthonormal set of vectors with the same span as
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
consists of linear combinations of
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
, and there are general methods for finding such an orthonormal set which use the entries of
Q
to find the coefficients. The simplest general method is the Gram–Schmidt procedure, but this depends on the order of the vectors
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
. If
Q
is nonsingular, then a more symmetric method is available. Indeed, in this case the polar decomposition of
V
is given by

V=(VQ^{-1/2})\cdot Q^{1/2},

(A.2)

and therefore

W:=[w_{1},\dots,w_{k}]:=VQ^{-1/2}

(A.3)

is a unitary embedding of
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
into
H
, and so its columns are an orthonormal basis for
\mathrm{span}\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
.

These calculations also yield a formula for the orthogonal projection
P
from
H
onto

M:=\mathrm{span}\,\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}=V[\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}].

This orthogonal projection is the same if we replace
V
by
W
, and since
W
is a unitary embedding this projection is simply given by

P=WW^{\ast}=VQ^{-1}V^{\ast}.

(A.4)

If
P
is an orthogonal projection on a Hilbert space, we sometimes
P^{\perp}:=1-P
for brevity.

A.3

Group algebras and other algebras

If
\Gamma
is a discrete group, then we write
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
for the complex group algebra of
\Gamma
, and regard it concretely as the space of functions from
\Gamma
to
\mathbf{C}
that have finite support. Given
g\in\Gamma
, we write
\delta_{g}
for its canonical image in
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
, which is the indicator function of the singleton
\{g\}
. We may express a general element of
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
as either an indexed family
(a_{g}:\ g\in\Gamma)
or a sum like
\sum_{g}a_{g}\delta_{g}
, where in either case
a_{g}
is zero for all but finitely many
g
. The vector space operations of
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
are pointwise, and its multiplication is convolution:

a\ast b:=\sum_{g,h}a_{g}b_{h}\delta_{gh}\qquad(a,b\in\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]).

We also always endow
\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]
with the involution

a^{\ast}:=\sum_{g}\overline{a_{g}}\delta_{g^{-1}}\qquad(a\in\mathbf{C}[\Gamma]),

(A.5)

making it a
\ast
-algebra. We extend all of these notations to any larger class of complex-valued functions on
\Gamma
on which they are still well-defined.

Given any algebra
A
over
\mathbf{C}
and a positive integer
k
, we write
\mathrm{M}_{k}(A)
for the algebra of
k
-by-
k
matrices with entries in
A
. If
A
is a
\ast
-algebra, then we define an involution on
\mathrm{M}_{k}(A)
by transposing and applying the involution of
A
entry-wise.

If
A
is an algebra and
\pi
is a representation of
A
on a vector space
H
, then using these rules it defines a representation
\pi^{(k)}
of
\mathrm{M}_{k}(A)
on
H^{\oplus k}
. If
A
is a
\ast
-algebra and
\pi
is a
\ast
-representation on a Hilbert space, then these features extend to
\pi^{(k)}
.

Slightly more generally, given such an algebra
A
and positive integers
k
and
\ell
, we write
\mathrm{M}_{k,\ell}(A)
for the vector space of
k
-by-
\ell
matrices with entries in
A
. We multiply entries of
M_{k,\ell}(A)
and
\mathrm{M}_{\ell,m}(A)
according to the usual rules of matrix multiplication. Similarly, if
A
is an algebra of operators on a vector space
H
, then
\mathrm{M}_{k,\ell}(A)
is naturally identified with a vector space of linear maps from
H^{\oplus\ell}
to
H^{\oplus k}
by the usual rule for multiplying a vector by a matrix.

A.4

Real analysis

Asymptotic notation

We use Landau’s ‘big-
O
’ and ‘little-
o
’ notation widely, but only for sequences indexed by natural numbers. In case these sequences involve other parameters as well, each function hidden behind this asymptotic notation may depend freely on those other parameters. Thus, for example, if
X
is any set and
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
, …and
f
are real-valued functions on
X
, then the assertion

``\ \ f_{n}(x)=f(x)+o(1)\ \ "

means that
f_{n}\to f
pointwise, whereas uniform convergence could be written

``\ \ \sup_{x}|f_{n}(x)-f(x)|=o(1)\ \ ".

At a few points, we write
f(n)\lesssim g(n)
or
g(n)\gtrsim f(n)
as an alternative notation for
f(n)=O(g(n))
. We write
f(n)\simeq g(n)
if both
f(n)\lesssim g(n)
and
f(n)\gtrsim g(n)
.

Measures

For any dimension
d
, we write
\mathrm{vol}_{d}
for Lebesgue measure on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
. For any positive integers
d
and
k
, we also write
\mathrm{vol}_{2kd}
for the measure on
\mathrm{M}_{k,d}(\mathbf{C})
obtained by identifying this space with
\mathbf{R}^{2kd}
.

If
G
is any compact metric group, then
m_{G}
is its Haar probability measure.

Appendix B
Positive semi-definite block matrices

The results in Section
B.1
are available in standard textbooks. Those in Section
B.2
are also standard among experts, but perhaps not known so widely.

B.1

Two-by-two block Gram matrices

Let
V=[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}]
and
U=[u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell}]
be two tuples in the same Hilbert space
H
. The combined tuple
[v_{1},\dots,v_{k},u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell}]
has a
(k+\ell)
-by-
(k+\ell)
Gram matrix which we can write in the block form

Q=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Q_{11}&R\\
R^{\ast}&Q_{22}\end{array}\right].

(B.1)

The diagonal blocks
Q_{11}
and
Q_{22}
are the separate Gram matrices of
V
and
U
, and

R=[\langle u_{j},v_{i}\rangle]_{1\leq i\leq k,\,1\leq j\leq\ell}=V^{\ast}U.

Let
M
be the span of
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
and
P
the orthogonal projection onto
M
. We can separate each
u_{i}
into
Pu_{i}
and
P^{\perp}u_{i}
, and then form two new Gram matrices from these tuples of components. Because
M
and
M^{\perp}
are orthogonal, the full Gram matrix
Q_{22}
of
u_{1}
, …,
u_{k}
is the sum of these two Gram matrices of projections.

Now assume that the block
Q_{11}
is nonsingular. To find the Gram matrix of
[Pu_{1},\dots,Pu_{\ell}]
, we can use (
A.4
) to express each
Pu_{i}
:

[\langle Pu_{i^{\prime}},Pu_{i}\rangle]_{i,i^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}=U^{\ast}P^{2}U=U^{\ast}PU=U^{\ast}VQ_{11}^{-1}V^{\ast}U=R^{\ast}Q_{11}^{-1}R.

(B.2)

It follows that, when
Q_{11}
is nonsingular, the Gram matrix of the orthogonal projections
P^{\perp}u_{1}
, …,
P^{\perp}u_{\ell}
is equal to the difference

Q_{22}-R^{\ast}Q_{11}^{-1}R.

(B.3)

This is the
Schur complement
of
Q_{11}
in
Q
. It appears widely in the analysis of positive semidefinite matrices, for instance in the formula for inverting any nonsingular
2
-by-
2
block matrix
[
102
, Section 0.7.3]
, and in the determinant formula

\det Q=\det Q_{11}\cdot\det(Q_{22}-R^{\ast}Q_{11}^{-1}R),

(B.4)

which holds whenever
Q_{11}
is nonsingular
[
102
, Section 0.8.5]
.

If
Q_{11}
is singular then the calculations above are not available, but of course the full matrix
Q
still determines the Gram matrix of the orthogonal projections
P^{\perp}u_{1}
, …,
P^{\perp}u_{\ell}
uniquely. In this case one can use generalized Schur complements instead: see
[
102
, Exercises 7.1.P28 and then 7.3.P7–8]
. For our purposes it is generally easiest to find ways to reduce our work to the nonsingular case.

Consider again a
2
-by-
2
block self-adjoint matrix
Q
written as in (
B.1
), and assume that
Q_{11}
and
Q_{22}
are both positive semidefinite. The next proposition characterizes those off-diagonal blocks for which the whole of
Q
is still positive semidefinite. It features the Schur complement again.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
Q
be as in (
B.1
), and assume that
Q_{11}
and
Q_{22}
are positive semidefinite. Then
Q
is positive semidefinite if and only if
R
is equal to
Q_{11}^{1/2}CQ_{22}^{1/2}
for some contraction
C\in\Xi(\ell,k)
(recall this notation from Section
A.1
).

If
Q_{11}
is nonsingular, then this holds if and only if the Schur complement Schur complement
Q_{22}-R^{\ast}Q^{-1}_{11}R
is positive semidefinite.

If
Q_{11}
and
Q_{22}
are both nonsingular, then the contraction
C
promised above is unique, and the following are equivalent:

•

the whole of
Q
is nonsingular;

•

the contraction
C
is strict;

•

the Schur complement
Q_{22}-R^{\ast}Q^{-1}_{11}R
is nonsingular. ∎

See
[
102
, Theorems 7.7.7 and 7.7.9]
.

The proof can be written fairly quickly in terms of matrix manipulations, but the conditions appearing here also have a natural geometric interpretation. If the whole of
Q
is positive semidefinite, then it is a Gram matrix, say of the concatenated tuple
[V,U]
as before. In that case we have already seen that the Schur complement
Q_{22}-R^{\ast}Q^{-1}_{11}R
is the Gram matrix of the projected tuple
P^{\perp}u_{1}
, …,
P^{\perp}u_{\ell}
.

To interpret
C
, assume first that
Q_{11}
and
Q_{22}
are both nonsingular. In this case two applications of (
A.3
) give new tuples

W:=VQ_{11}^{-1/2}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad Y:=UQ_{22}^{-1/2}

(B.5)

such that
W
(resp.
V
) is a unitary embedding of
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
(resp.
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus\ell}
) into
H
with the same image as
V
(resp.
U
). Therefore the linear map
C:=W^{\ast}PY
is a composition of contractions, and hence a contraction. Geometrically, it is the restricted orthogonal projection
P|\mathrm{span}\,\{u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell}\}
, written in terms of the canonical bases of
\mathrm{span}\,\{u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell}\}
and
\mathrm{span}\,\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
provided by (
B.5
). On the other hand, substituting from (
B.5
), this contraction satisfies

C=Q_{11}^{-1/2}V^{\ast}PUQ_{22}^{-1/2}=Q_{11}^{-1/2}RQ_{22}^{-1/2},

where the second equality holds because
P
is the orthogonal projection onto the image of
V
, and so
V^{\ast}P=V^{\ast}
. Re-arranging, we have shown that

R=Q_{11}^{1/2}CQ_{22}^{1/2}.

If either
Q_{11}
or
Q_{22}
is singular, then we cannot quite argue as above. For example, if
Q_{11}
is singular, then the first part of (
B.5
) cannot be used to select a
canonical
orthonormal basis of
\mathrm{span}\,\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
. However,
some
choice of basis is still possible, and an adjusted version of the reasoning above can then still be completed. This shows that the representation of
R
using a contraction still holds in case
Q_{11}
or
Q_{22}
is singular, but that this representation is no longer unique. (Alternatively, the singular case can be deduced from the nonsingular case by a limited argument: see, for instance, the use of
[
102
, Lemma 7.7.8]
.)

Example B.2
.

Suppose that
\ell=1
, so
Q
is the
(k+1)
-by-
(k+1)
Gram matrix of a tuple
[v_{1},\dots,v_{k},u]
. Assume that
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
are linearly independent and
u\neq 0
. In this case the top right block of
Q
is a column vector in
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
which specifies the inner product of
u
with each
v_{i}
. This column vector is parametrized by a contraction from
\mathbf{C}
to
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
, or equivalently by a vector in
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
of length at most
1
. Geometrically, we have a canonical choice of orthonormal basis for
\mathrm{span}\,\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
given by (
A.3
), and the parametrizing vector is the orthogonal projection of
u/\|u\|
onto that span written in this basis.

Most simply of all, if also
k=1
, then the parametrizing contraction is simply a complex number of modulus
\cos\theta
, where
\theta
is the angle between
v
and
u
. ∎

B.2

Three-by-three-block Gram matrices

This section extends the work of Section
B.1
by allowing three blocks rather than two. Let
H
be a complex inner product space as before. Let
k
,
\ell
and
m
be non-negative integers, and in
H
consider tuples of vectors

V=[v_{1},\dots,v_{k}],\quad U=[u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell}]\quad\hbox{and}\quad X=[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}].

(B.6)

Combine them into a single
(k+\ell+m)
-tuple, and write their joint Gram matrix explicitly in
3
-by-
3
block form

Q:=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}V^{\ast}V&V^{\ast}U&V^{\ast}X\\
U^{\ast}V&U^{\ast}U&U^{\ast}X\\
X^{\ast}V&X^{\ast}U&X^{\ast}X\end{array}\right].

(B.7)

Let
M
be the span of
u_{1}
, …,
u_{\ell}
(not
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
, as previously), and let
P
be the orthogonal projection onto
M
. Let us decompose the matrix
Q
into two summands by splitting each vector into its components in
M
and
M^{\perp}
. In terms of the linear maps in (
B.6
), this simply means we write
V=PV+P^{\perp}V
, and similarly. Since
PU=U
and
P^{\perp}U=0
, we find that
Q
is equal to

\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}V^{\ast}PV&V^{\ast}U&V^{\ast}PX\\
U^{\ast}V&U^{\ast}U&U^{\ast}X\\
X^{\ast}PV&X^{\ast}U&X^{\ast}PX\end{array}\right]+\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}V^{\ast}P^{\perp}V&0&V^{\ast}P^{\perp}X\\
0&0&0\\
X^{\ast}P^{\perp}V&0&X^{\ast}P^{\perp}X\end{array}\right].

(B.8)

The first summand here is the Gram matrix of three tuples of vectors that are all contained in
M
. On the other hand, by ignoring the middle row and column, the second summand is effectively just a two-by-two-block Gram matrix.

This decomposition leads naturally to a generalization of Proposition
B.1
to three-by-three-block self-adjoint matrices. This time we formulate it only in the case of some extra nonsingularity assumptions. These are not really necessary, but removing them requires a fiddlier discussion, and the case presented here covers our needs in this book.

Proposition B.3
.

Consider a
(k+\ell+m)
-by-
(k+\ell+m)
self-adjoint matrix

Q:=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}Q_{11}&Q_{12}&R\\
Q_{12}^{\ast}&Q_{22}&Q_{23}\\
R^{\ast}&Q_{23}^{\ast}&Q_{33}\end{array}\right].

(B.9)

Assume that the submatrices

Q_{(1\cup 2)}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Q_{11}&Q_{12}\\
Q_{12}^{\ast}&Q_{22}\end{array}\right]\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad Q_{(2\cup 3)}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Q_{22}&Q_{23}\\
Q_{23}^{\ast}&Q_{33}\end{array}\right]

are both positive semidefinite, and that the further submatrix
Q_{22}
is strictly positive definite. Then
Q
is positive semidefinite if and only if it has the form

\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}Q_{12}Q_{22}^{-1}Q_{12}^{\ast}&Q_{12}&Q_{12}Q_{22}^{-1}Q_{23}\\
Q_{12}^{\ast}&Q_{22}&Q_{23}\\
Q_{23}^{\ast}Q_{22}^{-1}Q_{12}^{\ast}&Q_{23}^{\ast}&Q_{23}^{\ast}Q_{22}^{-1}Q_{23}\end{array}\right]+\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}S_{11}&0&S_{11}^{1/2}CS_{33}^{1/2}\\
0&0&0\\
S_{33}^{1/2}C^{\ast}S_{11}^{1/2}&0&S_{33}\end{array}\right],

(B.10)

where

S_{11}:=Q_{11}-Q_{12}Q_{22}^{-1}Q_{12}^{\ast}\quad\hbox{and}\quad S_{33}:=Q_{33}-Q_{23}^{\ast}Q_{22}^{-1}Q_{23}

(B.11)

are Schur complements and
C\in\Xi(m,k)
.

If
Q_{(1\cup 2)}
and
Q_{(2\cup 3)}
are both nonsingular, then the contraction
C
promised here is unique, and the whole of
Q
is nonsingular if and only if
C
is a strict contraction.

Proof.

Necessity.
Let
n:=k+\ell+m
. If the whole of
Q
is positive semidefinite, then it is the Gram matrix of some tuples of vectors in
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
as in (
B.6
), and so it decomposes as in (
B.8
).

Applying (
B.2
) to the four corner blocks of the first summand in (
B.8
), we find that it equals the first summand in (
B.10
). On the other hand, by (
B.3
), the two nonzero diagonal blocks in the second summand in (
B.8
) are equal to
S_{11}
and
S_{22}
. Finally, Proposition
B.1
tells us that this second summand is positive semidefinite if and only if it has the asserted form for some contraction
C
.

Sufficiency.
Suppose that
Q
has the form in (
B.10
). The second summand is positive semidefinite by Proposition
B.1
. It therefore suffices to prove that
Q
is positive semidefinite in case it equals the first summand in (
B.10
), meaning that
Q_{ii}=Q_{i2}^{\ast}Q_{22}^{-1}Q_{2i}
for both
i
in
\{1,3\}
. By (
B.2
), this means that
Q_{(1\cup 2)}
and
Q_{(2\cup 3)}
are unchanged by the procedure of writing each as a Gram matrix and then projecting all the vectors onto the span of the vectors corresponding to the block
Q_{22}
. We may therefore pick a tuple
U
whose Gram matrix is
Q_{22}
and then two more tuples
V
and
X
contained in
\mathrm{ran}\,U
so that
Q_{(1\cup 2)}
and
Q_{(2\cup 3)}
are the Gram matrices of
[V,U]
and
[U,X]
respectively. Finally, applying (
B.2
) for the projection onto
\mathrm{ran}\,U
and the whole triple tuple
[V,U,X]
, we find that under these conditions the Gram matrix of
[V,U,X]
is the first summand in (
B.10
), so that summand must be positive semidefinite.

Uniqueness and nonsingularity.
These follow by checking the corresponding conditions for uniqueness and nonsingularity in our application of Proposition
B.1
above.
∎

We can illustrate the parametrization by contractions in Proposition
B.3
with the following continuation of Example
B.2
.

Example B.4
.

Suppose that
m=1
, and let
Q
as above be the Gram matrix of the combined tuple
[v_{1},\dots,v_{k},u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell},x]
in
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
. Let
P
be the orthogonal projection onto
M=\mathrm{span}\,\{u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell}\}
.

The parametrizing contraction
C
appears in the second summand in (
B.10
). That, in turn, corresponds to the second summand in (
B.8
), whose nonzero blocks together form the
(k+1)
-by-
(k+1)
Gram matrix of the tuple
[P^{\perp}v_{1},\dots,P^{\perp}v_{k},P^{\perp}x]
. As in Example
B.2
, the top right block here is a column vector in
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
which specifies the inner product of
P^{\perp}x
with each
P^{\perp}v_{i}
. This column vector is parametrized by an element of
\Xi(1,k)
, meaning simply a vector in
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus k}
of length at most
1
. Under the canonical choice of orthonormal basis for
\mathrm{span}\,\{P^{\perp}v_{1},\dots,P^{\perp}v_{k}\}
, this vector corresponds to the orthogonal projection of
P^{\perp}x/\|P^{\perp}x\|
onto that span. ∎

Proposition
B.3
can be regarded as the solution to a ‘matrix completion problem’. Referring to (
B.9
), imagine that we know all the blocks of this self-adjoint matrix apart from
R
and
R^{\ast}
, and that the submatrices
Q_{(1\cup 2)}
and
Q_{(2\cup 3)}
are positive semidefinite. The ‘completion problem’ asks whether we can choose
R
so that the whole of
Q
remains positive semidefinite. Assuming that
Q_{22}
is nonsingular, Proposition
B.3
tells us that the answer is Yes, and moreover that the possible choices for
R
are parametrized by the elements of
\Xi(m,k)
as in (
B.10
). Putting the various steps above together we arrive at the formula

R=Q_{12}Q_{22}^{-1}Q_{23}+S_{11}^{1/2}CS_{33}^{1/2},

(B.12)

where
S_{11}
and
S_{33}
are as in (
B.11
).

At some points in the sequel, it is important to think of this problem and its solution as a relationship between whole spaces of matrices. Recall that
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
denotes the closed cone of positive semidefinite elements of
\mathrm{M}_{k,sa}
, and that
\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}
denotes the subset of positive definite elements, which is the relative interior of
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
in
\mathrm{M}_{k,\mathrm{sa}}
.

The next space is less standard, so we define it formally. Let
k
,
\ell
and
m
be non-negative integers, as before.

Definition B.5
.

A
partial positive semidefinite
matrix with
block sizes

k
,
\ell
and
m
is a
5
-tuple

(Q_{11},Q_{12},Q_{22},Q_{23},Q_{33})\in\mathrm{M}_{k+}\times\mathrm{M}_{k,\ell}\times\mathrm{M}_{\ell+}\times\mathrm{M}_{\ell,m}\times\mathrm{M}_{m+}

with the property that both of the two-by-two-block matrices

\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Q_{11}&Q_{12}\\
Q_{12}^{\ast}&Q_{22}\end{array}\right]\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Q_{22}&Q_{23}\\
Q_{23}^{\ast}&Q_{33}\end{array}\right]

are positive semidefinite.

A partial positive semidefinite matrix is
partially nonsingular
if both of those two-by-two-block matrices are nonsingular; otherwise it is
partially singular
.

We write
\mathrm{PPSD}_{k,\ell,m}
for the space of partial positive semidefinite matrices with block sizes
k
,
\ell
and
m
, and
\mathrm{PPSD}^{\circ}_{k,\ell,m}
for the further subset of its partially nonsingular members.

Rather than writing out a
5
-tuple as above, henceforth we write the corresponding partial positive semidefinite matrices as a three-by-three-block matrix with some unknown entries:

\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}Q_{11}&Q_{12}&?\\
Q_{21}^{\ast}&Q_{22}&Q_{23}\\
?&Q_{23}^{\ast}&Q_{33}\end{array}\right].

We often indicate such an entity by a raised question mark, as in “
Q^{?}
”. There is a natural map

\pi^{?}:\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell+m)+}\to\mathrm{PPSD}_{k,\ell,m}

that simply erases the
k
-by-
m
blocks in the top right and bottom left of its argument. If
Q^{?}\in\mathrm{PPSD}_{k,\ell,m}
, then let us write

\Delta(Q^{?}):=\{Q\in\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell+m)+}:\ \pi^{?}(Q)=Q^{?}\},

and refer to this fibre as the set of
completions
of
Q^{?}
.

With these notations in hand, we can turn Proposition
B.3
into the following.

Corollary B.6
.

The map
\pi^{?}
is a surjection from
\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell+m)+}
onto
\mathrm{PPSD}_{k,\ell,m}
. Its restriction to
\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{(k+\ell+m)+}
is surjective onto
\mathrm{PPSD}^{\circ}_{k,\ell,m}
.

We can define a map

\mathrm{PPSD}^{\circ}_{k,\ell,m}\times\Xi(m,k)\\
\to\big\{Q\in\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell+m)+}:\ Q_{(1\cup 2)}\ \hbox{and}\ Q_{(2\cup 3)}\ \hbox{nonsingular}\big\}

(B.13)

according to

(Q^{?},C)\mapsto\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}Q_{11}&Q_{12}&R\\
Q_{12}^{\ast}&Q_{22}&Q_{23}\\
R^{\ast}&Q_{23}^{\ast}&Q_{33}\end{array}\right],

where
S_{11}
,
S_{22}
are given by (
B.11
) and then
R
is given by (
B.12
). This map is a homeomorphism, it maps
\mathrm{PPSD}^{\circ}_{k,\ell,m}\times\Xi^{\circ}(m,k)
onto
\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{(k+\ell+m)+}
, and it inverts the restriction of
\pi^{?}
to that set.

Proof.

This is mostly just a reformulation of Proposition
B.3
, together with the observation that the functions of matrices defined by (
B.11
) and (
B.12
) are continuous on the open subsets where the necessary matrices are invertible. The only outstanding issue is the surjectivity of
\pi^{?}
onto the whole of
\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell+m)+}
. This follows by a continuity argument and the fact that continuous images of compact sets are compact: compare, for instance, the use of
[
102
, Lemma 7.7.8]
to prove
[
102
, Theorem 7.7.9]
.
∎

For any partially nonsingular
Q^{?}
, the map in (
B.13
) defines a homeomorphic parametrization of
\Delta(Q^{?})
by
\Xi(m,k)
under which the nonsingular completions are identitied with the strict contractions. Moreover, this parametrization also varies continuously as
Q^{?}
varies in the open subset
\mathrm{PPSD}^{\circ}_{k,\ell,m}
of
\mathrm{PPSD}_{k,\ell,m}
.

If
Q^{?}
is partially singular then it still has completions, but the formula (
B.12
) may degenerate. As in the previous section, alternative constructions are available with the help of Moore–Penrose generalized inverses and generalized Schur complements, but we avoid the need for these in this book.

If
Q_{22}
is nonsingular, then a canonical choice of completion for
Q^{?}
results by setting
C
to be
0
. Equivalently, this is the choice for which the second summand in (
B.10
) vanishes. If
Q
is the combined Gram matrix of the tuples in (
B.6
), then this decomposition is the same as (
B.8
), and there the second term vanishes if and only if the subspaces
\mathrm{ran}\,V+\mathrm{ran}\,U
and
\mathrm{ran}\,U+\mathrm{ran}\,X
are relatively orthogonal over their common further subspace
\mathrm{ran}\,U
. This particular choice of
Q
is called the
central completion
of
Q_{(1\cup 2)}
and
Q_{(2\cup 3)}
.

Notes and further references

Completion problems for positive semidefinite matrices, as well as many other classes of matrices, have been studied in great depth, both within pure linear algebra and for the sake of numerous engineering applications. This is also an area of longstanding interaction between matrix analysis and abstract operator theory: see, for instance,
[
142
, Lemma 3.1]
for an abstract generalization of Proposition
B.1
in terms of
2\times 2
matrices over a C
∗
-algebra. The way we use Corollary
B.6
also belongs to that interaction.

A classic introduction to this branch of matrix analysis can be found in
[
104
]
, and the textbooks
[
76
]
and
[
13
]
both cover it very thoroughly. They can be consulted for topics we have left aside, such as how to handle singular or degenerate cases. In particular, positive two-by-two-block and three-by-three-block matrix completion are both treated thoroughly in
[
76
, Chapte XVI]
, and both that chapter and
[
13
, Chapter 2]
consider the natural further generalization of Proposition
B.3
to ‘banded’ matrices with larger numbers of blocks. For these, possible completions are parametrized by sequences of contractions in a way that generalizes (
B.12
): see
[
76
, Section XVI.8]
or
[
13
, Theorem 2.1.2]
.

For a different point of view on the more standard results in Section
B.1
above, the book
[
18
, Sections 1.3 and 1.5]
relates them to the positivity or monotonicity of certain maps between matrices. That book also explores some different but closely related completion questions for positive semidefinite matrices in
[
18
, Section 3.4]
, where they are also related to positivity of certain associated Schur product maps between spaces of matrices. See
[
142
, Chapter 3]
for operator theoretic connections with this point of view.

Appendix C
Integration over spaces of matrices

C.1

Polar decomposition of integrals

Recall the notations
\mathrm{GL}(k,n)
,
\mathrm{U}(k,n)
,
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
and
\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}
from Chapter
A
. The set
\mathrm{GL}(k,n)
is a dense open subset of
\mathrm{M}_{n,k}\cong(\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n})^{k}
. Its complement is defined by the vanishing of determinants of submatrices, so it has zero volume. Therefore we may regard Lebesgue measure
\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}
as a measure on
\mathrm{GL}(k,n)
.

The set
\mathrm{U}(k,n)
has a natural action of
\mathrm{U}(n)
by post-multiplication, and this action is transitive. So
\mathrm{U}(k,n)
is a homogeneous space of the compact Lie group
\mathrm{U}(n)
; the stabilizer of any fixed choice of
k
orthonormal vectors in
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
is a copy of
{\mathrm{U}(n-k)}
. As a result,
\mathrm{U}(k,n)
inherits a Haar probability measure
m_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}
from
\mathrm{U}(n)
. For example, if
k=1
, then
\mathrm{U}(1,n)
is the unit sphere
\mathrm{S}^{2n-1}
, and
m_{\mathrm{U}(1,n)}
is the normalized surface-area measure.

Lastly, the set
\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}
is relatively open in the real-linear subspace
\mathrm{M}_{k,\mathrm{sa}}
of
\mathrm{M}_{k}
, and
\mathrm{M}_{k,\mathrm{sa}}
has real dimension
k^{2}
, so the restriction of
\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}
to
\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}
gives a nontrivial sigma-finite measure.

The continuity of spectral calculus lets us define a continuous map

\mathrm{U}(k,n)\times\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}\to\mathrm{GL}(k,n):(V,Q)\mapsto VQ^{1/2}.

(C.1)

This is a homeomorphism; its inverse is given by polar decomposition:

T\mapsto(T(T^{\ast}T)^{-1/2},T^{\ast}T).

The map (
C.1
) leads to the following formula for integration over
\mathrm{GL}(k,n)
.

Proposition C.1
.

Any positive Borel function
f
on
\mathrm{GL}(k,n)
satisfies

\int_{\mathrm{GL}(k,n)}f(T)\,d\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}(T)\\
=v(k,n)\int_{\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}}(\det Q)^{n-k}\int_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}f(VQ^{1/2})\ dm_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}(V)\ d\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}(Q),

(C.2)

where

v(k,n):=\frac{\pi^{nk}}{\pi^{k(k-1)/2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(n-j)!}.

(C.3)

When
k=1
, (
C.2
) is simply the formula for integration over a complex vector space using polar coordinates:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{C}^{n}}f(z)\,d\mathrm{vol}_{2n}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{n}}{(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{\infty}q^{n-1}\int_{\mathrm{S}^{2n-1}}f(\sqrt{q}u)\ d\sigma_{2n-1}(u)\ dq

\displaystyle=\frac{2n\pi^{n}}{n!}\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{2n-1}\int_{\mathrm{S}^{2n-1}}f(ru)\ d\sigma_{2n-1}(u)\ dr,

where the second line follows by substituting
q:=r^{2}
. See, for instance,
[
154
, Subsections 1.4.3 and 1.4.9]
. The same reference also gives the identification

v(1,n)=\frac{\pi^{n}}{(n-1)!}=n\cdot v(n),

(C.4)

where
v(n)
is the ball-volume function defined in (
4.1
).

Proof of Proposition
C.1
.

The general case of (
C.2
) can be proved in various ways, but some careful manipulations with the change-of-variables formula are unavoidable. Happily, the result is known classically from the study of complex Wishart distributions. Real Wishart distributions are one of the standard families that arise in high-dimensional statistical inference: see, for instance,
[
19
, Subsection 2.3.6 and Appendix B]
. Their complex counterparts have been studied since at least Goodman’s work
[
91
]
, which includes the following result. Let
T
be a random matrix in
\mathrm{M}_{n,k}
whose rows are independent with complex
k
-variate Gaussian distribution with mean zero and complex covariance matrix
I_{k}
. This means that the overall distribution of
T
is

\frac{1}{\pi^{nk}}e^{-\mathrm{tr}_{k}(T^{\ast}T)}\ d\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}(T)\qquad(T\in\mathrm{M}_{n,k});

(C.5)

this is a product of
n
copies of
[
91
, equation (1.5)]
. Then the resulting random Gram matrix
Q:=T^{\ast}T
has the
complex Wishart distribution

\frac{1}{\pi^{k(k-1)/2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(n-j)!}(\det Q)^{n-k}e^{-\mathrm{tr}_{k}Q}\ d\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}(Q)\qquad(Q\in\mathrm{M}_{k+}):

(C.6)

see
[
91
, equation (1.6)]
.

To derive (
C.2
) from this fact, first observe that both sides of (
C.2
) are invariant under the action of
\mathrm{U}(n)
by left-multiplication. We may therefore average over that action first and so reduce to the case when
f(T)=g(T^{\ast}T)
for some positive Borel function
g
on
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
. Then, letting
h(Q):=e^{\mathrm{tr}_{k}Q}g(Q)
, the distribution formulas (
C.5
) and (
C.6
) give the equality of expectations

\frac{1}{\pi^{nk}}\int_{\mathrm{GL}(k,n)}e^{-\mathrm{tr}_{k}(T^{\ast}T)}h(T^{\ast}T)\ d\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}(T)\\
=\frac{1}{\pi^{k(k-1)/2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(n-j)!}\int_{\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}}(\det Q)^{n-k}e^{-\mathrm{tr}_{k}Q}h(Q)\ d\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}(Q),

and this is (
C.2
) after multiplying through by
\pi^{nk}
.
∎

We refer to the normalizing constants
v(k,n)
frequently in the sequel, but we generally do not need the exact formula for them. One conclusion we do use is the following asymptotic behaviour for
k
fixed and
n\to\infty
, which follows from (
C.3
) and Stirling’s approximation:

v(k,n)=\frac{(e\pi)^{nk}}{n^{nk}}\cdot e^{o(n)}\qquad\hbox{and hence}\qquad v(k,n)=v(n)^{k}\cdot e^{o(n)}.

(C.7)

Notes and further references

In connection with Wishart distributions, Proposition
C.1
has several intricate variants and enhancements, for example by passing from the distribution of
Q
to the distribution of its eigenvalues. This is a well-studied topic: see, for instance,
[
4
, Exercise 2.1.18 and Proposition 4.1.3]
(as well as the bibliographical notes to both chapters),
[
180
, Section 6.2]
, or
[
100
, pp129–131 and Lemma 4.4.7]
.

C.2

A method of types for Gram matrices

Section
10.2
discusses log-determinants as an analog of discrete Shannon entropy. One aspect of this analogy is a ‘method-of-types’ interpretation for log-determinants. It estimates the volumes of certain sets of ‘typical tuples’ of vectors in high dimensions. This is similar to the method of types in information theory
[
56
, Section 11.1]
, which interprets Shannon entropy in terms of some basic counting problems. When log-determinant entropy appears as the differential entropy of a jointly Gaussian random vector, it also has a standard method-of-types interpretation in terms of volumes: the basic idea is covered in
[
56
, Section 8.2]
.

For any
n\geq k
and
O\subset\mathrm{M}_{k+}
, let

T(n,O):=\{X\in\mathrm{M}_{n,k}:\ X^{\ast}X\in O\}.

(C.8)

Theorem C.2
.

Let
Q
be a
k
-by-
k
positive semidefinite matrix.

a.

(Lower bound) If
O
is any neighbourhood of
Q
in
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
, then

\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}T(n,O)}{v(n)^{k}}\geq(\det Q)^{n-o(n)}.

b.

(Upper bound) For any
a>\det Q
there is a neighbourhood
O
of
Q
in
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
such that

\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}T(n,O)}{v(n)^{k}}\leq a^{n+o(n)}.

This is proved by quite a soft argument as
[
8
, Theorem 9.3]
. It also has a fairly quick proof using Proposition
C.1
, which we include to illustrate the application of that result.

Proof.

When
Q
is nonsingular, we can prove both parts together rather directly from Proposition
C.1
. Indeed, in that case we can shrink
O
if necessary to assume that it is contained in
\mathrm{M}^{\circ}_{k+}
, and then that proposition gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}T(n,O)

\displaystyle=v(k,n)\int_{O}(\det Q)^{n-k}\int_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}1\ dm_{\mathrm{U}(k,n)}(V)\ d\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}(Q)

\displaystyle=v(k,n)\int_{O}(\det Q)^{n-k}\ d\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}(Q).

If
\varepsilon>0
, then we may choose
O
so small that every
Q^{\prime}\in O
satisfies

e^{-\varepsilon}\det Q\leq\det Q^{\prime}\leq e^{\varepsilon}\det Q,

(C.9)

at which point the above integral implies that

(e^{-\varepsilon}\det Q)^{n-k}\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}O\leq\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}T(n,O)}{v(k,n)}\leq(e^{\varepsilon}\det Q)^{n-k}\mathrm{vol}_{k^{2}}O.

This implies both the lower and upper bounds, because (i)
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, (ii)
k
is fixed while
n\to\infty
, (iii) the asymptotic (
C.7
) lets us replace
v(k,n)
with
v(n)^{k}
, and (iv) the lower bound for a smaller choice of
O
implies it for a larger choice of
O
, so
\varepsilon n
can be improved to
o(n)
in the lower bound.

The proof of the upper bound when
Q
is singular is analogous, except this time we choose
O
so small that every
Q^{\prime}\in O
satisfies
\det Q^{\prime}\leq a
, rather than (
C.9
).
∎

We can generalize Theorem
C.2
by considering the combined Gram matrix of one fixed tuple together with another that may vary. This leads to a method-of-types interpretation of the determinant of a Schur complement as in (
B.3
). Assume that
n\geq\ell+k
, let
e_{1}
, …,
e_{n}
be a fixed orthonormal basis for
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
, and let
E:=[e_{1},\dots,e_{\ell}]
. For any subset
O
of
\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell)+}
, let

T^{\prime}(n,O):=\{X\in\mathrm{M}_{n,k}:\ [E,X]^{\ast}[E,X]\in O.\}

(C.10)

Let

Q=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}I_{\ell}&R\\
R^{\ast}&Q_{22}\end{array}\right].

be a fixed
(k+\ell)
-by-
(k+\ell)
positive semidefinite matrix, and let
S
be the Schur complement
Q_{22}-R^{\ast}R
.

Theorem C.3
.

The following hold:

a.

(Lower bound) If
Q
is nonsingular and
O
is any neighbourhood of
Q
in
\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell)+}
, then

\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}T^{\prime}(n,O)}{v(n)^{k}}\geq(\det S)^{n-o(n)}.

b.

(Upper bound) For any
a>\det S
there is a neighbourhood
O
of
Q
in
\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell)+}
such that

\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{2kn}T^{\prime}(n,O)}{v(n)^{k}}\leq a^{n+o(n)}.

This reduces to Theorem
C.2
when
\ell=0
. We prove it in general by a reduction to that theorem.

Proof.

Denote a typical element of
\mathrm{M}_{(k+\ell)+}
by

Q^{\prime}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Q_{11}^{\prime}&R^{\prime}\\
(R^{\prime})^{\ast}&Q^{\prime}_{22}\end{array}\right].

(C.11)

In this space,
Q
has a neighbourhood base consisting of the sets of the form

O=\{Q^{\prime}:\ Q_{11}^{\prime}\in O_{11},\ R^{\prime}\in O_{12},\ Q^{\prime}_{22}-(R^{\prime})^{\ast}R^{\prime}\in O_{22}\}

as
O_{11}
,
O_{12}
and
Q_{22}
range over neighbourhoods of
I_{\ell}
in
\mathrm{M}_{\ell+}
, of
R
in
\mathrm{M}_{\ell,k}
, and of
S
in
\mathrm{M}_{k+}
respectively. It therefore suffices to prove parts (a) and (b) using sets of this form. However, for such a set
O
, we have

T^{\prime}(n,O)=\{X\in\mathrm{M}_{n,k}:E^{\ast}X\in O_{12}\ \hbox{and}\ (PX)^{\ast}(PX)\in O_{22}\},

where
P
is the orthogonal projection of
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus n}
onto the last
n-k
coordinates, regarded as spanning a copy of
\mathbf{C}^{\oplus(n-k)}
. As a result,
T^{\prime}(n,O)
has the same volume as the Cartesian product

O_{12}\times T(n-\ell,O_{22})\subset\mathrm{M}_{\ell,k}\times\mathrm{M}_{(n-\ell),k},

where
T(n-\ell,O_{22})
is defined as in (
C.8
). The resulting factor of
\mathrm{vol}_{2k\ell}O_{12}
does not depend on
n
, and the factor of
\mathrm{vol}_{2k(n-\ell)}T(n-\ell,O_{22})
is governed by Theorem
C.2
. This completes the proof when combined with (
C.7
), which now gives the asymptotic

v(k,n-\ell)=e^{o(n)}v(n)^{k}\qquad\hbox{as}\ n\to\infty\ \hbox{for fixed}\ k\ \hbox{and}\ \ell.

∎

C.3

Measure concentration

The groups
\mathrm{U}(n)
are examples of the measure concentration phenomenon as
n\to\infty
, and so are fixed Cartesian powers
\mathrm{U}(n)^{r}
. To formulate the result precisely, define the Hilbert–Schmidt metric on
\mathrm{U}(n)^{r}
by

d_{\mathrm{HS}}\big((U_{1,1},\dots,U_{1,r}),(U_{2,1},\dots,U_{2,r})\big):=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathrm{tr}_{n}\big((U_{1,i}-U_{2,i})^{\ast}(U_{1,i}-U_{2,i})\big)}.

Theorem C.4
.

If
F:\mathrm{U}(n)^{r}\to\mathbf{R}
is
L
-Lipschitz for
d_{\mathrm{HS}}
, then

m_{\mathrm{U}(n)}^{\times r}\Big\{\Big|F-\int F\,dm_{\mathrm{U}(n)}^{\times r}\Big|\geq t\Big\}\leq 2e^{-nt^{2}/24L^{2}}.

∎

This generalizes Lévy’s classical concentration inequality for the sphere
\mathrm{S}^{2n-1}
, which played a role in the foundations of AP entropy via
[
8
, Lemma 9.1]
.

The analog of Theorem
C.4
for
\mathrm{SU}(n)
can be deduced from the lower bound on its Ricci curvature as a Riemannian manifold. Starting from this curvature lower bound, one can either use the volume-comparison estimates of Gromov to show that these inequalities are implied by their counterparts on the sphere
[
92
]
, or run the semigroup argument of Bakry and Emery
[
14
,
118
]
to prove a logarithmic Sobolev inequality. Theorem
C.4
for
\mathrm{U}(n)
can be derived from the result for
\mathrm{SU}(n)
by a suitable coupling of the Haar measures on these groups. See
[
129
, Theorem 15]
for a proof of this form that gives the optimal constant.

Notes and further references

An overview of concentration inequalities and methods for proving them is given in several books, including
[
119
]
. See
[
128
, Section 5.3]
or
[
4
, Subsection 4.4.2]
for more specialized accounts of concentration for compact matrix groups with thorough selections of references.

Appendix D
Large deviations principles

Large deviations theory is treated well in several standards texts, including
[
174
]
and
[
61
]
. This chapter recalls the formulation that we use in the sequel, and a few technical results that we need.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a complete separable metric space, let
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of Borel probability measures on it, and let
I
be a function from
\mathcal{X}
to
[0,\infty]
.

Definition D.1

(Large deviations principle)
.

The sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
obeys the
large deviations principle
(‘
LDP
’) with
rate function

I
if the following hold:

i.

(Upper bound) If
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
a<I(x)
, then there is a neighbourhood
U
of
x
such that

\mu_{n}U\leq e^{-an+o(n)}.

ii.

(Lower bound) If
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
I(x)<a<\infty
, then any neighbourhood
U
of
x
satisfies

\mu_{n}U\geq e^{-an-o(n)}.

iii.

(Exponential tightness) For every
a>0
there is a compact subset
K
of
\mathcal{X}
such that

\mu_{n}(\mathcal{X}\setminus K)\leq e^{-an+o(n)}.

Remark D.2
.

In the main LDPs prove later, the relevant space
\mathcal{X}
is compact. In this case condition (iii) is superfluous. However, during the proofs we must sometimes restrict attention from
\mathcal{X}
to an open subset of it with its induced topology, and then the additional condition of exponential tightness becomes important. See Section
12.1
, in particular.
\lhd

Put together, conditions (i) and (ii) from Definition
D.1
imply that

I(x)=-\inf_{U}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\mu_{n}U=-\inf_{U}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\mu_{n}U,

where
U
runs over all neighbourhoods of
x
. These in turn imply that
I
must be lower semicontinuous because of the following general principle.

Lemma D.3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a topological space, let
\mathcal{U}
be an open cover of it, let
F
be a function from
\mathcal{U}
to
[-\infty,\infty)
, and let

f(x):=\inf\{F(U):\ U\in\mathcal{U},\,U\ni x\}\qquad(x\in\mathcal{X}).

Then
f
is upper semicontinuous.

Proof.

If
f(x)<a
, then there must be some
U\in\mathcal{U}
such that
U\ni x
and
{F(U)<a}
, and this implies that
f(x^{\prime})<a
for all
x^{\prime}\in U
.
∎

We apply Lemma
D.3
to various other examples later, sometimes with the opposite sign to conclude lower semicontinuity.

In addition, conditions (i)–(iii) from Definition
D.1
imply that
I
must be proper, meaning that its level set
\{I\leq a\}
is compat for every
a\in[0,\infty)
.

An alternative to Definition
D.1
is perhaps more standard. This alternative assumes explicitly that
I
is lower semicontinuous and proper, and then asserts that

\mu_{n}C\leq\exp\big(-\inf_{x\in C}I(x)\cdot n+o(n)\big)

(D.1)

for every closed subset
C
of
\mathcal{X}
, and that

\mu_{n}U\geq\exp\big(-\inf_{x\in U}I(x)\cdot n-o(n)\big)

for every open subset
U
of
\mathcal{X}
. The equivalence of this definition with Definition
D.1
can be found in
[
174
, Section 2.1]
or
[
61
, Subsection 4.1.2]
. (Beware that
[
174
, Section 2.1]
starts with an apparently weaker definition of ‘exponential tightness’ than our condition (iii), but then derives this equivalence as well.)

Now suppose that
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{X}
are complete separable metric spaces and that
\pi:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
is continuous. Suppose in addition that
(\nu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence of Borel probability measures on
\mathcal{Y}
, and let
\mu_{n}:=\pi_{\ast}\nu_{n}
. If the sequence
(\nu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
obeys the LDP with rate function
I
, then the sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
obeys the LDP with rate function

J(x):=\inf_{y\in\pi^{-1}\{x\}}I(x).

(D.2)

This is the
contraction principle
: see
[
174
, Section 2.3]
or
[
61
, Subsection 4.2.1]
.

The next lemma can simplify the family of open sets that we need to check when proving a LDP.

Lemma D.4
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a base for the topology of
\mathcal{X}
. Let (i)—(iii) be the conditions from Definition
D.1
. If condition (i) holds with the restriction that
U\in\mathcal{U}
, then it holds in full, and similarly with condition (ii).

Proof.

Among the neighbourhoods of a given point
x
, both conditions get stronger as
U
gets smaller, so these implications hold because
\mathcal{U}
is a base.
∎

Two simple applications of Lemma
D.4
are used in the sequel.

Lemma D.5
.

Suppose
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(\nu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
obey the LDPs on
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
with rate functions
I
and
J
, respectively. Then
(\mu_{n}\times\nu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
obeys the LDP on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
with the rate function

I(x)+J(y)\qquad((x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}).

∎

Lemma
D.5
holds by applying Lemma
D.4
to the base of product open sets, and by observing that products of compact sets can be used to verify exponential tightness of the product measures. If we assume that
I
and
J
both have minimum value
0
, then the reverse of the implication in Lemma
D.5
also holds, by applying the contraction principle to each coordinate projection.

Our second application of Lemma
D.4
requires a little more preamble. Let
\mathcal{X}_{i}
be a compact metric space for every
i\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{X}:=\prod_{i\geq 1}\mathcal{X}_{i}
, and let
\mathcal{Y}
be a nonempty closed subset of
\mathcal{X}
. For any subsets
F\subset G\subset\mathbf{N}
let

\pi_{F}:\prod_{i\in G}\mathcal{X}_{i}\to\prod_{i\in F}\mathcal{X}_{i}

be the coordinate projection, and let
\mathcal{Y}_{F}:=\pi_{F}[\mathcal{Y}]
. Let
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of Borel probability measures on
\mathcal{Y}
, and let
\mu_{F,n}:=\pi_{F\ast}\mu_{n}
for any
n\in\mathbf{N}
and
F\subset\mathbf{N}
. Finally, let
\mathcal{F}
be an upwards-directed cover of
\mathbf{N}
by finite subsets.

Lemma D.6
.

If
(\mu_{F,n})_{n\geq 1}
obeys the LDP with rate function
I_{F}:\mathcal{Y}_{F}\to[0,\infty]
for every
F\in\mathcal{F}
, then

a.

these rate functions are related by

I_{F}(x)=\inf\{I_{G}(y):\ y\in\mathcal{Y}_{G},\ \pi_{F}(y)=x\}

whenever
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
,
F\subset G
, and
x\in\mathcal{Y}_{F}
, and

b.

the original sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
obeys the LDP with rate function

I(y):=\sup\{I_{F}(\pi_{F}(y)):\ F\in\mathcal{F}\}\qquad(y\in\mathcal{Y}).

∎

Part (a) of Lemma
D.6
is a case of the contraction principle. Part (b) follows from part (a) by applying Lemma
D.4
to the base of open subsets of
\mathcal{Y}
that have the form
\mathcal{Y}\cap\pi_{F}^{-1}[U]
for some
F\in\mathcal{F}
and some open subset
U
of
\mathcal{Y}_{F}
. Indeed, for these sets we have

\mu_{n}(\mathcal{Y}\cap\pi_{F}^{-1}[U])=\mu_{F,n}U,

and for each fixed
F
the right-hand measure here is governed by
I_{F}
as
n\to\infty
.

The last result of this section concerns how an LDP depends on the underlying space on which it is formulated. Let
\mathcal{X}
be a separable and completely metrizable space with an open subset
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Let
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of Borel probability measures on
\mathcal{X}
such that
{\mu_{n}\mathcal{X}_{0}=1}
for all sufficiently large
n
. Then we can regard
\mathcal{X}_{0}
as a separable and completely metrizable space in its own right, and choose a sequence
(\nu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of Borel probability measures on it such that
\nu_{n}=\mu_{n}(\,\cdot\,\cap\mathcal{X}_{0})
for all sufficiently large
n
. It is usually easy to pass probability limit laws between the measures
\mu_{n}
on
\mathcal{X}
and
\nu_{n}
on
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. The next lemma gives general conditions for an LDP to survive such a passage. The important change here is not between one sequence of measures and the other, but rather between choosing
\mathcal{X}_{0}
or
\mathcal{X}
as the ambient space.

Lemma D.7
.

In the situation above, let
I:\mathcal{X}\to[0,\infty]
be lower semicontinuous, and assume that
I(x)=\infty
for every
x\in\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Then
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
obeys the LDP on
\mathcal{X}
with rate function
I
if and only if
(\nu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
obeys the LDP on
\mathcal{X}_{0}
with rate function
I|\mathcal{X}_{0}
.

Proof.

By omitting finitely many terms of the sequence, we may assume that
\mu_{n}\mathcal{X}_{0}=1
and
\nu_{n}=\mu_{n}(\,\cdot\,\cap\mathcal{X}_{0})
for all
n
. In both directions below we refer conditions (i)–(iii) from Definition
D.1
.

Step 1: (
\Rightarrow
)
. If conditions (i) and (ii) hold for neighbourhoods of arbitrary points in
\mathcal{X}
, then they also hold at points in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
if we use neighbourhoods contained in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. These are always available because
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is open.

Now let
a>0
, and let
K
be a compact subset of
\mathcal{X}
witnessing condition (iii) of Definition
D.1
for the sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
. By our assumptions on
I
, the compact set
\{I\leq a\}
is disjoint from the closed set
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{0}
, and so the set
\{I\leq a\}
has an open neighbourhood
U
whose closure is still disjoint from
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Now our choice of
K
and an application of (
D.1
) give

\mu_{n}(\mathcal{X}\setminus(K\cap\overline{U}))\leq\mu_{n}(\mathcal{X}\setminus K)+\mu_{n}(\mathcal{X}\setminus U)\leq e^{-an+o(n)}+e^{-an+o(n)}.

So
K\cap\overline{U}
is a compact subset of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
that witnesses condition (iii) for
(\nu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
.

Step 2: (
\Leftarrow
)
. Conditions (i) and (ii) for
(\nu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
imply the same conditions for
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
around any any point that lies in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. In addition, for any
a>0
, condition (iii) for
(\nu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
gives a compact subset
K
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
such that

\nu_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{0}\setminus K)\leq e^{-an+o(n)}.

Since
K
is also compact as a subset of
\mathcal{X}
, this verifies condition (iii) for the sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
as well. However, it also verifies condition (i) for
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
at every point of
\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{0}
, because
I(x)=\infty
for such points, and the set
\mathcal{X}\setminus K
is an open neighbourhood of them. Lastly, condition (ii) for
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is vacuous at any
x
satisfying
I(x)=\infty
, so we have now verified all three conditions for
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
everywhere.
∎

A concrete application of Lemma
D.7
appears as Corollary
10.23
below.

Notes and further references

Most of the work in this chapter is subsumed by more general machinery that can be found in dedicated textbooks such as
[
61
]
. See
[
61
, Section 1.2]
for a discussion of the difference between a ‘full’ LDP (as we have defined it) and a ‘weak LDP’, which promises (
D.1
) only for compact sets and does not assume exponential tightness. Lemma
D.6
is really a special case of the Dawson–Gärtner theorem about large deviations theory on ‘projective limit’ spaces: see, for instance,
[
61
, Theorems 4.6.1 and 4.6.9]
.

Appendix E
Hyperspaces and Vietoris topologies

E.1

Basic definitions and properties

If
T
is a topological space, then we write
\mathcal{K}(T)
for its
hyperspace
: the space of all nonempty compact subsets of
T
. Unless specified otherwise, this is endowed with the
Vietoris topology
. This is generated by the
Vietoris basic sets
: these are the sets

\mathcal{V}(U_{1},\dots,U_{k}):=\{K\in\mathcal{K}(T):\ K\subset U_{1}\cup\cdots\cup U_{k}\\
\hbox{and}\ K\cap U_{i}\neq\emptyset\ \hbox{for}\ i=1,2,\dots,k\}

as
U_{1}
, …,
U_{k}
range over all open subsets of
T
.

The basic properties of the Vietoris topology can be found in
[
68
, Ex.s 2.7.20 and 3.12.27]
or
[
109
, Subsection I.4.F]
. Here we recall a few that we need without proof.

If
T
is compact and metrizable, then so is
\mathcal{K}(T)
. For example, starting from a compact metric for
T
, a compact metric for
\mathcal{K}(T)
is given by the resulting Hausdorff metric
[
68
, Ex. 4.5.23]
. The examples in our applications below fall into this class, but often without any particular canonical choice of metric, so we generally use the more abstract description in terms of Vietoris basic sets. We restrict our attention to this case in the rest of this section.

Because of metrizability, the Vietoris topology on
\mathcal{K}(T)
may be described in terms of limits. In hyperspaces we can describe this in the following explicit way. Given a sequence
(K_{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
\mathcal{K}(T)
, its
topological upper
and
lower limits
are the sets

\mathrm{T}\limsup_{n\to\infty}K_{n}:=\{t\in T:\ \hbox{every neighbourhood of $t$ meets $K_{n}$}\\
\hbox{for infinitely many $n$}\}

and

\mathrm{T}\liminf_{n\to\infty}K_{n}:=\{t\in T:\ \hbox{every neighbourhood of $t$ meets $K_{n}$}\\
\hbox{for all sufficiently large $n$}\}.

These are both closed. If
t_{n}\in K_{n}
for each
n
, then any subsequential limit of
(t_{n})_{n\geq 1}
lies in
\mathrm{T}\limsup_{n}K_{n}
, so compactness implies that this set is nonempty. Finally, the sequence
(K_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges in the Vietoris topology if and only if

\mathrm{T}\limsup_{n}K_{n}=\mathrm{T}\liminf_{n}K_{n},

(E.1)

and then this common set is the limit of the sequence.

An alternative topology on
\mathcal{K}(T)
is the
lower Vietoris topology
: see, for instance,
[
132
, Appendix]
. This is generated by the basic open sets of the form

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}(U_{1},\dots,U_{k}):=\{K\in\mathcal{K}(T):\ K\cap U_{i}\neq\emptyset\ \hbox{for}\ i=1,2,\dots,k\}

for some open subsets
U_{1}
, …,
U_{k}
of
T
. In the previous notation, this is simply equal to
\mathcal{V}(U_{1},\dots,U_{k},T)
, so the lower Vietoris topology is weaker than the Vietoris topology. Like the Vietoris topology, it is second countable in case
T
is compact and metrizable.

However, in all but degenerate cases, the lower Vietoris topology is much weaker than the Vietoris topology, and also less well-behaved. Indeed, if
T
is a Hausdorff space with at least two points, then the lower Vietoris topology is not T
1
, because for any
K\in\mathcal{K}(T)
the lower-Vietoris closure of the singleton
\{K\}
is the whole of
\mathcal{K}(K)
, regarded as a subset of
\mathcal{K}(T)
. On the other hand, since the lower Vietoris topology is weaker than the Vietoris topology, any subset of
\mathcal{K}(T)
that is Vietoris-closed is still quasi-compact in the lower Vietoris topology (meaning that every open cover has a finite sub-cover, but the space fails to be Hausdorff
[
68
, p132]
).

E.2

Hyperspace-valued random variables

Now suppose that
T
is a compact metrizable space and that
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbf{P})
is a background probability space that supports a sequence of random variables
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
taking values in
\mathcal{K}(T)
. The next lemma is a probabilistic versino of the criterion (
E.1
) in this context.

Lemma E.1
.

The sequence
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges in probability to a deterministic limit in
\mathcal{K}(T)
if and only if every
t\in T
satisfies one of the following:

i.

every neighbourhood
U
of
t
satisfies
\mathbf{P}(U\cap X_{n}\neq\emptyset)\to 1
;

ii.

some neighbourhood
U
of
t
satisfies
\mathbf{P}(U\cap X_{n}=\emptyset)\to 1
.

In this case, the limit is the set of those
t
that satisfy (i), which is necessarily nonempty.

Proof.

Recall the notation
\mathcal{V}(\cdot)
for Vietoris neighbourhoods from Section
E.1
.

(
\Rightarrow
).
Suppose that
K\in\mathcal{K}(T)
and
X_{n}\to K
in probability. If
t\in K
, then for any neighbourhood
U
of
t
we have

\mathbf{P}(U\cap X_{n}\neq\emptyset)=\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\in\mathcal{V}(U,T))\to 1.

On the other hand, if
t\in T\setminus K
, then
t
has a neighbourhood
U
such that
K\subset T\setminus\overline{U}
, and now

\mathbf{P}(U\cap X_{n}=\emptyset)\geq\mathbf{P}(\overline{U}\cap X_{n}=\emptyset)=\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\in\mathcal{V}(T\setminus\overline{U}))\to 1.

(
\Leftarrow
).
Suppose that (i) and (ii) hold, and let
K
be the set of points
t
that satisfy (i). Then
K
is closed, because its complement is explicitly a union of open sets.

If
K
were empty, then for every
t\in T
assumption (ii) would give a neighbourhood
V_{t}
such that

\mathbf{P}(V_{t}\cap X_{n}=\emptyset)\to 1.

Choosing a finite subcover of the compact space
T
, this would imply that also

\mathbf{P}(X_{n}=\emptyset)\to 1,

contradicting our assumption that
X_{n}
takes values in
\mathcal{K}(T)
. So
K\neq\emptyset
, and hence
K\in\mathcal{K}(T)
.

Now consider a general Vietoris neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}(U_{1},\dots,U_{k})
of
K
. Let

L:=T\setminus(U_{1}\cup\cdots\cup U_{k}).

This is an element of
\mathcal{K}(T)
that is disjoint from
K
. Therefore every
t\in L
has a neighbourhood
V_{t}
such that

\mathbf{P}(V_{t}\cap X_{n}=\emptyset)\to 1.

By compactness we can choose a finite subcover
V_{t_{1}}
, …,
V_{t_{\ell}}
of
L
, and it follows that

\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\in\mathcal{V}(U_{1},\dots,U_{k}))\geq\mathbf{P}\Big(\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\{U_{i}\cap X_{n}\neq\emptyset\}\cap\bigcap_{j=1}^{\ell}\{V_{t_{j}}\cap X_{n}=\emptyset\}\Big)\to 1,

applying assumption (i) for the events featuring
U_{i}
and assumption (ii) for the events featuring
V_{t_{j}}
.
∎
